1Darktower 3 - The Waste LandsDarktower 3 - The Waste Lands
2Part 1Darktower 3 - The Waste Lands\n\nDarktower 3 - The Waste Lands\nBy King, Stephen\n\nARGUMENT\n\nThe Waste Lands is the third volume of a longer tale inspired by and to some\ndegree dependent upon Robert Browning’s narrative poem “Childe Roland to the\n\nDark Tower Came.”\n\nThe first volume, The Gunslinger, tells how Roland, the last gun-slinger in a\nworld which has “moved on,” pursues and finally catches the man in black, a\nsorcerer named Walter who falsely claimed the friendship of Roland’s father in\nthe days when the unity of Mid-World still held. Catching this half-human\nspell-caster is not Roland’s ultimate goal but only another landmark along the\nroad to the powerful and mysterious Dark Tower, which stands at the nexus of\ntime.\n\nWho, exactly, is Roland? What was his world like before it moved on? What is the\nTower and why does he pursue it? We have only frag-mentary answers. Roland is\nclearly a land of knight, one of those charged with holding (or possibly\nredeeming) a world Roland remembers as being “filled with love and light.” Just\nhow closely Roland’s memory resembles the way that world actually was is very\nmuch open to question, however.\n\nWe do know that he was forced to an early trial of manhood after discovering\nthat his mother had become the mistress of Marten, a much greater sorcerer than\nWalter; we know that Marten orchestrated Roland’s discovery of his mother’s\naffair, expecting Roland to fail his test of manhood and be “sent West” into the\nwastes; we know that Roland laid Marten’s plans at nines by passing the test.\nWe also know that the gunslinger’s world is related to our own in some strange\nbut fundamental way, and that passage between the worlds is sometimes possible.\nAt a way station on a long-deserted coach-road running through the desert,\nRoland meets a boy named Jake who died in our world, a boy who was, in fact,\npushed from a mid-Manhattan street corner and into the path of an oncoming car.\nJake Chambers died with the man in black—Walter—peering down at him, and awoke\nin Roland’s world.\n\nBefore they reach the man in black, Jake dies again . . . this time because the\ngunslinger, faced with the second most agonizing choice of his life, elects to\nsacrifice this symbolic son. Given a choice between the Tower and the child,\nRoland chooses the Tower. Jake’s last words to the gunslinger before plunging\ninto the abyss are: “Go, then—there are other worlds than these.”\nThe final confrontation between Roland and Walter occurs in a dusty Golgotha of\ndecaying bones. The man in black tells Roland’s future with a deck of Tarot\ncards. Three very strange cards—The Prisoner, The Lady of the Shadows, and Death\n(”but not for you, gunslinger”)—are called especially to Roland’s attention.\nThe second volume, The Drawing of the Three, begins on the edge of the Western\nSea not long after Roland’s confrontation with Walter has ended. An exhausted\ngunslinger awakes in the middle of the night to discover that the incoming tide\nhas brought a horde of crawling, carnivo-rous creatures—“lobstrosities”—with it.\nBefore he can escape their lim-ited range, Roland has been seriously wounded by\nthese creatures, losing the first two fingers of his right hand to them. He is\nalso poisoned by the venom of the lobstrosities, and as the gunslinger resumes\nhis journey north along the edge of the Western Sea, he is sickening . . .\nperhaps dying.\n\nHe encounters three doors standing freely upon the beach. Each door opens—for\nRoland and Roland alone—upon our world; upon the city where Jake lived, in fact.\nRoland visits New York at three points along our time continuum, both in an\neffort to save his own life and to draw the three who must accompany him on his\nroad to the Tower.\n\nEddie Dean is The Prisoner, a heroin addict from the New York of the late 1980s.\nRoland steps through the door on the beach of his world and into Eddie Dean’s\nmind as Eddie, serving a man named Enrico Balazar as a cocaine mule, lands at\nJFK airport. In the course of their harrowing adventures together, Roland is\nable to obtain a limited quantity of penicillin and to bring Eddie Dean back to\nhis own world. Eddie, a junkie who discovers he has been kidnapped to a world\nwhere there is no junk (or Popeye’s fried chicken, for that matter), is less\nthan overjoyed to be there.\n\nThe second door leads Roland to The Lady of the Shadows—actually two women in\none body. This time Roland finds himself in the New York of the early 1960s and\n\nface to face with a young wheelchair-bound civil-rights activist named Odetta\n\nHolmes. The woman hidden inside Odetta is the crafty and hate-filled Detta\nWalker. When this double woman is pulled into Roland’s world, the results are\nvolatile for Eddie and the rapidly sickening gunslinger. Odetta believes that\nwhat’s happening to her is either a dream or a delusion; Detta, a much more\nbrutally direct intellect, simply dedicates herself to the task of killing\nRoland and Eddie whom she sees as torturing white devils.\nJack Mort, a serial killer hiding behind the third door (the New York of the\nmid-1970s), is Death. Mort has twice caused great changes in the life of Odetta\nHolmes/Detta Walker, although neither of them knows it. Mort, whose modus\noperandi is to either push his victims or drop some-thing on them from above,\nhas done both to Odetta during the course of his mad (but oh so careful) career.\nWhen Odetta was a child, he dropped a brick on her head, sending the little girl\ninto a coma and also occasioning the birth of Detta Walker, Odetta’s hidden\nsister. Years later, in 1959, Mort encounters Odetta again and pushes her into\nthe path of an oncoming subway train in Greenwich Village. Odetta survives Mort\nagain, but at a price: the oncoming train severed both legs at the knee. Only\nthe presence of a heroic young doctor (and, perhaps, the ugly but indomitable\nspirit of Detta Walker) saves her life … or so it would seem. To Roland’s eye,\nthese interrelationships suggest a power greater than mere coincidence; he\nbelieves the titanic forces, which surround the Dark Tower, have begun to gather\nonce again.\n\nRoland learns that Mort may stand at the heart of another mystery as well, one\nwhich is also a potentially mind-destroying paradox. For the victim Mort is\nstalking at the time the gunslinger steps into his life is none other than Jake,\nthe boy Roland met at the way station and lost under the mountains. Roland has\nnever had any cause to doubt Jake’s story of how he died in our world, or any\ncause to question who Jake’s murderer was—Walter, of course. Jake saw him\ndressed as a priest as the crowd gathered around the spot where he lay dying,\nand Roland has never doubted the description.\nNor does he doubt it now; Walter was there, oh yes, no doubt about that. But\nsuppose it was Jack Mort, not Walter, who pushed Jake into the path of the\noncoming Cadillac? Is such a thing possible? Roland can’t say, not for sure, but\nif that is the case, where is Jake now? Dead? Alive?\nCaught somewhere in time? And if Jake Chambers is still alive and well in his\nown world of Manhattan in the mid-1970s, how is it that Roland still remembers\nhim?\n\nDespite this confusing and possibly dangerous development, the test of the\ndoors—and the drawing of the three—ends in success for Roland. Eddie Dean\naccepts his place in Roland’s world because he has fallen in love with The Lady\nof the Shadows. Detta Walker and Odetta Holmes, the other two of Roland’s three,\nare driven together into one personality combining elements of both Detta and\nOdetta when the gunslinger is finally able to force the two personalities to\nacknowledge each other. This hybrid is able to accept and return Eddie’s love.\nOdetta Susannah Holmes and Detta Susannah Walker thus become a new woman, a\nthird woman: Susannah Dean.\n\nJack Mort dies beneath the wheels of the same subway—that fabled A-train—which\ntook Odetta’s legs fifteen or sixteen years before. No great loss there.\nAnd for the first time in untold years, Roland of Gilead is no longer alone in\nhis quest for the Dark Tower. Cuthbert and Alain, his lost companions of yore,\nhave been replaced by Eddie and Susannah . . . but the gunslinger has a way of\nbeing bad medicine for his friends. Very bad medicine, indeed.\nThe Waste Lands takes up the story of these three pilgrims on the face of\nMid-World some months after the confrontation by the final door on the beach.\nThey have moved some fair way inland. The period of rest is ending, and a period\nof learning has begun. Susannah is learning to shoot . . . Eddie is learning to\ncarve . . . and the gunslinger is learning how it feels to lose one’s mind, a\npiece at a time.\n\n(One further note: My New York readers will know that I have taken certain\n\ngeographical liberties with the city. For these I hope I may be forgiven.)\n\nA heap of broken images, where the sun heats.\nAnd the dead tree gives no shelter, the cricket no relief,\nAnd the dry stone no sound of water. Only\nThere is shadow under this red rock,\n\n(Come in under the shadow of this red rock),\nAnd I will show you something different from either\nYour shadow in the morning striding behind you\nOr your shadow at evening rising to meet you;\nI will show you fear in a handful of dust.\n—T. S. ELIOT “The Waste Land”\n\nIf there pushed any ragged thistle-stalk\nAbove its mates, the head was chopped; the bents\nWere jealous else. What made those holes and rents\nIn the dock’s harsh swarth leaves, bruised as to balk\nAll hope of greenness? ’tis a brute must walk\nPashing their life out, with a brute’s intents.\n—ROBERT BROWNING “Childe Roland to the Dark Tower Came”\n“What river is it?” enquired Millicent idly.\n“It’s only a stream. Well, perhaps a little more than that.\nIt’s called the Waste.”\n\n“Is it really?”\n\n“Yes,” said Winifred, “it is.”\n\n—ROBERT AICKMAN “Hand in Glove”\n\nBOOK ONE\n\nJAKE\n\nFEAR IN A HANDFUL OF DUST\n\nI • BEAR AND BONE\n\nI BEAR AND BONE\n\nIT WAS HER THIRD time with live ammunition . . . and her first time on the draw\nfrom the holster Roland had rigged for her.\nThey had plenty of live rounds; Roland had brought back better than three\nhundred from the world where Eddie and Susannah Dean had lived their lives up\nuntil the time of their drawing. But having ammu-nition in plenty did not mean\nit could be wasted; quite the contrary, in fact. The gods frowned upon wastrels.\nRoland had been raised, first by his father and then by Cort, his greatest\nteacher, to believe this, and so he still believed. Those gods might not punish\nat once, but sooner or later the penance would have to be paid . . . and the\nlonger the wait, the greater the weight.\nAt first there had been no need for live ammunition, anyway. Roland had been\nshooting for more years than the beautiful brown-skinned woman in the wheelchair\nwould believe. He had corrected her at first simply by watching her aim and\ndry-fire at the targets he had set up. She learned fast. Both she and Eddie\nlearned fast.\n\nAs he had suspected, both were born gunslingers.\nToday Roland and Susannah had come to a clearing less than a mile from the camp\nin the woods which had been home to them for almost two months now. The days had\npassed with their own sweet similarity. The gunslinger’s body healed itself\nwhile Eddie and Susannah learned the things the gunslinger had to teach them:\nhow to shoot, to hunt, to gut and clean what they had killed; how to first\nstretch, then tan and cure the hides of those kills; how to use as much as it\nwas possible to use so that no part of the animal was wasted; how to find north\nby Old Star or south by Old Mother; how to listen to the forest in which they\nnow found themselves, sixty miles or more northeast of the Western Sea. Today\n\nEddie had stayed behind, and the gunslinger was not put out of countenance by\n\nthis. The lessons which are remembered the longest, Roland knew, are always the\nones that are self-taught.\n\nBut what had always been the most important lesson was still most important: how\nto shoot and how to hit what you shot at every time. How to kill.\nThe edges of this clearing had been formed by dark, sweet-smelling fir trees\nthat curved around it in a ragged semicircle. To the south, the ground broke off\nand dropped three hundred feet in a series of crumbling shale ledges and\nfractured cliffs, like a giant’s set of stairs. A clear stream ran out of the\nwoods and across the center of the clearing, first bubbling through a deep\nchannel in the spongy earth and friable stone, then pour-ing across the\nsplintery rock floor which sloped down to the place where the land dropped away.\nThe water descended the steps in a series of waterfalls and made any number of\npretty, wavering rainbows. Beyond the edge of the drop-off was a magnificent\ndeep valley, choked with more firs and a few great old elm trees which refused\nto be crowded out. These latter towered green and lush, trees which might have\nbeen old when the land from which Roland had come was yet young; he could see no\nsign that the valley had ever burned, although he supposed it must have drawn\nlight-ning at some time or other. Nor would lightning have been the only danger.\nThere had been people in this forest in some distant time; Roland had come\nacross their leavings on several occasions over the past weeks. They were\nprimitive artifacts, for the most part, but they included shards of pottery\nwhich could only have been cast in fire. And fire was evil stuff that delighted\nin escaping the hands which created it.\n\nAbove this picturebook scene arched a blameless blue sky in which a few crows\ncircled some miles off, crying in their old, rusty voices. They seemed restless,\nas if a storm were on the way, but Roland had sniffed the air and there was no\nrain in it.\n\nA boulder stood to the left of the stream. Roland had set up six chips of stone\non top of it. Each one was heavily flecked with mica, and they glittered like\nlenses in the warm afternoon light.\n\n“Last chance,” the gunslinger said. “If that holster’s uncomfortable—even tin-\nslightest bit—tell me now. We didn’t come here to waste ammunition.”\nShe cocked a sardonic eye at him, und for a moment he could see Detta Walker in\nthere. It was like ha/y sunlight winking off a bar of steel. “What would you do\nif it was uncomfortable and I didn’t tell you? If I missed all six of those itty\nbitty things? Whop me upside the head like that old teacher of yours used to\ndo?”\n\nThe gunslinger smiled. He had done more smiling these last five weeks than he\nhad done in the five years which had come before them. “I can’t do that, and you\nknow it. We were children, for one thing— children who hadn’t been through our\nrites of manhood yet. You may slap a child to correct him, or her, but—”\n“In my world, whoppin’ the kiddies is also frowned on by the better class of\npeople,” Susannah said dryly.\n\nRoland shrugged. It was hard for him to imagine that sort of world— did not the\nGreat Book say “Spare not the birch so you spoil not the child”?—but he didn’t\nbelieve Susannah was lying. “Your world has not moved on,” he said. “Many things\nare different there. Did I not see for myself that it is so?”\n“I guess you did.”\n\n“In any case, you and Eddie are not children. It would be wrong for me to treat\nyou as if you were. And if tests were needed, you both passed them.”\nAlthough he did not say so, he was thinking of how it had ended on the beach,\nwhen she had blown three of the lumbering lobstrosities to hell before they\ncould peel him and Eddie to the bone. He saw her answering smile and thought she\nmight be remembering the same thing.\n\n“So what you goan do if I shoot fo’ shit?”\n“I’ll look at you. I think that’s all I’ll need to do.”\nShe thought this over, then nodded. “Might be.”\nShe tested the gunbelt again. It was slung across her bosom almost like a\n\nshoulder-holster (an arrangement Roland thought of as a docker’s clutch) and\n\nlooked simple enough, but it had taken many weeks of trial and error—and a great\ndeal of tailoring—to get it just right. The belt and the revolver which cocked\nits eroded sandalwood grip out of the ancient oiled holster had once been the\ngunslinger’s; the holster had hung on his right hip. He had spent much of the\nlast five weeks coming to realize it was never going to hang there again. Thanks\nto the lobstrosi-ties, he was strictly a lefthanded gun now.\n“So how is it?” he asked again.\n\nThis time she laughed up at him. “Roland, this ole gunbelt’s as com’fable as\nit’s ever gonna be. Now do. you want me to shoot or are we just going to sit and\nlisten to crowmusic from over yonder?”\n\nHe felt tension worming sharp little fingers under his skin now, and he supposed\nCort had felt much the same at times like this under his gruff, bluff exterior.\nI le wanted her to be good … needed her to be good. But to show how badly he\nwanted and needed—that could lead to disaster.\n“Tell me your lesson again, Susannah.”\n\nShe sighed in mock exasperation . . . but as she spoke her smile faded and her\ndark, beautiful face became solemn. And from her lips he heard the old catechism\nagain, made new in her mouth. He had never expected to hear these words from a\nwoman. How natural they sounded . . . yet how strange and dangerous, as well.\n” ‘I do not aim with my hand; she who aims with her hand has forgotten the face\nof her father.\n\n” ‘I aim with my eye.\n\n” ‘I do not shoot with my hand; she who shoots with her hand has forgotten the\nface of her father.\n\n” ‘I shoot with my mind.\n\n” ‘I do not kill with my gun—’ “\n\nShe broke off and pointed at the mica-shiny stones on the boulder.\n“I’m not going to kill anything anyhow—they’re just itty bitty rocks.”\nHer expression—a little haughty, a little naughty—suggested that she expected\nRoland to be exasperated with her, perhaps even angry. Roland, however, had been\nwhere she was now; he had not forgotten that apprentice gunslingers were\nfractious and high-spirited, nervy and apt to bite exactly at the wrong moment .\n. . and he had discovered an unexpected capacity in himself. He could teach.\nMore, he liked to teach, and he found himself wondering, from time to time, if\nthat had been true of Cort, as well. He guessed that it had been.\nNow more crows began to call raucously, these from the forest behind them. Some\npart of Roland’s mind registered the fact that the new cries were agitated\nrather than merely quarrelsome; these birds sounded as if they had been scared\nup and away from whatever they had been feeding on. He had more important things\nto think about than whatever it was that had scared a bunch of crows, however,\nso he simply filed the information away and refocused his concentration on\nSusannah. To do otherwise with a ‘prentice was to ask for a second, less playful\nbite. And who would be to blame for that? Who but the teacher? For was he not\ntraining her to bite? Training both of them to bite? Wasn’t that what a\ngunslinger was, when you stripped off the few stern lines of ritual and stilled\nthe few iron grace-notes of catechism? Wasn’t he (or she) only a human hawk,\ntrained to bite on command?\n\n“No,” he said. “They’re not rocks.”\n\nShe raised her eyebrows a little and began to smile again. Now that she saw he\nwasn’t going to explode at her as he sometimes did when she was slow or\nfractious (or at least not yet), her eyes again took on the mocking sun-on-steel\nglint he associated with Detta Walker. “They ain’t?” The teasing in her voice\nwas still good - nut u red, but he thought it would turn mean if he let it. She\nwas tense, keyed up, her claws already halfway out of their sheaths.\n“No, they ain’t,” he said, returning her mockery. His own smile began to return,\nbut it was hard and humorless. “Susannah, do you remember the honk mahfahs?”\nHer smile began to fade.\n\n“The honk mahfahs in Oxford Town?”\n\nHer smile was gone.\n\n“Do you remember what the honk mahfahs did to you and your friends?”\n“That wasn’t me,” she said. “That was another woman.” Her eyes had taken on a\ndull, sullen cast. He hated that look, but he also liked it just fine. It was\nthe right look, the one that said the kindling was burning well and soon the\nbigger logs would start to catch.\n\n“Yes. It was. Like it or not, it was Odetta Susannah Holmes, daugh-ter of Sarah\nWalker Holmes. Not you as you are, but you as you were. Remember the fire-hoses,\nSusannah? Remember the gold teeth, how you saw them when they used the hoses on\nyou and your friends in Oxford? How you saw them twinkle when they laughed?”\nShe had told them these things, and many others, over many long nights as the\ncampfire burned low. The gunslinger hadn’t understood everything, but he had\nlistened carefully, just the same. And remem-bered. Pain was a tool, after all.\nSometimes it was the best tool.\n\n“What’s wrong with you, Roland? Why you want to go recallin that trash in my\nmind?”\n\nNow the sullen eyes glinted at him dangerously; they reminded him of Alain’s\neyes when good-natured Alain was finally roused.\n“Yonder stones are those men,” Roland said softly. “The men who locked you in a\ncell and left you to foul yourself. The men with the clubs and the dogs. The men\nwho called you a nigger cunt.”\n\nHe pointed at them, moving his finger from left to right.\n“There’s the one who pinched your breast and laughed. There’s the one who said\nhe better check and see if you had something stuffed up your ass. There’s the\none who called you a chimpanzee in a five-hundred-dollar dress. That’s the one\nthat kept running his billyclub over the spokes of your wheelchair until you\nthought the sound would send you mad. There’s the one who called your friend\nLeon pinko-fag. And the one on the end, Susannah, is Jack Mort.\n“There. Those stones. Those men.”\n\nShe was breathing rapidly now, her bosom rising and falling in swift little\njerks beneath the gunslinger’s gunbelt with its heavy freight of bul-lets. Her\neyes had left him; they were looking at the mica-flecked chips of stone. Behind\nthem and at some distance, a tree splintered and fell over. More crows called in\nthe sky. Deep in the game which was no longer a game, neither of them noticed.\n“Oh yeah?” she breathed. “That so?”\n\n“It is. Now say your lesson, Susannah Dean, and be true.” - This time the\nwords fell from her lips like small chunks of ice. Her right hand trembled\nlightly on the arm of her wheelchair like an idling engine.\n” ‘I do not aim with my hand; she who aims with her hand has forgotten the face\nof her father.\n\n” ‘I aim with my eye.’ “\n\n“Good.”\n\n” ‘I do not shoot with my hand; she who shoots with her hand has forgotten the\nface of her father.\n\n” ‘I shoot with my mind.’ “\n\n“So it has ever been, Susannah Dean.”\n\n” ‘I do not kill with my gun; she who kills with her gun has forgotten the face\nof her father.\n\n” ‘I kill with my heart.’ “\n\n“Then KILL them, for your father’s sake!” Roland shouted. “KILL THEM ALL!”\nHer right hand was a blur between the arm of the chair and the butt of Roland’s\nsixgun. It was out in a second, her left hand descending, fanning at the hammer\nin flutters almost as swift and delicate as the wing of a hummingbird. Six flat\ncracks pealed off across the valley, and five of the six chips of stone set atop\nthe boulder blinked out of existence.\n\nFor a moment neither of them spoke—did not even breathe, it seemed—as the echoes\nrolled back and forth, dimming. Even the crows were silent, at least for the\ntime being.\n\nThe gunslinger broke the silence with four toneless yet oddly emphatic words:\n\n“It is very well.”\n\nSusannah looked at the gun in her hand as if she had never seen it before. A\ntendril of smoke rose from the barrel, perfectly straight in the windless\nsilence. Then, slowly, she returned it to the holster below her bosom.\n“Good, but not perfect,” she said at last. “I missed one.”\n“Did you?” He walked over to the boulder and picked up the remaining chip of\nstone. He glanced at it, then tossed it to her.\nShe caught it with her left; her right stayed near the bolstered gun, he saw\nwith approval. She shot better and more naturally than Eddie, but had not\nlearned this particular lesson as swiftly as Eddie had done.\nIf she had been with them during the shootout at Balazar’s nightclub, she might\nhave. Now, Roland saw, she was at last learning that, too. She looked at the\nstone and saw the notch, barely a sixteenth of an inch deep, in its upper\ncorner.\n\n“You only clipped it,” Roland said, returning to her, “but in a shooting scrape,\nsometimes that’s all you wed. If you clip a fellow, throw his aim off …” He\npaused. “Why an- you looking at me that way?”\n“You don’t know, do you? You really don’t?”\n“No. Your mind is often closed to me, Susannah.”\nThere was no defensiveness in his voice, and Susannah shook her head in\nexasperation. The rapid turn-and-turn-about dance of her person-ality sometimes\nunnerved him; his seeming inability to say anything other than exactly what was\non his mind never failed to do the same to her. He was the most literal man she\nhad ever met.\n\n“All right,” she said, “I’ll tell you why I’m looking at you that way, Roland.\nBecause what you did was a mean trick. You said you wouldn’t slap me, couldn’t\nslap me, even if I cut up rough . . . but either you lied or you’re very stupid,\nand I know you ain’t stupid. People don’t always slap with their hands, as every\nman and woman of my race could testify. We have a little rhyme where I come\nfrom: ‘Sticks and stones will break my bones—’ ”\n” ‘—yet taunts shall never wound me,’ ” Roland finished.\n“Well, that’s not exactly the way we say it, but I guess it’s close enough. It’s\nbullshit no matter how you say it. They don’t call what you did a tongue-lashing\nfor nothing. Your words hurt me, Roland—are you gonna stand there and say you\ndidn’t know they would?”\n\nShe sat in her chair, looking up at him with bright, stern curiosity, and Roland\nthought—not for the first time—that the honk mahfahs of Susannah’s land must\nhave been either very brave or very stupid to cross her, wheelchair or no\nwheelchair. And, having walked among them, he didn’t think bravery was the\nanswer.\n\n“I did not think or care about your hurt,” he said patiently. “I saw you show\nyour teeth and knew you meant to bite, so I put a stick in your jaws. And it\nworked . . . didn’t it?”\n\nHer expression was now one of hurt astonishment. “You bastardl”\nInstead of replying, he took the gun from her holster, fumbled the cylinder open\nwith the remaining two fingers on his right hand, and began to reload the\nchambers with his left hand.\n\n“Of all the high-handed, arrogant—“\n\n“You needed to bite,” he said in that same patient tone. “Had you not, you would\nhave shot all wrong—with your hand and your gun instead of your eye and mind and\nheart. Was that a trick? Was it arrogant? I think not. I think. Susannah, that\nyou were the one with arrogance in her heart. I think you were the one with a\nmind to get up to tricks. That doesn’t distress me. Quite the opposite. A\ngunslinger without teeth is no gunslinger.”\n“Damn it, I’m not a gunslinger!”\n\nHe ignored that; he could afford to. If she was no gunslinger, then he was a\nbilly-bumbler. “If we were playing a game, I might have behaved differently. But\nthis is no game. It …”\n\nHis good hand went to his forehead for a moment and paused there, fingers tented\n\njust above the left temple. The tips of the fingers, she saw, were trembling\n\nminutely.\n\n“Roland, what’s ailing you?” she asked quietly.\nThe hand lowered slowly. He rolled the cylinder back into place and replaced die\nrevolver in the holster she wore. “Nothing.”\n“Yes there is. I’ve seen it. Eddie has, too. It started almost as soon as we\nleft the beach. It’s something wrong, and it’s getting worse.”\n“There is nothing wrong,” he repeated.\n\nShe put her hands out and took his. Her anger was gone, at least for the time\nbeing. She looked earnestly up into his eyes. “Eddie and I . . . this isn’t our\nworld, Roland. Without you, we’d die here. We’d have your guns, and we can shoot\nthem, you’ve taught us to do that well enough, but we’d die just the same. We\n… we depend on you. So tell me what’s wrong. Let me try to help. Let us try to\nhelp.”\n\nHe had never been a man who understood himself deeply or cared to; the concept\nof self-consciousness (let alone self-analysis) was alien to him. His way was to\nact—to quickly consult his own interior, utterly mysterious workings, and then\nact. Of them all, he had been the most perfectly made, a man whose deeply\nromantic core was encased in a brutally simple box which consisted of instinct\nand pragmatism. He took one of those quick looks inside now and decided to tell\nher everything. There was something wrong with him, oh yes. Many of the huge old elms which Roland had\nnoticed in the valley below had been little more than twigs sprouting from the\nground when the bear came out of the dim unknown reaches of Out-World like a\nbrutal, wandering king.\n\nOnce, the Old People had lived in the West Woods (it was their leavings which\nRoland had found from time to time during the last weeks), and they had gone in\nfear of the colossal, undying bear. They had tried to kill him when they first\ndiscovered they were not alone in the new territory to which they had come, but\nalthough their arrows enraged him, they did no serious damage. And he was not\nconfused about the source of his torment, as were the other beasts of the\nforest— even the predatory bushcats which denned and littered in the sandhills\nto the west. And for\nevery arrow which found its mark in the flesh below his shaggy pelt, he took\nthree, four, perhaps as many as half a dozen of the Old People. The cause didn’t matter; the\nultimate result—a rapidly multiplying colony of parasites foraging within his\nfabu-lous brain—did. Roland had spoken of a thousand things as the\ntwo of them made their tortuous way up the beach from the door marked THE\nPRISONER to the one marked THE LADY OF THE SHADOWS, and he had mentioned what\nseemed like a thousand names in his fever-heated monologues—Alain, Cort, Jamie\nde Curry, Cuthbert (this one more often than all the others), Hax, Martin (or\nper-haps it was Marten, like the animal), Walter, Susan, even a guy with the\nunlikely name of Zoltan.
