And, if he was to be fair, he guessed Roland had gotten as\ntired of Eddie’s own Fractured Fairy Tales—the ones about how he and Henry had\ngrown up together and turned into junkies together—as Eddie had of Roland’s.\n\nBut he couldn’t remember ever telling Roland he would gag him with his own shirt\n\nif he didn’t stop talking about some kid.\n“Nothing comes to you?” Roland asked. “Nothing at all?”\nWas there something? Some far-off tickle, like the feeling of deja vu he’d\ngotten when he saw the slingshot hiding inside the chunk of wood jutting out of\nthe stump? Eddie tried to find that tickle, but it was gone. He decided it had\nnever been there in the first place; he only wanted it to be there, because\nRoland was hurting so badly.\n\n“No,” he said. “Sorry, man.”\n\n“But I did tell you.” Roland’s tone was calm, but urgency ran and pulsed beneath\nit like a scarlet thread. “The boy’s name was Jake. I sacrificed him—killed\nhim—in order that I might finally catch up with Walter and make him talk. I\nkilled him under the mountains.”\n\nOn this point Eddie could be more positive. “Well, maybe that’s what happened,\nbut it’s not what you said happened. You said you went under the mountains\nalone, on some land of crazy handcar. You talked about that a lot while we were\ncoming up the beach, Roland. About how scary it was to be alone.”\n“I remember. But I also remember telling you about the boy, and how he fell from\nthe trestle into the chasm. And it’s the distance between those two memories\nthat is pulling my mind apart.”\n\n“I don’t understand any of this,” Susannah said worriedly.\n“I think,” Roland said, “that I’m just beginning to.”\nHe threw more wood on the fire, sending thick sheaves of red sparks spiralling\nup into the dark sky, and then settled back between them. “I’ll tell you a story\nthat’s true,” he said, “and then I’ll tell you a story that isn’t true . . . but\nshould be.\n\n“I bought a mule in Pricetown, and when I finally got to lull, the last town\nbefore the desert, it was still fresh . . .”\n\nSo THE GUNSLINGER EMBARKED on the most recent part of his long tale. Eddie had\nheard isolated fragments of the story, but he listened in utter fascination, as\ndid Susannah, for whom it was completely new. He told them about the bar with\nthe endless game of Watch Me going on in the corner, the piano player named\nSheb, the woman named Allie with the scar on her forehead . . . and about Nort,\nthe weed-eater who had died and then been brought back to some sort of tenebrous\nlife by the man in black. He told them about Sylvia Pittston, that avatar of\nreligious insanity, and about the final apocalyptic slaughter, in which he,\nRoland the Gunslinger, had killed every man, woman, and child in town.\n“Holy crispy crap!” Eddie said in a low, shaky voice. “Now I know why you were\nso low on shells, Roland.”\n\n“Be quiet!” Susannah snapped. “Let him finish!”\nRoland went on, telling his story as stolidly as he had crossed the desert after\npassing the hut of the last Dweller, a young man whose wild, strawberry-colored\nhair had reached almost to his waist. He told them about how his mule had\nfinally died. He even told them about how the Dweller’s pet bird, Zoltan, had\neaten the mule’s eyes.\n\nHe told them about the long desert days and the short desert nights which had\ncome next, and how he had followed the cool remains of Walter’s campfires, and\nhow he had come at last, reeling and dying of dehydration, to the way station.\n“It was empty. It had been empty, I think, since the days when yonder great bear\nwas still a newly made thing. I stayed a night and pushed on. That’s what\nhappened . . . but now I’ll tell you another story.”\n“The one that isn’t true but should be?” Susannah asked.\nRoland nodded. “In this made-up story—this fable—a gunslinger named Roland met a\nboy named Jake at the way station. This boy was from your world, from your city\nof New York, and from a when some-place between Eddie’s 1987 and Odetta Holmes’s\n1963.”\n\nEddie was leaning forward eagerly. “Is there a door in this story, Roland? A\n\ndoor marked THE BOY, or something like that?”\nRoland shook his head. “The boy’s doorway was death. He was on his way to school\nwhen a man—a man I believed to be Walter—pushed him into the street, where he\nwas run over by a car. He heard this man say something like ‘Get out of the way,\nlet me through, I’m a priest.’ Jake saw this man—just for an instant—and then he\nwas in my world.”\n\nThe gunslinger paused, looking into the fire.\n“Now I want to leave this story of the boy who was never there and go back to\nwhat really happened for a minute. All right?”\nEddie and Susannah exchanged a puzzled glance and then Eddie made an “after you,\nmy dear Alphonse” gesture with his hand.\n“As I have said, the way station was deserted. There was, however, a pump that\nstill worked. It was at the back of the stable where the coach-horses were kept.\nI followed my ears to it, but I would have found it even if it had been\ncompletely silent. I swelled the water, you see. After enough time in the\ndesert, when you are on the edge of dying from thirst, you can really do that. I\ndrank and then slept. When I woke, I drank again. I wanted to push on at\nonce—the need to do that was like a fever. The medicine you brought me from your\nworld—the astin—is wonderful stuff, Eddie, but there are fevers beyond the power\nof any medicine to cure, and this was one of them. I knew my body needed rest,\nbut it still took every ounce of my willpower to stay there even one night. In\nthe morning I felt rested, and so I refilled my waterskins and pushed on. I took\nnothing from that place but water. That’s the most important part of what really\nhappened.”\n\nSusannah spoke in her most reasonable, pleasant, and Odetta Holmes—like voice.\n“All right, that’s what really happened. You refilled your waterskins and went\non. Now tell us the rest of what didn’t happen, Roland.”\nThe gunslinger put the jawbone in his lap for a moment, curled his hands into\nfists, and rubbed his eyes with them—a curiously childlike gesture. Then he\ngrasped the jawbone again, as if for courage, and went on.\n“I hypnotized the boy who wasn’t there,” he said. “I did it with one of my\nshells. It’s a trick I’ve known for years, and I learned it from a very unlikely\nsource—Marten, my father’s court magician. The boy was a good subject. While he\nwas tranced, he told me the circumstances of his death, as I’ve told them to\nyou. When I’d gotten as much of his story as I felt I could without upsetting or\nactually hurting him, I gave him a command that he should not remember anything\nabout his dying when he woke up again.”\n\n“Who’d want to?” Eddie muttered.\n\nRoland nodded. “Who, indeed? The boy passed from his trance directly into a\nnatural sleep. I also slept. When we woke, I told the boy that I meant to catch\nthe man in black. He knew who I meant; Walter had also stopped at the way\nstation. Jake was afraid and hid from him. I’m sure Walter knew he was there,\nbut it suited his purpose to pretend he didn’t. He left the boy behind like a\nset trap.\n\n“I asked him if there was anything to eat there. It seemed to me there must be.\nHe looked healthy enough, and the desert climate is wonderful when it comes to\npreserving things. He had a little dried meat, and he said there was a cellar.\nHe hadn’t explored that, because he was afraid.” The gunslinger looked at them\ngrimly. “He was right to be afraid. I found food . . . and I also found a\nSpeaking Demon.”\n\nEddie looked down at the jawbone with widening eyes. Orange fire-light danced on\nits ancient curves and hoodoo teeth. “Speaking Demon? Do you mean that thing?”\n“No,” he said. “Yes. Both. Listen and you shall understand.”\nHe told them about the inhuman groans he’d heard coming from the earth beyond\nthe cellar; how he had seen sand running from between two of the old blocks\nwhich made up the cellar walls. He told them of approaching the hole that was\nappearing there as Jake screamed for him to come up.\nHe had commanded the demon to speak . . . and so the demon had, in the voice of\n\nAllie, the woman with the scar on her forehead, the woman who had kept the bar\n\nin Tull. Go slow past the Drawers, gun-slinger. While you travel with the boy,\nthe man in black travels with your soul in his pocket.\n“The Drawers?” Susannah asked, startled.\n“Yes.” Roland looked at her closely. “That means something to you, doesn’t it?”\n“Yes . . . and no.”\n\nShe spoke with great hesitation. Some of it, Roland divined, was simple\nreluctance to speak of things which were painful to her. He thought most of it,\nhowever, was a desire not to confuse issues which were already confused by\nsaying more than she actually knew. He admired that. He admired her.\n“Say what you can be sure of,” he said. “No more than that.”\n“All right. The Drawers was a place Detta Walker knew about. A place Detta\nthought about. It’s a slang term, one she picked up from listening to the\ngrownups when they sat out on the porch and drank beer and talked about the old\ndays. It means a place that’s spoiled, or useless, or both. There was something\nin the Drawers—in the idea of the Draw-ers—that called to Detta. Don’t ask me\nwhat; I might have known once, but I don’t anymore. And don’t want to.\n“Detta stole my Aunt Blue’s china plate—the one my folks gave her for a wedding\npresent—and took it to the Drawers—her Drawers—to break it. That place was a\ngravel-pit filled with trash. A dumping-ground. Later on, she sometimes picked\nup boys at roadhouses.”\n\nSusannah dropped her head for a moment, her lips pressed tightly together. Then\nshe looked up again and went on.\n\n“White boys. And when they took her back to their cars in the parking lot, she\ncock-teased them and then ran off. Those parking lots . . . they were the\nDrawers, too. It was a dangerous game, but she was young enough, quick enough,\nand mean enough to play it to the hilt and enjoy it. Later, in New York, she’d\ngo on shoplifting expeditions . . . you know about that. Both of you. Always to\nthe fancy stores—Macy’s, Gimbel’s, Bloomingdale’s—and steal trinkets. And when\nshe made up her mind to go on one of those sprees, she’d think: I’m goan to the\nDrawers today. Goan steal me some shitfum de white folks. Goan steal me sumpin\nforspecial and den break dot sumbitch.”\n\nShe paused, lips trembling, looking into the fire. When she looked around again,\nRoland and Eddie saw tears standing in her eyes.\n“I’m crying, but don’t let that fool you. I remember doing those things, and I\nremember enjoying them. I guess I’m crying because I know I’d do it all again,\nif the circumstances were right.”\n\nRoland seemed to have regained some of his old serenity, his weird equilibrium.\n“We have a proverb in my country, Susannah: ‘The wise thief always prospers.’”\n“I don’t see nothing wise about stealing a bunch of paste jewelry,” she said\nsharply.\n\n“Were you ever caught?”\n\n“No—“\n\nHe spread his hands as if to say, there you have it.\n“So for Detta Walker, the Drawers were bad places?” Eddie asked. “Is that right?\nBecause it doesn’t exactly feel right.”\n\n“Bad and good at the same time. They were powerful places, places where she . .\n. she reinvented herself; I suppose you could say . . . hut they were lost\nplaces, too. And this is all off the subject of Roland’s ghost-boy, isn’t it?”\n“Maybe not,” Roland said. “We had Drawers as well, you see, in my world. It was\nslang for us, too, and the meanings are very similar.”\n“What did it mean to you and your friends?” Eddie asked.\n“That varied slightly from place to place and situation to situation. It might\nmean a trash-midden. It might mean a whorehouse or a place where men came to\ngamble or chew devil-weed. But the most common’ meaning that I know is also the\nsimplest.”\n\nHe looked at them both.\n\n“The Drawers are places of desolation,” he said. “The Drawers are the waste\nlands.”\n\nTHIS TIME SUSANNAH THREW more wood on the fire. In the south, Old Mother blazed\non brilliantly, not flickering. She knew from her school studies what that\nmeant: it was a planet, not a star. Venus? She wondered. Or is the solar system\nof which this world is a part as different as everything else?\nAgain that feeling of unreality—the feeling that all this must surely be a\ndream—washed over her.\n\n“Go on,” she said. “What happened after the voice warned you about the Drawers\nand the little boy?”\n\n“I punched my hand into the hole the sand had come from, as I was taught to do\nif such a thing ever happened to me. What I plucked forth was a jawbone . . .\nbut not this one. The jawbone I took from the wall of the way station was much\nlarger; from one of the Great Old Ones, I have almost no doubt.”\n“What happened to it?” Susannah asked quietly.\n“One night I gave it to the boy,” Roland said. The fire painted his cheeks with\nhot orange highlights and dancing shadows. “As a protec-tion—a kind of talisman.\nLater I felt it had served its purpose and threw it away.”\n“So whose jawbone you got there, Roland?” Eddie asked.\nRoland held it up, looked at it long and thoughtfully, and let it drop back.\n“Later, after Jake . . . after he died … I caught up with the men I had been\nchasing.”\n\n“With Walter,” Susannah said.\n\n“Yes. We held palaver; he and I … long palaver. I fell asleep at some point,\nand when I woke up, Walter was dead. A hundred years dead at least, and probably\nmore. There was nothing left of him but bones, which was fitting enough, since\nwe were in a place of bones.”\n\n“Yeah, it must have been a pretty long palaver, all right,” Eddie said dryly.\nSusannah frowned slightly at this, but Roland only nodded. “Long and long,” he\nsaid, looking into the fire.\n\n“You came to in the morning and reached the Western Sea that very evening,”\nEddie said. “That night the lobstrosities came, right?”\nRoland nodded again. “Yes. But before I left the place where Walter and I had\nspoken … or dreamed … or whatever it was we did … I took this from the\nskull of his skeleton.” He lifted the bone and the orange light again skated off\nthe teeth.\n\nWalter’s jawbone, Eddie thought, and felt a little chill work through him. The\njawbone of the man in black. Remember this, Eddie my boy, the next time you get\nto thinking Roland’s maybe just another one of the guys. He’s been carrying it\naround with him all this time like some kind of a … a cannibal’s trophy.\nJee-sus.\n\n“I remember what I thought when I took it,” Roland said. “I remem-ber very well;\nit is the only memory I have of that time which hasn’t doubled on me. I thought,\n‘It was bad luck to throw away what I found when I found the boy. This will\nreplace it.’ Only then I heard Walter’s laughter—his mean, tittery laughter. I\nheard his voice, too.”\n\n“What did he say?” Susannah asked.\n\n” ‘Too late, gunslinger,’” Roland said. “That’s what he said. ‘Too late—your\nluck will be bad from now until the end of eternity—that is your ka.’ “\n\n“ALL RIGHT,” EDDIE SAID at last. “I understand the basic paradox. Your, memory\nis divided—“\n\n“Not divided. Doubled.”\n\n“All right; it’s almost the same thing, isn’t it?” Eddie grasped a twig and made\nhis own little drawing in the sand:\n\nHe tapped the line on the left. “This is your memory of the time before you got\n\nto the way station—a single track.”\n\n“Yes.”\n\nHe tapped the line on the right. “And after you came out on the far side of the\nmountains in the place of bones . . . the place where Walter was waiting for\nyou. Also a single track.”\n\n“Yes.”\n\nNow Eddie first indicated the middle area and then drew a rough circle around\nit.\n\n“That’s what you’ve got to do, Roland—close this double track off. Build a\nstockade around it in your mind and then forget it. Because it doesn’t mean\nanything, it doesn’t change anything, it’s gone, it’s done—”\n“But it isn’t.” Roland held up the bone. “If my memories of the boy Jake are\nfalse—and I know they are—how can I have this? I took it to replace the one I\nthrew away . . . but the one I threw away came from the cellar of the way\nstation, and along the track I know is true, / never went down cellar! I never\nspoke with the demon! I moved on alone, with fresh water and nothing else!”\n“Roland, listen to me,” Eddie said earnestly. “If that jawbone you’re holding\nwas the one from the way station, that would be one thing. But isn’t it possible\nthat if you hallucinated that whole thing—the way station, the kid, the Speaking\nDemon—then maybe you took Walter’s jawbone because—”\n“It was no hallucination,” Roland said. He looked at them both with his faded\nblue bombardier’s eyes and then did something neither expected . . . something\nEddie would have sworn Roland did not know he meant to do himself.\nHe threw the jawbone into the fire.\n\nFOR A MOMENT IT only lay there, a white relic bent in a ghostly half-grin. Then\nit suddenly blazed red, washing the clearing with dazzling scarlet light. Eddie\nand Susannah cried out and threw their hands up to shield their eyes from that\nburning shape.\n\nThe bone began to change. Not to melt, but to change. The teeth which leaned out\nof it like gravestones began to draw together in clumps.\nThe mild curve of the upper arc straightened, then snubbed down at the tip.\nEddie’s hands fell into his lap and he stared at the bone which was no longer a\nbone with gape-jawed wonder. It was now the color of burn-ing steel. The teeth\nhad become three inverted V’s, the middle one larger than those on the ends. And\nsuddenly Eddie saw what it wanted to become, just as he had seen the slingshot\nin the wood of the stump.\n\nHe thought it was a key.\n\nYou must remember the shape, he thought feverishly. You must, you must.\nHis eyes traced it desperately—three V’s, the one in the center larger and\ndeeper than the two on the end. Three notches . .. and the one closest the end\nhad a squiggle, the shallow shape of a lower-case s .. .\nThen the shape in the flames changed again. The bone which had become something\nlike a key drew inward, concentrating itself into bright, overlapping petals and\nfolds as dark and velvety as a moonless summer midnight. For a moment Eddie saw\na rose—a triumphant rose that might have bloomed in the dawn of this world’s\nfirst day, a thing of depthless, timeless beauty. His eye saw, and his heart was
4Part 3Darktower 3 - The Waste Lands\n\nopened. It was as if all love and life had suddenly risen from Roland’s dead\n\nartifact; it was there in the fire, burning out in triumph and some wonderful,\ninchoate defiance, declaring that despair was a mirage and death a dream.\nThe rose! he thought incoherently. First the key, then the rose! Behold! Behold\nthe opening of the way to the Tower!\n\nThere was a thick cough from the fire. A fan of sparks twisted outwards.\nSusannah screamed and rolled away, beating at the orange flecks on her dress as\nthe flames gushed upward toward the starry sky. Eddie didn’t move. He sat\ntransfixed in his vision, held in a cradle of wonder which was both gorgeous and\nterrible, unmindful of the sparks which danced across his skin. Then the flames\nsank back.\n\nThe bone was gone.\n\nThe key was gone.\n\nThe rose was gone.\n\nRemember, he thought. Remember the rose . . . and remember the shape of the key.\nSusannah was sobbing with shock and terror, but he ignored her for the moment\nand found the stick with which he and Roland had both drawn. And in the dirt he\nmade this shape with a shaking hand:\n\n“WHY DID YOU DO it?” Susannah asked at last. “Why, for God’s sake— and what was\nit?”\n\nFifteen minutes had gone by. The fire had been allowed to burn low; the\nscattered embers had either been stamped out or had gone out on their own. Eddie\nsat with his arms about his wife: Susannah sat before him, with her back against\nhis chest. Roland was off to one side, knees hugged to his chest, looking\nmoodily into the orange-red coals. So far as Eddie could tell, neither of them\nhad seen the bone change. They had both seen it glowing superhot, and Roland had\nseen it explode (or had it imploded? to Eddie that seemed closer to what he had\nseen), but that was all. Or so he believed; Roland, however, sometimes kept his\nown counsel, and when he decided to play his cards close to the vest, he played\nthem very close indeed, Eddie knew that from bitter experience. He thought of\ntelling them what he had seen—or thought he had seen—-and decided to play his\nown cards tight and close-up, at least for the time being.\nOf the jawbone itself there was no sign—not even a splinter.\n“I did it because a voice spoke in my mind and told me I must,” Roland said. “It\nwas the voice of my father; of all my fathers. When one hears such a voice, not\nto obey—and at once—is unthinkable. So I was taught. As to what it was, I can’t\nsay . . . not now, at least. I only know that the bone has spoken its final\nword. I have carried it all this way to hear it.”\nOr to see it, Eddie thought, and again: Remember. Remember the rose. And\nremember the shape of the key.\n\n“It almost flash-fried us!” She sounded both tired and exasperated.\nRoland shook his head. “I think it was more like the sort of firework the barons\nused to sometimes shoot into the sky at their year-end parties. Bright and\nstartling, but not dangerous.”\n\nEddie had an idea. “The doubling in your mind, Roland—is it gone? Did it leave\nwhen the bone exploded, or whatever it did?”\nHe was almost convinced that it had; in the movies he’d seen, such rough\nshock-therapy almost always worked. But Roland shook his head.\nSusannah shifted in Eddie’s arms. “You said you were beginning to understand.”\nRoland nodded. “I think so, yes. If I’m right, I fear for Jake. Wher-ever he is,\nwhenever he is, I fear for him.”\n\n“What do you mean?” Eddie asked.\n\nRoland got up, went to his roll of hides, and began to spread them out. “Enough\nstories and excitement for one night. It’s time to sleep. In the morning we’ll\nfollow the bear’s backtrail and see if we can find the portal he was set to\n\nguard. I’ll tell you what I know and what I believe has happened—what I believe\n\nis happening still—along the way.”\n\nWith that he wrapped himself in an old blanket and a new deerskin, rolled away\nfrom the fire, and would say no more.\n\nEddie and Susannah lay down together. When they were sure the gunslinger must be\nasleep, they made love. Roland heard them going about it as he lay wakeful and\nheard their quiet after-love talk. Most of it was about him. He lay quietly,\nopen eyes looking into the darkness long after their talk had ceased and their\nbreathing had evened out into a single easy note.\nIt was, he thought, fine to be young and in love. Even in the grave-yard which\nthis world had become, it was fine.\n\nEnjoy it while you can, he thought, because there is more death ahead. We have\ncome to a stream of blood. That it will lead us to a river of the same stuff I\nhave no doubt. And, further along, to an ocean. In this world the graves yawn\nand none of the dead rest easy.\n\nAs dawn began to come up in the east, he closed his eyes. Slept briefly. And\ndreamed of Jake.\n\nEDDIE ALSO DREAMED—DREAMED he was back in New York, walking along Second Avenue\nwith a book in his hand.\n\nIn this dream it was spring. The air was warm, the city was blooming, and\nhomesickness sobbed within him like a muscle with a fishhook caught deep within\nit. Enjoy this dream, and make it go on as long as you can, he thought. Savor it\n. . . because this is as close to New York as you’re going to get. You can’t go\nhome, Eddie. That part’s done.\n\nHe looked down at the book and was utterly unsurprised to find it was You Can’t\nGo Home Again, by Thomas Wolfe. Stamped into the dark red cover were three\nshapes; key, rose, and door. He stopped for a moment, flipped the book open, and\nread the first line. The man in black fled across the desert, Wolfe had written,\nand the gunslinger followed.\n\nEddie closed it and walked on. It was about nine in the morning, he judged,\nmaybe nine-thirty, and traffic on Second Avenue was light. Taxis honked and wove\ntheir way from lane to lane with spring sunshine twinkling off their windshields\nand bright yellow paintjobs. A bum on the corner of Second and Fifty-second\nasked him for a handout and Eddie tossed the book with the red cover into his\nlap. He observed (also without surprise) that the bum was Enrico Balazar. He was\nsitting cross-legged in front of a magic shop. HOUSE OF CARDS, the sign in the\nwindow read, and the display inside showed a tower which had been built of Tarot\ncards. Standing on top was a model of King Kong. There was a tiny radar-dish\ngrowing out of the great ape’s head.\n\nEddie walked on, lazing his way downtown, the street-signs floating past him. He\nknew where he was going as soon as he saw it: a small shop on the corner of\nSecond and Forty-sixth.\n\nYeah, he thought. A feeling of great relief swept through him. This is the\nplace. The very place. The window was full of hanging meats and cheeses. TOM AND\nGERRY’S ARTISTIC DELI, the sign read. PARTY PLATTERS OUR SPECIALTY!\nAs he stood looking in, someone else he knew came around the corner. It was Jack\nAndolini, wearing a three-piece suit the color of vanilla ice cream and carrying\na black cane in his left hand. Half of his face was gone, lopped off by the\nclaws of the lobstrosities.\n\nGo on in, Eddie, Jack said as he passed. After all, there are other worlds than\nthese and that fuckin train rolls through all of them.\nI can’t, Eddie replied. The door is locked. He didn’t know how he knew this, but\nhe did; knew it beyond a shadow of a doubt.\nDad-a-chum, dud-a-chee, not to worry, you’ve got the key, Jack said, not looking\nback. Eddie looked down and saw he did have a key; a primitive-looking thing\nwith three notches like inverted Vs.\n\nThat little s-shape at the end of the last notch is the secret, he thought. He\n\nstepped under the awning of Tom and Gerry’s Artistic Deli and inserted the key\nin the lock. It turned easily. He opened the door and stepped through into a\nhuge open field. He looked back over his shoulder and saw the traffic on Second\nAvenue hurrying by, and then the door slammed shut and fell over. There was\nnothing behind it. Nothing at all. He turned back to survey his new\nsurroundings, and what he saw filled him with terror at first. The field was a\ndeep scarlet, as if some titanic battle had been fought here and the ground had\nbeen drenched with so much blood that it could not all be absorbed.\nThen he realized that it was not blood he was looking at, but roses.\nThat feeling of mingled joy and triumph surged through him again, swelling his\nheart until he felt it might burst within him. He raised his clenched fists high\nover his head in a gesture of victory . . . and then froze that way.\nThe field stretched on for miles, climbing a gentle slope of land, and standing\nat the horizon was the Dark Tower. It was a pillar of dumb stone rising so high\ninto the sky that he could barely discern its tip. Its base, surrounded by red,\nshouting roses, was formidable, titanic with weight and size, yet the Tower\nbecame oddly graceful as it rose and tapered. The stone of which it had been\nmade was not black, as he had imagined it would be, but soot-colored. Narrow,\nslitted windows marched about it in a rising spiral; below the windows ran an\nalmost endless flight of stone stairs, circling up and up. The Tower was a dark\ngray exclamation point planted in the earth and rising above the field of\nblood-red roses. The sky arched above it was blue, but filled with puffy white\nclouds like sailing ships. They flowed above and around the top of the Dark\nTower in an endless stream.\n\nHow gorgeous it is! Eddie marvelled. How gorgeous and strange! But his feeling\nof joy and triumph had departed; he was left with a sense of deep malaise and\nimpending doom. He looked about him and realized with sudden horror that he was\nstanding in the shadow of the Tower. No, not just standing in it; buried alive\nin it.\n\nHe cried out but his cry was lost in the golden blast of some tremen-dous horn.\nIt came from the top of the Tower, and seemed to fill the world. As that note of\nwarning held and drew out over the field where he stood, blackness welled from\nthe windows which girdled the Tower. It overspilled them and spread across the\nsky in flaggy streams which came together and formed a growing blotch of\ndarkness. It did not look like a cloud; it looked like a tumor hanging over the\nearth. The sky was blotted out. And, he saw, it was not a cloud or a tumor but a\nshape, some tenebrous, cyclopean shape racing toward the place where he stood.\nIt would do no good to run from that beast coalescing in the sky above the field\nof roses; it would catch him, clutch him, and bear him away. Into the Dark Tower\nit would bear him, and the world of light would see him no more.\nRents formed in the darkness and terrible inhuman eyes, each easily the size of\nthe bear Shardik which lay dead in the forest, peered down at him. They were\nred—red as roses, red as blood.\n\nJack Andolini’s dead voice hammered in his ears: A thousand worlds, Eddie—ten\nthousand!—and that train rolls through every one. If you can get it started. And\nif you do get it started, your troubles are only begin-ning, because this device\nis a real bastard to shut down.\n\nJack’s voice had become mechanical, chanting. A real bastard to shut down, Eddie\nboy, you better believe it, this bastard is—\n“—SHUTTING DOWN! SHUTDOWN WILL BE COMPLETE IN ONE HOUR AND SIX MINUTES!”\nIn his dream, Eddie threw his hands up to shield his eyes …\n\n. . . AND WOKE, SITTING BOLT upright beside the dead campfire. He was looking at\nthe world from between his own spread fingers. And still that voice rolled on\nand on, the voice of some heartless SWAT Squad com-mander bellowing through a\nbullhorn.\n\n“THERE IS NO DANGER! REPEAT, THERE IS NO DANGER! FIVE SUBNUCLEAR CELLS ARE\n\nDORMANT, TWO SUBNUCLEAR CELLS ARE NOW IN SHUTDOWN PHASE, ONE SUBNUCLEAR CELL IS\nOPERATING AT TWO PER CENT CAPACITY. THESE CELLS ARE OF NO VALUE! REPEAT, THESE\nCELLS ARE OF NO VALUE! REPORT LOCATION TO NORTH CENTRAL POSITRONICS, LIMITED!\nCALL 1-900-44! THE CODE WORD FOR THIS DEVICE IS ‘SHARDIK.’ REWARD IS OFFERED!\nREPEAT, REWARD ZS OFFERED!”\n\nThe voice fell silent. Eddie saw Roland standing at the edge of the clearing,\nholding Susannah in the crook of one arm. They were staring toward the sound of\nthe voice, and as the recorded announcement began again, Eddie was finally able\nto shake off the chill remnants of his nightmare. He got up and joined Roland\nand Susannah, wondering how many centuries it had been since that announcement,\npro-grammed to broadcast only in the event of a total system breakdown, had been\nrecorded.\n\n“THIS DEVICE IS SHUTTING DOWN! SHUTDOWN WILL BE COMPLETE IN ONE HOUR AND FIVE\nMINUTES! THERE IS NO DANGER! REPEAT—“\n\nEddie touched Susannah’s arm and she looked around. “How long has this been\ngoing on?”\n\n“About fifteen minutes. You were dead to the w—” She broke off. “Eddie, you look\nterrible! Are you sick?”\n\n“No. I just had a bad dream.”\n\nRoland was studying him in a way that made Eddie feel uncomfort-able. “Sometimes\nthere’s truth in dreams, Eddie. What was yours?”\nHe thought for a moment, then shook his head. “I don’t remember.”\n“You know, I doubt that.”\n\nEddie shrugged and favored Roland with a thin smile. “Doubt away, then—be my\nguest. And how are you this morning, Roland?”\n“The same,” Roland said. His faded blue eyes still conned Eddie’s face.\n“Stop it,” Susannah said. Her voice was brisk, but Eddie caught an undertone of\nnervousness. “Both of you. I got better things to do than watch you two dance\naround and kick each other’s shins like a couple of little kids playin Two for\nFlinching. Specially this morning, with that dead bear trying to yell down the\nwhole world.”\n\nThe gunslinger nodded, but kept his eyes on Eddie. “All right . . . but are you\nsure there’s nothing you want to tell me, Eddie?”\nHe thought about it then—really thought about telling. What he had seen in the\nfire, what he had seen in his dream. He decided against it. Perhaps it was only\nthe memory of the rose in the fire, and the roses\nwhich had blanketed that dream-field in such fabulous profusion. Me knew he\ncould not tell these things as his eyes had seen them and his heart had felt\nthem; he could only cheapen them. And, at least for the time being, he wanted to\nponder these things alone.\n\nBut remember, he told himself again . . . except the voice in his mind didn’t\nsound much like his own. It seemed deeper, older—the voice of a stranger.\nRemember the rose . . . and the shape of the key.\n“I will,” he murmured.\n\n“You will what?” Roland asked.\n\n“Tell,” Eddie said. “If anything comes up that seems, you know, really\nimportant, I’ll tell you. Both of you. Right now there isn’t. So if we’re going\nsomewhere, Shane, old buddy, let’s saddle up.”\n“Shane? Who is this Shane?”\n\n“I’ll tell you that some other time, too. Meantime, let’s go.”\nThey packed the gear they had brought with them from the old campsite and headed\nback, Susannah riding in her wheelchair again. Eddie had an idea she wouldn’t be\nriding in it for long.\n\nONCE, BEFORE EDDIE HAD become too interested in the subject of heroin to be\n\ninterested in much else, he and a couple of friends had driven over to New\nJersey to see a couple of speed-metal groups— Anthrax and Megadeth—in concert at\nthe Meadowlands. He believed that Anthrax had been slightly louder than the\nrepeating announcement coming from the fallen bear, but he wasn’t a hundred per\ncent sure. Roland stopped them while they were still half a mile from the\nclearing in the woods and tore six small scaps of cloth from his old shirt. They\nstuffed them in their ears and then went on. Even the cloth didn’t do much to\ndeaden the steady blast of sound.\n\n“THIS DEVICE IS SHUTTING DOWN!” the bear blared as they stepped into the\nclearing again. It lay as it had lain, at the foot of the tree Eddie had\nclimbed, a fallen Colossus with its legs apart and its knees in the air, like a\nfurry female giant who had died trying to give birth. “SHUTDOWN WILL BE COMPLETE\nIN FORTY-SEVEN MINUTES! THERE IS NO DANGER—”\nYes, there is, Eddie thought, picking up the scattered hides which had not been\nshredded in either the bear’s attack or its flailing death-throes. Plenty of\ndanger. To my fucking ears. He picked up Roland’s gunbelt and silently handed it\nover. The chunk of wood he had been working on lay nearby; he grabbed it and\ntucked it into the pocket in the hack of Susannah’s wheelchair as the gunslinger\nslowly buckled the wide leather belt around his waist and cinched the rawhide\ntiedown.\n\n“—IN SHUTDOWN PHASE, ONE SUBNUCLEAR CELL OPERATING AT ONE PER CENT CAPACITY.\nTHESE CELLS—“\n\nSusannah followed Eddie, holding in her lap a carry-all bag she had sewn\nherself. As Eddie handed her the hides, she stuffed them into the bag. When all\nof them were stored away, Roland tapped Eddie on the arm and handed him a\nshoulderpack. What it contained mostly was deer-meat, heavily salted from a\nnatural lick Roland had found about three miles up the little creek. The\ngunslinger had already donned a similar pack. His purse—restocked and once again\nbulging with all sorts of odds and ends—hung from his other shoulder.\nA strange, home-made harness with a seat of stitched deerskin dan-gled from a\nnearby branch. Roland plucked it off, studied it for a moment, and then draped\nit over his back and knotted the straps below his chest. Susannah made a sour\nface at diis, and Roland saw it. He did not try to speak—this close to the bear,\nhe couldn’t have made himself heard even by shouting at the top of his voice—but\nhe shrugged sympatheti-cally and spread his hands: You know well need it.\nShe shrugged back. / know . . . but that doesn’t mean I like it.\nThe gunslinger pointed across the clearing. A pair of leaning, splin-tered\nspruce trees marked the place where Shardik, who had once been known as Mir in\nthese parts, had entered the clearing.\n\nEddie leaned toward Susannah, made a circle with his thumb and forefinger, then\nraised his eyebrows interrogatively. Okay?\nShe nodded, then pressed the heels of her palms against her ears. Okay—but let’s\nget out of here before I go deaf.\n\nThe three of them moved across the clearing, Eddie pushing Susan-nah, who held\nthe bag of hides in her lap. The pocket in the back of her wheelchair was\nstuffed with other items; die piece of wood with the slingshot still mostly\nhidden inside it was only one of them.\n\nFrom behind them the bear continued to roar out its final communi-cation to the\nworld, telling them shutdown would be complete in forty minutes. Eddie couldn’t\nwait. The broken spruces leaned in toward each other, forming a rude gate, and\nEddie thought: This is where the quest for Roland’s Dark Tower really begins, at\nleast for us.\n\nHe thought of his dream again—the spiraling windows issuing their unfurling\nflags of darkness, flags which spread over the field of roses like a stain—and\nas they passed beneath the leaning trees, a deep shudder gripped him.\n\nTHEY WERE ABLE TO use the wheelchair longer than Roland had expected. The firs\n\nof this forest were very old, and their spreading branches had created a deep\ncarpet of needles which discouraged most undergrowth. Susannah’s arms were\nstrong—stronger than Eddie’s, although Roland did not think that would be true\nmuch longer—and she wheeled herself along easily over the level, shady forest\nfloor. When they came to one of the trees the bear had pushed over, Roland\nlifted her out of the chair and Eddie boosted it over the obstacle.\nFrom behind them, only a little deadened by distance, the bear told them, at the\ntop of its mechanical voice, that the capacity of its last operating nuclear\nsubcell was now negligible.\n\n“I hope you keep that damn harness lying empty over your shoulders all day!”\nSusannah shouted at the gunslinger.\n\nRoland agreed, but less than fifteen minutes later the land began to slope\ndownward and this old section of the forest began to be invaded with smaller,\nyounger trees: birch, alder, and a few stunted maples scrab-bling grimly in the\nsoil for purchase. The carpet of needles thinned and the wheels of Susannah’s\nchair began to catch in the low, tough bushes which grew in the alleys between\nthe trees. Their thin branches boinged and rattled in the stainless steel\nspokes. Eddie threw his weight against the handles and they were able to go on\nfor another quarter of a mile that way. Then the slope began to grow more steep,\nand the ground underfoot became mushy.\n\n“Time for a pig-back, lady,” Roland said.\n“Let’s try the chair a little longer, what do you say? Going might get easier—”\nRoland shook his head. “If you try that hill, you’ll . . . what did you call it,\nEddie? … do a dugout?”\n\nEddie shook his head, grinning. “It’s called doing a doughnut, Roland. A term\nfrom my misspent sidewalk-surfing days.”\n“Whatever you call it, it means landing on your head. Come on, Susannah. Up you\ncome.”\n\n“I hate being a cripple,” Susannah said crossly, but allowed Eddie to hoist her\nout of the chair and worked with him to seat herself firmly in the harness\nRoland wore on his back. Once she was in place, she touched the butt of Roland’s\npistol. “Y’all want this baby?” she asked Eddie.\nHe shook his head. “You’re faster. And you know it, too.”\nShe grunted and adjusted the belt, settling the gunbutt so it was easily\naccessible to her right hand. “I’m slowing you boys down and I know that . . .\nbut if we ever make it to some good old two-lane blacktop, I’ll leave the both\nof you kneelin in the blocks.”\n\n“I don’t doubt it,” Roland said . . . and then cocked his head. The woods had\nfallen silent.\n\n“Br’er Bear has finally given up,” Susannah said. “Praise God.”\n“I thought it still had seven minutes to go,” Eddie said.\nRoland adjusted the straps of the harness. “Its clock must have started running\na little slow during the last five or six hundred years.”\n“You really think it was that old, Roland?”\nRoland nodded. “At least. And now it’s passed . . . the last of the Twelve\nGuardians, for all we know.”\n\n“Yeah, ask me if I give a shit,” Eddie replied, and Susannah laughed.\n“Are you comfortable?” Roland asked her.\n“No. My butt hurts already, but go on. Just try not to drop me.”\nRoland nodded and started down the slope. Eddie followed, pushing the empty\nchair and trying not to bang it too badly on the rocks which had begun to jut\nout of the ground like big white knuckles. Now that the bear had finally shut\nup, he thought the forest seemed much too quiet—it almost made him feel like a\ncharacter in one of those hokey old jungle movies about cannibals and giant\napes.\n\nTHE BEAR’S BACKTRAIL WAS easy to find but tougher to follow. Five miles or so\n\nout of the clearing, it led them through a low, boggy area that was not quite a\nswamp. By the time the ground began to rise and firm up a little again, Roland’s\nfaded jeans were soaked to the knees and he was breathing in long, steady rasps.\nStill, he was in slightly better shape than Eddie, who had found wrestling\nSusannah’s wheelchair through the muck and standing water hard going.\n“Time to rest and eat something,” Roland said.\n“Oh boy, gimme eats,” Eddie puffed. He helped Susannah out of the harness and\nset her down on the bole of a fallen tree with claw-marks slashed into its trunk\nin long diagonal grooves. Then he half-sat, half-collapsed next to her.\n“You got my wheelchair pretty muddy, white boy,” Susannah said. “It’s all goan\nbe in my repote.”\n\nHe cocked an eyebrow at her. “Next carwash we come to, I’ll push you through\nmyself. I’ll even Turtle-wax the goddamn thing. Okay?”\nShe smiled. “You got a date, handsome.”\n\nEddie had one of Roland’s waterskins cinched around his waist. He tapped it.\n“Okay?”\n\n“Yes,” Roland said. “Not too much now; a little more for all of us before we set\nout again. That way no one takes a cramp.”\n“Roland, Eagle Scout of Oz,” Eddie said, and giggled as he unslung the\nwaterskin.\n\n“What is this Oz?”\n\n“A make-believe place in a movie,” Susannah said.\n“Oz was a lot more than that. My brother Henry used to read me the stories once\nin a while. I’ll tell you one some night, Roland.”\n“That would be fine,” the gunslinger replied seriously. “I am hungry to know\nmore of your world.”\n\n“Oz isn’t our world, though. Like Susannah said, it’s a make-believe place—”\nRoland handed them chunks of meat which had been wrapped in broad leaves of some\nsort. “The quickest way to learn about a new place is to know what it dreams of.\nI would hear of this Oz.”\n\n“Okay, that’s a date, too. Suze can tell you the one about Dorothy and Toto and\nthe Tin Woodman, and I’ll tell you all the rest.” He bit into his piece of meat\nand rolled his eyes approvingly. It had taken the flavor of the leaves in which\nit had been rolled, and was delicious. Eddie wolfed his ration, stomach gurgling\nbusily all the while. Now that he was getting his breath back, he felt\ngood—great, in fact. His body was growing a solid sheath of muscle, and every\npart of it felt at peace with every other part.\nDon’t worry, he thought. Everything will be arguing again by tonight. I think\nhe’s gonna push on until I’m ready to drop in my tracks.\nSusannah ate more delicately, chasing every second or third bite with a little\nsip of water, turning the meat in her hands, eating from the outside in. “Finish\nwhat you started last night,” she invited Roland. “You said you thought you\nunderstood these conflicting memories of yours.”\nRoland nodded. “Yes. I think both memories are true. One is a little truer than\nthe other, but that does not negate the truth of that other.”\n“Makes no sense to me,” Eddie said. “Either this boy Jake was at the way station\nor he wasn’t, Roland.”\n\n“It is a paradox—something that is and isn’t at the same time. Until it’s\nresolved, I will continue divided. That’s bad enough, but the basic split is\nwidening. I can feel that happening. It is … unspeakable.”\n“What do you think caused it?” Susannah asked.\n“I told you the boy was pushed in front of a car. Pushed. Now, who do we know\nwho liked to push people in front of things?”\nUnderstanding dawned in her face. “Jack Mort. Do you mean he was the one who\npushed this boy into the street?”\n\n“Yes.”\n\n“But you said the man in black did it,” Eddie objected. “Your buddy Walter. You\nsaid that the boy saw him—a man who looked like a priest. Didn’t the kid even\n\nhear him say he was? ‘Let me through, I’m a priest,’ something like that?”\n\n“Oh, Walter was there. They were both there, and they both pushed Jake.”\n“Somebody bring the Thorazine and the strait-jacket,” Eddie called. “Roland just\nwent over the high side.”\n\nRoland paid no attention to this; he was coming to understand that Eddie’s jokes\nand clowning were his way of dealing with stress. Cuthbert had not been much\ndifferent … as Susannah was, in her way, not so different from Alain. “What\nexasperates me about all of this,” he said, “is that I should have known. I was\nin Jack Mort, after all, and I had access to his thoughts, just as I had access\nto yours, Eddie, and yours, Susannah. I saw Jake while I was in Mort. I saw him\nthrough Mort’s eyes, and I knew Mort planned to push him. Not only that; I\nstopped him from doing it. All I had to do was enter his body. Not that he knew\nthat was what it was; he was concentrating so hard on what he planned to do that\nhe actually thought I was a fly landing on his neck.”\nEddie began to understand. “If Jake wasn’t pushed into the street, he never\ndied. And if he never died, he never came into this world. And if he never came\ninto this world, you never met him at the way station. Right?”\n“Right. The thought even crossed my mind that if Jack Mort meant to kill the\nboy, I would have to stand aside and let him do it. To avoid creating the very\nparadox that is tearing me apart. But I couldn’t do that. I … I …”\n“You couldn’t kill this kid twice, could you?” Eddie asked softly. “Every time I\njust about make up my mind that you’re as mechanical as that bear, you surprise\nme with something that actually seems human. Goddam.”\n“Quit it, Eddie,” Susannah said.\n\nEddie took a look at the gunslinger’s slightly lowered face and gri-maced.\n“Sorry, Roland. My mother used to say that my mouth had a bad habit of running\naway with my mind.”\n\n“It’s all right. I had a friend who was the same way.”\n“Cuthbert?”\n\nRoland nodded. He looked at his diminished right hand for a long moment, then\nclenched it into a painful fist, sighed, and looked up at them again. Somewhere,\ndeeper in the forest, a lark sang sweetly.\n“Here is what I believe. If I had not entered Jack Mort when I did, he still\nwouldn’t have pushed Jake that day. Not then. Why not? Ka-tet. Simply that. For\nthe first time since the last of the friends with whom I set forth on this quest\ndied, I have found myself once again at the center of ka-tet.”\n“Quartet?” Eddie asked doubtfully.\n\nThe gunslinger shook his head. “Ka—the word you think of as ‘des-tiny,’ Eddie,\nalthough the actual meaning is much more complex and hard to define, as is\nalmost always the case with words of the High Speech. And tet, which means a\ngroup of people with the same interests and goals. We three are a tet, for\ninstance. Ka-tet, is the place where many lives are joined by fate.”\n“Like in The Bridge of San Luis Rey,” Susannah murmured.\n“What’s that?” Roland asked.\n\n“A story about some people who die together when the bridge they’re crossing\ncollapses. It’s famous in our world.”\n\nRoland nodded his understanding. “In this case, ka-tet bound Jake, Walter, Jack\nMort, and me. There was no trap, as I first suspected when I realized who Jack\nMort meant to be his next victim, because ka-tet cannot be changed or bent to\nthe will of any one person. But ka-tet can be seen, known, and understood.\nWalter saw, and Walter knew.” The gunslinger struck his thigh with his fist and\nexclaimed bitterly, “How he must have been laughing inside when I finally caught\nup to him!”\n\n“Let’s go back to what would have happened if you hadn’t messed up Jack Mort’s\nplans on the day he was following Jake,” Eddie said. “You’re saying that if you\nhadn’t stopped Mort, someone or something else would have. Is that right?”\n“Yes—because it wasn’t the right day for Jake to die. It was close to the right\nday, but not the right day. I felt that, too. Perhaps, just before he did it,\nMort would have seen someone watching him. Or a perfect stranger would have\n\nintervened. Or—“\n\n“Or a cop,” Susannah said. “He might have seen a cop in the wrong place and at\nthe wrong time.”\n\n“Yes. The exact reason—the agent of ka-tet—doesn’t matter. I know from firsthand\nexperience that Mort was as wily as an old fox. If he sensed any slightest thing\nwrong, he would have called it off and waited for another day.\n“I know something else, as well. He hunted in disguise. On the day he dropped\nthe brick on Detta Holmes’s head, he was wearing a knitted cap and an old\nsweater several sizes too big for him. He wanted to look like a winebibber,\nbecause he pushed the brick from a building where a large number of sots kept\ntheir dens. You see?”\n\nThey nodded.\n\n“On the day, years later, when he pushed you in front of the train, Susannah, he\nwas dressed as a construction worker. He was wearing a big yellow helmet he\nthought of as a ‘hardhat’ and a fake moustache. On the day when he actually\nwould have pushed Jake into traffic, causing his death, he would have been\ndressed as a priest.”\n\n“Jesus,” Susannah nearly whispered. “The man who pushed him in New York was Jack\nMort, and the man he saw at the way station was this fella you were\nchasing—Walter.”\n\n“Yes.”\n\n“And the little boy thought they were the same man because they were both\nwearing the same kind of black robe?”\n\nRoland nodded. “There was even a physical resemblance between Walter land Jack\nMort. Not as if they were brothers, I don’t mean that, but both were tall men\nwith dark hair and very pale complexions. And given the fact that Jake was dying\nwhen he got his only good look at Mort and was in a strange place and scared\nalmost witless when he got his only good look at Walter, I think his mistake was\nboth understandable and forgivable. If there’s a horse’s ass in this picture,\nit’s me, for not realizing the truth sooner.”\n“Would Mort have known he was being used?” Eddie asked. Think-ing back to his\nown experiences and wild thoughts when Roland had invaded his mind, he didn’t\nsee how Mort could not know . . . but Roland was shaking his head.\n“Walter would have been extremely subtle. Mort would have thought the priest\ndisguise his own idea … or so I believe. And so I ended up saving Jake’s life\nafter all. Why are you\nshaking your head?”\n\n“Because I don’t think Walter would want that to happen. What Walter wanted is\nwhat is happening … for Roland to be losing his mind, bit by bit. Now he looked up, and\nthe blazing clarity of his eyes made Eddie flinch backward. “Have I?” he asked\nharshly. “Have I really? I’m going insane an inch at a time, trying to live with\ntwo versions of the same reality. I had hoped at first that one or the other\nwould begin to fade away, but that’s not happening. Eddie stared into the faded blue\neyes blazing out of Roland’s pale face and could not think of a thing to say.\n\nTHEY CAMPED ABOUT FIFTEEN miles due east of the dead bear that night, slept the\nsleep of the completely exhausted (even Roland slept the night through, although\nhis dreams were nightmare carnival-rides), and were up the next morning at\nsunrise. Eddie kindled a small fire without speaking, and glanced at Susannah as\na pistol-shot rang out in the woods nearby.\n“Breakfast,” she said.\n\nRoland returned three minutes later with a hide slung over one shoulder. On it\nlay the freshly gutted corpse of a rabbit. Susannah cooked it. They ate and\nmoved on.\n\nEddie kept trying to imagine what it would be like to have a memory of your own\ndeath. On that one he kept coming up short.\n\nSHORTLY AFTER NOON THEY entered an area where most of the trees had been pulled\nover and the bushes mashed flat—it looked as though a cyclone had touched down\nhere many years before, creating a wide and dismal alley of destruction.\n“We’re close to the place we want to find,” Roland said. “He pulled down\neverything to clear the sightlines. Our friend the bear wanted no surprises. He\nwas big, but not complacent.”\n\n“Has it left us any surprises?” Eddie asked.\n“He may have done so.” Roland smiled a little and touched Eddie on the shoulder.\n“But there’s this—they’ll be old surprises.”\nTheir progress through this zone of destruction was slow. Most of the fallen\ntrees were very old—many had almost rejoined the soil from which they had\nsprung—but they still made enough of a tangle to create a formidable obstacle\ncourse. It would have been difficult enough if all three of them had been\nable-bodied; with Susannah strapped to the gunslinger’s back in her harness, it\nbecame an exercise in aggravation and endurance.\nThe flattened trees and jumbles of underbrush served to obscure the bear’s\nbacktrail, and that also worked to slow their speed. Until mid-day they had\nfollowed claw-marks as clear as trail-blazes on the trees. Here, however, near\nits starting point, the bear’s rage had not been full-blown, and these handy\nsigns of its passage disappeared.
