A steel plate proclaimed it to be the work of North\nCentral Positronics, Ltd. There was a serial number, but no name. Probably too\nunimportant to name, he thought. Just a sophisti-cated mechanical Roto-Rooter\ndesigned to give old Br’er Bear an enema every once In a while, keep him\nregular, or something equally disgusting.\nHe dropped the snake and wiped his hands on his pants.\nRoland had picked up the tractor-gadget. He yanked at one of the treads. It came\noff easily, showering a cloud of rust down between his boots. He tossed it\naside.\n\n“Everything in the world is either coming to rest or falling to pieces,” he said\nflatly. “At the same time, the forces which interlock and give the world its\ncoherence—in time and size as well as in space—are weakening. We knew that even\nas children, but we had no idea what the time of the end would be like. How\ncould we? Yet now I am living in those times, and I don’t believe they affect my\nworld alone. They affect yours, Eddie and Susannah; they may affect a billion\nothers. The Beams are breaking down. I don’t know if that’s a cause or only\nanother symptom, but I know it’s true. Come! Draw close! Listen!”\nAs Eddie approached the metal box with its alternating diagonal slashes of\nyellow and black, a strong and unpleasant memory seized him—for the first time\nin years he found himself thinking of a crumbling Victorian wreck in Dutch Hill,\nabout a mile away from the neighborhood he and Henry had grown up. This wreck,\nwhich was known as The Mansion to the neighborhood kids, occupied a plot of\nweedy, untended lawn on Rhinehold Street. Eddie guessed that practically all the\nkids in the borough had heard spooky stories about The Mansion. The house stood\nslumped beneath its steep roofs, seeming to glare at passersby from the deep\nshadows thrown by its eaves. The windows were gone, of course—kids can throw\nrocks through windows without getting too close to a place—hut it had not been\nspray-painted, and it had not become a make-out spot or a shooting gallery.\nOddest of all was the simple fact of its continued existence: no one had set it\non fire to collect the insurance or just to see it bum. The kids said it was\nhaunted, of course, and as Eddie stood on the sidewalk with Henry one day,\nlooking at it (they had made the pilgrimage specifically to see this object of\nfabulous rumor, although Henry had told their mother they were only going for\nHoodsie Rockets at Dahlberg’s with some of his friends), it had seemed that it\nreally might be haunted. Hadn’t he felt some strong and unfriendly force seeping\nfrom that old Victorian’s shadowy windows, windows that seemed to look at him\nwith the fixed stare of a dangerous lunatic? Hadn’t he felt some subtle wind\nstirring the hairs on his arms and the back of his neck? Hadn’t he had the clear\nintuition that if he stepped inside that place, the door would slam and lock\nbehind him and the walls would begin to close in, grinding the bones of dead\nmice to powder, wanting to crush his bones the same way?\nHaunting. Haunted.\n\nHe felt that same old sense of mystery and danger now, as he approached the\nmetal box. Gooseflesh began to ripple up his legs and down his arms; the hair on\nthe back of his neck bushed out and became rough, overlapping hackles. He felt\nthat same subtle wind blowing past him, although the leaves on the trees which\nringed the clearing were perfectly still.\nYet he walked toward the door anyway (for that was what it was, of course,\nanother door, although this one was locked and always would be against the likes\nof him), not stopping until his ear was pressed against it.\nIt was as if he had dropped a tab of really strong acid half an hour ago and it\nwas just beginning to come on heavy. Strange colors flowed across the darkness\nbehind his eyeballs. He seemed to hear voices mur-muring up to him from long\nhallways like stone throats, halls which were lit with guttering electric\ntorches. Once these flambeaux of the modern age had thrown a bright glare across\neverything, but now they were only sullen cores of blue light. He sensed\nemptiness . . . desertion . . . desola-tion . . . death.\n\nThe machinery rumbled on and on, but wasn’t there a rough under-tone to the\n\nsound? A land of desperate thudding beneath the hum, like the arrhythmia of a\ndiseased heart? A feeling that the machinery produc-ing this sound, although far\nmore sophisticated even than that within the bear had been, was somehow falling\nout of tune with itself?\n\n“All is silent in the halls of the dead,” Eddie heard himself whisper in a\nfalling, fainting voice. “All is forgotten in the stone halls of the dead.\nBehold the stairways which stand in darkness; behold the rooms of ruin.\nThese are the halls of the dead where the spiders spin and the great circuits\ntall quiet, one by one.”\n\nRoland pulled him roughly back, and Eddie looked at him with dazed eyes.\n“That’s enough,” Roland said.\n\n“Whatever they put in there isn’t doing so well, is it?” Eddie heard himself\nask. His trembling voice seemed to come from far away. He could still feel the\npower coming out of that box. It called to him.\n“No. Nothing in my world is doing so well these days.”\n“If you boys are planning to camp here for the night, you’ll have to do without\nthe pleasure of my company,” Susannah said. Her face was a white blur in the\nashy aftermath of twilight. “I’m going over yonder. I don’t like the way that\nthing makes me feel.”\n\n“We’ll all camp over yonder,” Roland said. “Let’s go.”\n“What a good idea,” Eddie said. As they moved away from the box, the sound of\nthe machinery began to dim. Eddie felt its hold on him weakening, although it\nstill called to him, invited him to explore the half-lit hallways, the standing\nstairways, the rooms of ruin where the spiders spun and the control panels were\ngoing dark, one by one.\n\nIN His DREAM THAT night, Eddie again went walking down Second Ave-nue toward Tom\nand Gerry’s Artistic Deli on the corner of Second and Forty-sixth. He passed a\nrecord store and the Rolling Stones boomed from the speakers:\n“I see a red door and I want to paint it black,\nNo colours anymore, I want them to turn black,\nI see the girls walk by dressed in their summer clothes,\nI have to turn my head until my darkness goes …”\nHe walked on, passing a store called Reflections of You between Forty-ninth and\nForty-eighth. He saw himself in one of the mirrors hang-ing in the display\nwindow. He thought he looked better than he had in years—hair a little too long,\nbut otherwise tanned and fit. The clothes, though . . . uh-uh, man. Square-bear\nshit all the way. Blue blazer, white shirt, dark red tie, gray dress pants …\nhe had never owned a yuppie-from-hell outfit like that in his life.\nSomeone was shaking him.\n\nEddie tried to burrow deeper into the dream. He didn’t want to wake up now. Not\nbefore he got to the deli and used his key to go through the door and into the\nfield of roses. He wanted to see it all again—the endless blanket of red, the\noverarching blue sky where those great white cloud-ships sailed, and the Dark\nTower. He was afraid of the darkness which lived within that eldritch column,\nwaiting to eat anyone who got too close, but he wanted to see it again just the\nsame. Needed to see it.\n\nThe hand, however, would not stop shaking. The dream began to darken, and the\nsmells of car exhaust along Second Avenue became the smell of woodsmoke—thin\nnow, because the fire was almost out.\n\nIt was Susannah. She looked scared. Eddie sat up and put an arm around her. They\nhad camped on the far side of the alder grove, within earshot of the stream\nbabbling through the bone-littered clearing. On the other side of the glowing\nembers which had been their campfire, Roland lay asleep. His sleep was not easy.\n\nHe had cast aside his single blanket and lay with his knees drawn up almost to\n\nhis chest. With his boots off, his feet looked white and narrow and defenseless.\nThe great toe of the right foot was gone, victim of the lobster-thing which had\nalso snatched away part of his right hand.\nHe was moaning some slurred phrase over and over again. After a few repetitions,\nEddie realized it was the phrase he had spoken before keeling over in the\nclearing where Susannah had shot the bear: Go, then—there are other worlds than\nthese. He would fall silent for a moment, then call out the boy’s name: “Jake!\nWhere are you? Jake!”\n\nThe desolation and despair in his voice filled Eddie with horror. His arms stole\naround Susannah and he pulled her tight against him. He could feel her\nshivering, although the night was warm.\n\nThe gunslinger rolled over. Starlight fell into his open eyes.\n“Jake, where are you?” he called to the night. “Come back!”\n“Oh Jesus—he’s off again. What should we do, Suze?”\n“I don’t know. I just knew I couldn’t listen to it anymore by myself. He sounds\nso far away. So far away from everything.”\n“Go, then,” the gunslinger murmured, rolling back onto his side and drawing his\nknees up once more, “there are other worlds than these.” He was silent for a\nmoment. Then his chest hitched and he loosed the boy’s name in a long,\nbloodcurdling cry. In the woods behind them, some large bird flew away in a dry\nwhirr of wings toward some less exciting part of the world.\n“Do you have any ideas?” Susannah asked. Her eyes were wide and wet with tears.\n“Maybe we should wake him up?”\n\n“I don’t know.” Eddie saw the gunslinger’s revolver, the one he wore on his left\nhip. It had been placed, in its holster, on a neatly folded square of hide\nwithin easy reach of the place where Roland lay. “I don’t think I dare,” he\nadded at last.\n\n“It’s driving him crazy.”\n\nEddie nodded.\n\n“What do we do about it? Eddie, what do we do?”\nEddie didn’t know. An antibiotic had stopped the infection caused by the bite of\nthe lobster-thing; now Roland was burning with infection again, but Eddie didn’t\nthink there was an antibiotic in the world that would cure what was wrong with\nhim this time.\n\n“I don’t know. Lie down with me, Suze.”\n\nEddie threw a hide over both of them, and after a while her trembling quieted.\n“If he goes insane, he may hurt us,” she said.\n“Don’t I know it.” This unpleasant idea had occurred to him in terms of the\nbear—its red, hate-filled eyes (and had there not been bewilderment as well,\nlurking deep in those red depths?) and its deadly slashing claws. Eddie’s eyes\nmoved to the revolver, lying so close to the gunslinger’s good left hand, and he\nremembered again how fast Roland had been when he’d seen the mechanical bat\nswooping down toward them. So fast his hand had seemed to disappear. If the\ngunslinger went mad, and if he and Susannah became the focus of that madness,\nthey would have no chance. No chance at all.\nHe pressed his face into the warm hollow of Susannah’s neck and closed his eyes.\nNot long after, Roland ceased his babbling. Eddie raised his head and looked\nover. The gunslinger appeared to be sleeping naturally again. Eddie looked at\nSusannah and saw that she had also gone to sleep. He lay down beside her, gently\nkissed the swell of her breast, and closed his own eyes.\nNot you, buddy; you’re gonna be awake a long, long time.\nBut they had been on the move for two days and Eddie was bone-tired. He drifted\noff … drifted down.\n\nBack to the dream, he thought as he went. I want to go back to Second Avenue . .\n. back to Tom and Gerry’s. That’s what I want.\nThe dream did not return that night, however.\n\nTHEY ATE A QUICK breakfast as the sun came up, repacked and redistrib-uted the\ngear, and then returned to the wedge-shaped clearing. It didn’t look quite so\nspooky in the clear light of morning, but all three of them were still at pains\nto keep well away from the metal box with its warning slashes of black and\nyellow. If Roland had any recollection of the bad dreams which had haunted him\nin the night, he gave no sign. He had gone about the morning chores as he always\ndid, in thoughtful, stolid silence.\n\n“How do you plan to keep to a straight-line course from here?” Susannah asked\nthe gunslinger.\n\n“If the legends are right, that should be no problem. Do you remem-ber when you\nasked about magnetism?”\n\nShe nodded.\n\nHe rummaged deep into his purse and at last emerged with a small square of old,\nsupple leather. Threaded through it was a long silver needle.\n“A compass!” Eddie said. “You really are an Eagle Scout!”\nRoland shook his head. “Not a compass. I know what they are, of course, but\nthese days I keep my directions by the sun and stars, and even now they serve me\nquite well.”\n\n“Even now?” Susannah asked, a trifle uneasily.\nHe nodded. “The directions of the world are also in drift.”\n“Christ,” Eddie said. He tried to imagine a world where true north was slipping\nslyly off to the east or west and gave up almost at once. It made him feel a\nlittle ill; the way looking down from the top of a high building had always made\nhim feel a little ill.\n\n“This is just a needle, but it is steel and it should serve our purpose as well\nas a compass. The Beam is our course now, and the needle will show it.” He\nrummaged in his purse again and came out with a poorly made pottery cup. A crack\nran down one side. Roland had mended this artifact, which he had found at the\nold campsite, with pine-gum. Now he went to the stream, dipped the cup into it,\nand brought it back to where Susannah sat in her wheelchair. He put the cup down\ncarefully on the wheelchair’s arm, and when the surface of the water inside was\ncalm, he dropped the needle in. It sank to the bottom and rested there.\n“Wow!” Eddie said. “Great! I’d fall at your feet in wonder, Roland, but I don’t\nwant to spoil the crease in my pants.”\n\n“I’m not finished. Hold the cup steady, Susannah.”\nShe did, and Roland pushed her slowly across the clearing. When she was about\ntwelve feet in front of the door, he turned the chair carefully so she was\nfacing away from it.\n\n“Eddie!” she cried. “Look at this!”\n\nHe bent over the pottery cup, marginally aware that water was already oozing\nthrough Roland’s makeshift seal. The needle was rising slowly to the surface. It\nreached it and bobbed there as serenely as a cork would have done. Its direction\nlay in a straight line from the portal behind them and into the old, tangled\nforest ahead. “Holy shit—a floating needle. Now I really have seen everything.”\n“Hold the cup, Susannah.”\n\nShe held it steady as Roland pushed the wheelchair further into the clearing, at\nright angles to the box. The needle lost its steady point, bobbed randomly for a\nmoment, then sank to the bottom of the cup again. When Roland pulled the chair\nbackward to its former spot, it rose once more and pointed the way.\n“If we had iron filings and a sheet of paper,” the gunslinger said, “we could\nscatter the filings on the paper’s surface and watch them draw together into a\nline which would point that same course.”\n“Will that happen even when we leave the Portal?” Eddie asked.\nRoland nodded. “Nor is that all. We can actually see the Beam.”\nSusannah looked over her shoulder. Her elbow bumped the cup a little as she did.\nThe needle swung aimlessly as the water inside sloshed . . . and then settled\nfirmly back in its original direction.\n\n“Not that way,” Roland said. “Look down, both of you—Eddie at your feet,\n\nSusannah into your lap.”\n\nThey did as he asked.\n\n“When I tell you to look up, look straight ahead, in the direction the needle\npoints. Don’t look at any one thing; let your eye see whatever it will. Now—look\nup!”\n\nThey did. For a moment Eddie saw nothing but the woods. He tried to make his\neyes relax . . . and suddenly it was there, the way the shape of the slingshot\nhad been there, inside the knob of wood, and he knew why Roland had told them\nnot to look at any one thing. The effect of the Beam was everywhere along its\ncourse, but it was subtle. The needles of the pines and spruces pointed that\nway. The greenberry bushes grew slightly slanted, and the slant lay in the\ndirection of the Beam. Not all the trees the bear had pushed down to clear its\nsightlines had fallen along that camouflaged path—which ran southeast, if Eddie\nhad his direc-tions right—but most had, as if the force coming out of the box\nhad pushed them that way as they tottered. The clearest evidence was in the way\nthe shadows lay on the ground. With the sun coming up in the east they all\npointed west, of course, but as Eddie looked southeast, he saw a rough\nherringbone pattern that existed only along the line which the needle in the cup\nhad pointed out.\n\n“I might see something” Susannah said doubtfully, “but—”\n“Look at the shadows! The shadows, Suze!”\nEddie saw her eyes widen as it all fell into place for her. “My God! It’s there!\nRight there! It’s like when someone has a natural part in their hair!”\nNow that Eddie had seen it, he could not unsee it; a dim aisle driving through\nthe untidy tangle which surrounded the clearing, a straight-edge course that was\nthe way of the Beam. He was suddenly aware of how huge the force flowing around\nhim (and probably right through him, like X-rays) must be, and had to control an\nurge to step away, either to the right or left. “Say, Roland, this won’t make me\nsterile, will it?”\n\nRoland shrugged, smiling faintly.\n\n“It’s like a riverbed,” Susannah marvelled. “A riverbed so over-grown you can\nbarely see it … but it’s still there. The pattern of shadows will never change\nas long as we stay inside the path of the Beam, will it?”\n“No,” Roland said. “They’ll change direction as the sun moves across the sky, of\ncourse, but we’ll always be able to see the course of the Beam. You must\nremember that it has been flowing along this same path for thousands—perhaps\ntens of thousands—of years. Look up, you two, into the sky!”\nThey did, and saw that the thin cirrus clouds had also picked up that\nherringbone pattern along the course of the Beam . . . and those clouds within\nthe alley of its power were flowing faster than those to either side. They were\nbeing pushed southeast. Being pushed in the direction of the Dark Tower.\n“You see? Even the clouds must obey.”\n\nA small flock of birds coursed toward them. As they reached the path of the\nBeam, they were all deflected toward the southeast for a moment. Although Eddie\nclearly saw this happen, his eyes could hardly credit it. When the birds had\ncrossed the narrow corridor of the Beam’s influence, they resumed their former\ncourse.\n\n“Well,” Eddie said, “I suppose we ought to get going. A journey of a thousand\nmiles begins with a single step, and all that shit.”\n“Wait a minute.” Susannah was looking at Roland. “It isn’t just a thousand\nmiles, is it? Not anymore. How far are we talking about, Roland? Five thousand\nmiles? Ten?”\n\n“I can’t say. It will be very far.”\n\n“Well, how in the hell we ever goan get there, with you two pushing me in this\ngoddam wheelchair? We’ll be lucky to make three miles a day through yonder\nDrawers, and you know it.”\n\n“The way has been opened,” Roland said patiently, “and that’s enough for now.\nThe time may come, Susannah Dean, when we travel faster than you would like.”\n“Oh yeah?” She looked at him truculently, and both men could see Detta Walker\n\ndancing a dangerous hornpipe in her eyes again. “You got a race-car lined up? If\n\nyou do, it might be nice if we had a damn road to run it on!”\n“The land and the way we travel on it will change. It always does.”\nSusannah flapped a hand at the gunslinger; go on with you, it said. “You sound\nlike my old mamma, sayin God will provide.”\n“Hasn’t He?” Roland asked gravely.\n\nShe looked at him for a moment in silent surprise, then threw her head back and\nlaughed at the sky. “Wt-11, I guess that depends on how you look at it. All I\ncan say is that if this is providin, Roland, I’d hate to see what’d happen if He\ndecided to let us go hungry.”\n\n“Come on, let’s do it,” Eddie said. “I want to get out of this place. I don’t\nlike it.” And that was true, but that wasn’t all. He also felt a deep eagerness\nto set his feet upon that concealed path, that highway in hiding. Every step was\na step closer to the field of roses and the Tower which dominated it. He\nrealized—not without some wonder—that he meant to see that Tower … or die\ntrying.\n\nCongratulations, Roland, he thought. You’ve done it. I’m one of the converted.\nSomeone say hallelujah.\n\n“There’s one other thing before we go.” Roland bent and untied the rawhide lace\naround his left thigh. Then he slowly began to unbuckle his gunbelt.\n“What’s this jive?” Eddie asked.\n\nRoland pulled the gunbelt free and held it out to him. “You know why I’m doing\nthis,” he said calmly.\n\n“Put it back on, man!” Eddie felt a terrible stew of conflicting emo-tions\nroiling inside him; could feel his fingers trembling even inside his clenched\nfists. “What do you think you’re doing?”\n“Losing my mind an inch at a time. Until the wound inside me closes—if it ever\ndoes—I am not fit to wear this. And you know it.”\n“Take it, Eddie,” Susannah said quietly.\n“If you hadn’t been wearing this goddamn thing last night, when that bat came at\nme, I’d be gone from the nose up this morning!”\nThe gunslinger replied by continuing to hold his remaining gun out to Eddie. The\nposture of his body said he was prepared to stand that way all day, if that was\nwhat it took.\n\n“All right!” Eddie cried. “Goddammit, all right!”\nHe snatched the gunbelt from Roland’s hand and buckled it about his own waist in\na series of rough gestures. He should have been relieved, he supposed—hadn’t he\nlooked at this gun, lying so close to Roland’s hand in the middle of the night,\nand thought about what might happen if Roland really did go over the high side?\nHadn’t he and Susannah both thought about it? But there was no relief. Only fear\nand guilt and a strange, aching sadness far too deep for tears.\nHe looked so strange without his guns.\n\nSo wrong.\n\n“Okay? Now that the numb-fuck apprentices have the guns and the master’s\nunarmed, can we please go? If something big comes out of the bush at us, Roland,\nyou can always throw your knife at it.”\n\n“Oh, that,” he murmured. “I almost forgot.” He took the knife from his purse and\nheld it out, hilt first, to Eddie.\n\n“This is ridiculous!” Eddie shouted.\n\n“Life is ridiculous.”\n\n“Yeah, put it on a postcard and send it to the fucking Reader’s Digest.” Eddie\njammed the knife into his belt and then looked defiantly at Roland. “Now can we\ngo?”\n\n“There is one more thing,” Roland said.\n\n“Weeping, creeping Jesus!”\n\nThe smile touched Roland’s mouth again. “Just joking,” he said.\nEddie’s mouth dropped open. Beside him, Susannah began to laugh again. The sound\nrose, as musical as bells, in the morning stillness.\n\nIT TOOK THEM MOST of the morning to clear the zone of destruction with which the\ngreat bear had protected itself, but the going was a little easier along the\npath of the Beam, and once they had put the deadfalls and tangles of underbrush\nbehind them, deep forest took over again and they were able to move at better\nspeed. The brook which had emerged from the rock wall in the clearing ran busily\nalong to their right. It had been joined by several smaller streamlets, and its\nsound was deeper now. There were more animals here—they heard them moving\nthrough the woods, going about their daily round—and twice they saw small groups\nof deer. One of them, a buck with a noble rack of antlers on its upraised and\nquestioning head, looked to be at least three hundred pounds. The brook bent\naway from their path as they began to climb again. And, as the afternoon began\nto slant down toward evening, Eddie saw something.\n“Could we stop here? Rest a minute?”\n\n“What is it?” Susannah asked.\n\n“Yes,” Roland said. “We can stop.”\n\nSuddenly Eddie felt Henry’s presence again, like a weight settling on his\nshoulders. Oh lookit the sissy. Does the sissy see something in the twee? Does\nthe sissy want to carve something? Does he? Ohhhh, ain’t that CUTE?\n“We don’t have to stop. I mean, no big deal. I just—”\n“—saw something,” Roland finished for him. “Whatever it is, stop running your\neverlasting mouth and get it.”\n\n“It’s really nothing.” Eddie felt warm blood mount into his face. He tried to\nlook away from the ash tree which had caught his eye.\n“But it is. It’s something you need, and that’s a long way from nothing. If you\nneed it, Eddie, we need it. What we don’t need is a man who can’t let go of the\nuseless baggage of his memories.”\n\nThe warm blood turned hot. Eddie stood with his flaming face pointed at his\nmoccasins for a moment longer, feeling as if Roland had looked directly into his\nconfused heart with his faded blue bombardier’s eyes.\n“Eddie?” Susannah asked curiously. “What is it, dear?”\nHer voice gave him the courage he needed. He walked to the slim, straight ash,\npulling Roland’s knife from his belt.\n\n“Maybe nothing,” he muttered, and then forced himself to add: “Maybe a lot. If I\ndon’t fuck it up, maybe quite a lot.”\n\n“The ash is a noble tree, and full of power,” Roland remarked from behind him,\nbut Eddie barely heard. Henry’s sneering, hectoring voice was gone; his shame\nwas gone with it. He thought only of the one branch that had caught his eye. It\nthickened and bulged slightly as it ran into the trunk. It was this oddly shaped\nthickness that Eddie wanted.\n\nHe thought the shape of the key was buried within it—the key he had seen briefly\nin the fire before the burning remains of the jawbone had changed again and the\nrose had appeared. Three inverted V’s, the center V both deeper and wider than\nthe other two. And the little s-shape at the end. That was the secret.\nA breath of his dream recurred: Dad-a-chum, dud-a-chee, not to worry, you’ve got\nthe key.\n\nMaybe, he thought. But this time I’ll have to get all of it. I think that this\ntime ninety per cent just won’t do.\n\nWorking with great care, he cut the branch from the tree and then trimmed the\nnarrow end. He was left with a fat chunk of ash about nine inches long. It felt\nheavy and vital in his hand, very much alive and willing enough to give up its\nsecret shape … to a man skillful enough to tease it out, that was.\nWas he that man? And did it matter?\n\nEddie Dean thought the answer to both questions was yes.\nThe gunslinger’s good left hand closed over Eddie’s right hand. “I think you\nknow a secret.”\n\n“Maybe I do.”\n\n“Can you tell?”\n\nHe shook his head. “Better not to, I think. Not yet.”\n\nRoland thought this over, then nodded. “All right. I want to ask you one\nquestion, and then we’ll drop the subject. Have you perhaps seen some way into\nthe heart of my . . . my problem?”\n\nEddie thought: And that’s as dose as he’ll ever come to showing the desperation\nthat’s eating him alive.\n\n“I don’t know. Right now I can’t tell for sure. But I hope so, man. I really,\nreally do.”\n\nRoland nodded again and released Eddie’s hand. “I thank you. We still have two\nhours of good daylight—why don’t we make use of them?”\n“Fine by me.”\n\nThey moved on. Roland pushed Susannah and Eddie walked ahead of them, holding\nthe chunk of wood with the key buried in it. It seemed to throb with its own\nwarmth, secret and powerful.\n\nTHAT NIGHT, AFTER SUPPER was eaten, Eddie took the gunslinger’s knife from his\nbelt and began to carve. The knife was amazingly sharp, and seemed never to lose\nits edge. Eddie worked slowly and carefully in the firelight, turning the chunk\nof ash this way and that in his hands, watching the curls of fine-grained wood\nrise ahead of his long, sure strokes.\n\nSusannah lay down, laced her hands behind her head, and looked Up at the stars\nwheeling slowly across the black sky.\n\nAt the edge of the campsite, Roland stood beyond the glow of the fire and\nlistened as the voices of madness rose once more in his aching, confused mind.\nThere was a boy.\n\nThere was no boy.\n\nWas.\n\nWasn’t.\n\nWas—\n\nHe closed his eyes, cupped his aching forehead in one cold hand, and wondered\nhow long it would be until he simply snapped like an overwound bowstring.\nOh Jake, he thought. Where are you? Where are you?\nAnd above the three of them, Old Star and Old Mother rose into their appointed\nplaces and stared at each other across the starry ruins of their ancient broken\nmarriage.\n\nII • KEY AND ROSE\n\nII\n\nKEY AND ROSE\n\nFOR THREE WEEKS JOHN “Jake” Chambers fought bravely against the madness rising\ninside him. During that time he felt like the last man aboard a foundering ocean\nliner, working the bilge-pumps for dear life, trying to keep the ship afloat\nuntil the storm ended, the skies cleared, and help could arrive . . . help from\nsomewhere. Help from anywhere. On May 31st, 1977, four days before school ended\nfor the summer, he finally faced up to the fact that no help was going to come.\nIt was time to give up; time to let the storm carry him away.\nThe straw that broke the camel’s back was his Final Essay in English Comp.\nJohn Chambers, who was Jake to the three or four boys who were almost his\nfriends (if his father had known this little factoid, he undoubt-edly would have\nhit the roof), was finishing his first year at The Piper School. Although he was\neleven and in the sixth grade, he was small for his age, and people meeting him\nfor the first time often thought he was much younger. In fact, he had sometimes\nbeen mistaken for a girl until a year or so ago, when he had made such a fuss\nabout having his hair cut short that his mother had finally relented and allowed\nit. With his father, of course, there had been no problem about the haircut. His\nfather had just grinned his hard, stainless steel grin and said, The kid wants\nto look like a Marine, Laurie. Good for him.\nTo his father, he was never Jake and rarely John. To his father, he was usually\njust “the kid.”\n\nThe Piper School, his father had explained to him the summer before (the\nBicentennial Summer, that had been—all bunting and flags and New York Harbor\nfilled with Tall Ships), was, quite simply, The Best Damned School In The\nCountry For A Boy Your Age. The fact that Jake had been accepted there had\nnothing to do with money, Elmer Chambers explained . . . almost insisted. He\nhad been savagely proud of this fact, although, even at ten, Jake had suspected\nit might not be a true fact, that it might really be a bunch of bullshit his\nfather had turned into a fact so he could casually drop it into the conversation\nat lunch or over cocktails: My kid? Oh, he’s going to Piper. Best Damned School\nIn The Country For A Boy His Age. Money won’t buy you into that school, you\nknow; for Piper, it’s brains or nothing.\nJake was perfectly aware that in the fierce furnace of Elmer Cham-bers’s mind,\nthe gross carbon of wish and opinion was often blasted into the hard diamonds\nwhich he called facts. … or, in more informal circumstances, “factoids.” His\nfavorite phrase, spoken often and with rev-erence, was the fact is, and he used\nit every chance he got.\n\nThe fact is, money doesn’t get anyone into The Piper School, his father had told\nhim during that Bicentennial Summer, the summer of blue skies and bunting and\nTall Ships, a summer which seemed golden in Jake’s mem-ory because he had not\nyet begun to lose his mind and all he had to worry about was whether or not he\ncould cut the mustard at The Piper School, which sounded like a nest for newly\nhatched geniuses. The only thing that gets you into a place like Piper is what\nyou’ve got up here. Elmer Chambers had reached over his desk and tapped the\ncenter of his son’s forehead with a hard, nicotine-stained finger. Get me, kid?\nJake had nodded. It wasn’t necessary to talk to his father, because his father\ntreated everyone—including his wife—the way he treated his underlings at the TV\nnetwork where he was in charge of programming and an acknowledged master of The\n\nKill. All you had to do was listen, nod in the right places, and after a while\n\nhe let you go.\n\nGood, his father said, lighting one of the eighty Camel cigarettes he smoked\neach and every day. We understand each other, then. You’re going to have to work\nyour buttsky off, but you can cut it. They never would have sent us this if you\ncouldn’t. He picked up the letter of acceptance from The Piper School and\nrattled it. There was a kind of savage triumph in the gesture, as if the letter\nwas an animal he had killed in the jungle, an animal he would now skin and eat.\nSo work hard. Make your grades. Make your mother and me proud of you. If you end\nthe year with an A average in your courses. there’s a trip to Disney World in it\nfor you. That’s something to shoot for, right, kiddo?\nJake had made his grades—A’s in everything (until the last three weeks, that\nwas). He had, presumably, made his mother and father proud of him, although they\nwere around so little that it was hard to tell. Usually there was nobody around\nwhen he came home from school except for Greta Shaw—the housekeeper—and so he\nended up showing his A papers to her. After that, they migrated to a dark corner\nof his room. Sometimes Jake looked through them and wondered if they meant\nany-thing. He wanted them to, but he had serious doubts.\nJake didn’t think he would be going to Disney World this summer, A average or no\nA average.\n\nHe thought the nuthouse was a much better possibility.\nAs he walked in through the double doors of The Piper School at 8:45 on the\nmorning of May 31st, a terrible vision came to him. He saw his father in his\noffice at 70 Rockefeller Plaza, leaning over his desk with a Camel jutting from\nthe corner of his mouth, talking to one of his underlings as blue smoke wreathed\nhis head. All of New York was spread out behind and below his father, its thump\nand hustle silenced by two layers of Thermopane glass.\nThe fact is, money doesn’t get anyone into Sunnyvale Sanitarium, his father was\ntelling the underling in a tone of grim satisfaction. He reached out and tapped\nthe underling’s forehead. The only thing that gets you into a place like that is\nwhen something big-time goes wrong up here in the attic. That’s what happened to\nthe kid. But he’s working his goddam buttsky off. Makes the best fucking baskets\nin the place, they tell me. And when they let him out—if they ever do—there’s a\ntrip in it for him. A trip to—\n\n“—the way station,” Jake muttered, then touched his forehead with a hand that\nwanted to tremble. The voices were coming back. The yelling, conflicting voices\nwhich were driving him mad.\n\nYou’re dead, Jake. You were run over by a car and you’re dead.\nDon’t be stupid! Look—see that poster? REMEMBER THE CLASS ONE PICNIC, it says.\nDo you think they have Class Picnics in the afterlife?\nI don’t know. But I know you were run over by a car.\nNo!\n\nYes. It happened on May 9th, at 8:25 AM You died less than a minute later.\nNo! No! No!\n\n“John?”\n\nHe looked around, badly startled. Mr. Bissette, his French teacher, was standing\nthere, looking a little concerned. Behind him, the rest of the student body was\nstreaming into the Common Room for the morning assembly. There was very little\nskylarking, and no yelling at all. Presumably these other students, like Jake\nhimself, had been told by their parents how lucky they were to be attending\nPiper, where money didn’t matter (although tuition was $22,000 a year), only\nyour brains. Presumably many of them had been promised trips this summer if\ntheir grades were good enough. Presumably the parents of the lucky trip-winners\nwould even go along in some cases. Presumably—\n“John, are you okay?” Mr. Bissette asked.\n“Sure,” Jake said. “Fine. I overslept a little this morning. Not awake yet, I\nguess.”\n\nMr. Bissette’s face relaxed and he smiled. “Happens to the best of us.”\nNot to my dad. The master of The Kill never oversleeps.\n\n“Are you ready for your French final?” Mr. Bissette asked. “Voulez-vous faire\n\nI’examen cet apres-midi?”\n\n“I think so,” Jake said. In truth he didn’t know if he was ready for the exam or\nnot. He couldn’t even remember if he had studied for the French final or not.\nThese days nothing seemed to matter much except for the voices in his head.\n“I want to tell you again how much I enjoyed having you this year, John. I\nwanted to tell your folks, too, but they missed Parents’ Night—”\n“They’re pretty busy,” Jake said.\n\nMr. Bissette nodded. “Well, I have enjoyed you. I just wanted to say so … and\nthat I’m looking forward to having you back for French II next year.”\n“Thanks,” Jake said, and wondered what Mr. Bissette would say if he added, But I\ndon’t think I’ll be taking French II next year, unless I can get a\ncorrespondence course delivered to my postal box at good old Sunnyvale.\nJoanne Franks, the school secretary, appeared in the doorway of the Common Room\nwith her small silver-plated bell in her hand. At The Piper School, all bells\nwere rung by hand. Jake supposed that if you were a parent, that was one of its\ncharms. Memories of the Little Red Schoolhouse and all that. He hated it\nhimself. The sound of that bell seemed to go right through his head—\nI can’t hold on much longer, he thought despairingly. I’m sorry, but I’m losing\nit. I’m really, really losing it.\n\nMr. Bissette had caught sight of Ms. Franks. He turned away, then turned back\nagain. “Is everything all right, John? You’ve seemed preoccupied these last few\nweeks. Troubled. Is something on your mind?”\nJake was almost undone by the kindness in Mr. Bissette’s voice, but then he\nimagined how Mr. Bissette would look if he said: Yes. Something is on my mind.\nOne hell of a nasty little factoid. I died, you see, and I went into another\nworld. And then I died again. You’re going to say that stuff like that doesn’t\nhappen, and of course you’re right, and part of my mind knows you’re right, but\nmost of my mind knows that you’re wrong. It did happen. I did die.\nIf he said something like that, Mr. Bissette would be on the phone to Elmer\nChambers at once, and Jake thought that Sunnyvale Sanitarium would probably look\nlike a rest-cure after all the stuff his father would have to say on the subject\nof lads who started having crazy notions just before Finals Week. Kids who did\nthings that couldn’t be discussed over lunch or cocktails. Kids Who Let Down The\nSide.
6Part 5Darktower 3 - The Waste Lands\n\nJake forced himself to smile at Mr. Bissette. “I’m a little worried about exams,\nthat’s all.”\n\nMr. Bissette winked. “You’ll do fine.”\n\nMs. Franks began to ring the Assembly Bell. Each peal stabbed into Jake’s ears\nand then seemed to flash across his brain like a small rocket.\n“Come on,” Mr. Bissette said. “We’ll be late. Can’t be late on the first day of\nFinals Week, can we?”\n\nThey went in past Ms. Franks and her clashing bell. Mr. Bissette headed toward\nthe row of seats called Faculty Choir. There were lots of cute names like that\nat Piper School; the auditorium was the Com-mon Room, lunch-hour was Outs,\nseventh- and eighth-graders were Upper Boys and Girls, and, of course, the\nfolding chairs over by the piano (which Ms. Franks would soon begin to pound as\nmercilessly as she rang her silver bell) was Faculty Choir. All part of the\ntradition, Jake supposed. If you were a parent who knew your kid had Outs in the\nCommon Room at noon instead of just slopping up Tuna Surprise in the caff, you\nrelaxed into the assurance that everything was A-OK in the education department.\nHe slipped into a seat at the rear of the room and let the morn-ing’s\nannouncements wash over him. The terror ran endlessly on in his mind, making him\nfeel like a rat trapped on an exercise wheel. And when he tried to look ahead to\nsome better, brighter time, he could see only darkness.\nThe ship was his sanity, and it was sinking.\nMr. Harley, the headmaster, approached the podium and imparted a brief exordium\nabout the importance of Finals Week, and how the grades they received would\nconstitute another step upon The Great Road of Life. He told them that the\n\nschool was depending on them, he was depending on them, and their parents were\n\ndepending on them. Me did not tell them that the entire free world was depending\non them, but he strongly implied that this might be so. He finished by telling\nthem that bells would be suspended during Finals Week (the first and only piece\nof good news Jake had received that morning).\nMs. Franks, who had assumed her seat at the piano, struck an invocatory chord.\nThe student body, seventy boys and fifty girls, each turned out in a neat and\nsober way that bespoke their parents’ taste and financial stability, rose as one\nand began to sing the school song. Jake mouthed the words and thought about the\nplace where he had awakened after dying. At first he had believed himself to be\nin hell . . . and when the man in the black hooded robe came along, he had been\nsure of it.\n\nThen, of course, the other man had come along. A man Jake had almost come to\nlove.\n\nBut he let me fall. He killed me.\n\nHe could feel prickly sweat breaking out on the back of his neck and between his\nshoulderblades.\n\n“So we hail the halls of Piper,\n\nHold its banner high;\n\nHail to thee, our alma mater,\n\nPiper, do or die!”\n\nGod, what a shitty song, Jake thought, and it suddenly occurred to him that his\nfather would love it.\n\nPERIOD ONE WAS ENGLISH Comp, the only class where there was no final. Their\nassignment had been to write a Final Essay at home. This was to be a typed\ndocument between fifteen hundred and four thousand words long. The subject Ms.\nAvery had assigned was My Understanding of Truth. The Final Essay would count as\ntwenty-five per cent of their final grade for the semester.\nJake came in and took his seat in the third row. There were only eleven pupils\nin all. Jake remembered Orientation Day last September, when Mr. Harley had told\nthem that Piper had The Highest Teacher To Student Ratio Of Any Fine Private\nMiddle School In The East. He had popped his fist repeatedly on the lectern at\nthe front of the Common Room to emphasize this point. Jake hadn’t been terribly\nimpressed, but he had passed the information along to his lather. He thought his\nfather would be impressed, and he had not been wrong.\nHe unzipped his bookbag and carefully removed the blue folder which contained\nhis Final Essay. He laid it on his desk, meaning to give it a final look-over,\nwhen his eye was caught by the door at the left side of the room. It led, he\nknew, to the cloakroom, and it was closed today because it was seventy degrees\nin New York and no one had a coat which needed storage. Nothing back there\nexcept a lot of brass coathooks in a line on the wall and a long rubber mat on\nthe floor for boots. A few boxes of school supplies—chalk, blue-books and\nsuch—were stored in the far corner.\n\nNo big deal.\n\nAll the same, Jake rose from his seat, leaving the folder unopened on the desk,\nand walked across to the door. He could hear his classmates murmuring quietly\ntogether, and the riffle of pages as they checked their own Final Essays for\nthat crucial misplaced modifier or fuzzy phrase, but these sounds seemed far\naway.\n\nIt was the door which held his attention.\nIn the last ten days or so, as the voices in his head grew louder and louder,\nJake had become more and more fascinated with doors—all kinds of doors. He must\nhave opened the one between his bedroom and the upstairs hallway five hundred\ntimes in just the last week, and the one between his bedroom and the bathroom a\n\nthousand. Each time he did it, he felt a tight ball of hope and anticipation in\n\nhis chest, as if the answer to all of his problems lay somewhere behind this\ndoor or that one and he would surely find it … eventually. But each time it\nwas only the hall, or the bathroom, or the front walk, or whatever.\nLast Thursday he had come home from school, thrown himself on his bed, and had\nfallen asleep—sleep, it seemed, was the only refuge which remained to him.\nExcept when he’d awakened forty-five minutes later, he had been standing in the\nbathroom doorway, peering dazedly in at nothing more exciting than the toilet\nand the basin. Luckily, no one had seen him.\nNow, as he approached the cloakroom door, he felt that same daz-zling burst of\nhope, a certainty that the door would not open on a shad-owy closet containing\nonly the persistent smells of winter—flannel, rubber, and wet wool—but on some\nother world where he could be whale again. Hot, dazzling light would fall across\nthe classroom floor in a widening triangle, and he would see birds circling in a\nfaded blue sky the color of\n\n(his eyes)\n\nold jeans. A desert wind would blow his hair back and dry the nervous sweat on\nhis brow.\n\nHe would step through this door and be healed.\nJake turned the knob and opened the door. Inside was only darkness and a row of\ngleaming brass hooks. One long-forgotten mitten lay near the stacked piles of\nblue-books in the corner.\n\nHis heart sank, and suddenly Jake felt like simply creeping into that dark room\nwith its bitter smells of winter and chalkdust. He could move the mitten and sit\nin the corner under the coathooks. He could sit on the rubber mat where you were\nsupposed to put your boots in the winter-time. He could sit there, put his thumb\nin his mouth, pull his knees tight against his chest, close his eyes, and . . .\nand . . .\n\nAnd just give up.\n\nThis idea—the relief of this idea—was incredibly attractive. It would be an end\nto the terror and confusion and dislocation. That last was somehow the worst;\nthat persistent feeling that his whole life had turned into a funhouse\nmirror-maze.\n\nYet there was deep steel in Jake Chambers as surely as there was deep steel in\nEddie and Susannah. Now it flashed out its dour blue lighthouse gleam in the\ndarkness. There would be no giving up. What-ever was loose inside him might tear\nhis sanity away from him in the end, but he would give it no quarter in the\nmeantime. Be damned if he would.\n\nNever! he thought fiercely. Never! Nev—\n\n“When you’ve finished your inventory of the school-supplies in the cloakroom,\nJohn, perhaps you’d care to join us,” Ms. Avery said from behind him in her dry,\ncultured voice.\n\nThere was a small gust of giggles as Jake turned away from the cloakroom. Ms.\nAvery was standing behind her desk with her long fingers tented lightly on the\nblotter, looking at him out of her calm, intelligent face. She was wearing her\nblue suit today, and her hair was pulled back in its usual bun. Nathaniel\nHawthorne looked over her shoulder, frowning at Jake from his place on the wall.\n“Sorry,” Jake muttered, and closed the door. He was immediately seized by a\nstrong impulse to open it again, to double-check, to see if this time that other\nworld, with its hot sun and desert vistas, was there.\nInstead he walked back to his seat. Petra Jesserling looked at him with merry,\ndancing eyes. “Take me in there with you next time,” she whispered. “Then you’ll\nhave something to look at.”\n\nJake smiled in a distracted way and slipped into his seat.\n“Thank you, John,” Ms. Avery said in her endlessly calm voice. “Now, before you\npass in your Final Essays—which I am sure will all be very fine, very neat, very\nspecific—I should like to pass out the English Department’s Short List of\nrecommended summer reading. I will have a word to say about several of these\nexcellent books—“\n\nAs she spoke she gave a small stack of mimeographed sheets to David Surrey.\n\nDavid began to hand them out, and Jake opened his folder to take a final look at\nwhat he had written on the topic My Understanding of Truth. He was genuinely\ninterested in this, because he could no more remember writing his Final Essay,\nthan he could remember studying for his French final.\nHe looked at the title page with puzzlement and growing unease. MY UNDERSTANDING\nOF TRUTH, By John Chambers, was neatly typed and centered on the sheet, and that\nwas all right, but he had for some reason pasted two photographs below it. One\nwas of a door—he thought it might be the one at Number 10, Downing Street, in\nLondon—and the other was of an Amtrak train. They were color shots, undoubtedly\nculled from some magazine.\n\nWhy did I do that? And when did I do it?\nHe turned the page and stared down at the first page of his Final Essay, unable\nto believe or understand what he was seeing. Then, as understanding began to\ntrickle through his shock, he felt an escalating sense of horror. It had finally\nhappened; he had finally lost enough of his mind so that other people would be\nable to tell.\n\nMY UNDERSTANDING OF TRUTH\n\nBy John Chambers\n\n“I will show you fear in a handful of dust.”\n\n—T. Once in a while someone\nrequested permission to “step out for a moment,” and that was all.\nMs. Avery sighed. “Must you, John?”\n\n“Yes, ma’am.”\n\n“All right. Return as soon as possible.”\n“Yes, Ms. Avery.”\n\nHe closed the folder as he got up, took hold of it, then reluctantly let go\nagain. No good. He looked into Mr. Bissette’s French II\nclass and Mr. Knopf’s Introduction to Geometry class. In both rooms the pupils\nsat with pencils in hand and heads bowed over open blue-books. He looked into\nMr. Harley’s Spoken Arts class and saw Stan Dorfman—one of those acquaintances\nwho were not quite friends—beginning his Final Speech. Stan looked scared to\ndeath, but Jake could have told Stan he didn’t have the slightest idea what\nfear— real fear—was all about..\n\nI died.\n\nNo. I didn’t.\n\nDid too.\n\nDid not.\n\nDid.\n\nDidn’t.\n\nHe came to a door marked GIRLS. He pushed it open, expecting to see a bright\ndesert sky and a blue haze of mountains on the horizon. Instead he saw Belinda\nStevens standing at one of the sinks, looking into the mirror above the basin\nand squeezing a pimple on her forehead.\n\n“Jesus Christ, do you mind?” she asked.\n\n“Sorry. Wrong door. I thought it was the desert.”\n\n“What?”\n\nBut he had already let the door go and it was swinging shut on its pneumatic\nelbow. He passed the drinking fountain and opened the door marked BOYS. This was\nit, he knew it, was sure of it, this was the door which would take him back—\nThree urinals gleamed spotlessly under the fluorescent lights. A tap dripped\nsolemnly into a sink. That was all.\n\nJake let the door close. He walked on down the hall, his heels making firm\nlittle clicks on the tiles. He glanced into the office before passing it and saw\nonly Ms. Franks. She was talking on the telephone, swinging back and forth in\nher swivel chair and playing with a lock of her hair. The silver-plated bell\nstood on the desk beside her. Jake waited until she swivelled away from the door\nand then hurried past. Thirty seconds later he was emerging into the bright\nsunshine of a morning in late May.\n\nI’ve gone truant, he thought. Even his distraction did not keep him from being\namazed at this unexpected development. When I don’t come back from the bathroom\nin five minutes or so, Ms. Avery will send somebody to check . . . and then\nthey’ll know. They’ll all know that I’ve left school, gone truant.\nHe thought of the folder lying on his desk.\nThey’ll read it and they’ll think I’m crazy. Fou. Sure they will. Of course.\nBecause I am.\n\nThen another voice spoke. It was, he thought, the voice of the man with the\nbombardier’s eyes, the man who wore the two big guns slung low on his hips. The\nvoice was cold . . . but not without comfort.\nNo, Jake, Roland said. You’re not crazy. You’re lost and scared, but you’re not\ncrazy and need fear neither your shadow in the morning striding behind you nor\nyour shadow at evening rising to meet you. Yow have to find your way back home,\nthat’s all.\n\n“But where do I go?” Jake whispered. He stood on the sidewalk of Fifty-sixth\nStreet between Park and Madison, watching the traffic bolt past. A city bus\nsnored by, laying a thin trail of acrid blue diesel smoke. “Where do I go?\nWhere’s the fucking door?”\n\nBut the voice of the gunslinger had fallen silent.\nJake turned left, in the direction of the East River, and began to walk blindly\nforward. He had no idea where he was going—no idea at all. He could only hope\nhis feet would carry him to the right place . . . as they had carried him to the\nwrong one not long ago.\n\nIT HAD HAPPENED THREE weeks earlier.\n\nOne could not say It all began three weeks earlier, because that gave the\nimpression that there had been some sort of progression, and that wasn’t right.\nThere had been a progression to the voices, to the violence with which each\ninsisted on its own particular version of reality, but the rest of it had\nhappened all at once.\n\nHe left home at eight o’clock to walk to school—he always walked when the\nweather was good, and the weather this May had been abso-lutely fine. His father\nhad left for the Network, his mother was still in bed, and Mrs. Greta Shaw was\nin the kitchen, drinking coffee and reading her New York Post.\n“Goodbye, Greta,” he said. “I’m going to school now.”\nShe raised a hand to him without looking up from the paper. “Have a good day,\nJohnny.”\n\nAll according to routine. Just another day in the life.\nAnd so it had been for the next fifteen hundred seconds. Then everything had\nchanged forever.\n\nHe idled along, bookbag in one hand, lunch sack in the other, looking in the\nwindows. Seven hundred and twenty seconds from the end of his life as he had\nalways known it, he paused to look in the window of Brendio’s, where mannequins\n\ndressed in fur coats and Edwardian suits stood in stiff poses of conversation.\n\nHe was thinking only of going bowling that afternoon after school. His average\nwas 158, great for a kid who was only eleven. His ambition was to some day be a\nbowler on the pro tour (and if his father had known this little factoid, he also\nwould have hit the roof).\n\nClosing in now—closing in on the moment when his sanity would be suddenly\neclipsed.\n\nHe crossed Thirty-ninth and there were four hundred seconds left. Had to wait\nfor the WALK light at Forty-first and there were two hundred and seventy. Paused\nto look in the novelty shop on the corner of Fifth and Forty-second and there\nwere a hundred and ninety. And now, with just over three minutes left in his\nordinary life, Jake Cham-bers walked beneath the unseen umbrella of that force\nwhich Roland called ka-tet.\n\nAn odd, uneasy feeling began to creep over him. At first he thought it was a\nfeeling of being watched, and then he realized it wasn’t that at all … or not\nprecisely that. He felt that he had been here before; that he was reliving a\ndream he had mostly forgotten. He waited for the feeling to pass, but it didn’t.\nIt grew stronger, and now began to mix with a sensation he reluctantly\nrecognized as terror.\n\nUp ahead, on the near corner of Fifth and Forty-third, a black man in a Panama\nhat was setting up a pretzel-and-soda cart.\nHe’s the one that yells “Oh my God, he’s kilt!” Jake thought.\nApproaching the far corner was a fat lady with a Bloomingdale’s bag in her hand.\nShe’ll drop the bag. Drop the bag and put her hands to her mouth and scream. The\nbag will split open. There’s a doll inside the bag. It’s wrapped in a red towel.\nI’ll see this from the street. From where I’ll be lying in the street with my\nblood soaking into my pants and spreading around me in a pool.\nBehind the fat woman was a tall man in a gray nailhead worsted suit. He was\ncarrying a briefcase.\n\nHe’s the one who vomits on his shoes. He’s the one who drops his briefcase and\nthrows up on his shoes. What’s happening to me?\nYet his feet carried him numbly forward toward the intersection, where people\nwere crossing in a brisk, steady stream. Somewhere behind him, closing in, was a\nkiller priest. He knew this, just as he knew that the priest’s hands would in a\nmoment be outstretched to push . . . but he could not look around. It was like\nbeing locked in a nightmare where things simply had to take their course.\nFifty-three seconds left now. Ahead of him, the pretzel vendor was opening a\nhatch in the side of his cart.\n\nHe’s going to take out a bottle of Yoo-Hoo, Jake thought. Not a can but a\nbottle. He’ll shake it up and drink it all at once.\nThe pretzel vendor brought out a bottle of Yoo-Hoo, shook it vigor-ously, and\nspun off the cap.\n\nForty seconds left.\n\nNow the light will change.\n\nWhite WALK went out. Red DONT WALK began to flash rapidly on and off. And\nsomewhere, less than half a block away, a big blue Cadillac was now rolling\ntoward the intersection of Fifth and Forty-third. Jake knew this, just as he\nknew the driver was a fat man wearing a hat almost the exact same blue shade as\nhis car.\n\nI’m going to die!\n\nHe wanted to scream this aloud to the people walking heedlessly all around him,\nbut his jaws were locked shut. His feet swept him serenely onward toward the\nintersection. The DONT WALK sign stopped flashing and shone out its solid red\nwarning. The pretzel vendor tossed his empty Yoo-Hoo bottle into the wire trash\nbasket on the corner. The fat lady stood on the corner across the street from\nJake, holding her shopping bag by the handles. The man in the nailhead suit was\ndirectly behind her. Now there were eighteen seconds left.\nTime for the toy truck to go by, Jake thought.\nAhead of him a van with a picture of a happy jumping-jack and the words TOOKER’S\n\nWHOLESALE TOYS printed on the side swept through the intersection, jolting up\n\nand down in the potholes. Behind him, Jake knew, the man in the black robe was\nbeginning to move faster, closing the gap, now reaching out with his long hands.\nYet he could not look around, as you couldn’t look around in dreams when\nsomething awful was gaining on you.\n\nRun! And if you can’t run, sit down and grab hold of a No Parking sign! Don’t\njust let it happen!\n\nBut he was powerless to stop it from happening. Ahead, on the edge of the curb,\nwas a young woman in a white sweater and a black skirt. To her left was a young\nChicano guy with a boombox. A Donna Summer disco tune was just ending. The next\nsong, Jake knew, would be “Dr. Love,” by Kiss.\nThey’re going to move apart—\n\nEven as the thought came, the woman moved a step to her right. The Chicano guy\nmoved a step to his left, creating a gap between them. Jake’s traitor feet swept\nhim into the gap. Nine seconds now.\n\nDown the street, bright May sunshine twinkled on a Cadillac hood ornament. It\nwas, Jake knew, a 1976 Sedan de Ville. Six seconds. The Caddy was speeding up.\nThe light was getting ready to change and the man driving the de Ville, the fat\nman in the blue hat with the feather stuck jauntily in the brim, meant to scat\nthrough the intersection before it could. Three seconds. Behind Jake, the man in\nblack was lunging forward. On the young man’s boombox, “Love to Love You, Baby”\nended and “Dr. Love” began.\n\nTwo.\n\nThe Cadillac changed to the lane nearest Jake’s side of the street and charged\ndown on the intersection, its killer grille snarling.\nOne.\n\nJake’s breath stopped in his throat.\n\nNone.\n\n“Uh!” Jake cried as the hands struck him firmly in the back, pushing him,\npushing him into the street, pushing him out of his life—\nExcept there were no hands.\n\nHe reeled forward nevertheless, hands flailing at the air, his mouth a dark O of\ndismay. The Chicano guy with the boombox reached out, grabbed Jake’s arm, and\nhauled him backward. “Look out, little hero,” he said. “That traffic turn you\ninto bratwurst.”\n\nThe Cadillac floated by. Jake caught a glimpse of the fat man in the blue hat\npeering out through the windshield, and then it was gone.\nThat was when it happened; that was when he split down the middle and became two\nboys. One lay dying in the street. The other stood here on the corner, watching\nin dumb, stricken amazement as DONT WALK turned to WALK again and people began\nto cross around him just as if nothing had happened … as, indeed, nothing had.\nI’m alive! half of his mind rejoiced, screaming with relief.\nDead! the other half screamed back. Dead in the street! They’re all gathering\naround me, and the man in black who pushed me is saying, “I am a priest. Let me\nthrough.”\n\nWaves of faintness rushed through him and turned his thoughts to billowing\nparachute silk. He saw the fat lady approaching, and as she passed, Jake looked\ninto her bag. He saw the bright blue eyes of a doll peeping above the edge of a\nred towel, just as he had known he would. Then she was gone. The pretzel vendor\nwas not yelling Oh my God, he’s kilt; he was continuing to set up for the day’s\nbusiness while he whistled the Donna Summer tune that had been playing on the\nChicano guy’s radio.\n\nJake turned around, looking wildly for the priest who was not a priest. He\nwasn’t there.\n\nJake moaned.\n\nSnap out of it! What’s wrong with you?\n\nHe didn’t know. He only knew he was supposed to be lying in the street right\nnow, getting ready to die while the fat woman screamed and the guy in the\nnailhead worsted suit threw up and the man in black pushed through the gathering\n\ncrowd.\n\nAnd in part of his mind, that did seem to be happening.\nThe faintness began to return. Jake suddenly dropped his lunch sack to the\npavement and slapped himself across the face as hard as he could. A woman on her\nway to work gave him a queer look. Jake ignored her. He left his lunch lying on\nthe sidewalk and plunged into the intersection, also ignoring the red DONT WALK\nlight, which had begun to stutter on and off again. It didn’t matter now. Death\nhad approached . . . and then passed by without a second glance. It hadn’t been\nmeant to happen that way, and on the deepest level of his exis-tence he knew\nthat, but it had.\n\nMaybe now he would live forever.\n\nThe thought made him feel like screaming all over again.\n\nHis HEAD HAD CLEARED a little by the time he got to school, and his mind had\ngone to work trying to convince him that nothing was wrong, really nothing at\nall. Maybe something a little weird had happened, some sort of psychic flash, a\nmomentary peek into one possible future, but so what? No big deal, right? The\nidea was actually sort of cool—the kind of thing they were always printing in\nthe weird supermarket newspapers Greta Shaw liked to read when she was sure\nJake’s mother wasn’t around—papers like the National Enquirer and Inside View.\nExcept, of course, in those papers the psychic flash was always a kind of\ntactical nuclear strike—a woman who dreamed of a plane crash and changed her\nreservations, or a guy who dreamed his brother was being held prisoner in a\nChinese fortune cookie factory and it turned out to be true. When your psychic\nflash consisted of knowing that a Kiss song was going to play next on the radio,\nthat a fat lady had a doll wrapped in a red towel in her Bloomingdale’s bag, and\nthat a pretzel vendor was going to drink a bottle of Yoo-Hoo instead of a can,\nhow big a deal could it be?\n\nForget it, he advised himself. It’s over.\nA great idea, except by period three he knew it wasn’t over; it was just\nbeginning. He sat in pre-algebra, watching Mr. Knopf solving simple equations on\nthe board, and realized with dawning horror that a whole new set of memories was\nsurfacing in his mind. It was like watching strange objects float slowly toward\nthe surface of a muddy lake.\n\nI’m in a place I don’t know, he thought. I mean, I will know it—or would have\nknown it if the Cadillac had hit me. It’s the way station—but the part of me\nthat’s there doesn’t know that yet. That part only knows it’s in the desert\nsomeplace, and there are no people. I’ve been crying, because I’m scared. I’m\nscared that this might be hell.\n\nBy three o’clock, when he arrived at Mid-Town Lanes, he knew he had found the\npump in the stables and had gotten a drink of water. The water was very cold and\ntasted strongly of minerals. Soon he would go inside and find a small supply of\ndried beef in a room which had once been a kitchen. He knew this as clearly and\nsurely as he’d known the pretzel vendor would select a bottle of Yoo-Hoo, and\nthat the doll peek-ing out of the Bloomingdale’s bag had blue eyes.\nIt was like being able to remember forward in time.\nHe bowled only two strings—the first a 96, the second an 87. Timmy looked at his\nsheet when he turned it in at the counter and shook his head. “You’re having an\noff-day today, champ,” he said.\n\n“You don’t know the half of it,” Jake said.\nTimmy took a closer look. “You okay? You look really pale.”\n“I think I might be coming down with a bug.” This didn’t feel like a lie,\neither. He was sure as hell coming down with something.\n“Go home and go to bed,” Timmy advised. “Drink lots of clear liquids—gin, vodka,\nstuff like that.”\n\nJake smiled dutifully. “Maybe I will.”\n\nHe walked slowly home. All of New York was spread out around him, New York at\n\nits most seductive—a late-afternoon street serenade with a musician on every\n\ncorner, all the trees in bloom, and everyone apparently in a good mood. Jake saw\nall this, but he also saw behind it: saw himself cowering in the shadows of the\nkitchen as the man in black drank like a grinning dog from the stable pump, saw\nhimself sobbing with relief as he—or it—moved on without discovering him, saw\nhimself falling deeply asleep as the sun went down and the stars began to come\nout like chips of ice in the harsh purple desert sky.\nHe let himself into the duplex apartment with his key and walked into the\nkitchen to get something to eat. He wasn’t hungry, but it was, habit. He was\nheaded for the refrigerator when his eye happened on the pantry door and he\nstopped. He realized suddenly that the way station— and all the rest of that\nstrange other world where he now belonged— was behind that door. All he had to\ndo was push through it and rejoin the Jake that already existed there. The queer\ndoubling in his mind would end; the voices, endlessly arguing the question of\nwhether or not he had been dead since 8:25 that morning, would fall silent.\nJake pushed open the pantry door with both hands, his face already breaking into\na sunny, relieved smile . . . and then froze as Mrs. Shaw, who was standing on a\nstep-stool at the back of the pantry, screamed. The can of tomato paste she had\nbeen holding dropped out of her hand and fell to the floor. She tottered on the\nstool and Jake rushed forward to steady her before she could join the tomato\npaste.\n\n“Moses in the bullrushes!” she gasped, fluttering a hand rapidly against the\nfront of her housedress. “You scared the bejabbers out of me, Johnny!”\n“I’m sorry,” he said. He really was, but he was also bitterly disap-pointed. It\nhad only been the pantry, after all. He had been so sure—\n“What are you doing, creeping around here, anyway? This is your bowling day! I\ndidn’t expect you for at least another hour! I haven’t even made your snack yet,\nso don’t be expecting it.”\n\n“That’s okay. I’m not very hungry, anyway.” He bent down and picked up the can\nshe had dropped.\n\n“Wouldn’t know it from the way you came bustin in here,” she grumbled.\n“I thought I heard a mouse or something. I guess it was just you.”\n“I guess it was.” She descended the step-stool and took the can from him. “You\nlook like you’re comin down with the flu or something, Johnny.” She pressed her\nhand against his forehead. “You don’t feel hot, but that doesn’t always mean\nmuch.”\n\n“I think I’m just tired,” Jake said, and thought: If only that was all it was.\n“Maybe I’ll just have a soda and watch TV for a while.”\nShe grunted. “You got any papers you want to show me? If you do, make it fast.\nI’m behind on supper.”\n\n“Nothing today,” he said. He left the pantry, got a soda, then went into the\nliving room. He turned on Hollywood Squares and watched vacantly as the voices\nargued and the new memories of that dusty other world continued to surface.\n\nHis MOTHER AND FATHER didn’t notice anything was wrong with him— his father\ndidn’t even get in until 9:30—and that was fine by Jake. He went to bed at ten\nand lay awake in the darkness, listening to the city outside his window: brakes,\nhorns, wailing sirens.\n\nYou died.\n\nI didn’t, though. I’m right here, safe in my own bed.\nThat doesn’t matter. You died, and you know it.\nThe hell of it was, he knew both things.\nI don’t know which voice is true, but I know I can’t go on like this. So just\nquit it, both of you. Stop arguing and leave me alone. Okay? Please?\nBut they wouldn’t. Couldn’t, apparently. And it came to Jake that he ought to\n\nget up—right now—and open the door to the bathroom. The other world would be\n\nthere. The way station would be there and the rest of him would be there, too,\nhuddled under an ancient blanket in the stable, trying to sleep and wondering\nwhat in hell had happened.\n\nI can tell him, Jake thought excitedly. He threw back the covers, suddenly\nknowing that the door beside his bookcase no longer led into the bathroom but to\na world that smelled of heat and purple sage and fear in a handful of dust, a\nworld that now lay under the shadowing wing of night. I can tell him, but I\nwon’t have to . . . because I’ll be IN him . . . I’ll BE him!\nHe raced across his darkened room, almost laughing with relief, and shoved open\nthe door. And—\n\nAnd it was his bathroom. Just his bathroom, with the framed Marvin Gaye poster\non the wall and the shapes of the Venetian blinds lying on the tiled floor in\nbars of light and shadow.\n\nHe stood there for a long time, trying to swallow his disappointment. It\nwouldn’t go. And it was bitter.\n\nBitter.\n\nTHE THREE WEEKS BETWEEN then and now stretched like a grim, blighted terrain in\nJake’s memory—a nightmare wasteland where there had been no peace, no rest, no\nrespite from pain. He had watched, like a helpless prisoner watching the sack of\na city he had once ruled, as his mind buckled under the steadily increasing\npressure of the phantom voices and memories. He had hoped the memories would\nstop when he reached the point in them where the man named Roland had allowed\nhim to drop into the chasm under the mountains, but they didn’t. Instead they\nsimply recycled and began to play themselves over again, like a tape set to\nrepeat and repeat until it either breaks or someone comes along, and shuts it\noff.\n\nHis perceptions of his more-or-less real life as a boy in New York City grew\nincreasingly spotty as this terrible schism grew deeper. He could remember going\nto school, and to the movies on the weekend, and out to Sunday brunch with his\nparents a week ago (or had it been two?), but he remembered these things the way\na man who has suffered malaria may remember the deepest, darkest phase of his\nillness: people became shadows, voices seemed to echo and overlap each other,\nand even such a simple act as eating a sandwich or obtaining a Coke from the\nmachine in the gymnasium became a struggle. Jake had pushed through those days\nin a fugue of yelling voices and doubled memories. His obsession with doors—all\nkinds of doors—deepened; his hope that the gunslinger’s world might lie behind\none of them never quite died. Nor was that so strange, since it was the only\nhope he had.\n\nBut as of today the game was over. He’d never had a chance of winning anyway,\nnot really. He had given up. He had gone truant. Jake walked blindly east along\nthe gridwork of streets, head down, with no idea of where he was going or what\nhe would do when he got there.\n\nAFTER WALKING FOR A while, he began to come out of this unhappy daze and take\nsome notice of his surroundings. He was standing on the corner of Lexington\nAvenue and. Fifty-fourth Street with no memory at all of how he had come to be\nthere. He noticed for the first time that it was an absolutely gorgeous morning.\nMay 9th, the day this madness had started, had been pretty, but today was ten\ntimes better—that day, perhaps, when spring looks around herself and sees summer\nstanding nearby, strong and handsome and with a cocky grin on his tanned face.\nThe sun shone brightly off the glass walls of the midtown buildings; the shadow\nof each pedestrian was black and crisp. The sky overhead was a clear and\nblameless blue, dotted here and there with plump foul-weather clouds.\n\nDown the street, two businessmen in expensive, well-cut suits were standing at a\n\nboard wall which had been erected around a construction site. They were laughing\nand passing something back and forth. Jake walked in their direction, curious,\nand as he drew closer he saw that the two businessmen were playing tic-tac-toe\non the wall, using an expensive Mark Cross pen to draw the grids and make the\nX’s and O’s. Jake thought this was a complete gas. As he approached, one of them\nmade an O in the upper right-hand corner of the grid and then slashed a diagonal\nline through the middle.\n\n“Skunked again!” his friend said. Then this man, who looked like a high-powered\nexecutive or lawyer or big-time stockbroker, took the Mark Cross pen and drew\nanother grid.\n\nThe first businessman, the winner, glanced to his left and saw Jake. He smiled.\n“Some day, huh, kid?”\n\n“It sure is,” Jake said, delighted to find he meant every word.\n“Too nice for school, huh?”\n\nThis time Jake actually laughed. Piper School, where you had Outs instead of\nlunch and where you sometimes stepped out but never had to take a crap, suddenly\nseemed far away and not at all important. “You know it.”\n“You want a game? Billy here couldn’t beat me at this when we were in the fifth\ngrade, and he still can’t.’\n\n“Leave the kid alone,” the second businessman said, holding out the Mark Cross\npen. “This time you’re history.” He winked at Jake, and Jake amazed himself by\nwinking back. He walked on, leaving the men to their game. The sense that\nsomething totally wonderful was going to happen— had perhaps already begun to\nhappen—continued to grow, and his feet no longer seemed to be quite touching the\npavement.\n\nThe WALK light on the corner came on, and he began to cross Lexington Avenue. He\nstopped in the middle of the street so suddenly that a messenger-boy on a\nten-speed bike almost ran him down. It was a beautiful spring day—agreed. But\nthat wasn’t why he felt so good, so suddenly aware of everything that was going\non around him, so sure that some great thing was about to occur.\nThe voices had stopped.\n\nThey weren’t gone for good—he somehow knew this—but for the time being they had\nstopped. Why?\n\nJake suddenly thought of two men arguing in a room. They sit facing each other\nover a table, jawing at each other with increasing bitterness. After a while\nthey begin to lean toward each other, thrusting their faces pugnaciously\nforward, bathing each other with a fine mist of outraged spittle. Soon they will\ncome to blows. But before that can happen, they hear a steady thumping noise—the\nsound of a bass drum—and then a jaunty flourish of brass. The two men stop\narguing and look at each other, puzzled.\nWhat’s that? one asks.\n\nDunno, the other replies. Sounds like a parade.\nThey rush to the window and it is a parade—a uniformed band marching in\nlock-step with the sun blazing off their horns, pretty majo-rettes twirling\nbatons and strutting their long, tanned legs, convertibles decked with flowers\nand filled with waving celebrities.\n\nThe two men stare out the window, their quarrel forgotten. They will undoubtedly\nreturn to it, but for the time being they stand together like the best of\nfriends, shoulder to shoulder, watching as the parade goes by—\n\nA HORN BLARED, STARTLING Jake out of this story, which was as vivid as a\npowerful dream. He realized he was still standing in the middle of Lexington,\nand the light had changed. He looked around wildly, expecting to see the blue\nCadillac bearing down on him, but the guy who had tooted his horn was sitting\nbehind the wheel of a yellow Mustang convert-ible and grinning at him. It was as\nif everyone in New York had gotten a whiff of happy-gas today.\n\nJake waved at the guy and sprinted to the other side of the street. The guy in\n\nthe Mustang twirled a finger around his ear to indicate that Jake was crazy,\nthen waved back and drove on.\n\nFor a moment Jake simply stood on the far corner, face turned up to the May\nsunshine, smiling, digging the day. He supposed prisoners condemned to die in\nthe electric chair must feel this way when they learn they have been granted a\ntemporary reprieve.\n\nThe voices were still.\n\nThe question was, what was the parade which had temporarily diverted their\nattention? Was it just the uncommon beauty of this spring morning?\nJake didn’t think that was all. He didn’t think so because that sensa-tion of\nknowing was creeping over him and through him again, the one which had taken\npossession of him three weeks ago, as he approached the corner of Fifth and\nForty-sixth. But on May 9th, it had been a feeling of impending doom. Today it\nwas a feeling of radiance, a sense of goodness and anticipation. It was as if\n… as if …\n\nWhite. This was the word that came to him, and it clanged in his mind with clear\nand unquestionable lightness.\n\n“It’s the White!” he exclaimed aloud. “The coming of the White!”\nHe walked on down Fifty-fourth Street, and as he reached the cor-ner of Second\nand Fifty-fourth, he once more passed under the umbrella of ka-tet.\n\nHE TURNED RIGHT, THEN stopped, turned, and retraced his steps to the corner. He\nneeded to walk down Second Avenue now, yes, that was unquestionably correct, but\nthis was the wrong side again. When the light changed, he hurried across the\nstreet and turned right again. That feeling, that sense of\n(Whiteness)\n\nrightness, grew steadily stronger. He felt half-mad with joy and relief. He was\ngoing to be okay. This time there was no mistake. He felt sure that he would\nsoon begin to see people he recognized, as he had recognized the fat lady and\nthe pretzel vendor, and they would be doing things he remembered in advance.\nInstead, he came to the bookstore.\n\nTHE MANHATTAN RESTAURANT OF THE MIND, the sign painted in the window read.
