It sang for him.\nThen, lying in a cluster of scrubby burdock plants, Jake saw the key . . . and\nbeyond that, the rose.\n\nHis LEGS BETRAYED HIM and he fell to his knees. He was vaguely aware that he was\nweeping, even more vaguely aware that he had wet his pants a little. He crawled\nforward on his knees and reached toward the key lying in the snarl of burdocks.\nIts simple shape was one he seemed to have seen in his dreams:\nHe thought: The little s-shape at the end—that’s the secret.\nAs he closed his hand around the key, the voices rose in a harmonic shout of\ntriumph. Jake’s own cry was lost in the voice of that choir, lie saw the key\nflash white within his fingers, and felt a tremendous jolt of power run up his\narm. It was as if he had grasped a live high-tension wire, but there was no\npain.\n\nHe opened Charlie the Choo-Choo and put the key inside. Then his eyes fixed upon\nthe rose again, and he realized that it was the real key— the key to everything.\nHe crawled toward it, his face a flaming corona of light, his eyes blazing wells\nof blue fire.\n\nThe rose was growing from a clump of alien purple grass.\nAs Jake neared this clump of alien grass, the rose began to open before his\neyes. It disclosed a dark scarlet furnace, petal upon secret petal, each burning\nwith its own secret fury. He had never seen anything so intensely and utterly\nalive in his whole life.\n\nAnd now, as he stretched one grimy hand out toward this wonder, the voices began\nto sing his own name . . . and deadly fear began to steal in toward the center\nof his heart. It was as cold as ice and as heavy as stone.\nThere was something wrong. He could feel a pulsing discord, like a deep and ugly\nscratch across some priceless work of art or a deadly fever smoldering beneath\nthe chilly skin of an invalid’s brow.\n\nIt was something like a worm. An invading worm. And a shape. One which lurks\njust beyond the next turn of the road.\n\nThen the heart of the rose opened for him, exposing a yellow dazzle of light,\nand all thought was swept away on a wave of wonder. Jake thought for a moment\nthat what he was seeing was only pollen which had been invested with the\nsupernatural glow which lived at the heart of every object in this deserted\n\nclearing—he thought it even though he had never heard of pollen within a rose.\n\nHe leaned closer and saw that the concentrated circle of blazing yellow was not\npollen at all. It was a sun: a vast forge burning at the center of this rose\ngrowing in the purple grass.\n\nThe fear returned, only now it had become outright terror. It’s right, he\nthought, everything here is right, but it could go wrong—has started going wrong\nalready, I think. I’m being allowed to feel as much of that wrongness as I can\nbear . . . but what is it? And what can I do?\nIt was something like a worm.\n\nHe could feel it beating like a sick and dirty heart, warring with the serene\nbeauty of the rose, screaming harsh profanities against the choir of voices\nwhich had so soothed and lifted him.\n\nHe leaned closer to the rose and saw that its core was not just one sun but many\n. . . perhaps all suns contained within a ferocious yet fragile shell.\nBut it’s wrong. It’s all in danger.\n\nKnowing it would almost surely mean his death to touch that glowing microcosm\nInit helpless to stop himself, Jake reached forward. There was no curiosity or\nterror in this gesture; only a great, inarticulate need to protect the rose.\n\nWHEN HE CAME BACK to himself, he was at first only aware that a great deal of\ntime had passed and his head hurt like hell.\nWhat happened? Was I mugged?\n\nHe rolled over and sat up. Another blast of pain went through his head. He\nraised a hand to his left temple, and his fingers came away sticky with blood.\nHe looked down and saw a brick poking out of the weeds. Its rounded comer was\ntoo red.\n\nIf it had been sharp, I’d probably be dead or in a coma.\nHe looked at his wrist and was surprised to find he was still wearing his watch.\nIt was a Seiko, not terribly expensive, but in this city you didn’t snooze in\nvacant lots without losing your stuff. Expensive or not, someone would be more\nthan happy to relieve you of it. This time he had been lucky, it seemed.\nIt was quarter past four in the afternoon. He had been lying here, dead to the\nworld, for at least five hours. His father probably had the cops out looking for\nhim by now, but that didn’t seem to matter much. It seemed to Jake that he had\nwalked out of Piper School about a thou-sand years ago.\nJake walked half the distance to the fence between the vacant lot and the Second\nAvenue sidewalk, then stopped.\n\nWhat exactly had happened to him?\n\nLittle by little, the memories came back. Hopping the fence. Slip-ping and\ntwisting his ankle. He reached down, touched it, and winced. Yes—that much had\nhappened, all right. Then what?\n\nSomething magical.\n\nHe groped for that something like an old man groping his way across a shadowy\nroom. Everything had been full of its own light. Everything— even the empty\nwrappers and discarded beer-bottles. There had been voices—they had been singing\nand telling thousands of overlapping stories.\n“And faces,” he muttered. This memory made him look around apprehensively. He\nsaw no faces. The piles of bricks were just piles of bricks, and the tangles of\nweeds were just tangles of weeds. There were no faces, but—\n—but they were here. It wasn’t your imagination.\nHe believed that. He couldn’t capture the essence of the memory, its quality of\nbeauty and transcendence, but it seemed perfectly real. It was just that his\nmemory of those moments before he had passed out seemed like photographs taken\non the best day of your life. You can remember what that day was like—sort of,\nanyway—but the pictures are flat and almost powerless.\nJake looked around the desolate lot, now filling up with the violet shadows of\nlate afternoon, and thought: / want you back. God, I want you back the way you\n\nwere.\n\nThen he saw the rose, growing in its clump of purple grass, very close to the\nplace where he had fallen. His heart leaped into his throat. Jake blundered back\ntoward it, unmindful of the beats of pain each step sent up from his ankle. He\ndropped to his knees in front of it like a worshipper at an altar. He leaned\nforward, eyes wide.\n\nIt’s just a rose. Just a rose after all. And the grass—\nThe grass wasn’t purple after all, he saw. There were splatters of purple on the\nblades, yes, but the color beneath was a perfectly normal green. He looked a\nlittle further and saw splashes of blue on another clump of weeds. To his right,\na straggling burdock bush bore traces of both red and yellow. And beyond the\nburdocks was a little pile of dis-carded paint-cans. Glidden Spread Satin, the\nlabels said.\n\nThat’s all it was. Just splatters of paint. Only with your head all messed up\nthe way it was, you thought you were seeing—\nThat was bullshit.\n\nHe knew what he had seen then, and what he was seeing now. “Camouflage,” he\nwhispered. “It was all right here. Everything was. And … it still is.”\nNow that his head was clearing, he could again feel the steady, harmonic power\nthat this place held. The choir was still here, its voice just as musical,\nalthough now dim and distant. He looked at a pile of bricks and old broken\nchunks of plaster and saw a barely discernible face hiding within it. It was the\nface of a woman with a scar on her forehead.\n“Allie?” Jake murmured. “Isn’t your name Allie?”\nThere was no answer. The face was gone. He was only looking at an unlovely pile\nof bricks and plaster again.\n\nHe looked back at the rose. It was, he saw, not the dark red that lives at the\nheart of a blazing furnace, but a dusty, mottled pink. It was very beautiful,\nbut not perfect. Some of the petals had curled back; the outer edges of these\nwere brown and dead. It wasn’t the sort of cultivated flower he had seen in\nflorists’ shops; he supposed it was a wild rose.\n“You’re very beautiful,” he said, and once more stretched his hand out to touch\nit.\n\nAlthough there was no breeze, the rose nodded toward him. For just a moment the\npads of his fingers touched its surface, smooth and velvety and marvellously\nalive, and all around him the voice of the choir seemed to swell.\n“Are you sick, rose?”\n\nThere was no answer, of course. When his fingers left the faded pink bowl of the\nflower, it nodded back to its original position, growing out of the\npaint-splattered weeds in its quiet, forgotten splendor.\nDo roses bloom at this time of year? Jake wondered. Wild ones? Why would a wild\nrose grow in a vacant lot, anyway? And if there’s one, how come there aren’t\nmore?\n\nHe remained on his hands and knees a little longer, then realized he could stay\nhere looking at the rose for the rest of the afternoon (or maybe the rest of his\nlife) and not come any closer to solving its mystery. He had seen it plain for a\nmoment, as he had seen everything else in this forgotten, trash-littered corner\nof the city; he had seen it with its mask off and its camouflage tossed aside.\nHe wanted to see that again, but wanting would not make it so.\nIt was time to go home.\n\nHe saw the two books he’d bought at The Manhattan Restaurant of the Mind lying\nnearby. As he picked them up, a bright silver object slipped from the pages of\nCharlie the Choo-Choo and fell into a scruffy patch of weeds. Jake bent,\nfavoring his hurt ankle, and picked it up. As he did so, the choir seemed to\nsigh and swell, then fell back to its almost inaudible hum.\n“So that part was real, too,” he murmured. He ran the ball of his thumb over the\nblunt protruding points of the key and into those primi-tive V-shaped notches.\nHe sent it skating over the mild s-curves at the end of the third notch. Then he\ntucked it deep into the right front pocket of his pants and began to limp back\n\ntoward the fence.\n\nHe had reached it and was preparing to scramble over the top when a terrible\nthought suddenly seized his mind.\n\nThe rose! What if somebody comes in here and picks it?\nA little moan of horror escaped him. He turned back and after a moment his eyes\npicked it out, although it was deep in the shadow of a neighboring building\nnow—a tiny pink shape in the dimness, vulnerable, beautiful, and alone.\nI can’t leave it—I have to guard it!\n\nBut a voice spoke up in his mind, a voice that was surely that of the man he had\nmet at the way station in that strange other life. No one will pick it. Nor will\nany vandal crush it beneath his heel because his dull eyes cannot abide the\nsight of its beauty. That is not the danger. It can protect itself from such\nthings as those.\n\nA sense of deep relief swept through Jake.\nCan I come here again and look at it? he asked the phantom voice.\nWhen I’m low, or if the voices come hack and start their argument again? Can I\ncome back and look at it and have some peace?\nThe voice did not answer, and after a few moments of listening, Jake decided it\nwas gone. He tucked Charlie the Choo-Choo and Riddle-De-Dum! into the waistband\nof his pants—which, he saw, were streaked with dirt and dotted with clinging\nburdocks—and then grabbed the board fence. He boosted himself up, swung over the\ntop, and dropped onto the sidewalk of Second Avenue again, being careful to land\non his good foot.\n\nTraffic on the Avenue—both pedestrian and vehicular—was much heavier now as\npeople made their way home for the night. A few passersby looked at the dirty\nboy in the torn blazer and untucked, flapping shirt as he jumped awkwardly down\nfrom the fence, but not many. New Yorkers are used to the sight of people doing\npeculiar things.\n\nHe stood there a moment, feeling a sense of loss and realizing some-thing else,\nas well—the arguing voices were still absent. That, at least, was something.\nHe glanced at the board fence; and the verse of spray-painted dog-gerel seemed\nto leap out at him, perhaps because the paint was the same color as the rose.\n“See the TURTLE of enormous girth” Jake muttered. “On his shell he holds the\nearth.” He shivered. “What a day! Boy!”\n\nHe turned and began to limp slowly in the direction of home.\n\nTHE DOORMAN MUST HAVE buzzed up as soon as Jake entered the lobby, because his\nfather was standing outside the elevator when it opened on the fifth floor.\nElmer Chambers was wearing faded jeans and cowboy boots that improved his\nfive-ten to a rootin, tootin six feet. His black, crewcut hair bolted up from\nhis head; for as long as Jake could remem-ber, his father had looked like a man\nwho had just suffered some tremen-dous, galvanizing shock. As soon as Jake\nstepped out of the elevator, Chambers seized him by the arm.\n“Look at you!” His father’s eyes flicked up and down, taking in Jake’s dirty\nface and hands, the blood drying on his cheek and temple, the dusty pants, the\ntorn blazer, and the burdock that clung to his tie like some peculiar clip. “Get\nin here! Where the hell have you been? Your mother’s just about off her fucking\ngourd!”\n\nWithout giving Jake a chance to answer, he dragged him through the apartment\ndoor. Jake saw Greta Shaw standing in the archway between the dining room and\nthe kitchen. She gave him a look of guarded sympathy, then disappeared before\nthe eyes of “the mister” could chance upon her.\nJake’s mother was sitting in her rocker. She got to her feet when she saw Jake,\nbut she did not leap to her feet; neither did she pelt across to the foyer so\nshe could cover him with kisses and invective. As she came toward him, Jake\nassessed her eyes and guessed she’d had at least three Valium since noon. Maybe\nfour. Both of his parents were firm believers in better living through\n\nchemistry.\n\n“You’re bleeding! Where have you been?” She made this inquiry in her cultured\nVassar voice, pronouncing been so it rhymed with seen. She might have been\ngreeting an acquaintance who had been involved in a minor traffic accident.\n“Out,” he said.\n\nHis father gave him a rough shake. Jake wasn’t prepared for it. He stumbled and\ncame down on his bad ankle. The pain flared again, and he was suddenly furious.\nJake didn’t think his father was pissed because he had disappeared from school,\nleaving only his mad composition behind; his father was pissed because Jake had\nhad the temerity to fuck up his own precious schedule.\nTo this point in his life, Jake had been aware of only three feelings about his\nfather: puzzlement, fear, and a species of weak, confused love. Now a fourth and\nfifth surfaced. One was anger; the other was disgust. Mixed in with these\nunpleasant feelings was that sense of homesickness. It was the largest thing\ninside him right now, weaving through everything else like smoke. He looked at\nhis father’s flushed cheeks and screaming haircut and wished he was back in the\nvacant lot, looking at the rose and listening to the choir. This is not my\nplace, he thought. Not anymore. I have work to do. If only I knew what it was.\n“Let go of me,” he said.\n\n“What did you say to me?” His father’s blue eyes widened. They were very\nbloodshot tonight. Jake guessed he had been dipping heavily into his supply of\nmagic powder, and that probably made this a bad time to cross him, but Jake\nrealized he intended to cross him just the same. He would not be shaken like a\nmouse in the jaws of a sadistic tomcat. Not tonight. Maybe not ever again. He\nsuddenly realized that a large part of his anger stemmed from one simple fact:\nhe could not talk to them about what had happened—what was still happening. They\nhad closed all the doors.\n\nBut I have a key, he thought, and touched its shape through the fabric of his\npants. And the rest of that strange verse occurred to him: If you want to run\nand play, /Come along the BEAM today.\n\n“I said let go of me,” he repeated. “I’ve got a sprained ankle and you’re\nhurting it.”\n\n“I’ll hurt more than your ankle if you don’t—”\nSudden strength seemed to How into Jake. He seized the hand clamped on his arm\njust below the shoulder and shoved it violently away. His father’s mouth dropped\nopen.\n\n“I don’t work for you,” Jake said. “I’m your son, remember? If you forgot, check\nthe picture on your desk.”\n\nHis father’s upper lip pulled back from his perfectly capped teeth in a snarl\nthat was two parts surprise and one part fury. “Don’t you talk to me like that,\nmister—where in the hell is your respect?”\n“I don’t know. Maybe I lost it on the way home.”\n“You spend the whole goddamn day absent without leave and then you stand there\nrunning your fat, disrespectful mouth—“\n\n“Stop it! Stop it, both of you!” Jake’s mother cried. She sounded near tears in\nspite of the tranquilizers perking through her system.\nJake’s father reached for Jake’s arm again, then changed his mind. The\nsurprising force with which his son had torn his hand away a moment ago might\nhave had something to do with it. Or perhaps it was only the look in Jake’s\neyes. “I want to know where you’ve been.”\n“Out. I told you that. And that’s all I’m going to tell you.”\n“Fuck that! Your headmaster called, your French teacher actually came here, and\nthey both had beaucoup questions for you! So do I, and I want some answers!”\n“Your clothes are dirty,” his mother observed, and then added tim-idly: “Were\nyou mugged, Johnny? Did you play hookey and get mugged?”\n“Of course he wasn’t mugged,” Elmer Chambers snarled. “Still wearing his watch,\nisn’t he?”\n\n“But there’s blood on his head.”\n\n“It’s okay, Mom. I just bumped it.”\n\n“But—“\n\n“I’m going to go to bed. I’m very, very tired. If you want to talk about this in\nthe morning, okay. Maybe we’ll all be able to make some sense then. The seam under the right arm, already strained, let go with a rough\npurring sound.\n\nHis father saw those blazing eyes and stepped away. The rage on his face was\ndoused by something that looked like terror. That blaze was not metaphorical;\nJake’s eyes actually seemed to be on fire. His mother gave voice to a\nstrengthless little scream, clapped one hand to her mouth, took two large,\nstumbling steps backward, and dropped into her rocking chair with a small thud.\n“Leave. . me . . . alone,” Jake said.\n\n“What’s happened to you?” his father asked, and now his tone was almost\nplaintive. “What in the hell’s happened to you? You bug out of school without a\nword to anyone on the first day of exams, you come back filthy from head to toe\n. . . and you act as if you’ve gone crazy.”\nWell, there it was—you act as if you’ve gone crazy. What he’d been afraid of\never since the voices started three weeks ago. The Dread Accu-sation. Only now\nthat it was out, Jake found it didn’t frighten him much at all, perhaps because\nhe had finally put the issue to rest in his own mind. Yes, something had\nhappened to him. Was still happening. But no—he had not gone crazy. At least,\nnot yet.\n\n“We’ll talk about it in the morning,” he repeated. He walked across the dining\nroom, and this time his father didn’t try to stop him. His mother’s voice was only\na murmur, his father’s voice a little louder.\nHis mother said something about blood, and a doctor.\nHis father said the kid was fine; the only thing wrong with the kid was the junk\ncoming out of his mouth, and he would fix that.\nHis mother said something about calming down.\nHis father said he was calm.\n\nHis mother said—\n\nHe said, she said, blah, blah, blah. Jake still loved them—he was pretty sure he\ndid, anyway—but other stuff had happened now, and these things had made it\nnecessary that still other things must occur.\nWhy? Because something was wrong with the rose. And maybe because he wanted to\nrun and play . . . and see his eyes again, as blue as the sky above the way\nstation had been.\n\nJake walked slowly over to his desk, removing his blazer as he went. It was\npretty wasted—one sleeve torn almost completely off, the lining hanging like a\nlimp sail. He slung it over the back of his chair, then sat down and put the\nbooks on his desk. He had been sleeping very badly over the last week and a\nhalf, hut he thought tonight he would sleep well. He couldn’t remember ever\nbeing so tired. When he woke up in the morning, perhaps he would know what to\ndo.\n\nThere was a light knock at the door, and Jake turned warily in that direction.\n\n“It’s Mrs. Shaw, John. May I come in for a minute?”\nHe smiled. Mrs. Shaw—of course it was. His parents had drafted her as an\nintermediary. Or perhaps translator might be a better word.\nYou go see him, his mother would have said. Hell tell you what’s wrong with him.\nI’m his mother and this man with the bloodshot eyes and the runny nose is his\nfather and you’re only the housekeeper, but he’ll tell you what he wouldn’t tell\nus. Did you buy this today, Johnny?”\n“Yes. Did my parents ask you to find out what I’d been up to?”\nShe nodded. No acting, no put-on. It was just a chore, like taking out the\ntrash. You can tell me if you want to, her face said, or you can keep still. I\nlike you, Johnny, but it’s really nothing to me, one way or the other. I just\nwork here, and it’s already an hour past my regular quitting time.\nHe was not offended by what her face had to say; on the contrary, he was further\ncalmed by it. Mrs. Shaw was another acquaintance who was not quite a friend . .\n. but he thought she might be a little closer to a friend than any of the kids\nat school were, and much closer than either his mother or father. Mrs. Shaw was\nhonest, at least. She didn’t dance. It all went on the bill at the end of the\nmonth, and she always cut the crusts off the sandwiches.\nJake picked up a sandwich and took a large bite. Bologna and cheese, his\nfavorite. That was another thing in Mrs. Shaw’s favor—she knew all his\nfavorites. His mother was still under the impression that he liked corn on the\ncob and hated Brussels sprouts.\n\n“Please tell them I’m fine,” he said, “and tell my father I’m sorry that I was\nrude to him.”\n\nHe wasn’t, but all his father really wanted was that apology. Once Mrs. Shaw\nconveyed it to him, he would relax and begin to tell himself the old lie—he had\ndone his fatherly duty and all was well, all was well, and all manner of things\nwere well.\n\n“I’ve been studying very hard for my exams,” he said, chewing as he talked, “and\nit all came down on me this morning, I guess. I might have to re-do my Final Essay in English,\nbut-“\n\n“Oh!” Mrs. Shaw said. A startled looked crossed her face. She put Charlie the\nChoo-Choo back down on Jake’s desk. “I almost forgot! Your French teacher left\nsomething for you. I’ll just get it.”\n\nShe left the room. Jake hoped he hadn’t worried Mr. Bissette, who was a pretty\ngood guy, but he supposed he must have, since Bissette had actually made a\n\npersonal appearance. His best\nguess was an invitation to talk with Mr. Hotchkiss, the school shrink. She’s very concerned about you, and so\nam I, although we have both seen this sort of thing before, especially during\nExam Week. Please come and see me first thing tomorrow, okay? It is stun-ningly original, and superior to any student\nwork I have read in the last few years. Your use of incremental repetition (”. .\n. and that is the truth”) is inspired, but of course incremental repetition is\nreally just a trick. The real worth of the composition is in its symbolic\nquality, first stated by the images of the train and the door on the title page\n\nand carried through splendidly within. This reaches its logical conclusion with\n\nthe picture of the “black tower,” which I take as your statement that\nconventional ambitions are not only false but dangerous.\nI do not pretend to understand all the symbolism (e.g., “Lady of Shadows,”\n“gunslinger”) but it seems clear that you yourself are “The Prisoner” (of\nschool, society, etc.) and that the educational system is “The Speaking Demon.”\nIs it possible that both “Roland” and “the gunslinger” are the same authority\nfigure—your father, perhaps? I became so intrigued by this possibility that up his name in your records. I note it is Elmer, but I further note that\nhis middle initial is R.
8Part 7Darktower 3 - The Waste Lands\n\nI find this extremely provocative. Or is this name a double symbol, drawn both\nfrom your father and from Robert Brow-ning’s poem “Childe Roland to the Dark\nTower Came”? This is not a question I would ask most students, but of course I\nknow how omnivorously you read!\n\nAt any rate, I am extremely impressed. Younger students are often attracted to\nso-called “stream-of-consciousness” writing, but are rarely able to control it.\nYou have done an outstanding job of merging s-of-c with symbolic language.\nBravo!\n\nDrop by as soon as you’re “back at it”—I want to discuss possible publication of\nthis piece in the first issue of next year’s student literary magazine.\nB. Avery\n\nP. S. If you left school today because you had sudden doubts about my ability to\nunderstand a Final Essay of such unexpected richness, I hope I have assuaged\nthem.\n\nJake pulled the sheet off the clip, revealing the title page of his stunningly\noriginal and richly symbolic Final Essay. Written and circled there in the red\nink of Ms. Avery’s marking pen was the notation A +. Below this she had written\nEXCELLENT JOB!!!\n\nJake began to laugh.\n\nThe whole day—the long, scary, confusing, exhilarating, terrify-ing, mysterious\nday—was condensed in great, roaring sobs of laughter. He slumped in his chair,\nhead thrown hack, hands clutching his belly, tears streaming down his face. He\nlaughed himself hoarse. He would almost stop and then some line from Ms. Avery’s\nwell-meaning cri-tique would catch his eye and he would be off to the races\nagain. He didn’t see his father come to the door, look in at him with puzzled,\nwary eyes, and then leave again, shaking his head.\nAt last he did become aware that Mrs. Shaw was still sitting on his bed, looking\nat him with an expression of friendly detachment tinctured with faint curiosity.\nHe tried to speak, but the laughter pealed out again before he could.\nI gotta stop, he thought. I gotta stop or it’s gonna kill me. I’ll have a stroke\nor a heart attack, or something.\n\nThen he thought, 7 wonder what she made of “choo-choo, choo-choo?,” and he began\nto laugh wildly again.\n\nAt last the spasms began to taper off to giggles. He wiped his arm across his\nstreaming eyes and said, “I’m sorry, Mrs. Shaw—it’s just that . . . well … I\ngot an A-plus on my Final Essay. It was all very . . . very rich . . . and very\nsym . . . sym . . .”\n\nBut he couldn’t finish. He doubled up with laughter again, hold-ing his\nthrobbing belly.\n\nMrs. Shaw got up, smiling. “That’s very nice, John. I’m happy it’s all turned\nout so well, and I’m sure your folks will be, too. I’m awfully late—I think I’ll\nask the doorman to call me a cab. Goodnight, and sleep well.”\n“Goodnight, Mrs. Shaw,” Jake said, controlling himself with an effort. “And\nthanks.”\n\nAs soon as she was gone, he began to laugh again.\n\nDURING THE NEXT HALF hour he had separate visits from both parents. They had\n\nindeed calmed down, and the A + grade on Jake’s Final Essay seemed to calm them\nfurther. Jake received them with his French text open on the desk before him,\nbut he hadn’t really looked at it, nor did he have any intention of looking at\nit. He was only waiting for them to be gone so he could study the two books he\nhad bought earlier that day. He had an idea that the real Final Exams were still\nwaiting just over the horizon, and he wanted desperately to pass.\nHis father poked his head into Jake’s room around quarter of ten, about twenty\nminutes after Jake’s mother had concluded her own short, vague visit. Elmer\nChambers was holding a cigarette in one hand and a glass of Scotch in the other.\nHe seemed not only calmer but almost zonked. Jake wondered briefly and\nindifferently if he had been hitting his mother’s Valium supply.\n“Are you okay, kid?”\n\n“Yes.” He was once again the small, neat boy who was always com-pletely in\ncontrol of himself. The eyes he turned to his father were not blazing but\nopaque.\n\n“I wanted to say I’m sorry about before.” His father was not a man who made many\napologies, and he did it badly. Jake found himself feeling a little sorry for\nhim.\n\n“It’s all right.”\n\n“Hard day,” his father said. He gestured with the empty glass. “Why don’t we\njust forget it happened?” He spoke as if this great and logical idea had just\ncome to him.\n\n“I already have.”\n\n“Good.” His father sounded relieved. “Time for you to get some sleep, isn’t it?\nYou’ll have some explaining to do and some tests to take tomorrow.”\n“I guess so,” Jake said. “Is Mom okay?”\n\n“Fine. Fine. I’m going in the study. Got a lot of paperwork tonight.”\n“Dad?”\n\nHis father looked back at him warily.\n\n“What’s your middle name?”\n\nSomething in his father’s face told Jake that he had looked at the Final Essay\ngrade but hadn’t bothered to read either the paper itself or Ms. Avery’s\ncritique.\n\n“I don’t have one,” he said. “Just an initial, like Harry S Truman. Except\nmine’s an R. What brought that on?”\n\n“Just curious,” Jake said.\n\nHe managed to hold onto his composure until his father was gone . . . but as\nsoon as the door was closed, he ran to his bed and stuffed his face into his\npillow to muffle another bout of wild laughter.\n\nWHEN HE WAS SURE he was over the current fit (although an occasional snicker\nstill rumbled up his throat like an aftershock) and his father would be safely\nlocked away in his study with his cigarettes, his Scotch, his papers, and his\nlittle bottle of white powder, Jake went back to his desk, turned on the study\nlamp, and opened Charlie the Choo-Choo. He glanced briefly at the copyright page\nand saw it had originally been published in 1942; his copy was from the fourth\nprinting. He looked at the back, but there was no information at all about Beryl\nEvans, the book’s author.\n\nJake turned back to the beginning, looked at the picture of a grin-ning,\nblonde-haired man sitting in the cab of a steam locomotive, consid-ered the\nproud grin on the man’s face, and then began to read.\nBob Brooks was an engineer for the Mid-World Railway Company, on the St. Louis\nto Topeka run. Engineer Bob was the best trainman The Mid-World Railway Company\never had, and Charlie was the best train!\nCharlie was a 402 Big Boy Steam Locomotive, and Engineer Bob was the only man\nwho had ever been allowed to sit in his peak-seat and pull the whistle. It showed Charlie the Choo-Choo pulling two\nbunting-decked passenger cars filled with happy children from the roller coaster\nto the Ferns wheel. Engineer Bob sat in the cab, pulling the whistle-cord and\nlooking as happy as a pig in shit. Jake sup-posed Engineer Bob’s smile was\nsupposed to convey supreme happiness, but to him it looked like the grin of a\nlunatic. Please,\njust let us off this train alive.\n\nAnd be a happy choo-choo train until the day I die.\nJake closed the book and looked at it thoughtfully. Then he opened it again and\n\nbegan to leaf through the pages, circling certain words and phrases that seemed\n\nto call out to him.\n\nThe Mid-World Railway Company . . . The voices began to argue again\nabout whether he was alive or dead, and they kept him awake. At last he sat up\nin bed with his eyes closed and his fisted hands planted against his temples.\nQuit! he screamed at them. Just quit! You were gone all day, be gone again!\nI would if he’d just admit I’m dead, one of the voices said sulkily.\nI would if he’d just take a for God’s sake look around and admit I’m clearly\nalive, the other snapped back.\n\nHe was going to scream right out loud. There was no way to hold it back; he\ncould feel it coming up his throat like vomit. He opened his eyes, saw his pants\nlying over the seat of his desk chair, and an idea occurred to him. He got out\nof bed, went to the chair, and felt in the right front pocket of the pants.\nThe silver key was still there, and the moment his fingers closed around it, the\nvoices ceased.\n\nTell him, he thought, with no idea who the thought was for. Tell him to grab the\nkey. The key makes the voices go.\n\nHe went back to bed and was asleep with the key clasped loosely in his hand\nthree minutes after his head hit the pillow.\n\nIII • DOOR AND DEMON\n\n•Ill•\n\nDOOR AND DEMON\n\nEDDIE WAS ALMOST ASLEEP when a voice spoke clearly in his ear: Tell him to grab\nthe key. The key makes the voices go.\n\nHe sat bolt upright, looking around wildly. Susannah was sound asleep beside\nhim; that voice had not been hers.\n\nNor anyone else’s, it seemed. They had been moving through the woods and along\nthe path of the Beam for eight days now, and this evening they had camped in the\ndeep cleft of a pocket valley. Close by on the left, a large stream roared\nbrashly past, headed in the same direc-tion as they were: southeast. To the\nright, firs rose up a steep slope of land. There were no intruders here; only\nSusannah asleep and Roland awake. He sat huddled beneath his blanket at the edge\nof the stream’s cut, staring out into the darkness.\nTell him to grab the key. The key makes the voices go.\nEddie hesitated for only a moment. Roland’s sanity was in the bal-ance now, the\nbalance was tipping the wrong way, and the worst part of it was this: no one\nknew it better than the man himself. At this point, Eddie was prepared to clutch\nat any straw.\n\nHe had been using a folded square of deerskin as a pillow. He reached beneath it\nand removed a bundle wrapped in a piece of hide. He walked over to Roland, and\nwas disturbed to see that the gunslinger did not notice him until he was less\nthan four steps from his unprotected back. There had been a time—and it was not\nso long ago—when Roland would have known Eddie was awake even before Eddie sat\nup. He would have heard the change in his breathing.\nHe was more alert than this back on the beach, when he was half-dead from the\nlobster-thing’s bite, Eddie thought grimly.\nRoland at last turned his head and glanced at him. His eyes were bright with\npain and weariness, but Eddie recognized these things as no more than a surface\nglitter. Beneath it, he sensed a growing confusion that would almost surely\nbecome madness if it continued to develop unchecked. Pity tugged at Eddie’s\nheart.\n\n“Can’t sleep?” Roland asked. His voice was slow, almost drugged.\n“I almost was, and then I woke up,” Eddie said. “Listen—”\n“I think I’m getting ready to die.” Roland looked at Eddie. The bright shine\nleft his eyes, and now looking into them was like staring into a pair of deep,\ndark wells that seemed to have no bottom. Eddie shud-dered, more because of that\nempty stare than because of what Roland had said. “And do you know what I hope\n\nlies in the clearing where the path ends, Eddie?”\n\n“Roland—“\n\n“Silence,” Roland said. He exhaled a dusty sigh. “Just silence. That will be\nenough. An end to … this.”\n\nHe planted his fists against his temples, and Eddie thought: I’ve seen someone\nelse do that, and not long ago. But who? Where?\nIt was ridiculous of course; he had seen no one but Roland and Susannah for\nalmost two months now. But it felt true, all the same.\n“Roland, I’ve been making something,” Eddie said.\nRoland nodded. A ghost of a smile touched his lips. “I know. What is it? Are you\nfinally ready to tell?”\n\n“I think it might be part of this ka-tet thing.”\nThe vacant look left Roland’s eyes. He gazed at Eddie thoughtfully but said\nnothing.\n\n“Look.” Eddie began to unfold the piece of hide.\nThat won’t do any good! Henry’s voice suddenly brayed. It was so loud that Eddie\nactually flinched a little. It’s just a stupid piece of wood-carving! He’ll take\none look and laugh at it! He’ll laugh at you! “Oh, lookit this!” he’ll say. “Did\nthe sissy carve something?”\n\n“Shut up,” Eddie muttered.\n\nThe gunslinger raised his eyebrows.\n\n“Not you.”\n\nRoland nodded, unsurprised. “Your brother comes to you often, doesn’t he,\nEddie?”\n\nFor a moment Eddie only stared at him, his carving still hidden in the hide\nsquare. Then he smiled. It was not a very pleasant smile. “Not as often as he\nused to, Roland. Thank Christ for small favors.”\n“Yes,” Roland said. “Too many voices weigh heavy on a man’s heart . . . What is\nit, Eddie? Show me, please.”\n\nEddie held up the chunk of ash. The key, almost complete, emerged from it like\nthe head of a woman from the prow of a sailing ship … or the hilt of a sword\nfrom a chunk of stone. Eddie didn’t know how close he had come to duplicating\nthe key-shape he had seen in the fire (and never would, he supposed, unless he\nfound the right lock in which to try it), but he thought it was close. Of one\nthing he was quite sure: it was the best carving he had ever done. By far.\n“By the gods, Eddie, it’s beautiful!” Roland said. The apathy was gone from his\nvoice; he spoke in a tone of surprised reverence Eddie had never heard before.\n“Is it done? It’s not, is it?”\n\n“No—not quite.” He ran his thumb into the third notch, and then over the s-shape\nat the end of the last notch. “There’s a little more to do on this notch, and\nthe curve at the end isn’t right yet. I don’t know how I know that, but I do.”\n“This is your secret.” It wasn’t a question.\n“Yes. Now if only I knew what it meant.”\nRoland looked around. Eddie followed his gaze and saw Susannah. He found some\nrelief in the fact that Roland had heard her first.\n“What you boys doin up so late? Chewin the fat?” She saw the wooden key in\nEddie’s hand and nodded. “I wondered when you were going to get around to\nshowing that off. It’s good, you know. I don’t know what it’s for, but it’s\ndamned good.”\n\n“You don’t have any idea what door it might open?” Roland asked Eddie. “That was\nnot part of your khef?”\n\n“No—but it might be good for something even though it isn’t done.” He held the\nkey out to Roland. “I want you to keep it for me.”\nRoland didn’t move to take it. He regarded Eddie closely. “Why?”\n“Because . . . well. . . because I think someone told me you should.”\n“Who?”\n\nYour boy, Eddie thought suddenly, and as soon as the thought came he knew it was\ntrue. It was your goddamned boy. But he didn’t want to say so. He didn’t want to\nmention the boy’s name at all. It might just set Roland off again.\n\n“I don’t know. But I think you ought to give it a try.”\n\nRoland reached slowly for the key. As his fingers touched it, a bright glimmer\nseemed to flash down its barrel, but it was gone so quickly that Eddie could not\nbe sure he had seen it. It might have been only starlight.\nRoland’s hand closed over the key growing out of the branch. For a moment his\nface showed nothing. Then his brow furrowed and his head cocked in a listening\ngesture.\n\n“What is it?” Susannah asked. “Do you hear—”\n“Shhhh!” The puzzlement on Roland’s face was slowly being replaced with wonder.\nHe looked from Eddie to Susannah and then back to Eddie. His eyes were filling\nwith some great emotion, as a pitcher fills with water when it is dipped in a\nspring.\n\n“Roland?” Eddie asked uneasily. “Are you all right?”\nRoland whispered something. Eddie couldn’t hear what it was.\nSusannah looked scared. She glanced frantically at Eddie, as if to ask, What did\nyou do to him?\n\nEddie took one of her hands in both of his own. “I think it’s all right.”\nRoland’s hand was clamped so tightly on the chunk of wood that Eddie was\nmomentarily afraid he might snap it in two, but the wood was strong and Eddie\nhad carved thick. The gunslinger’s throat bulged; his Adam’s apple rose and fell\nas he struggled with speech. And suddenly he yelled at the sky in a fair, strong\nvoice:\n\n“GONE! THE VOICES ARE GONE!”\n\nHe looked back at them, and Eddie saw something he had never expected to see in\nhis life—not even if that life stretched over a thousand years.\nRoland of Gilead was weeping.\n\nTHE GUNSLINGER SLEPT SOUNDLY and dreamlessly that night for the first time in\nmonths, and he slept with the not-quite finished key clenched tightly in his\nhand.\n\nIN ANOTHER WORLD, BUT beneath the shadow of the same ka-tet, Jake Chambers was\nhaving the most vivid dream of his life.\nHe was walking through the tangled remains of an ancient forest— a dead zone of\nfallen trees and scruffy, aggravating bushes that bit his ankles and tried to\nsteal his sneakers. He came to a thin belt of younger trees (alders, he thought,\nor perhaps beeches—he was a city boy, and the only thing he knew for sure about\ntrees was that some had leaves and some had needles) and discovered a path\nthrough them. He made his way along this, moving a little faster. There was a\nclearing of some sort up ahead.\n\nHe stopped once before reaching it, when he spied some sort of stone marker to\nhis right. He left the path to look at it. There were letters carved into it,\nbut they were so eroded he couldn’t make them out. At last he closed his eyes\n(he had never done this in a dream before) and let his fingers trace each\nletter, like a blind boy reading Braille. Each formed in the darkness behind his\nlids until they made a sentence which stood forth in an outline of blue light:\nTRAVELLER, BEYOND LIES MID-WORLD.\n\nSleeping in his bed, Jake drew his knees up against his chest. The hand holding\nthe key was under his pillow, and now his fingers tightened their grip on it.\nMid-World, he thought, of course. St. Louis and Topeka and Oz and the World’s\nFair and Charlie the Choo-Choo.\n\nHe opened his dreaming eyes and pressed on. The clearing behind the trees was\npaved with old cracked asphalt. A faded yellow circle had been painted in the\n\nmiddle. Jake realized it was a playground basketball court even before he saw\n\nthe boy at the far end, standing at the foul line and shooting baskets with a\ndusty old Wilson ball. His hair was darker, and when he looked at Jake, he\nsaw that the stranger’s eyes were hazel. His own were blue.\nWhat do you think? the strange boy asked, and bounce-passed the ball to Jake.\nNo, of course not, Jake said. He spoke apologetically. It’s just that I’ve been\ncut in two for the last three weeks or so. He dipped and shot from mid-court.\nThe ball arched high and dropped silently through the hoop. He was delighted . .\n. but he discovered he was also afraid of what this strange boy might have to\ntell him.\n\nI know, the boy said. It’s been a bitch for you, hasn’t it? He was wearing faded\nmadras shorts and a yellow t-shirt that said NEVER A DULL MOMENT IN MID-WORLD.\nHe had tied a green bandanna around his forehead to keep his hair out of his\neyes. And things are going to get worse before they get better.\nWhat is this place? Jake asked. And who are you?\nIt’s the Portal of the Bear . . . but it’s also Brooklyn.\nThat didn’t seem to make sense, and yet somehow it did. Jake told himself that\nthings always seemed that way in dreams, but this didn’t really feel like a\ndream.\n\nAs for me, I don’t matter much, the boy said. He hooked the basket-ball over his\nshoulder. It rose, then dropped smoothly through the hoop. I’m supposed to guide\nyou, that’s all. I’ll take you where you need to go, and I’ll show you what you\nneed to see, but you have to be careful because I won’t know you. And strangers\nmake Henry nervous. He can get mean when he’s nervous, and he’s bigger than you.\nWho’s Henry? Jake asked.\n\nNever mind. Just don’t let him notice you. All you have to do is hang out . . .\nand follow us. Just\ntake the subway to Co-Op City. You’ll find me.\nNo, I won’t! Jake cried. Co-Op City’s huge! There must be a hundred thousand\npeople living there!\n\nNow the boy was just a milky outline. You found the key and the rose, didn’t you?\nYou’ll find me the same way. This afternoon, Jake. Around three o’clock should\nbe good. You’ll have to be careful, and you’ll have to be quick. You have to tell me what all this is about! You have to\ntell me why these things are happening to me!\nBecause of the Beam, the boy who was now only a pair of floating eyes replied,\nand because of the Tower. In the end, all things, even the Beams, serve the Dark\nTower. Did you think you would be any different?\nJake flailed and stumbled to his feet. Will I find him? There was something\nwrong with that sound, and he thought that what was wrong with the machinery was\naffecting the rose, or vice-versa. It was all hooked together somehow.\nHe picked up the old, scuffed-up basketball and shot. It went neatly through the\nhoop . . . and disappeared.\n\nA river, the strange boy’s voice sighed. It was like a puff of breeze. It came\nfrom nowhere and everywhere. The answer is a river.\n\nJAKE WOKE IN THE first milky light of dawn, looking up at the ceiling of his\nroom. He was thinking of the guy in The Manhattan Restaurant of the Mind—Aaron\nDeepneau, who’d been hanging around on Bleecker Street back when Bob Dylan only\nknew how to blow open G on his Hohner. A river ran; a river had a mouth; a river had a bed; a\nriver had a head. The boy had told him the answer. The boy in the dream.\nAnd suddenly he thought of something else Deepneau had said: That’s only half\nthe answer. Across the room, set into a wall\notherwise lined with books, were three Mitsubishi television monitors. Each was\ntuned to one of the rival networks, and at night, when his father was in here,\neach played out its progression of prime-time images with the sound off.\nThe curtains were drawn, and Jake had to turn on the desk lamp in order to see.\nHe felt nervous just being in here, even wearing sneakers. Several seconds passed. When the creak\ndidn’t come again, Jake pulled out the weapon his father kept for “home\ndefense”—a .44 Ruger automatic. He\nwas moving southeast, along the path of the Beam, resuming his own interrupted\nquest for the Dark Tower.\n\nTWO DAYS AFTER EDDIE had given Roland his unfinished key, the three\ntravellers—hot, sweaty, tired, and out of sorts—pushed through a particu-larly\ntenacious tangle of bushes and second-growth trees and discovered what first\nappeared to be two faint paths, running in tandem beneath the interlacing\nbranches of the old trees crowding close on either side. After a few moments of\nstudy, Eddie decided they weren’t just paths but the remains of a long-abandoned\nroad. Bushes and stunted trees grew like untidy quills along what had been its\ncrown. The grassy indentations were wheelruts, and either of them was wide\nenough to accommodate Susannah’s wheelchair.\n“Hallelujah!” he cried. “Let’s drink to it!”\nRoland nodded and unslung the waterskin he wore around his waist. 9Part 8Darktower 3 - The Waste Lands\n\nEddie hesitated. “A little, but they’re confused. I’m a kid again, I know that\nmuch. It’s after school. He was holding the last of his burritos a la\nRoland in one hand, but he had lost his appetite. “I think it’s the kid you met\n\nat the way station,” he said at last. “I think your old friend Jake was hanging\n\naround, watching me and Henry on the afternoon we went over to Dutch Hill. I\nthink he followed us. Because he hears the voices, just like you, Roland. And\nbecause he’s sharing my dreams, and I’m sharing his. I think that what I\nremember is what’s happening now, in Jake’s when. The kid is trying to come back\nhere. And if the key isn’t done when he makes his move—or if it’s done\nwrong—he’s probably going to die.”\n\nRoland said, “Maybe he has a key of his own. Is that possible?”\n“Yeah, I think it is,” Eddie said, “but it isn’t enough.” He sighed and stuck\nthe last burrito in his pocket for later. “And I don’t think he knows that.”\n\nTHEY MOVED ALONG, ROLAND and Eddie trading off on Susannah’s wheelchair. They\npicked the left-hand wheelrut. The chair bumped and pitched, and every now and\nthen Eddie and Roland had to lift it over the cobbles which stuck out of the\ndirt here and there like old teeth. They were still making faster, easier time\nthan they had in a week, how-ever. The ground was rising, and when Eddie looked\nover his shoulder he could see the forest sloping away in what looked like a\nseries of gentle steps. Far to the northwest, he could see a ribbon of water\nspilling over a fractured rock face. It was, he realized with wonder, the place\nthey had dubbed “the shooting gallery.” Now it was almost lost behind them in\nthe haze of this dreaming summer afternoon.\n“Whoa down, boy!” Susannah called sharply. Eddie faced forward again just in\ntime to keep from pushing the wheelchair into Roland. The gunslinger had stopped\nand was peering into the tangled bushes at the left of the road.\n“You keep that up, I’m gonna revoke your driver’s license,” Susannah said\nwaspily.\n\nEddie ignored her. He was following Roland’s gaze. “What is it?”\n“One way to find out.” He turned, hoisted Susannah from her chair, ~and planted\nher on his hip. “Let’s all take a look.”\n“Put me down, big boy—I can make my way. Easier’n you boys, if you really want\nto know.”\n\nAs Roland gently lowered her to the grassy wheelrut, Eddie peered into the\nwoods. The late light threw overlapping crosses of shadow, but he thought he saw\nwhat had caught Roland’s eye. It was a tall gray stone, almost completely hidden\nin a shag of vines and creepers.\n\nSusannah slipped into the woods at the side of the road with eely sinuousness.\nRoland and Eddie followed.\n\n“It’s a marker, isn’t it?” Susannah was propped on her hands study-ing die\nrectangular chunk of rock. It had once been straight, but now it leaned\ndrunkenly to the right, like an old gravestone.\n“Yes. Give me my knife, Eddie.”\n\nEddie handed it over, then hunkered next to Susannah as the gun-slinger cut away\nthe vines. As they fell, he could see eroded letters carved into the stone, and\nhe knew what they said before Roland had uncovered even half of the inscription:\nTRAVELLER, BEYOND LIES MID-WORLD.\n\n“WHAT DOES IT MEAN?” Susannah asked at last. Her voice was soft and awestruck;\nher eyes ceaselessly measured the gray stone plinth.\n“It means that we’re nearing the end of this first stage.” Roland’s face was\nsolemn and thoughtful as he handed his knife back to Eddie. “I think that we’ll\nkeep to this old coach-road now—or rather, it will keep to us. It has taken up\nthe path of the Beam. The woods will end soon. I expect a great change.”\n“What is Mid-World?” Eddie asked.\n\n“One of the large kingdoms which dominated the earth in the times before these.\n\nA kingdom of hope and knowledge and light—the sort of things we were trying to\n\nhold onto in my land before the darkness over-took us, as well. Some day if\nthere’s time, I’ll tell you all the old stories . . . the ones I know, at least.\nThey form a large tapestry, one which is beautiful but very sad.\n“According to the old tales, a great city once stood at the edge of\nMid-World—perhaps as great as your city of New York. It will be in ruins now, if\nit still exists at all. But there may be people … or monsters … or both.\nWe’ll have to be on our guard.”\n\nHe reached out his two-fingered right hand and touched the inscrip-tion.\n“Mid-World,” he said in a low, meditative voice. “Who would have thought . . .”\nHe trailed off.\n\n“Well, there’s no help for it, is there?” Eddie asked.\nThe gunslinger shook his head. “No help.”\n“Ka,” Susannah said suddenly, and they both looked at her.\n\nTHERE WERE TWO HOURS of daylight left, and so they moved on. The road continued\nsoutheast, along the path of the Beam, and two other overgrown roads—smaller\nones—joined the one they were following. Along one side of the second were the\nmossy, tumbled remains of what must have once been an immense rock wall. Nearby,\na dozen fat billy-bumblers sat upon the ruins, watching the pilgrims with their\nodd gold-ringed eyes. To Eddie they looked like a jury with hanging on its mind.\nThe road continued to grow wider and more clearly defined. Twice they passed the\nshells of long-deserted buildings. The second one, Roland said, might have been\na windmill. Susannah said it looked haunted. “I wouldn’t be surprised,” the\ngunslinger replied. His matter-of-fact tone chilled both of them.\nWhen darkness forced a halt, the trees were thinning and the breeze which had\nchased around tin-in all day became a light, warm wind. Ahead, the land\ncontinued to rise.\n\n“We’ll come to the top of the ridge in a day or two,” Roland said. “Then we’ll\nsee.”\n\n“See what?” Susannah asked, but Roland only shrugged.\nThat night Eddie began to carve again, but with no real feeling of inspiration.\nThe confidence and happiness he’d felt as the key first began to take shape had\nleft him. His fingers felt clumsy and stupid. For the first time in months he\nthought longingly of how good it would be to have some heroin. Not a lot; he\nfelt sure that a nickel bag and a rolled-up dollar bill would send him flying\nthrough this little carving project in no time flat.\n“What are you smiling about, Eddie?” Roland asked. He was sitting on the other\nside of the campfire; the low, wind-driven flames danced capriciously between\nthem.\n\n“Was I smiling?”\n\n“Yes.”\n\n“I was just thinking about how stupid some people can be—you put them in a room\nwith six doors, they’ll still walk into the walls. And then have the nerve to\nbitch about it.”\n\n“If you’re afraid of what might be on the other side of the doors, maybe\nbouncing off the walls seems safer,” Susannah said.\nEddie nodded. “Maybe so.”\n\nHe worked slowly, trying to see the shapes in the wood—that little s-shape in\nparticular. He discovered it had become very dim.\nPlease, God, help me not to fuck this up, he thought, but he was terribly afraid\nthat he had already begun to do just that. At last he gave up, returned the key\n(which he had barely changed at all) to the gun-slinger, and curled up beneath\none of the hides. Five minutes later, the dream about the boy and the old Markey\nAvenue playground had begun to unspool again.\n\nJAKE STEPPED OUT OF his apartment building at about quarter of seven, which left\nhim with over eight hours to kill. He considered taking the train out to\nBrooklyn right away, then decided it was a bad idea. A kid out of school was apt\nto attract more attention in the hinterlands than in the heart of a big city,\nand if he really had to search for the place and the boy he was supposed to meet\nthere, he was cooked already.\n\nNo problem-o, the boy in the yellow T-shirt and green bandanna had said. You\nfound the key and the rose, didn’t you? You’ll find me the same way.\nExcept Jake could no longer remember just how he had found the key and the rose.\nHe could only remember the joy and the sense of surety which had filled his\nheart and head. He would just have to hope that would happen again. In the\nmeantime, he’d keep moving. That was the best way to keep from being noticed in\nNew York.\n\nHe walked most of the way to First Avenue, then headed back the way he had come,\nonly sliding uptown little by little as he followed the pattern of the WALK\nlights (perhaps knowing, on some deep level, that even they served the Beam).\nAround ten o’clock he found himself in front of the Metropolitan Museum of Art\non Fifth Avenue. He was hot, tired, and depressed. He wanted a soda, but he\nthought he ought to hold onto what little money he had for as long as he could.\nHe’d taken every cent out of the box he kept by his bed, but it only amounted to\neight dollars, give or take a few cents.\nA group of school-kids were lining up for a tour. Public school, Jake was almost\nsure—they were dressed as casually as he was. No blazers from Paul Stuart, no\nties, no jumpers, no simple little skirts that cost a hundred and twenty-five\nbucks at places like Miss So Pretty or Tweenity. This crowd was Kmart all the\nway. On impulse, Jake stood at the end of the line and followed them into the\nmuseum.\n\nThe tour took an hour and fifteen minutes. Jake enjoyed it. The museum was\nquiet. Even better, it was air-conditioned. And the pictures were nice. He was\nparticularly fascinated by a small group of Frederick Remington’s Old West\npaintings and a large picture by Thomas Hart Benton that showed a steam\nlocomotive charging across the great plains toward Chicago while beefy farmers\nin bib overalls and straw hats stood in their fields and watched. He wasn’t\nnoticed by either of the teachers with the group until the very end. Then a\npretty black woman in a severe blue suit tapped him on the shoulder and asked\nwho he was.\n\nJake hadn’t seen her coming, and for a moment his mind froze. Without thinking\nabout what he was doing, he reached into his pocket and closed his hand around\nthe silver key. His mind cleared immediately, and he felt calm again.\n“My group is upstairs,” he said, smiling guiltily. “We’re supposed to be looking\nat a bunch of modern art, but I like the stuff down here a lot better, because\nthey’re real pictures. So I sort of … you know . . .”\n“Snuck away?” the teacher suggested. The comers of her lips twitched in a\nsuppressed smile.\n\n“Well, I’d rather think of it as French leave.” These words simply popped out of\nhis mouth.\n\nThe students now staring at Jake only looked puzzled, but this time the teacher\nactually laughed. “Either yon don’t know or have forgotten,” she said, “but in\nthe French Foreign Legion they used to shoot deserters. I suggest you rejoin\nyour class at once, young man.”\n\n“Yes, ma’am. Thank you. They’ll be almost done now, anyway.”\n“What school is it?”\n\n“Markey Academy,” Jake said. This also just popped out.\nHe went upstairs, listening to the disembodied echo of foot-falls and low voices\nin the great space of the rotunda and wondering why he had said that. He had\nnever heard of a place called Markey Academy in his life.\n\nHE WAITED AWHILE IN the upstairs lobby, then noticed a guard looking at him with\ngrowing curiosity and decided it wouldn’t be wise to wait any longer—he would\njust have to hope the class he had joined briefly was gone.\nHe looked at his wristwatch, put an expression on his face that he hoped looked\nlike Gosh! Look how late it’s getting!, and trotted back downstairs. The\nclass—and the pretty black teacher who had laughed at the idea of French\nleave—was gone, and Jake decided it might be a good idea to get gone himself. He\nwould walk awhile longer—slowly, in deference to the heat—and catch a subway.\nHe stopped at a hot-dog stand on the comer of Broadway and Forty-second, trading\nin a little of his meager cash supply for a sweet sausage and a Nehi. He sat on\nthe steps of a bank building to eat his lunch, and that turned out to be a bad\nmistake.\n\nA cop came walking toward him, twirling his nightstick in a complex series of\nmaneuvers. He seemed to be paying attention to nothing but this, but when he\ncame abreast of Jake he abruptly shoved his stick back into his loop and turned\nto him.\n\n“Say-hey, big guy,” he said. “No school today?”\nJake had been wolfing his sausage, but the last bite abruptly stuck in his\nthroat. This was a lousy piece of luck … if luck was all it was. They were in\nTimes Square, sleaze capital of America; there were push-ers, junkies, whores,\nand chicken-chasers everywhere . .. but this cop was ignoring them in favor of\nhim.\n\nJake swallowed with an effort, then said, “It’s finals week at my school. I only\nhad one test today. Then I could leave.” He paused, not liking the bright,\nsearching look in the cop’s eyes. “I had permission,” he concluded uneasily.\n“Uh-huh. Can I see some ID?”\n\nJuke’s heart sank. Had his mother and father already called the cops? He\nsupposed that, after yesterday’s adventure, that was pretty likely. Under\nordinary circumstances, the NYPD wouldn’t take much notice of another missing\nkid, especially one that had been gone only half a day, but his father was a big\ndeal at the Network, and he prided himself on the number of strings he could\npull. Jake doubted if this cop had his picture . . . but he might very well have\nhis name.\n\n“Well,” Jake said reluctantly, “I’ve got my student discount card from Mid-World\nLanes, but that’s about all.”\n\n“Mid-World Lanes? Never heard of it. Where’s that? Queens?”\n“Mid-Town, I mean,” Jake thought. God, this was going north instead of south . .\n. and fast. “You know? On Thirty-third?”\n“Uh-huh. That’ll do fine.” The cop held out his hand.\nA black man with dreadlocks spilling over the shoulders of his canary-yellow\nsuit glanced over. “Bussim, ossifer!” this apparition said cheerfully. “Bussiz\nlil whitebread ass! Do yo duty, now!”\n\n“Shut up and get in the wind, Eli,” the cop said without looking around.\nEli laughed, exposing several gold teeth, and moved along.\n“Why don’t you ask him for some ID?” Jake asked.\n“Because right now I’m asking you. Snap it up, son.”\nThe cop either had his name or had sensed something wrong about him—which wasn’t\nso surprising, maybe, since he was the only white in the area who wasn’t\nobviously trolling. Either way, it came to the same: sitting down here to eat\nhis lunch had been dumb. But his feet had hurt, and he’d been hungry,\ndammit—hungry.\n\nYou’re not going to stop me, Jake thought. / can’t let you stop me. There’s\nsomeone I’m supposed to meet this afternoon in Brooklyn . . . and I’m going to\nbe there.\n\nInstead of reaching for his wallet, he reached into his front pocket and brought\nout the key. He held it up to the policeman; the late-morning sunshine bounced\nlittle coins of reflected light onto the man’s cheeks and forehead. His eyes\nwidened.\n\n“Heyy!” he breathed. “What you got there, kid?”\n\nHe reached for it, and Jake pulled the key back a little. The reflected circles\nof light danced hypnotically on the cop’s face. “You don’t need to take it,”\nJake said. “You can read my name without doing that, can’t you?”\n“Yes, sure.”\n\nThe curiosity had left the cop’s face. He looked only at the key. His gaze was\nwide and fixed, but not quite empty. Jake read both amazement and unexpected\nhappiness in his look. That’s me, Jake thought, just spread-ing joy and goodwill\nwherever I go. The question is, what do I do now?\nA young woman (probably not u librarian, judging from the green silk hotpants\nand see-through blouse she was wearing) came wiggle-wob-bling up the sidewalk on\na pair of purple fuck-me shoes with three-inch heels. She glanced first at the\ncop, then at Jake to see what the cop was looking at. When she got a good look,\nshe stopped cold. One of her hands drifted up and touched her throat. A man\nbumped into her and told her to watch where the damn-hell she was going. The\nyoung woman who was probably not a librarian took no notice whatever. Now Jake\nsaw that four or five other people had stopped as well. All were staring at the\nkey. They were gathering as people sometimes will around a very good\nthree-card-monte dealer plying his trade on a streetcorner.\nYou’re doing a great job of being inconspicuous, he thought. Oh yeah. He glanced\nover the cop’s shoulder, and his eye caught a sign on the far side of the\nstreet. Denby’s Discount Drug, it said.\n\n“My name’s Tom Denby,” he told the cop. “It says so right here on my discount\nbowling card—right?”\n\n“Right, right,” the cop breathed. He had lost all interest in Jake; he was only\ninterested in the key. The little coins of reflected light bounced and spun on\nhis face.\n\n“And you’re not looking for anybody named Tom Denby, are you?”\n“No,” the cop said. “Never heard of him.”\nNow there were at least half a dozen people gathered around the cop, all of them\nstaring with silent wonder at the silver key in Jake’s hand.\n“So I can go, can’t I?”\n\n“Huh? Oh! Oh, sure—go, for your father’s sake!”\n“Thanks,” Jake said, but for a moment he wasn’t sure how to go. He was hemmed in\nby a silent crowd of zombies, and more were joining it all the time. They were\nonly coming to see what the deal was, he realized, but the ones who saw the key\njust stopped dead and stared.\n\nHe got to his feet and backed slowly up the wide bank steps, holding the key out\nin front of him like a lion-tamer with a chair. When he got to the wide concrete\nplaza at the top, he stuffed it back into his pants pockets, turned, and fled.\nHe stopped just once on the far side of the plaza, and looked back. The small\ngroup of people around the place where he had been standing was coming slowly\nback to life. They looked around at each other with dazed expressions, then\nwalked on. The cop glanced vacantly to his left, to his right, and then straight\nup at the sky, as if trying to remember how he had gotten here and what he had\nbeen meaning to do. Jake had seen enough. It was time to find a subway station\nand get his ass over to Brooklyn before anything else weird could happen.\n\nAT QUARTER OF TWO that afternoon he walked slowly up the steps of the subway\nstation and stood on the corner of Castle and Brooklyn Ave-nues, looking at the\nsandstone towers of Co-Op City. He waited for that feeling of sureness and\ndirection—that feeling that was like being able to remember forward in time—to\novertake him. It didn’t come. Nothing came. He was just a kid standing on a hot\nBrooklyn streetcorner with his short shadow lying at his feet like a tired pet.\nWell, I’m here . . , now what do I do?\n\nJake discovered he didn’t have the slightest idea.\n\nROLAND’S SMALL BAND OF travellers reached the crest of the long, gentle hill\nthey had been climbing and stood looking southeast. For a long time none of them\nspoke. Susannah opened her mouth twice, then closed it again. For the first time\nin her life as a woman, she was completely speechless.\nBefore them, an almost endless plain dozed in the long golden light of a\nsummer’s afternoon. The grass was lush, emerald green, and very high. Groves of\ntrees with long, slender trunks and wide, spreading tops dotted the plain.\nSusannah had once seen similar trees, she thought, in a travelogue film about\nAustralia.\n\nThe road they had been following swooped down the far side of the hill and then\nran straight as a string into the southeast, a bright white lane cutting through\nthe grass. To the west, some miles off, she could see a herd of large animals\ngrazing peacefully. They looked like buffalo. To the east, the last of the\nforest made a curved peninsula into the grassland. This incursion was a dark,\ntangled shape that looked like a forearm with a cocked fist at the end.\nThat was the direction, she realized, in which all the creeks and streams they\nhad encountered had been flowing. They were tributaries of the vast river that\nemerged from that jutting arm of forest and flowed, placid and dreaming under\nthe summer sun, toward the eastern edge of the world. It was wide, that\nriver—perhaps two miles from bank to bank.\nAnd she could see the city.\n\nIt lay dead ahead, a misty collection of spires and towers rising above the far\nedge of the horizon. Those airy ramparts might have been a hundred miles away,\nor two hundred, or four hundred. The air of this world seemed to be totally\nclear, and that made judging distances a fool’s game. All she knew for sure was\nthat the sight of those dim towers filled her with silent wonder . . . and a\ndrop, aching homesickness for New York. She thought, I believe I’d do most\nanything just to see the Manhat-tan skyline from the Triborough Bridge again.\nThen she had to smile, because that wasn’t the truth. The truth was that she\nwouldn’t trade Roland’s world for anything. Its silent mystery and empty spaces\nwere intoxicating. And her lover was here. In New York—the New York of her own\ntime, at least—they would have been objects of scorn and anger, the butt of\nevery idiot’s crude, cruel jokes: a black woman of twenty-six and her whitebread\nlover who was three years younger and who had a tendency to talk like dis and\ndat when he got excited. Her whitebread lover who had been carrying a heavy\nmonkey on his back only eight months before. Here, there was no one to jeer or\nlaugh. Here, no one was pointing a finger. Here, there were only Roland, Eddie,\nand herself, the world’s last three gunslingers.\nShe took Eddie’s hand and felt it close over hers, warm and reassuring.\nRoland pointed. “That must be the Send River,” he said in a low voice. “I never\nthought to see it in my life . . . wasn’t even sure it was real, like the\nGuardians.”\n\n“It’s so lovely,” Susannah murmured. She was unable to take her eyes from the\nvast landscape before her, dreaming richly in the cradle of summer. She found\nher eyes tracing the shadows of the trees, which trailed across the plain for\nwhat seemed miles as the sun sank toward the horizon. “It’s the way our Great\nPlains must have looked before they were settled—even before the Indians came.”\nShe raised her free hand and pointed toward the place where the Great Road\nnarrowed to a point. “There’s your city,” she said. “Isn’t it?”\n“Yes.”\n\n“It looks okay,” Eddie said. “Is that possible, Roland? Could it still be pretty\nmuch intact. Did the old-timers build that well?”\n“Anything is possible in these times,” Roland said, but he sounded doubtful.\n“You shouldn’t get your hopes up, though, Eddie.”\n“Huh? No.” But Eddie’s hopes were up. That dimly sketched skyline had awakened\nhomesickness in Susannah’s heart; in Eddie’s it kindled a sudden blaze of\nsupposition. If the city was still there—and it clearly was—it might still be\n\npopulated, and maybe not just by the subhuman things Roland had met under the\n\nmountains, either. The city-dwellers might be\n(Americans, Eddie’s subconscious whispered)\nintelligent and helpful; they might, in fact, spell the difference between\nsuccess and failure for the quest of the pilgrims … or even between life and\ndeath. In Eddie’s mind a vision (partly cribbed from movies like The Last\nStarfighter and The Dark Crystal) gleamed brightly: a council of gnarled but\ndignified City Elders who would serve them a whopping meal drawn from the\nunspoiled stores of the city (or perhaps from special gardens cradled within\nenvironmental bubbles) and who would, as he and Roland and Susannah ate\nthemselves silly, explain exactly what lay ahead and what it all meant. Their\nparting gift to the wayfarers would be an AAA-approved Tour Guide map with the\nbest route to the Dark Tower marked in red.\nEddie did not know the phrase deus ex machina, but he knew—had now grown up\nenough to know—that such wise and kindly folk lived mostly in comic books and\nB-movies. The idea was intoxicating, all the same: an enclave of civilization in\nthis dangerous, mostly empty world; wise old elf-men who would tell them just\nwhat the fuck it was they were supposed to be doing. And the fabulous shapes of\nthe city disclosed in that hazy skyline made the idea seem at least possible.\nEven if the city was totally deserted, the population wiped out by some long-ago\nplague or outbreak of chemical warfare, it might still serve them as a kind of\ngiant toolbox—a huge Army-Navy Surplus Store where they could outfit themselves\nfor the hard passages Eddie was sure must lie ahead. Besides, he was a city boy,\nborn and bred, and the sight of all those tall towers just naturally got him up.\n“All right!” he said, almost laughing out loud in his excitement. “Hey-ho, let’s\ngo! Bring on those wise fuckin elves!”\n\nSusannah looked at him, puzzled but smiling. “What you ravin about, white boy?”\n“Nothing. Never mind. I just want to get moving. What do you say, Roland? Want\nto—“\n\nBut something on Roland’s face or just beneath it—some lost, dreaming\nthing—caused him to fall silent and put one arm around Susan-nah’s shoulders, as\nif to protect her.\n\nAFTER ONE BRIEF, DISMISSIVE glance at the city skyline, Roland’s gaze had been\ncaught by something a good deal closer to their current posi-tion, something\nthat filled him with disquiet and foreboding. He had seen such things before,\nand the last time he’d come across one, Jake had been with him. He remembered\nhow they had finally come out of the desert, the trail of the man in black\nleading them through the foothills and toward the mountains. Hard going, it had\nbeen, but at least there had been water again. And grass.\nOne night he had awakened to find Jake gone. He had heard stran-gled, desperate\ncries coming from a willow-grove hard by a narrow trickle of stream. By the time\nhe had fought his way through to the clearing at the center of the grove, the\nboy’s cries had ceased. Roland had found him standing in a place exactly like\nthe one which lay below and ahead. A place of stones; a place of sacrifice; a\nplace where an Oracle lived . . . and spoke when it was forced to … and killed\nwhenever it could.\n\n“Roland?” Eddie asked. “What is it? What’s wrong?”\n“Do you see that?” Roland pointed. “It’s a speaking ring. The shapes you see are\ntall standing stones.” He found himself staring at Eddie, whom he had first met\nin the frightening but wonderful air-carriage of that strange other world where\nthe gunslingers wore blue uniforms and there was an endless supply of sugar,\npaper, and wonderful drugs like astin. Some strange expression—some\nforeknowledge—was dawning on Eddie’s face. The bright hope which had lit his\neyes as he surveyed the city whiffed out, leaving him with a look both gray and\nbleak. It was the expression of a man studying the gallows on which he will soon\nbe hanged.\n\nFirst Jake, and now Eddie, the gunslinger thought. The wheel which turns our\n\nlives is remorseless; always it comes around to the same place again.\n“Oh shit,” Eddie said. His voice was dry and scared. “I think that’s the place\nwhere the kid is going to try and come through.”\nThe gunslinger nodded. “Very likely. They’re thin places, and they’re also\nattractive places. I followed him to such a place once before. The Oracle that\nkept there came very close to killing him.”\n“How do you know this?” Susannah asked Eddie. “Was it a dream?”\nHe only shook his head. “I don’t know. But the minute Roland pointed that\ngoddamn place out . . .” He broke off and looked at the gunslinger. “We have to\nget there, just as fast as we can.” Eddie sounded both frantic and fearful.\n“Is it going to happen today?” Roland asked. “Tonight?”\nEddie shook his head again, and licked his lips. “I don’t know that, either. Not\nfor sure. Tonight? I don’t think so. Time … it isn’t the same over here as it\nis where the kid is. It goes slower in his where and when. Maybe tomorrow.” He\nhad been battling panic, but now it broke free. He turned and grabbed Roland’s\nshirt with his cold, sweating fingers. “But I’m supposed to finish the key, and\nI haven’t, and I’m supposed to do something else, and I don’t have a clue about\nwhat it is. And if the kid dies, it’ll be my fault!”\nThe gunslinger locked his own hands over Eddie’s and pulled them away from his\nshirt. “Get control of yourself.”\n\n“Roland, don’t you understand—“\n\n“I understand that whining and puling won’t solve your problem. I understand\nthat you have forgotten the face of your father.”\n“Quit that bullshit! I don’t care dick about my father!” Eddie shouted\nhysterically, and Roland hit him across the face. His hand made a sound like a\nbreaking branch.\n\nEddie’s head rocked back; his eyes widened with shock. He stared at the\ngunslinger, then slowly raised his hand to touch the reddening handprint on his\ncheek. “You bastard!” he whispered. His hand dropped to the butt of the revolver\nhe still wore on his left hip. Susannah tried to put her own hands over it;\nEddie pushed them away.\n\nAnd now I must teach again, Roland thought, only this time I teach for my own\nlife, I think, as well as for his.\n\nSomewhere in the distance a crow hailed its harsh cry into the stillness, and\nRoland thought for a moment of his hawk, David. He was\nstanding on the far corner of Rhinehold Street and Brooklyn Avenue, seventy\nyards from Henry and Eddie Dean, but even from where he was he could feel The\nMansion ignoring them and reaching for him with its eager invisible hands, lie\nthought there were talons at the ends of those hands. Sharp ones.\nIt wants me, and I can’t run away. It’s death to go in … but it’s madness not\nto. Because somewhere inside that place is a locked door. She had had legs in\nthose days, and, as the song says, she knew how to use them.
