The hand clenched brutally, but Jake’s jeans slid down to his\nknees and his back slammed to the floor, with the pack to cushion the blow. The\nhand loosened, perhaps wanting to secure a firmer grip upon its prey. Jake was\nable to draw his knees up a little, and when the hand tightened again, Jake\ndrove his legs forward. The hand yanked backward at the same time, and what Jake\nhad hoped for happened: his jeans (and his remaining sneaker) were peeled from\nhis body, leaving him free again, at least for the moment. He saw the hand\nrotate on his wrist of boards and disintegrating plaster and jam his dungarees\ninto his mouth. Then he was crawling back toward the blocked doorway on his\nhands and knees, oblivious of the glass fragments from the fallen lamp, wanting\nonly to get his key again.\n\nHe had almost reached the door when the hand closed over his naked legs and\nbegan to pull him back once more.\n\nTHE SHAPE WAS THERE now, finally all there.\nEddie put the key back into the keyhole and applied pressure. For a moment there\nwas resistance . . . and then it revolved beneath his hand. He heard the locking\nmechanism turn, heard the bar pull back, felt the key crack in two the moment it\nhad served its purpose. He grasped the dark, polished knob with both hands and\npulled. There was a sense of great weight wheeling on an unseen pivot. A feeling\nthat his arm had been gifted with boundless strength. And a clear knowledge that\ntwo worlds had suddenly come in contact, and a way had been opened between them.\nHe felt a moment of dizziness and disorientation, and as he looked through the\ndoorway he realized why: although he was looking down—vertically—he was seeing\nhorizontally. It was like a strange optical illusion created with prisms and\nmirrors. Then he saw Jake being pulled backward down the glass- and\nplaster-littered hallway, elbows dragging, calves pinned-together by a giant\nhand. And he saw the monstrous mouth which awaited him, fuming some white fog\nthat might have been either smoke or dust.\n“Roland!” Eddie shouted. “Roland, it’s got h—”\nThen he was knocked aside.\n\nSUSANNAH WAS AWARE OF being hauled up and whirled around. The world was a\ncarousel blur: standing stones, gray sky, hailstone-littered ground… and a\nrectangular hole that looked like a trapdoor in the ground. Screams drifted up\nfrom it. Within her, the demon raved and struggled, wanting only to escape but\nhelpless to do so until she allowed it.\n\n“Now!” Roland was shouting. “Let it go now, Susannah! For your father’s sake,\nlet it go NOW!”\n\nAnd she did.\n\nShe had (with Detta’s help) constructed* a trap for it in her mind, something\n\nlike a net of woven rushes, and now she cut them. She felt the demon fly back\n\nfrom her at once, and there was an instant of terrible hollowness, terrible\nemptiness. These feelings were at once overshadowed by relief and a grim sense\nof nastiness and defilement.\n\nAs its invisible weight fell away, she glimpsed it—an inhuman shape like a\nmanta-ray with huge, curling wings and something that looked like a cruel baling\nhook curving out and up from beneath. She saw/sensed the thing flash above the\nopen hole in the ground. Saw Eddie looking up with wide eyes. Saw Roland spread\nhis arms wide to catch the demon.\n\nThe gunslinger staggered back, almost knocked off his feet by the unseen weight\nof the demon. Then he rocked forward again with an armload of nothing.\nClutching it, he jumped through the doorway and was gone.\n\nSUDDEN WHITE LIGHT FLOODED the hallway of The Mansion; hailstones struck the\nwalls and bounced up from the broken boards of the floor. Jake heard confused\nshouts, then saw the gunslinger come through. He seemed to leap through, as if\nhe had come from above. His arms were held far out in front of him, the tips of\nthe fingers locked.\n\nJake felt his feet slide into the doorkeeper’s mouth.\n“Roland!” he shrieked. “Roland, help me!”\nThe gunslinger’s hands parted and his arms were immediately thrown wide. He\nstaggered backward. Jake felt serrated teeth touch his skin, ready to tear flesh\nand grind bone, and then something huge rushed over his head like a gust of\nwind. A moment later the teeth were gone. The hand which had pinned his legs\ntogether relaxed. He heard an unearthly shriek of pain and surprise begin to\nissue from the doorkeeper’s dusty throat, and then it was muffled, crammed back.\nRoland grabbed Jake and hauled him to his feet.\n“You came!” Jake shouted. “You really came!”\n“I came, yes. By the grace of the gods and the courage of my friends, I came.”\nAs the doorkeeper roared again, Jake burst into tears of relief and terror. Now\nthe house sounded like a ship foundering in a heavy sea. Chunks of wood and\nplaster fell all around them. Roland swept Jake into his arms and ran for the\ndoor. The plaster hand, groping wildly, struck one of his booted feet and spun\nhim into the wall, which again tried to bite. Roland pushed forward, turned, and\ndrew his gun. He fired twice into the aimlessly thrashing hand, vaporizing one\nof the crude plaster fingers. Behind them, the face of the doorkeeper had gone\nfrom white to a dingy purplish-black, as if it were choking on\nsomething—something which had been fleeing so rapidly that it had entered the\nmonster’s mouth and jammed in its gullet before it realized what it was doing.\nRoland turned again and ran through the doorway. Although there was now no\nvisible barrier, he was stopped cold for a moment, as if an unseen meshwork had\nbeen drawn across the chair.\n\nThen he felt Eddie’s hands in his hair and he was yanked not for-ward but\nupward.\n\nTHEY EMERGED INTO WET air and slackening hail like babies being born. Eddie was\nthe midwife, as die gunslinger had told him he must be. He was sprawled forward\non his chest and belly, his arms out of sight in the doorway, his hands\nclutching fistfuls of Roland’s hair.\n\n“Suze! Help me!”\n\nShe wriggled forward, reached through, and groped a hand under Roland’s chin. He\ncame up to her with his head cocked backward and his lips parted in a snarl of\npain and effort.\n\nEddie felt a tearing sensation and one of his hands came free holding a thick\nlock of the gunslinger’s gray-streaked hair. “He’s slipping!”\n\n“This motherfucker . . . ain’t . . . nowhere!” Susannah gasped, and gave a\n\nterrific wrench, as if she meant to snap Roland’s neck.\nTwo small hands shot out of the doorway in the center of the circle and clutched\none of the edges. Freed of Jake’s weight, Roland got an elbow up, and a moment\nlater he was boosting himself out. As he did it, Eddie grabbed Jake’s wrists and\nhauled him up.\n\nJake rolled onto his back and lay there, panting.\nEddie turned to Susannah, took her in his arms, and began to rain kisses on her\nforehead, cheeks, and neck. He was laughing and crying at the same time. She\nclung to him, breathing hard . . . but there was a small, satisfied smile on her\nlips and one hand slipped over Eddie’s wet hair in slow, contented strokes.\nFrom below them came a cauldron of black sounds: squeals, grunts, thuds,\ncrashes.\n\nRoland crawled away from the hole with his head down. His hair stood up in a\nwild wad. Threads of blood trickled down his cheeks. “Shut it!” he gasped at\nEddie. “Shut it, for your father’s sake!”\nEddie got the door moving, and those vast, unseen hinges did the rest. The door\nfell with a gigantic, toneless bang, cutting off all sound from below. As Eddie\nwatched, the lines that had marked its edges faded back to smudged marks in the\ndirt. The doorknob lost its dimension and was once more only a circle he’d drawn\nwith a stick. Where the keyhole had been there was only a crude shape with a\nchunk of wood sticking out of it, like the hilt of a sword from a stone.\nSusannah went to Jake and pulled him gently to a sitting position. “You all\nright, sugar?”\n\nHe looked at her dazedly. “Yes, I think so. Where is he? The gunslinger? There’s\nsomething I have to ask him.”\n\n“I’m here, Jake,” Roland said. He got to his feet, drunk-walked over to Jake,\nand hunkered beside him. He touched the boy’s smooth cheek almost unbelievingly.\n“You won’t let me drop this time?”\n\n“No,” Roland said. “Not this time, not ever again.” But in the deep-est darkness\nof his heart, he thought of the Tower and wondered.\n\nTHE HAIL CHANGED TO a hard, driving rain, but Eddie could see gleams of blue sky\nbehind the unravelling clouds in the north. The storm was going to end soon, but\nin the meantime, they were going to get drenched.\nHe found he didn’t mind. He could not remember when he had felt so calm, so at\npeace with himself, so utterly drained. This mad adven-ture wasn’t over yet—he\nsuspected, in fact, that it had barely begun— but today they had won a big one.\n“Su/e?” He pushed her hair away from her face and looked into her dark eyes.\n“Are you okay? Did it hurt you?”\n\n“Hurt me a little, but I’m okay. I think that bitch Detta Walker is still the\nundefeated Roadhouse Champeen, demon or no demon.”\n“What’s that mean?”\n\nShe grinned impishly. “Not much, not anymore . . . thank God. How about you,\nEddie? All right?”\n\nEddie listened for Henry’s voice and didn’t hear it. He had an idea that Henry’s\nvoice might be gone for good. “Even better than that,” he said, and, laughing,\nfolded her into his arms again. Over her shoulder he could see what was left of\nthe door: only a few faint lines and angles. Soon the rain would wash those\naway, too.\n\n“WHAT’s YOUR NAME?” JAKE asked the woman whose legs stopped just above the knee.\nHe was suddenly aware that he had lost his pants in his struggle to escape the\ndoorkeeper, and he pulled the tail of his shirt down over his underwear. He was a dentist who went and invented a few things and got rich.\nWhat makes you ask a thing like that?”\n\nJake didn’t answer. He had turned his attention to Eddie. Are you okay?”\n“Yes,” Jake said. “Some scratches, that’s all.” He looked around. “You haven’t\nfound the train yet.” This was not a question.\nEddie and Susannah exchanged puzzled looks, but Roland only shook his head. “No\ntrain.”\n\n“Are your voices gone?”\n\nRoland nodded. “All gone. Yours?”\n\n“Gone. I’m all together again. We both are.”\nThey looked at the same instant, with the same impulse. As Roland swept Jake\ninto his arms, the boy’s unnatural self-possession broke and he began to cry—it\nwas the exhausted, relieved weeping of a child who has been lost long, suffered\nmuch, and is finally safe again. As Roland’s arms closed about his waist, Jake’s\nown arms slipped about the gunslinger’s neck and gripped like hoops of steel.\n“I’ll never leave you again,” Roland said, and now his own tears came. “I swear\nto you on the names of all my fathers: I’ll never leave you again.”\nYet his heart, that silent, watchful, lifelong prisoner of ka, received the\nwords of this promise not just with wonder but with doubt.\nBOOK TWO\n\nLUD\n\nA HEAP OF BROKEN IMAGES\n\nIV-TOWN AND KA-TET\n\nIV\n\nTOWN AND KA-TET\n\nFOUR DAYS AFTER EDDIE had yanked him through the doorway between worlds, minus\nhis original pair of pants and his sneakers but still in possession of his pack\nand his life, Jake awoke with something warm and wet nuzzling at his face.\nIf he had come around to such a sensation on any of the three previous mornings,\nhe undoubtedly would have wakened his companions with his screams, for he had\nbeen feverish and his sleep had been haunted by nightmares of the plaster-man.\nIn these dreams his pants did not slide free, the doorkeeper kept its grip, and\nit tucked him into its unspeakable mouth, where its teeth came down like the\nbars guarding a castle keep. Jake awoke from these dreams shuddering and moaning\nhelplessly.\n\nThe fever had been caused by the spider-bite on the back of his neck. When\nRoland examined it on the second day and found it worse instead of better, he\nhad conferred briefly with Eddie and had then given Jake a pink pill. “You’ll\nwant to take four of these every day for at least a week,” he said.\nJake had gazed at it doubtfully. “What is it?”\n“Cheflet,” Roland said, then looked disgustedly at Eddie. “You tell him. I still\ncan’t say it.”\n\n“Keflex. You can trust it, Jake; it came from a government-approved pharmacy in\ngood old New York. Roland swallowed a bunch of it, and he’s as healthy as a\nhorse. Looks a little like one, too, as you can see.”\nJake was astonished. “How did you get medicine in New York?”\n“That’s a long story,” the gunslinger said. “You’ll hear all of it in time, but\nfor now just take the pill.”\n\nJake did. The response was both quick and satisfying. The angry red swelling\naround the bite began to fade in twenty-four hours, and now the fever was gone\nas well.\n\nThe warm thing nuzzled again and Jake sat up with a jerk, his eyes flying open.\nThe creature which had been licking his cheek took two hasty steps backward. It\nwas a billy-bumbler, but Jake didn’t know that; he had never seen one before\nnow. It was skinnier than the ones Roland’s party had seen earlier, and its\nblack- and gray-striped fur was matted and mangy. There was a clot of old dried\nblood on one flank. Its gold-ringed black eyes looked at Jake anxiously; its\nhindquarters switched hopefully back and forth. Jake relaxed. He supposed there\nwere exceptions to the rule, but he had an idea that something wagging its\ntail—or trying to—was probably not too dangerous.\nIt was just past first light, probably around five-thirty in the morning. Jake\ncould peg it no closer than that because his digital Seiko no longer worked …\nor rather, was working in an extremely eccentric way. When he had first glanced\nat it after coming through, the Seiko claimed it was 98:71:65, a time which did\nnot, so far as Jake knew, exist. A longer look showed him that the watch was now\n\nrunning backward. If it had been doing this at a steady rate, he supposed it\n\nmight still have been of some use, but it wasn’t. It would unwind its numbers at\nwhat seemed like the right speed for awhile (Jake verified this by saying the\nword “Mississippi” between each number), and then the readout would either stop\nentirely for ten or twenty seconds—making him think the watch had finally given\nup the ghost—or a bunch of numbers would blur by all at once.\nHe had mentioned this odd behavior to Roland and had shown him the watch,\nthinking it would amaze him, but Roland examined it closely for only a moment or\ntwo before nodding in a dismissive way and telling Jake it was an interesting\nclock, but as a rule no timepiece did very good work these days. So the Seiko\nwas useless, but Jake still found himself loath to throw it away . . . because,\nhe supposed, it was a piece of his old life, and there were only a few of those\nleft.\n\nRight now the Seiko claimed it was sixty-two minutes past forty on a Wednesday,\nThursday, and Saturday in both December and March.\nThe morning was extremely foggy; beyond a radius of fifty or sixty feet, the\nworld simply disappeared. If this day was like the previous three, the sun would\nshow up as a faint white circle in another two hours or so, and by nine-thirty\nthe day would be clear and hot. Jake looked around and saw his travelling\ncompanions (he didn’t quite dare call them friends, at least not yet) asleep\nbeneath their hide blankets—Roland close by, Eddie and Susannah a larger hump on\nthe far side of the dead campfire.\n\nHe once more turned his attention to the animal which had awak-ened him. It\nlooked like a combination raccoon and woodchuck, with a dash of dachshund thrown\nin for good measure.\n\n“How you doin, boy?” he asked softly.\n\n“Oy!” the billy-bumbler replied at once, still looking at him anx-iously. Its\nvoice was low and deep, almost a bark; the voice of an English footballer with a\nbad cold in his throat.\n\nJake recoiled, surprised. The billy-bumbler, startled by the quick movement,\ntook several further steps backward, seemed about to flee, and then held its\nground. The whiskers on its\nsnout trembled.\n\n“This one remembers men,” a voice remarked at Jake’s shoulder. He did not want to startle it away; he was\nenchanted. “Its eyes are beautiful!”\n\n“Billy-bumbler,” Roland said.\n\n“Umber!” the creature ejaculated, and retreated another step.\n“It talks!”\n\n“Not really. Bumblers just repeat what they hear—or used to. I haven’t heard one\ndo it in years. This fellow looks almost starved. Proba-bly came to forage.”\n“He was licking my face. Can I feed it?”\n“We’ll never get rid of it if you do,” Roland said, then smiled a little and\nsnapped his fingers. “Hey! Billy!”\n\nThe creature mimicked the sound of the snapping fingers somehow; it sounded as\nif it were clucking its tongue against the roof of its mouth. “Ay!” it called in\nits hoarse voice. “Ay, Illy!” Now its ragged hindquarters were positively\ngagging back and forth.\n\n“Go ahead and give it a bite. I knew an old groom once who said a good bumbler\nis good luck. This looks like a good one.”\n“Yes,” Jake agreed. “It does.”\n\n“Once they were tame, and every barony had half a dozen roaming around the\ncastle or manor-house. They weren’t good for much except amusing the children\nand keeping the rat population down. They can be quite faithful—or were in the\nold days—although I never heard of one that would remain as loyal as a good dog.\n\nThe wild ones are scavengers. Not dangerous, but a pain in the ass.”\n\n“Ass!” cried the bumbler. We fed it, didn’t we?”\nBefore Jake could reply, the sound of the drums began again. Spears and clubs, as well.”\n“Don’t forget rocks,” Eddie said bleakly, looking down at the cluster of wooden\nbuildings. The place looked like a ghost-town, but who knew for sure? “And if\nthey’re hard up for rocks, there’s always the cobbles from the road.”\n“Yes, there’s always something,” Roland agreed. “But we’ll start no trouble\nourselves—is that clear?”\n\nThey nodded.\n\n“Maybe it would be easier to detour around.” Susannah said.\nRoland nodded, eyes never leaving the simple geography ahead. Another road\ncrossed the Great Road at the center of the town, making the dilapidated\nbuildings look like a target centered in the telescopic sight of a high-powered\nrifle. “It would, but we won’t. Detouring’s a bad habit that’s easy to get into.\nIt’s always better to go straight on, unless there’s a good visible reason not\nto. I see no reason not to here. And if there are people, well, that might be a\ngood thing. We could do with a little palaver.”\nSusannah reflected that Roland seemed different now, and she didn’t think it was\nsimply because the voices in his mind had ceased. This is the way he was when he\nstill had wars to fight and men to lead and his old friends around him, she\nthought. How he was before the world moved on and he moved on with it, chasing\nthat man Walter. This is how he was before the Big Empty turned him inward on\nhimself and made him strange.\n\n“They might know what those drum sounds are,” Jake suggested.\nRoland nodded again. “Anything they know—particularly about the city—would come\nin handy, but there’s no need to think ahead too much about people who may not\neven be there.”\n\n“Tell you what,” Susannah said, “I wouldn’t come out if I saw us. Four people,\nthree of them armed? We probably look like a gang of those old-time outlaws in\nyour stories, Roland—what do you call them?”\n“Harriers.” His left hand dropped to the sandalwood grip of his remaining\nrevolver and he pulled it a little way out of the holster. “But no harrier ever\nborn carried one of these, and if there are old-timers in yon village, they’ll\nknow it. Let’s go.”\n\nJake glanced behind them and saw the bumbler lying in the road with his muzzle\n\nbetween his short front paws, watching them closely. “Oy!” Jake called.\n“Oy!” the bumbler echoed, and scrambled to its feet at once.\nThey started down the shallow knoll toward the town with Oy trot-ting along\nbehind them.\n\nTwo BUILDINGS ON THE outskirts had been burned; the rest of the town appeared\ndusty but intact. They passed an abandoned livery stable on the left, a building\nthat might have been a market on the right, and then they were in the town\nproper—such as it was. There were perhaps a dozen rickety buildings standing on\neither side of the road. Alleys ran between some of them. The other road, this\none a dirt track mostly overgrown with plains grass, ran northeast to southwest.\nSusannah looked at its northeast arm and thought: Once there were barges on the\nriver, and somewhere down that road there was a landing, and probably another\nshacky little town, mostly saloons and cribs, built up around it. That was the\nlast point of trade before the barges went on down to the city. The wagons came\nthrough this place going to that place and then back again. How long ago was\nthat?\n\nShe didn’t know—but a long time, from the look of this place.\nSomewhere a rusty hinge squalled monotonously. Somewhere else one shutter\nclapped lonesomely to and fro in the plains wind.\nThere were hitching rails, most of them broken, in front of the buildings. Once\nthere had been board sidewalks, but now most of the boards were gone and grass\ngrew up through the holes where they had been. The signs on the buildings were\nfaded, but some were still read-able, written in a bastardized form of English\nwhich was, she supposed, what Roland called the low speech. FOOD AND GRAIN, one\nsaid, and she guessed that might mean feed and grain. On the false front next to\nit, below a crude drawing of a plains-buffalo lying in the grass, were the words\nREST EAT DRINK. Under the sign, batwing doors hung crookedly, moving a little in\nthe wind.\n\n“Is that a saloon?” She didn’t know exactly why she was whispering, only that\nshe couldn’t have spoken in a normal tone of voice. It would have been like\nplaying “Clinch Mountain Breakdown” on the banjo at a funeral.\n“It was,” Roland said. He didn’t whisper, but his voice was low-pitched and\nthoughtful. Jake was walking close by his side, looking around nervously. Behind\nthem, Oy had closed up his distance to ten yards. He trotted quickly, head\nswinging from side to side like a pendulum as he examined the buildings.\nNow Susannah began to feel it: that sensation of being watched. It was exactly\nas Roland had said it would be, a feeling sunshine had been replaced by shade.\n“There are people, aren’t there?” she whispered.\nRoland nodded.\n\nStanding on the northeast corner of the crossroads was a building with another\nsign she recognized: HOSTEL, it said, and COTTS. Except for a church with a\ntilted steeple up ahead, it was the tallest building in town—three stories. She\nglanced up in time to see a white blur, surely a face, draw away from one of the\nglassless windows. Suddenly she wanted to get out of here. Roland was setting a\nslow, deliberate pace, however, and she supposed she knew why. Hurrying might\ngive the watchers the impression that they were scared .. . and that they could\nbe taken. All the same—\n\nAt the crossroads the intersecting streets widened out, creating a town square\nwhich had been overrun by grass and weeds. In the center was an eroded stone\nmarker. Above it, a metal box hung on a sagging length of rusty cable.\nRoland, with Jake by his side, walked toward the marker. Eddie pushed Susannah’s\nchair after. Grass whispered in its spokes and the wind tickled a lock of hair\nagainst her cheek. Further along the street, the shutter banged and the hinge\nsquealed. She shivered and brushed the hair away.\n“I wish he’d hurry up,” Eddie said in a low voice. “This place gives me the\n\ncreeps.”\n\nSusannah nodded. She looked around the square and again she could almost see how\nit must have been on market-day—the sidewalks thronged with people, a few of\nthem town ladies with their baskets over their arms, most of them waggoners and\nroughly-dressed bargemen (she did not know why she was so sure of the barges and\nbargemen, but she was); the wagons passing through the town square, the ones on\nthe unpaved road raising choking clouds of yellow dust as the drivers flogged\ntheir carthorses\n\n(oxen they were oxen)\n\nalong. She could see those carts, dusty swatches of canvas tied down over bales\nof cloth on some and pyramids of tarred barrels on others; could see the oxen,\ndouble-yoked and straining patiently, flicking their ears at the flies buzzing\naround their huge heads; could hear voices, and laughter, and the piano in the\nsaloon pounding out a lively tune like “Buffalo Gals” or “Darlin’ Katy.”\nIt’s as if I lived here in another life, she thought.\nThe gunslinger bent over the inscription on the marker. “Great Road,” he read.\n“Lud, one hundred and sixty wheels.”\n\n“Wheels?” Jake asked.\n\n“An old form of measurement.”\n\n“Have you heard of Lud?” Eddie asked.\n\n“Perhaps,” the gunslinger said. “When I was very small.”\n“It rhymes with crud,” Eddie said. “Maybe not such a good sign.”\nJake was examining the east side of the stone. “River Road. It’s written funny,\nbut that’s what it says.”\n\nEddie looked at the west side of the marker. “It says Jimtown, forty wheels.\nIsn’t that the birthplace of Wayne Newton, Roland?”\nRoland looked at him blankly.\n\n“Shet ma mouf,” Eddie said, and rolled his eyes.\nOn the southwest corner of the square was the town’s only stone building—a\nsquat, dusty cube with rusty bars on the windows. Combina-tion county jail and\ncourthouse, Susannah thought. She had seen similar ones down south; add a few\nslant parking spaces in front and you wouldn’t be able to tell the difference.\nSomething had been daubed across the facade of the building in fading yellow\npaint. She could read it, and although she couldn’t understand it, it made her\nmore anxious than ever to get out of this town. PUBES DIE, it said.\n“Roland!” When she had his attention, she pointed at the graffito. “What does\nthat mean?”\n\nHe read it, then shook his head. “Don’t know.”\nShe looked around again. The square now seemed smaller, and the buildings seemed\nto be leaning over them. “Can we get out of here?”\n“Soon.” He bent down and pried a small chunk of cobble out of the roadbed. He\nbounced it thoughtfully in his left hand as he looked up at the metal box which\nhung over the marker. He cocked his arm and Susannah realized, an instant too\nlate, what he meant to do.\n\n“No, Roland!” she cried, then cringed back at the sound of her own horrified\nvoice.\n\nHe took no notice of her but fired the stone upward. His aim was as true as\never, and it struck the box dead center with a hollow, metallic bang. There was\na whir of clockwork from within, and a rusty green flag unfolded from a slot in\nthe side. When it locked in place, a bell rang briskly. Written in large black\nletters on the side of the flag was the word GO.\n“I’ll be damned,” Eddie said. “It’s a Keystone Kops traffic-light. If you hit it\nagain, does it say STOP?”\n\n“We have company,” Roland said quietly, and pointed toward the building Susannah\nthought of as the county courthouse. A man and a woman had emerged from it and\nwere descending the stone steps. You win the kewpie doll, Roland, Susannah\nthought. They’re older n God, the both of em.\nThe man was wearing bib overalls and a huge straw sombrero. The woman walked\nwith one hand clamped on his naked sunburned shoulder. She wore homespun and a\n\npoke bonnet, and as they drew closer to the marker, Susannah saw she was blind,\n\nand that the accident which had taken her sight must have been exceedingly\nhorrible. Where her eyes had been there were now only two shallow sockets filled\nwith scar-tissue. She looked both terrified and confused.\n“Be they harriers, Si?” she cried in a cracked, quavering voice. “You’ll have us\nkilled yet, I’ll warrant!”\n\n“Shut up, Mercy,” he replied. Like the woman, he spoke with a thick accent\nSusannah could barely understand. “They ain’t harriers, not these. There’s a\nPube with em, I told you that—ain’t no harrier ever been travellin’ with a\nPube.”\n\nBlind or not, she tried to pull away from him. He cursed and caught her arm.\n“Quit it, Mercy! Quit it, I say! You’ll fall down and do y’self evil, dammit!”\n“We mean you no harm,” the gunslinger called. He used the High Speech, and at\nthe sound of it the man’s eyes lit up with incredulity. The woman turned back,\nswinging her blind face in their direction.\n“A gunslinger!” the man cried. His voice cracked and wavered with excitement. ”\n‘Fore God! I knew it were! I knew!”\n\nHe began to run across the square toward them, pulling the woman after. She\nstumbled along helplessly, and Susannah waited for the inevita-ble moment when\nshe must fall. But the man fell first, going heavily to his knees, and she\nsprawled painfully beside him on the cobbles of the Great Road.\n\nJAKE FELT SOMETHING FURRY against his ankle and looked down. Oy was crouched\nbeside him, looking more anxious than ever. Jake reached down and cautiously\nstroked his head, as much to receive comfort as to give it. Its fur was silky,\nincredibly soft. For a moment he thought the bumbler was going to run, but it\nonly looked up at him, licked his hand, and then looked back at the two new\npeople. The man was trying to help the woman to her feet and not succeeding very\nwell. Her head craned this way and that in avid confusion.\nThe man named Si had cut his palms on the cobblestones, but he took no notice.\nHe gave up trying to help the woman, swept off his sombrero, and held it over\nhis chest. To Jake the hat looked as big as a bushel basket. “We bid ye welcome,\ngunslinger!” he cried. “Welcome indeed! I thought all your land had perished\nfrom the earth, so I did!”\n\n“I thank you for your welcome,” Roland said in the High Speech. He put his hands\ngently on the blind woman’s upper arms. She cringed for a moment, then relaxed\nand allowed him to help her up. “Put on your hat, old-timer. The sun is hot.”\nHe did, then just stood there, looking at Roland with shining eyes. After a\nmoment or two, Jake realized what that shine was. Si was crying.\n“A gunslinger! I told you, Mercy! I seen the shooting-iron and told you!”\n“No harriers?” she asked, as if unable to believe it. “Are you sure they ain’t\nharriers, Si?”\n\nRoland turned to Eddie. “Make sure of the safety and then give her Jake’s gun.”\nEddie pulled the Ruger from his waistband, checked the safety, and then put it\ngingerly in the blind woman’s hands. She gasped, almost dropped it, then ran her\nhands over it wonderingly. She turned the empty sockets where her eyes had been\nup to the man. “A gun!” she whispered. “My sainted hat!”\n“Ay, some kind,” the old man replied dismissively, taking it from her and giving\nit back to Eddie, “but the gunslinger’s got a real one, and there’s a woman got\nanother. She’s got a brown skin, too, like my da’ said the people of Garlan\nhad.”
12Part 11Darktower 3 - The Waste Lands\n\nOy gave his shrill, whistling bark. Jake turned and saw more people coming up\nthe street—five or six in all. Like Si and Mercy, they were all old, and one of\nthem, a woman hobbling over a cane like a witch in a fairy-tale, looked\npositively ancient. As they neared, Jake realized that two of the men were\nidentical twins. Long white hair spilled over the shoulders of their patched\nhomespun shirts. Their skin was as white as fine linen, and their eyes were\n\npink. Albinos, he thought.\n\nThe crone appeared to be their leader. She hobbled toward Roland’s party on her\ncane, staring at them with gimlet eyes as green as emeralds. Her toothless mouth\nwas tucked deeply into itself. The hem of the old shawl she wore fluttered in\nthe prairie breeze. Her eyes settled upon Roland.\n“Hail, gunslinger! Well met!” She spoke the High Speech herself, and, like Eddie\nand Susannah, Jake understood the words perfectly, although he guessed they\nwould have been gibberish to him in his own world. “Welcome to River Crossing!”\nThe gunslinger had removed his own hat, and now he bowed to her, tapping his\nthroat three times, rapidly, with his diminished right hand. “Thankee-sai, Old\nMother.”\n\nShe cackled freely at this and Eddie suddenly realized Roland had at the same\ntime made a joke and paid a compliment. The thought which had already occurred\nto Susannah now came to him: This is how he was . . . and this is what he did.\nPart of it, anyway.\n\n“Gunslinger ye may be, but below your clothes you’re but another foolish man,”\nshe said, lapsing into low speech.\n\nRoland bowed again. “Beauty has always made me foolish, mother.”\nThis time she positively cawed laughter. Oy shrank against Jake’s leg. One of\nthe albino twins rushed forward to catch the ancient as she rocked backward\nwithin her dusty cracked shoes. She caught her balance on her own, however, and\nmade an imperious shooing gesture with one hand. The albino retreated.\n“Be ye on a quest, gunslinger?” Her green eyes gleamed shrewdly at him; the\npuckered pocket of her mouth worked in and out.\n“Ay,” Roland said. “We go in search of the Dark Tower.”\nThe others only looked puzzled, but the old woman recoiled and forked the sign\nof the evil eye—not at them, Jake realized, but to the southeast, along the path\nof the Beam.\n\n“I’m sorry to hear it!” she cried. “For no one who ever went in search of that\nblack dog ever came back! So said my grandfather, and his grandfather before\nhim! Not ary one!”\n\n“Ka,” the gunslinger said patiently, as if this explained everything . . . and,\nJake was coming to realize, to Roland it did.\n“Ay,” she agreed, “black dog ka! Well-a-well; ye’ll do as ye’re called, and live\nalong your path, and die when it comes to the clearing in the trees. Will ye\nbreak bread with us before you push on, gunslinger? You and your band of\nknights?”\n\nRoland bowed again. “It has been long and long since we have broken bread in\ncompany other than our own, Old Mother. We cannot stay long, but yes—we’ll eat\nyour food with thanks and pleasure.”\n\nThe old woman turned to the others. She spoke in a cracked and ringing voice—yet\nit was the words she spoke and not the tone in which they were spoken that sent\nchills racing down Jake’s back: “Behold ye, the return of the White! After evil\nways and evil days, the White comes again! Be of good heart and hold up your\nheads, for ye have lived to see the wheel of ka begin to turn once more!”\n\nTHE OLD WOMAN, WHOSE name was Aunt Talitha, led them through the town square and\nto the church with the leaning spire—it was The Church of the Blood Everlasting,\naccording to the faded board on the run-to-riot lawn. Written over the words, in\ngreen paint that had faded to a ghost, was another message: DEATH TO GRAYS.\nShe led them through the ruined church, hobbling rapidly along the center aisle\npast the splintered and overturned pews, down a short flight of stairs, and into\na kitchen so different from the ruin above that Susan-nah blinked in surprise.\nHere everything was neat as a pin. The wooden floor was very old, but it had\nbeen faithfully oiled and glowed with its own serene inner light. The black\ncookstove took up one whole corner. It was immaculate, and the wood stacked in\nthe brick alcove next to it looked both well-chosen and well-seasoned.\n\nTheir party had been joined by three more senior citizens, two women and a man\n\nwho limped along on a crutch and a wooden leg. Two of the women went to the\ncupboards and began to make themselves busy; a third opened the belly of the\nstove and struck a long sulphur match to the wood already laid neatly within; a\nfourth opened another door and went down a short set of narrow steps into what\nlooked like a cold-pantry. Aunt Talitha, meanwhile, led the rest of them into a\nspacious entry at the rear of the church building. She waved her cane at two\ntrestle tables which had been stored there under a clean but ragged dropcoth,\nand the two elderly albinos immediately went over and began to wrestle with one\nof them.\n\n“Come on, Jake,” Eddie said. “Let’s lend a hand.”\n“Nawp!” Aunt Talitha said briskly. “We may be old, but we don’t need comp’ny to\nlend a hand! Not yet, youngster!”\n\n“Leave them be,” Roland said.\n\n“Old fools’ll rupture themselves,” Eddie muttered, but he followed the others,\nleaving the old men to their chosen table.\nSusannah gasped as Eddie lifted her from her chair and carried her through the\nback door. This wasn’t a lawn but a showplace, with beds of flowers blazing like\ntorches in the soft green grass. She saw some she recognized—marigolds and\nzinnias and phlox—but many others were strange to her. As she watched, a\nhorsefly landed on a bright blue petal . . . which at once folded over it and\nrolled up tight.\n\n“Wow!” Eddie said, staring around. “Busch Gardens!”\nSi said, “This is the one place we keep the way it was in the old days, before\nthe world moved on. And we keep it hidden from those who ride through—Pubes,\nGrays, harriers. They’d bum it if they knew . . . and kill us for keeping such a\nplace. They hate anything nice—all of em. It’s the one thing all those bastards\nhave in common.”\n\nThe blind woman tugged his arm to shush him.\n“No riders these days,” the old man with the wooden leg said. “Not for a long\ntime now. They keep closer in to the city. Guess they find all they need to keep\nem well right there.”\n\nThe albino twins struggled out with the table. One of the old women followed\nthem, urging them to hurry up and get the hell out of her way. She held a\nstoneware pitcher in each hand.\n\n“Sit ye down, gunslinger!” Aunt Talitha cried, sweeping her hand at the grass.\n“Sit ye down, all!”\n\nSusannah could smell a hundred conflicting perfumes. They made her feel dazed\nand unreal, as if this was a dream she was having. She could hardly believe this\nstrange little pocket of Eden, carefully hidden behind the crumbling facade of\nthe dead town.\n\nAnother woman came out with a tray of glasses. They were mismatched but\nspotless, twinkling in the sun like fine crystal. She held the tray out first to\nRoland, then to Aunt Talitha, Eddie, Susannah, and Jake at the last. As each\ntook a glass, the first woman poured a dark golden liquid into it.\nRoland leaned over to Jake, who was sitting tailor-fashion near an oval bed of\nbright green flowers with Oy at his side. He murmured: “Drink only enough to be\npolite, Jake, or we’ll be carrying you out of town—this is graf—strong\napple-beer.”\n\nJake nodded.\n\nTalitha held up her glass, and when Roland followed suit, Eddie, Susannah, and\nJake did the same.\n\n“What about the others?” Eddie whispered to Roland.\n“They’ll be served after the voluntary. Now be quiet.”\n“Will ye set us on with a word, gunslinger?” Aunt Talitha asked.\nThe gunslinger got to his feet, his glass upraised in his hand. He lowered his\nhead, as if in thought. The few remaining residents of River Crossing watched\nhim respectfully and, Jake thought, a little fearfully. At last he raised his\nhead again. “Will you drink to the earth, and to the days which have passed upon\n\nit?” he asked. His voice was hoarse,’ trembling with emotion. “Will you drink to\n\nthe fullness which was, and to friends who have passed on? Will you drink to\ngood company, well met? Will these things set us on, Old Mother?”\nShe was weeping, Jake saw, but her face broke into a smile of radiant happiness\nall the same . . . and for a moment she was almost young. Jake looked at her\nwith wonder and sudden, dawning happiness. For the first time since Eddie had\nhauled him through the door, he felt the shadow of the doorkeeper truly leave\nhis heart.\n\n“Ay, gunslinger!” she said. “Fair spoken! They’ll set us on by the league, so\nthey shall!” She tilted her glass up and drank it at a draught. When the glass\nwas empty, Roland emptied his own. Eddie and Susannah also drank, although less\ndeeply.\n\nJake tasted his own drink, and was surprised to find he liked it— the brew was\nnot bitter, as he had expected, but both sweet and tart, like cider. He could\nfeel the effects almost at once, however, and he put the glass carefully aside.\nOy sniffed at it, then drew back, and dropped his muzzle on Jake’s ankle.\nAround them, the little company of old people—the last residents of River\nCrossing—were applauding. Most, like Aunt Talitha, were weeping openly. And now\nother glasses—not so fine but wholly serviceable—were passed around. The party\nbegan, and a fine party it was on that long summer’s afternoon beneath the wide\nprairie sky.\n\nEDDIE THOUGHT THE MEAL, he ate that day was the best he had had since the mythic\nbirthday feasts of his childhood, when his mother had made it her business to\nserve everything he liked—meatloaf and roasted potatoes and corn on the cob and\ndevil’s food cake with vanilla ice cream on the side.\nThe sheer variety of the edibles put before them—especially after the months\nthey had spent eating nothing but lobster meat, deer meat, and the few bitter\ngreens which Roland pronounced safe—undoubtedly had something to do with the\npleasure he took in the food, but Eddie didn’t think that was the sole answer;\nhe noticed that the kid was packing it away by the plateful (and feeding a chunk\nof something to the bumbler crouched at his feet every couple of minutes), and\nJake hadn’t been here a week yet.\n\nThere were bowls of stew (chunks of buffalo meat floating in a rich brown gravy\nloaded with vegetables), platters of fresh biscuits, crocks of sweet white\nbutter, and bowls of leaves that looked like spinach but weren’t . . . exactly.\nEddie had never been crazy about greens, but at the first taste of these, some\ndeprived part of him awoke and cried for them. He ate well of everything, but\nhis need for the green stuff approached greed, and he saw Susannah was also\nhelping herself to them again and again. Among the four of them, the travellers\nemptied three bowls of the leaves.\n\nThe dinner dishes were swept away by the old women and the albino twins. They\nreturned with chunks of cake piled high on two thick white plates and a bowl of\nwhipped cream. The cake gave off a sweetly fragrant smell that made Eddie feel\nas if he had died and gone to heaven.\n\n“Only buffaler cream,” Aunt Talitha said dismissively. “No more cows—last one\ncroaked thirty year ago. Buffaler cream ain’t no prize-winner, but better’n\nnothin, by Daisy!”\n\nThe cake turned out to be loaded with blueberries. Eddie thought it beat by a\ncountry mile any cake he’d ever had. He finished three pieces, leaned back, and\nbelched ringingly before he could clap a hand over his mouth. He looked around\nguiltily.\n\nMercy, the blind woman, cackled. “I heard that! Someone be thankin’ the cook,\nAuntie!”\n\n“Ay,” Aunt Talitha said, laughing herself. “So he do.”\nThe two women who had served the food were returning yet again. One carried a\nsteaming jug; the other had a number of thick ceramic cups balanced precariously\n\non her tray.\n\nAunt Talitha was sitting at the head of the table with Roland by her right hand.\nNow he leaned over and murmured something in her ear. She listened, her smile\nfading a little, then nodded.\n\n“Si, Bill, and Till,” she said. “You three stay. We are going to have us a\nlittle palaver with this gunslinger and his friends, on account of they mean to\nmove along this very afternoon. The rest of you take your coffee in the kitchen\nand so cut down the babble. Mind you make your manners before you go!”\nBill and Till, the albino twins, remained sitting at the foot of the table.
