The boy accepted\nthis with good grace, but Eddie could see he was both surprised and embarrassed.\nWhen they reached Roland, they knelt before him and touched the sandalwood butt\nof the revolver which jutted from the holster he wore on his left hip. He put\nhis hands on their shoulders and kissed their old brows. Mercy was the last; she\nflung her arms around Roland’s waist and baptized his cheek with a wet, ringing\nkiss.\n\n“Gods bless and keep ye, gunslinger! If only I could see ye!”\n“Mind your manners, Mercy!” Aunt Talitha said sharply, but Roland ignored her\nand bent over the blind woman.\n\nHe took her hands gently but firmly in his own, and raised them to his face.\n“See me with these, beauty,” he said, and closed his eyes as her fingers,\nwrinkled and misshapen with arthritis, patted gently over his brow, his cheeks,\nhis lips and chin.\n\n“Ay, gunslinger!” she breathed, lifting the sightless sockets of her eyes to his\nfaded blue ones. “I see you very well! ‘Tis a good face, but full of sadness and\ncare. I fear for you and yours.”\n\n“Yet we are well met, are we not?” he asked, and planted a gentle kiss on the\nsmooth, worn skin of her forehead.\n\n“Ay—so we are. So we are. Thank’ee for your kiss, gunslinger. From my heart I\nthank’ee.”\n\n“Go on, Mercy,” Aunt Talitha said in a gentler voice. “Get your coffee.”\nMercy rose to her feet. The old man with the crutch and peg leg guided her hand\nto the waistband of his pants. She seized it and, with a final salute to Roland\nand his band, allowed him to lead her away.\nEddie wiped at his eyes, which were wet. “Who blinded her?” he asked hoarsely.\n“Harriers,” Aunt Talitha said. “Did it with a branding-iron, they did. Said it\nwas because she was looking at em pert. Twenty-five years agone, that was. Drink\nyour coffee, now, all of you! It’s nasty when it’s hot, but it ain’t nothin but\nroadmud once it’s cold.”\n\nEddie lifted the cup to his mouth and sipped experimentally. He wouldn’t have\ngone so fur us to call it roadmud, hut it wasn’t exactly Blue Mountain Blend,\neither.\n\nSusannah tasted hers and looked amazed. “Why, this is chicory!” Talitha glanced\nat her. “I know it not. Dockey is all I know, and dockey-coffee’s all we’ve had\nsince I had the woman’s curse—and that curse was lifted from me long, long ago.”\n“How old are you, ma’am?” Jake asked suddenly.\nAunt Talitha looked at him, surprised, then cackled. “In truth, lad, I\ndisremember. I recall sitting in this same place and having a party to celebrate\nmy eighty, but there were over fifty people settin out on this lawn that day,\nand Mercy still had her eyes.” Her own eyes dropped to the humbler lying at\nJake’s feet. Oy didn’t remove his muzzle from Jake’s ankle, but he raised his\ngold-ringed eyes to gaze at her. “A billy-bumbler, by Daisy! It’s been long and\nlong since I’ve seen a humbler in company with people . . . seems they have lost\nthe memory of the days when they walked with men.”\nOne of the albino twins bent down to pat Oy. Oy pulled away from him.\n“Once they used to herd sheep,” Bill (or perhaps it was Till) said to Jake. “Did\nye know that, youngster?”\n\nJake shook his head.\n\n“Do he talk?” the albino asked. “Some did, in the old days.”\n\n“Yes, he does.” He looked down at the humbler, who had returned his head to\nJake’s ankle as soon as the strange hand left his general area. “Say your name,\nOy.”\n\nOy only looked up at him.\n\n“Oy!” Jake urged, but Oy was silent. Jake looked at Aunt Talitha and the twins,\nmildly chagrined. “Well, he does . . . but I guess he only does it when he wants\nto.”\n\n“That boy doesn’t look as if he belongs here,” Aunt Talitha said to Roland. “His\nclothes are strange . . . and his eyes are strange, as well.”\n“He hasn’t been here long.” Roland smiled at Jake, and Jake smiled uncertainly\nback. “In a month or two, no one will be able to see his strangeness.”\n“Ay? I wonder, so I do. And where does he come from?”\n“Far from here,” the gunslinger said. “Very far.”\nShe nodded. “And when will he go back?” ^\n“Never,” Jake said. “This is my home now.”\n“Gods pity you, then,” she said, “for the sun is going down on the world. It’s\ngoing down forever.”\n\nAt that Susannah stirred uneasily; one hand went to her belly, as if her stomach\nwas upset.\n\n“Suze?” Eddie asked. “You all right?”\n\nShe tried to smile, but it was a weak effort; her normal confidence and\nself-possession seemed to have temporarily deserted her. “Yes, of course. A\ngoose walked over my grave, that’s all.”\nAunt Talitha gave her a long, assessing look that seemed to make Susannah\nuncomfortable . . . and then smiled. ” ‘A goose on my grave’— ha! I haven’t\nheard that one in donkey’s years.”\n\n“My dad used to say it all the time.” Susannah smiled at Eddie—a stronger smile\nthis time. “And anyway, whatever it was is gone now. I’m fine.”\n“What do you know about the city, and the lands between here and there?” Roland\nasked, picking up his coffee cup and sipping. “Are there harriers? And who are\nthese others? These Grays and Pubes?”\n\nAunt Talitha sighed deeply.\n\n“YE’D HEAR MUCH, GUNSLINGER, and we know but little. One thing I do know is\nthis: the city’s an evil place, especially for this youngster. Any youngster. Is\nthere any way you can steer around it as you go your course?”\nRoland looked up and observed the now familiar shape of the clouds as they\nflowed along the path of the Beam. In this wide plains sky, that shape, like a\nriver in the sky, was impossible to miss.\n“Perhaps,” he said at last, but his voice was oddly reluctant. “I suppose we\ncould skirt around Lud to the southwest and pick up the Beam on the far side.”\n“It’s the Beam ye follow,” she said. “Ay, I thought so.”\nEddie found his own consideration of the city colored by the steadily\nstrengthening hope that when and if they got there, they would find\nhelp—abandoned goodies which would aid them in their quest, or maybe even some\npeople who could tell them a little more about the Dark Tower and what they were\nsupposed to do when they got there. The ones called the Grays, for instance—they\nsounded like the sort of wise old elves he kept imagining.\nThe drums were creepy, true enough, reminding him of a hundred low-budget jungle\nepics (mostly watched on TV with Henry by his side and a bowl of popcorn between\nthem) where the fabulous lost cities the explorers had come looking for were in\nruins and the natives had degen-erated into tribes of blood-thirsty cannibals,\nbut Eddie found it impossible to believe something like that could have happened\nin a city that looked, at least from a distance, so much like New York. If there\nwere not wise old elves or abandoned goodies, there would surely be books, at\nleast; he had listened to Roland talk about how rare paper was here, hut every\n\ncity Eddie had ever been in was absolutely drowning in books. They might even\n\nfind some working transportation; the equivalent of a Land Rover would be nice.\nThat was probably just a silly dream, but when you had thousands of miles of\nunknown territory to cover, a few silly dreams were undoubtedly in order, if\nonly to keep your spirits up. And weren’t those things at least possible, damn\nit?\n\nHe opened his mouth to say some of these things, but Jake spoke before he could.\n“I don’t think we can go around,” he said, then blushed a little when they all\nturned to look at him. Oy shifted at his feet.\n“No?” Aunt Talitha said. “And why do ye think that, pray tell?”\n“Do you know about trains?” Jake asked.\n\nThere was a long silence. Bill and Till exchanged an uneasy glance. Aunt Talitha\nonly looked at Jake steadily. Jake did not drop his eyes.\n“I heard of one,” she said. “Mayhap even saw it. Over there.” She pointed in the\ndirection of the Send. “Long ago, when I was but a child and the world hadn’t\nmoved on … or at least not s’far’s it has now. Is it Blaine ye speak of, boy?”\nJake’s eyes flashed in surprise and recognition. “Yes! Blaine!” Roland was\nstudying Jake closely.\n\n“And how would ye know of Blaine the Mono?” Aunt Talitha asked.\n“Mono?” Jake looked blank.\n\n“Ay, so it was called. How would you know of that old lay?”\nJake looked helplessly at Roland, then back at Aunt Talitha. “I don’t know how I\nknow.”\n\nAnd that’s the truth, Eddie thought suddenly, but it’s not all the truth. He\nknows more than he wants to tell here . . . and I think he’s scared.\n“This is our business, I think,” Roland said in a dry, brisk administra-tor’s\nvoice. “You must let us work it out for ourselves, Old Mother.”\n“Ay,” she agreed quickly. “You’ll keep your own counsel. Best that such as us\nnot know.”\n\n“What of the city?” Roland prompted. “What do you know of Lud?”\n“Little now, but what we know, ye shall hear.” And she poured herself another\ncup of coffee.\n\nIT WAS THE TWINS, Bill and Till, who actually did most of the talking, one\ntaking up the tale smoothly whenever the other left off. Every now and then Aunt\nTalitha would add something or correct something, and the twins would wait\nrespectfully until they were sure she was done. Si didn’t speak at all—merely\nsat with his untouched coffee in front of him, plucking at the pieces of straw\nwhich bristled up from the wide brim of his sombrero.\nThey knew little, indeed, Roland realized quickly, even about the history of\ntheir own town (nor did this surprise him; in these latter days, memories faded\nrapidly and all but the most recent past seemed not to exist), but what they did\nknow was disturbing. Roland was not surprised by this, either.\nIn the days of their great-great-grandparents, River Crossing had been much the\ntown Susannah had imagined: a trade-stop at the Great Road, modestly prosperous,\na place where goods were sometimes sold but more often exchanged. It had been at\nleast nominally part of River Barony, although even then such things as Baronies\nand Estates o’ Land had been passing.\n\nThere had been buffalo-hunters in those days, although the trade had been dying\nout; the herds were small and badly mutated. The meat of these mutant beasts was\nnot poison, but it had been rank and bitter. Yet River Crossing, located between\na place they simply called The Land-ing and the village of Jimtown, had been a\nplace of some note. It was on the Great Road and only six days travel from the\ncity by land and three by barge. “Unless the river were low,” one of the twins\nsaid. “Then it took longer, and my gran’da said there was times when there was\nbarges grounded all the way upriver to Tom’s Neck.”\nThe old people knew nothing of the city’s original residents, of course, or the\n\ntechnologies they had used to build the towers and turrets; these were the Great\n\nOld Ones, and their history had been lost in the furthest reaches of the past\neven when Aunt Talitha’s great-great-grandfa-ther had been a boy.\n“The buildings are still standing,” Eddie said. “I wonder if the machines the\nGreat Golden Oldies used to build them still run.”\n“Mayhap,” one of the twins said. “If so, young fella, there don’t be ary man or\nwoman that lives there now who’d still know how to run em … or so I believe,\nso I do.”\n\n“Nay,” his brother said argumentatively, “I doubt the old ways are entirely lost\nto the Grays ‘n Pubes, even now.” He looked at Eddie. “Our da’ said there was\nonce electric candles in the city. There are those who say they mought still\nburn.”\n\n“Imagine that,” Eddie replied wonderingly, and Susannah pinched his leg, hard,\nunder the table.\n\n“Yes,” the other twin said. He spoke seriously, unaware of Eddie’s sarcasm. “You\npushed a button and they came on—bright, heatless can-dles with ary wicks or\nreservoirs for oil. And I’ve heard it said that once, in the old days, Quick,\nthe outlaw prince, actually Hew up into the sky in a mechanical bird. But one of\nits wings broke and he died in a great fall, like Icarus.”\nSusannah’s mouth dropped open. “You know the story of Icarus?”\n“Ay, lady,” he said, clearly surprised she should find this strange. “He of the\nbeeswax wings.”\n\n“Children’s stories, both of them,” Aunt Talitha said with a sniff. “I know the\nstory of the endless lights is true, for I saw them with my own eyes when I was\nbut a green girl, and they may still glow from time to time, ay; there are those\nI trust who say they’ve seen diem on clear nights, although it’s been long years\nsince I have myself. But no man ever flew, not even the Great Old Ones.”\nNonetheless, there were strange machines in the city, built to do peculiar and\nsometimes dangerous things. Many of them might still run, but the elderly twins\nreckoned that none now in the city knew how to start them up, for they hadn’t\nbeen heard in years.\n\nMaybe that could change, though, Eddie thought, his eyes gleaming. If, that is,\nan enterprising, travel-minded young man with a little knowl-edge of strange\nmachinery and endless lights came along. It could be just a matter of finding\nthe ON switches. I mean, it really could be that simple. Or maybe they just blew\na bunch of fuses—think of that, friends and neighbors! Just replace half a dozen\n400-amp Busses and light the whole place up like a Reno Saturday night!\nSusannah elbowed him and asked, in a low voice, what was so funny. Eddie shook\nhis head and put a finger to his lips, earning an irritated look from the love\nof his life. The albinos, meanwhile, were continuing their story, handing its\nthread back and forth with the unconscious ease which probably nothing but\nlifetime twinship can provide.\n\nFour or five generations ago, they said, the city had still been quite heavily\npopulated and reasonably civilized, although the residents drove wagons and\nbuckboards along the wide boulevards the Great Old Ones had constructed for\ntheir fabulous horseless vehicles. The city-dwellers were artisans and what the\ntwins called “manufactories,” and trade both on the river and over it had been\nbrisk.\n\n“Over it?” Roland asked.\n\n“The bridge over the Send still stands,” Aunt Talitha said, “or did twenty year\nago.”\n\n“Ay, old Bill Muffin and his boy saw it not ten year agone,” Si agreed, making\nhis first contribution to the conversation.\n“What sort of bridge?” the gunslinger asked.\n“A great thing of steel cables,” one of the twins said. “It stands in the sky\nlike the web of some great spider.” He added shyly: “I should like to see it\nagain before I die.”\n\n“Probably fallen in by now,” Aunt Talitha said dismissively, “and good riddance.\nDevil’s work.” She turned to the twins. “Tell them what’s happened since, and\n\nwhy the city’s so dangerous now—apart from any haunts that may den there, that\n\nis, and I’ll warrant there’s a power of em. These folks want to get on, and the\nsun’s on the wester.”\n\nTHE REST OF THE story was but another version of a tale Roland of Gilead had\nheard many times and had, in some measure, lived through himself. It was\nfragmentary and incomplete, undoubtedly shot through with myth and\nmisinformation, its linear progress distorted by the odd changes—both temporal\nand directional—which were now taking place in the world, and it could be summed\nup in a single compound sentence: Once there was a world we knew, but that world\nhas moved on.\n\nThese old people of River Crossing knew of Gilead no more than Roland knew of\nthe River Barony, and the name of John Parson, the man who had brought ruin and\nanarchy on Roland’s land, meant nothing to them, but all stories of the old\nworld’s passing were similar . . . too similar, Roland thought, to be\ncoincidence.\n\nA great civil war—perhaps in Garlan, perhaps in a more distant land called\nPorla—had erupted three, perhaps even four hundred years ago. Its ripples had\nspread slowly outward, pushing anarchy and dissension ahead of them. Few if any\nkingdoms had been able to stand against those slow waves, and anarchy had come\nto this part of the world as surely as night follows sunset. At one time, whole\narmies had been on the roads, sometimes in advance, sometimes in retreat, always\nconfused and without long-term goals. As time passed, they crumbled into smaller\ngroups, and these degenerated into roving bands of harriers. Trade faltered,\nthen broke down entirely. Travel went from a matter of inconvenience to one of\ndanger. In the end, it became almost impossible. Communication with the city\nthinned steadily and had all but ceased a hundred and twenty years ago.\nLike a hundred other towns Roland had ridden through—first with Cuthbert and the\nother gunslingers cast out of Gilead, then alone, in pursuit of the man in\nblack—River Crossing had been cut off and thrown on its own resources.\nAt this point Si roused himself, and his voice captured the travellers at once.\nHe spoke in the hoarse, cadenced tones of a lifelong teller of tales—one of\nthose divine fools born to merge memory and mendacity into dreams as airily\ngorgeous as cobwebs strung with drops of dew.\n“We last sent tribute to the Barony castle in the time of my greatgran’da,” he\nsaid. “Twenty-six men went with a wagon of hides—there was no hard coin anymore\nby then, o’ course, and ’twas the best they could do. It was a long and\ndangerous journey of almost eighty wheels, and six died on the way. Half fell to\nharriers bound for the war in the city; the other half died either of disease or\ndevilgrass.\n\n“When they finally arrived, they found the castle deserted but for the rooks and\nblack-birds. The walls had been broken; weeds o’ergrew the Court o’ State. There\nhad been a great slaughter on the fields to the west; it were white with bones\nand red with rusty armor, so my da’s gran’da said, and the voices of demons\ncried out like the east wind from the jawbones o’ those who’d fallen there. The\nvillage beyond the castle had been burned to the ground and a thousand or more\nskulls were posted along the walls of the keep. Our folk left their bounty o’\nhides without the shattered barbican gate—for none would venture inside that\nplace of ghosts and moaning voices—and began the homeward way again. Ten more\nfell on that journey, so that of the six-and-twenty who left only ten returned,\nmy great-gran’da one of them . . . but he picked up a ring-worm on his neck and\nbosom that never left until the day he died. It were the radiation sickness, or\nso they said. After that, gunslinger, none left the town. We were on our own.”\nThey grew used to the depredations of the harriers, Si continued in his cracked\nbut melodious voice. Watches were posted; when bands of riders were seen\napproaching—almost always moving southeast along the Great Road and the path of\nthe Beam, going to the war which raged endlessly in Lud—the townspeople hid in a\n\nlarge shelter they had dug beneath the church. Casual damages to the town were\n\nnot repaired, lest they make those roving bands curious. Most were beyond\ncuriosity; they only rode through at a gallop, bows or battle-axes slung over\ntheir shoul-ders, bound for the killing-zones.\n“What war is it that you speak of?” Roland asked.\n“Yes,” Eddie said, “and what about that drumming sound?”\nThe twins again exchanged a quick, almost superstitious glance.\n“We know not of the god-drums,” Si told them. “Ary word or watch. The war of the\ncity, now …”\n\nThe war had originally been the harriers and outlaws against a loose\nconfederation of artisans and “manufactories” who lived in the city. The\nresidents had decided to fight instead of allowing the harriers to loot them,\nburn their shops, and then turn the survivors out into the Big Empty, where they\nwould almost certainly die. And for some years they had successfully defended\nLud against the vicious but badly organized groups of raiders which tried to\nstorm across the bridge or invade by boat and barge.\n“The city-folk used the old weapons,” one of the twins said, “and though their\nnumbers were small, the harriers could not stand against such things with their\nbows and maces and battle-axes.”\n\n“Do you mean the city-people used guns?” Eddie asked.\nOne of the albinos nodded. “Ay, guns, but not just guns. There were things that\nhurled the firebangs over a mile or more. Explosions like dynamite, only more\npowerful. The outlaws—who are now the Grays, as you must ken—could do nothing\nbut lay siege beyond the river, and that was what they did.”\nLud became, in effect, the last fortress-refuge of the latter world. The\nbrightest and most able travelled there from the surrounding coun-tryside by\nones and twos. When it came to intelligence tests, sneaking through the tangled\nencampments and front lines of the besiegers was the newcomers’ final exam. Most\ncame unarmed across the no-man’s-land of the bridge, and those who made it that\nfar were let through. Some were found wanting and sent packing again, of course,\nbut those who had a trade or a skill (or brains enough to learn one) were\nallowed to stay. Farming skills were particularly prized; according to the\nstories, every large park in Lud had been turned into a vegetable garden. With\nthe countryside cut off, it was grow food in the city or starve amid the glass\ntowers and metal alleys. The Great Old Ones were gone, their machines were a\nmystery, and the silent wonders which remained were inedible.\nLittle by little, the character of the war began to change. The bal-ance of\npower had shifted to the besieging Grays—so called because they were, on\naverage, much older than the city-dwellers. Those latter were also growing\nolder, of course. They were still known as Pubes, but in most cases their\npuberty was long behind them. And they eventually either forgot how the old\nweapons worked or used them up.\n\n“Probably both,” Roland grunted.\n\nSome ninety years ago—within the lifetimes of Si and Aunt Talitha— a final band\nof outlaws had appeared, one so large that the outriders had gone galloping\nthrough River Crossing at dawn and the drogues did not pass until almost\nsundown. It was the last army these parts had ever seen, and it was led by a\nwarrior prince named David Quick—the same fellow who supposedly later fell to\nhis death from the sky. He had orga-nized the raggle-taggle remnants of the\noutlaw bands which still hung about the city, killing anyone who showed\nopposition to his plans. Quick’s army of Grays used neither boat nor bridge to\nattempt entry into the city, but instead built a pontoon bridge twelve miles\nbelow it and attacked on the flank.\n\n“Since then the war has guttered like a chimney-fire,” Aunt Talitha finished.\n“We hear reports every now and then from someone who has managed to leave, ay,\nso we do. These come a little more often now, for the bridge, they say, is\nundefended and I think the fire is almost out. Within the city, the Pubes and\nGrays squabble over the remaining spoils, only I reckon that the descendents of\nthe harriers who followed Quick over the pontoon bridge are the real Pubes now,\n\nalthough they are still called Grays. The descendents of the original\n\ncity-dwellers must now be almost as old as we are, although there are still some\nyounkers who go to be among them, drawn by the old stories and the lure of the\nknowl-edge which may still remain there.\n“These two sides still keep up their old enmity, gunslinger, and both would\ndesire this young man you call Eddie. If the dark-skinned woman is fertile, they\nwould not kill her even though her legs are short-ended; they would keep her to\nbear children, for children are fewer now, and although the old sicknesses are\npassing, some are still born strange.”\n\nAt this, Susannah stirred, seemed about to say something, then only drank the\nlast of her coffee and settled back into her former listening position.\n“But if they would desire the young man and woman, gunslinger, I think they\nwould lust for the boy.”\n\nJake bent and began to stroke Oy’s fur again. Roland saw his face and knew what\nhe was thinking: it was the passage under the mountains all over again, just\nanother version of the Slow Mutants.\n\n“You they’d just as soon kill,” Aunt Talitha said, “for you are a gunslinger, a\nman out of his own time and place, neither fish nor fowl, and no use to either\nside. But a boy can be taken, used, schooled to remember some things and to\nforget all the others. They’ve all forgotten whatever it was they had to fight\nabout in the first place; the world has moved on since then. Now they just fight\nto the sound of them awful drumbeats, some few still young, most of them old\nenough for the rocking chair, like us here, all of them stupid grots who only\nlive to kill and kill to live.” She paused. “Now that you’ve heard us old\ncullies to the end, are ye sure it would not be best to go around, and leave\nthem to their business?”\n\nBefore Roland could reply, Jake spoke up in a clear, firm voice. “Tell what you\nknow about Blaine the Mono,” he said. “Tell about Blaine and Engineer Bob.”\n\n“ENGINEER WHO?” EDDIE ASKED, but Jake only went on looking at the old people.\n“Track lies over yonder,” Si answered at last. He pointed toward the river. “One\ntrack only, set up high on a colyum of man-made stone, such as the Old Ones used\nto make their streets and walls.”\n\n“A monorail!” Susannah exclaimed. “Blaine the Monorail!”\n“Blaine is a pain,” Jake muttered.\n\nRoland glanced at him but said nothing.\n\n“Does this train run now?” Eddie asked Si.\nSi shook his head slowly. His face was troubled and uneasy. “No, young sir—but\nin my lifetime and Auntie’s, it did. When we were green and the war of the city\nstill went forrad briskly. We’d hear it before we saw it—a low humming noise, a\nsound like ye sometimes hear when a bad summer storm’s on the way—one that’s\nfull of lightning.”\n\n“Ay,” Aunt Talitha said. Her face was lost and dreaming.\n“Then it’d come—Blaine the Mono, twinkling in the sun, with a nose like one of\nthe bullets in your revolver, gunslinger. Maybe two wheels long. I know that\nsounds like it couldn’t be, and maybe it wasn’t (we were green, ye must\nremember, and that makes a difference), but I still think it was, for when it\ncame, it seemed to run along the whole horizon. Fast, low, and gone before you\ncould even see it proper!\n\n“Sometimes, on days when the weather were foul and the air low, it’d shriek like\na harpy as it came out of the west. Sometimes it’d come in the night with a long\nwhite light spread out before it, and that shriek would wake all of us. It were\nlike the trumpet they say will raise the dead from their graves at the end of\nthe world, so it was.”\n\n“Tell em about the bang, Si!” Bill or Till said in a voice which trembled with\nawe. “Tell em about the godless bang what always came after!”\n“Ay, I was just getting to that,” Si answered with a touch of annoy-ance. “After\n\nit passed by, there would be quiet for a few seconds . . . sometimes as long as\n\na minute, maybe . . . and then there’d come an explosion that rattled die boards\nand knocked cups off the shelves and sometimes even broke the glass in the\nwindow-panes. But never did anyone see ary flash nor fire. It was like an\nexplosion in the world of spirits.”\n\nEddie tapped Susannah on the shoulder, and when she turned to him he mouthed two\nwords: Sonic boom. It was nuts—no train he had ever heard of travelled faster\nthan the speed of sound—but it was also the only thing that made sense.\nShe nodded and turned back to Si.\n\n“It’s the only one of the machines the Great Old Ones made that I’ve ever seen\nrunning with my own eyes,” he said in a soft voice, “and if it weren’t the\ndevil’s work, there be no devil. The last time I saw it was the spring I married\nMercy, and that must have been sixty year agone.”\n“Seventy,” Aunt Talitha said with authority.\n“And this train went into the city,” Rolund said. “From back the way we came . .\n. from the west . . . from the forest.”\n\n“Ay,” a new voice said unexpectedly, “but there was another . . . one that went\nout from the city .. . and mayhap that one still runs.”\n\nTHEY TURNED. MERCY STOOD by a bed of flowers between the back of the church and\nthe table where they sat. She was walking slowly toward the sound of their\nvoices, with her hands spread out before her.\nSi got clumsily to his feet, hurried to her as best he could, and took her hand.\nShe slipped an arm about his waist and they stood there looking like the world’s\noldest wedding couple.\n\n“Auntie told you to take your coffee inside!” he said.\n“Finished my coffee long ago,” Mercy said. “It’s a bitter brew and I hate it.\nBesides—I wanted to hear the palaver.” She raised a trembling finger and pointed\nit in Roland’s direction. “I wanted to hear his voice. It’s fair and light, so\nit is.”\n\n“I cry your pardon, Auntie,” Si said, looking at the ancient woman a little\nfearfully. “She was never one to mind, and the years have made her no better.”\nAunt Talitha glanced at Roland. He nodded, almost imperceptibly. “Let her come\nforward and join us,” she said.\n\nSi led her over to the table, scolding all the while. Mercy only looked over his\nshoulder with her sightless eyes, her mouth set in an intractable line.\nWhen Si had gotten her seated, Aunt Talitha leaned forward on her forearms and\nsaid, “Now do you have something to say, old sister-sai, or were you just\nbeating your gums?”\n\n“I hear what I hear. My ears are as sharp as they ever were, Tali-tha—sharper!”\nRoland’s hand dropped to his belt for a moment. When he brought it back to the\ntable, he was holding a cartridge in his fingers. He tossed it to Susannah, who\ncaught it. “Do you, sai?” he asked.\n\n“Well enough,” she said, turning in his direction, “to know that you just threw\nsomething. To your woman, I think—the one with the brown skin. Something small.\nWhat was it, gunslinger? A biscuit?”\n\n“Close enough,” he said, smiling. “You hear as well as you say. Now tell us what\nyou meant.”\n\n“There is another mono,” she said, “unless ’tis the same one, running a\ndifferent course. Either way, a different course was run by some mono . . .\nuntil seven or eight year ago, anyways. I used to hear it leaving the city and\ngoing out into the waste lands beyond.”\n\n“Dungheap!” one of the albino twins ejaculated. “Nothing goes to the waste\nlands! Nothing can live there!”\n\nShe turned her face to him. “Is a train alive, Till Tudbury?” she asked. “Does a\n\nmachine fall sick with sores and puking?”\nWell, Eddie thought of saying, there was this bear . . .\nHe thought it over a little more and decided it might be better to keep his\nsilence.\n\n“We would have heard it,” the other twin was insisting hotly. “A noise like the\none Si always tells of—“\n\n“This one didn’t make no bang,” she admitted, “but I heard that other sound,\nthat humming noise like the one you hear sometimes after lightning has struck\nsomewhere close. When the wind was strong, blowing out from the city, I heard\nit.” She thrust out her chin and added: “I did hear the bang once, too. From\nfar, far out. The night Big Charlie Wind came and almost blew the steeple off\nthe church. Must have been two hundred wheels from here. Maybe two hundred and\nfifty.”\n\n“Bulldink!” the twin cried. “You been chewing the weed!”\n“I’ll chew on you, Bill Tudbury, if you don’t shut up your honkin. You’ve no\nbusiness sayin bulldink to a lady, either. Why—”\n“Stop it, Mercy!” Si hissed, but Eddie was barely listening to this exchange of\nrural pleasantries. What the blind woman had said made sense to him. Of course\nthere would be no sonic boom, not from a train which started its run in Lud; he\ncouldn’t remember exactly what the speed of sound was, but he thought it was\nsomewhere in the neighbor-hood of six hundred and fifty miles an hour. A train\nstarting from a dead stop would take some time getting up to that speed, and by\nthe time it reached it, it would be out of earshot . . . unless the listening\nconditions happened to be just right, as Mercy claimed they had been on the\nnight when the Big Charlie Wind—whatever that was—had come.\nAnd there were possibilities here. Blaine the Mono was no Land Rover, but maybe\n. . . maybe …\n\n“You haven’t heard the sound of this other train for seven or eight years, sai?”\nRoland asked. “Are you sure it wasn’t much longer?”\n“Couldn’t have been,” she said, “for the last time was the year old Bill Muffin\ntook blood-sick. Poor Bill!”\n\n“That’s almost ten year agone,” Aunt Talitha said, and her voice was queerly\ngentle.\n\n“Why did you never say you heard such a thing?” Si asked. He looked at the\ngunslinger. “You can’t believe everything she says, lord— always longing to be\nin the middle of the stage is my Mercy.”\n“Why, you old slumgullion!” she cried, and slapped his arm. “I didn’t say\nbecause I didn’t want to o’ertop the story you’re so proud of, but now that it\nmatters what I heard, I’m bound to tell!”\n“I believe you, sai,” Roland said, “but are you sure you haven’t heard the\nsounds of the mono since then?”\n\n“Nay, not since then. I imagine it’s finally reached the end of its path.”\n“I wonder,” Roland said. “Indeed, I wonder very much.” He looked down at the\ntable, brooding, suddenly far away from all of them,\nChoo-choo, Jake thought, and shivered.\n\nHALF AN HOUR LATER they were in the town square again, Susannah in her\nwheelchair, Jake adjusting the straps of his pack while Oy sat at his heel,\nwatching him attentively. Only the town elders had attended the dinner-party in\nthe little Eden behind the Church of the Blood Everlast-ing, it seemed, because\nwhen they returned to the square, another dozen people were waiting. They\nglanced at Susannah and looked a bit longer at Jake (his youth apparently more\ninteresting to them than her dark skin), but it was clearly Roland they had come\nto see; their wondering eyes were full of ancient awe.\nHe’s a living remnant of a past they only know from stories, Susan-nah thought.\nThey look at him the way religious people would look at one of the saints—Peter\n\nor Paul or Matthew—if he decided to drop by the Saturday night bean supper and\n\ntell them stories of how it was, traipsing around the Sea of Galilee with Jesus\nthe Carpenter.\n\nThe ritual which had ended the meal was now repeated, only this time everyone\nleft in River Crossing participated. They shuffled forward in a line, shaking\nhands with Eddie and Susannah, kissing Jake on the cheek or forehead, then\nkneeling in front of Roland for his touch and his blessing. Mercy threw her arms\nabout him and pressed her blind face against his stomach. Roland hugged her back\nand thanked her for her news.\n\n“Will ye not stay the night with us, gunslinger? Sunset comes on apace, and it’s\nbeen long since you and yours spent the night beneath a roof, I’ll warrant.”\n“It has been, but it’s best we go on. Thankee-sai.”\n“Will ye come again if ye may, gunslinger?”\n“Yes,” Roland said, but Eddie did not need to look into his strange friend’s\nface to know the chances were small. “If we can.”\n“Ay.” She Imaged him a final time, then passed on with her hand resting on Si’s\nsunburned shoulder. “Fare ye well.”\n\nAunt Talitha came last. When she began to kneel, Roland caught her by the\nshoulders. “No, sai. You shall not do.” And before Eddie’s amazed eyes, Roland\nknelt before her in the dust of the town square. “Will you bless me, Old Mother?\nWill you bless all of us as we go our course?”\n“Ay,” she said. There was no surprise in her voice, no tears in her eyes, but\nher voice throbbed with deep feeling, all the same. “I see your heart is true,\ngunslinger, and that you hold to the old ways of your kind; ay, you hold to them\nvery well. I bless you and yours and will pray that no harm will come to you.\nNow take this, if you will.” She reached into the bodice of her faded dress and\nremoved a silver cross at the end of a fine-link silver chain. She took it off\nNow it was Roland’s turn to be surprised. “Are you sure? I did not come to take\nwhat belongs to you and yours, Old Mother.”\n“I’m sure as sure can be. I’ve worn this day and night for over a hundred years,\ngunslinger. Now you shall wear it, and lay it at the foot of the Dark Tower, and\nspeak the name of Talitha Unwin at the far end of the earth.” She slipped the\nchain over his head. The cross dropped into the open neck of his deerskin shirt\nas if it belonged there. “Go now. We have broken bread, we have held palaver, we\nhave your blessing, and you have ours. Go your course in safety. Stand and be\ntrue.” Her voice trembled and broke on the last word.\nRoland rose to his feet, then bowed and tapped his throat three times.\n“Thankee-sai.”\n\nShe bowed back, but did not speak. He did not trust himself to speak.\n“All right,” Roland said. “Let’s go.”\n\nThey walked down what remained of the town’s high street, Jake pushing\nSusannah’s wheelchair. The old people were still gathered about the\nstone marker, a forlorn cluster of humanity in the middle of this wide, empty\nplain. Jake raised his hand. Up to this point he had managed to hold himself in,\nbut when several of the old folks—Si, Bill, and Till among them—raised their own\nhands in return, Jake burst into tears himself.\nEddie put an arm around his shoulders. “Just keep walking, sport,” he said in an\nuneasy voice. “That’s the only way to do it.”\n“They’re so old!” Jake sobbed. “How can we just leave them like this? It’s not\nright!”\n\n“It’s ka,” Eddie said without thinking.\n\n“Is it? Well ka suh-suh-sucks!”\n\n“Yeah, hard,” Eddie agreed . . . but he kept walking. So did Jake, and he didn’t\nlook back again. He was afraid they would still be there, standing at the center\nof their forgotten town, watching until Roland and his friends were out of view.\n\nAnd he would have been right.\n\nTHEY HAD MADE LESS than seven miles before the sky began to darken and sunset\ncolored the western horizon blaze orange. There was a grove of Susannah’s\neucalyptus trees nearby; Jake and Eddie foraged there for wood.\n“I just don’t see why we didn’t stay,” Jake said. “The blind lady invited us,\nand we didn’t get very far, anyway. I’m still so full I’m practi-cally\nwaddling.”\n\nEddie smiled. “Me, too. And I can tell you something else: your good friend\nEdward Cantor Dean is looking forward to a long and lei-surely squat in this\ngrove of trees first thing tomorrow morning. You wouldn’t believe how tired I am\nof eating deermeat and crapping rabbit-turds. If you’d told me a year ago that a\ngood dump would be the high point of my day, I would have laughed in your face.”\n“Is your middle name really Cantor?”\n\n“Yes, but I’d appreciate it if you didn’t spread it around.”\n“I won’t. Why didn’t we stay, Eddie?”\n\nEddie sighed. “Because we would have found out they needed firewood.”\n“Huh?”\n\n“And after we got the firewood, we would’ve found they also needed fresh meat,\nbecause they served us the last of what they had. And we’d be real creeps not to\nreplace what we ate, right? Especially when we’re packing guns and the best they\ncan probably do is a bunch of bows and arrows fifty or a hundred years old. So\nwe would have gone hunting for them. By then it would be night again, and when\nwe got up the next day, Susannah would be saying we ought to at least make a few\nrepairs before we moved on—oh, not to the front of the town, that’d be\ndanger-ous, but maybe in the hotel or wherever it is they live. Only a few days,\nand what’s a few days, right?”
13Part 12Darktower 3 - The Waste Lands\n\nRoland materialized out of the gloom. He moved as quietly as ever, but he looked\ntired and preoccupied. “I thought maybe you two fell into a quickpit,” he said.\n“Nope. I’m starting to sound just\nlike him, he thought. And yet he knew that he was only speaking the truth.\n“Maybe we’d see that their spring is getting silted up, and it wouldn’t be\npolite to go until we’d dug it out for them. They’re old,\nand have no more foot.” He glanced at Roland, and his voice was tinged with\nreproach. “I tell you what—when I think of Bill and Till there stalking a herd\nof wild buffalo, I get the shivers.”\n\n“They’ve been doing it a long time,” Roland said, “and I imagine they could show\nus a thing or two. I’m\nsaving it would never be any easier to leave than it was today. Harder, maybe,\nbut no easier.”\n\n“It still doesn’t seem right.”\n\nThey reached the place that would become, once the fire was lit, just another\ncampsite on the road to the Dark Tower. Susannah had eased herself out of her\nchair and was lying on her back with her hands behind her head, looking up at\nthe stars. Now she sat up and began to arrange the wood in the way Roland had\nshown her months ago.\n\n“Right is what all this is about,” Roland said. “But if you look too long at the\nsmall rights, Jake—the ones that lie close at hand— it’s easy to lose sight of\n\nthe big ones that stand farther off. Things are out of joint—going wrong and\n\ngetting worse. We see it all around us, but the answers are still ahead. While\nwe were helping the twenty or thirty people left in River Crossing, twenty or\nthirty thousand more might be suffering or dying somewhere else. And if there is\nany place in the universe where these things can be set right, it’s at the Dark\nTower.”\n\n“Why? How?” Jake asked. “What is this Tower, anyway?”\nRoland squatted beside the fire Susannah had built, produced his flint and\nsteel, and began to flash sparks into the kindling. Soon small flames were\ngrowing amid the twigs and dried handfuls of grass. “I can’t answer those\nquestions,” he said. “I wish I could.”\n\nThat, Eddie thought, was an exceedingly clever reply. Roland had said I can’t\nanswer . . . but that wasn’t the same thing as I don’t know. Far from it.\n\nSUPPER CONSISTED OF WATER and greens. They were all still recovering from the\nheavy meal they’d eaten in River Crossing; even Oy refused the scraps Jake\noffered him after the first one or two.\n\n“How come you wouldn’t talk back there?” Jake scolded the bum-bier. “You made me\nlook like an idiot!”\n\n“Id-yit!” Oy said, and put his muzzle on Jake’s ankle.\n“He’s talking better all the time,” Roland remarked. “He’s even starting to\nsound like you, Jake.”\n\n“Ake,” Oy agreed, not lifting his muzzle. Jake was fascinated by the gold rings\nin Oy’s eyes; in the flickering light of the fire, they seemed to revolve\nslowly.\n\n“But he wouldn’t talk to the old people.”\n“Bumblers are choosy about that sort of thing,” Roland said. “They’re odd\ncreatures. If I had to guess, I’d say this one was driven away by its own pack.”\n“Why do you think so?”\n\nRoland pointed at Oy’s flank. He had lain down beside\nSusannah and put an arm about her shoulders.\n“Maybe they did,” Roland said, “especially if he was the only one of them who\nwas still trying to talk. Animals don’t know as much about jealousy as people, but\nthey’re not ignorant of it, either.”\n\nThe object of this discussion closed his eyes and appeared to go to sleep . . .\nbut Jake noticed his ears began twitching when the talk resumed.\n“How bright are they?” Jake asked.\n\nRoland shrugged. “The old groom I told you about—the one who said a good bumbler\nis good luck—swore he had one in his youth that could add. After a moment, the other three followed.\n\nUNTIL THEY MET THE old people in River Crossing, Susannah had seen Roland\nstrictly in terms of television shows she rarely watched: Cheyenne, The\nRifleman, and, of course, the archetype of them all, Gunsmoke. That was one she\nhad sometimes listened to on tin- radio with her father before it came on TV\n(she thought of how foreign the idea of radio drama would be to Eddie and Jake\nand smiled—Roland’s was not the only world which had moved on). She could still\nremember what the narrator said at the beginning of every one of those radio\nplaylets: “It makes a man watchful . . . and a little lonely.”\nUntil River Crossing, that had summed Roland up perfectly for her. He was not\nbroad-shouldered, as Marshal Dillon had been, nor anywhere near as tall, and his\nface seemed to her more that of a tired poet than a wild-west lawman, but she\nhad still seen him as an existential version of that make-believe Kansas peace\nofficer, whose only mission in life (other than an occasional drink in The\nLongbranch with his friends Doc and Kitty) had been to Clean Up Dodge.\nNow she understood that Roland had once been much more than a cop riding a\nDaliesque range at the end of the world. He had been a diplomat; a mediator;\nperhaps even a teacher. Most of all, he had been a soldier of what these people\ncalled “the white,” by which she guessed they meant the civilizing forces that\nkept people from killing each other enough of the time to allow some sort of\nprogress. In his time he had been more wandering knight-errant than bounty\nhunter. And in many ways, this still was his time; the people of River Crossing\nhad certainly thought so. Why else would they have knelt in the dust to receive\nhis blessing?\n\nIn light of this new perception, Susannah could see how cleverly the gunslinger\nhad managed them since that awful morning in the speaking ring, Each time they\nhad begun a line of conversation which would lead to the comparing of notes—and\nwhat could be more natural, given the cataclysmic and inexplicable “drawing”\neach of them had experienced?— Roland had been there, stepping in quickly and\nturning the conversation into other channels so smoothly that none of them (even\nshe, who had spent almost four years up to her neck in the civil-rights\nmovement) had noticed what he was doing.\nSusannah thought she understood why—he had done it in order to give Jake time to\nheal. But understanding his motives didn’t change her own feelings—astonishment,\n\namusement, chagrin—about how neatly he had handled them. She remembered\n\nsomething Andrew, her chauffeur, had said shortly before Roland had drawn her\ninto this world. Something about President Kennedy being the last gunslinger of\nthe western world. She had scoffed then, but now she thought she understood.\nThere was a lot more JFK than Matt Dillon in Roland. Don’t forget guile.\nShe surprised herself by suddenly bursting into laughter.\nRoland had seated himself cross-legged. Now he turned toward her, raising his\neyebrows. “Something funny?”\n\n“Very. Roland grinned as he said it.\nJake grinned back. “What does it mean?”\n\nRoland put an arm around the boy’s shoulders for a moment. “That we have a lot\nof things to talk about.”\n\n“WE ARE KA-TET,” ROLAND began, “which means a group of people bound together by\nfate. The philosophers of my land said a ka-tet could only be broken by death or\ntreachery. My great teacher, Cort, said that since death and treachery are also\nspokes on the wheel of ka, such a binding can never be broken. As the years pass\nand I see more, I come more and more to Cort’s way of looking at it.\n“Each member of a ka-tet is like a piece in a puzzle. Taken by itself, each\npiece is a mystery, but when they are put together, they make a picture … or\npart of a picture. It may take a great many ka-tets to finish one picture. It’s easier for me to see, no doubt,\nbecause I am not a full member of this ka-tet—possibly because I am not from\nyour world—and so cannot take part completely in the thought-sharing ability.\nBut I can send. Susannah … do you remember when we were in the circle?”\n“Yes. You told me to let the demon go when you told me. But you didn’t say that\nout loud.”\n\n“Eddie … do you remember when we were in the bear’s clearing, and the\nmechanical bat came at you?”\n\n“Yes. You told me to get down.”\n\n“He never opened his mouth, Eddie,” Susannah said.\n“Yes, you did! You yelled! I heard you, man!”\n“I yelled, all right, but I did it with my mind.” The gunslinger turned to Jake.\n“Do you remember? In the house?”\n\n“When the board I was pulling on wouldn’t come up, you told me to pull on the\nother one. But if you can’t read my mind, Roland, how did you know what land of\ntrouble I was in?”\n\n“I saw. I heard nothing, but I saw—just a little, as if through a dirty window.”\nHis eyes surveyed them. “This closeness and sharing of minds is called khef, a\nword that means many other things in the original tongue of the Old World—water,\n\nbirth, and life-force are only three of them. Be aware of it. For now, that’s\n\nall I want.”\n\n“Can you be aware of something you don’t believe in?” Eddie asked.\nRoland smiled. “Just keep an open mind.”\n“That I can do.”\n\n“Roland?” It was Jake. “Do you think Oy might be part of our ka-tet?”\nSusannah smiled. Roland didn’t. “I’m not prepared to even guess right now, but\nI’ll tell you this, Jake—I’ve been thinking about your furry friend a good deal.\nKa does not rule all, and coincidences still happen . . . but the sudden\nappearance of a billy-bumbler that still remembers people doesn’t seem\ncompletely coincidental to me.”\n\nHe glanced around at them.\n\n“I’ll begin. Eddie will speak next, taking up from the place where I leave off.\nThen Susannah. Jake, you’ll speak last. All right?”\nThey nodded.\n\n“Fine,” Roland said. “We are ka-tet—one from many. Let the pala-ver begin.”\n\nTHE TALK WENT ON until sundown, stopping only long enough for them to eat a cold\nmeal, and by the time it was over, Eddie felt as if he had gone twelve hard\nrounds with Sugar Ray Leonard. He no longer doubted that they had been “sharing\nkhef,” as Roland put it; he and Jake actually seemed to have been living each\nother’s life in their dreams, as if they were two halves of the same whole.\nRoland began with what had happened under the mountains, where Jake’s first life\nin this world had ended. He told of his own palaver with the man in black, and\nWalter’s veiled words about a Beast and someone he called the Ageless Stranger.\nHe told of the strange, daunting dream which had come to him, a dream in which\nthe whole universe had been swallowed in a beam of fantastic white light. And\nhow, at the end of that dream, there had been a single blade of purple grass.\nEddie glanced sideways at Jake and was stunned by the knowledge— the\nrecognition—in the boy’s eyes.\n\nROLAND HAD BABBLED PARTS of this story to Eddie in his time of delir-ium, but it\nwas entirely new to Susannah, and she listened with wide eyes. As Roland\nrepeated the things Walter had told him, she caught glints of her own world,\nlike reflections in a smashed mirror: automobiles, cancer, rockets to the moon,\nartificial insemination. She had no idea who the Beast might be, but she\nrecognized the name of the Ageless Stranger as a variation upon the name of\nMerlin, the magician who had supposedly orchestrated the career of King Arthur.\nCuriouser and curiouser.\n\nRoland told of how he had awakened to find Walter long years dead—time had\nsomehow slipped forward, perhaps a hundred years, per-haps five hundred. Jake\nlistened in fascinated silence as the gunslinger told of reaching the edge of\nthe Western Sea, of how he had lost two of the fingers on his right hand, and\nhow he had drawn Eddie and Susannah before encountering Jack Mort, the dark\nthird.\n\nThe gunslinger motioned to Eddie, who took up the tale with the coming of the\ngreat bear.\n\n“Shardik?” Jake interjected. “But that’s the name of a book! A book in our\nworld! It was written by the man who wrote that famous book about the rabbits—”\n“Richard Adams!” Eddie shouted. “And the book about the bunnies was Watership\nDown! I knew I knew that name! But how can that be, Roland? How is it that the\npeople in your world know about things in ours?”\n“There are doors, aren’t there?” Roland responded. “Haven’t we seen four of them\nalready? Do you think they never existed before, or never will again?”\n“But—“\n\n“All of us have seen the leavings of your world in mine, and when I was in your\n\ncity of New York, I saw the marks of my world in yours. I saw gunslingers. Most\nwere lax and slow, but they were gunslingers all the same, clearly members of\ntheir own ancient ka-tet.”\n\n“Roland, they were just cops. You ran rings around them.”\n“Not the last one. When Jack Mort and I were in the underground railway station,\nthat one almost took me down. Except for blind luck— Mort’s flint-and-steel—he\nwould have done. That one … I saw his eyes. He knew the face of his father. I\nbelieve he knew it very well. And then … do you remember the name of Balazar’s\nnightclub?”\n\n“Sure,” Eddie said uneasily. “The Leaning Tower. But it could have been\ncoincidence; you yourself said ka doesn’t rule everything.”\nRoland nodded. “You really are like Cuthbert—I remember some-thing he said when\nwe were boys. We were planning a midnight lark in the cemetery, but Alain\nwouldn’t go. He said he was afraid of offending the shades of his fathers and\nmothers. Cuthbert laughed at him. He said he wouldn’t believe in ghosts until he\ncaught one in his teeth.”\n\n“Good for him!” Eddie exclaimed. “Bravo!”\nRoland smiled. “I thought you’d like that. At any rate, let’s leave this ghost\nfor now. Go on with your story.”\n\nEddie told of the vision which had come to him when Roland threw the jawbone\ninto the fire—the vision of the key and the rose. He told of his dream, and how\nhe had walked through the door of Tom and Gerry’s Artistic Deli and into the\nfield of roses which was dominated by the tall, soot-colored Tower. He told of\nthe blackness which had issued from its windows, forming a shape in the sky\noverhead, speaking directly to Jake now, because Jake was listening with hungry\nconcentration and growing wonder. He tried to convey some sense of the\nexaltation and terror which had permeated the dream, and saw from their\neyes—Jake’s most of all— that he was either doing a better job of that than he\ncould have hoped for … or that they’d had dreams of their own.\nHe told of following Shardik’s backtrail to the Portal of the Bear, and how,\nwhen he put his head against it, he’d found himself remember-ing the day he had\ntalked his brother into taking him to Dutch Hill, so he could see The Mansion.\nHe told about die cup and the needle, and how the pointing needle had become\nunnecessary once they realized they could see the Beam at work in everything it\ntouched, even the birds in the sky.\n\nSusannah took up the tale at this point. As she spoke, telling of how Eddie had\nbegun to carve his own version of the key, Jake lay back, laced his hands\ntogether behind his head, and watched the clouds run slowly toward the city on\ntheir straight southeasterly course. The orderly shape they made showed the\npresence of the Beam as clearly as smoke leaving a chimney shows die direction\nof the wind.\n\nShe finished with the story of how they had finally hauled Jake into this world,\nclosing the split track of his and Roland’s memories as sud-denly and as\ncompletely as Eddie had closed the door in the speaking ring. The only fact she\nleft out was really not a fact at all—at least, not yet. She’d had no morning\nsickness, after all, and a single missed period meant nothing by itself. As\nRoland himself might have said, that was a tale best left for another day.\nYet as she finished, she found herself wishing she could forget what Aunt\nTalitha had said when Jake told her this was his home now: Gods pity you, then,\nfor the sun is going down on this world. It’s going down forever.\n“And now it’s your turn, Jake,” Roland said.\nJake sat up and looked toward Lud, where the windows of the west-em towers\nreflected back the late afternoon light in golden sheets. “It’s all crazy,” he\nmurmured, “but it almost makes sense. Like a dream when you wake up.”\n“Maybe we can help you make sense of it,” Susannah said.\n“Maybe you can. At least you can help me think about the train. I’m tired of\ntrying to make sense of Blaine by myself.” He sighed. “You know what Roland went\nthrough, living two lives at the same time, so I can skip that part. I’m not\n\nsure I could ever explain how it felt, anyway, and I don’t want to. It was\n\ngross. I guess I better start with my Final Essay, because that’s when I finally\nstopped thinking that the whole thing might just go away.” He looked around at\nthem somberly. “That was when I gave up.”\n\nJAKE TALKED THE SUN down.\n\nHe told them everything he could remember, beginning with My Understanding of\nTruth and ending with the monstrous doorkeeper which had literally come out of\nthe woodwork to attack him. The other three listened without a single\ninterruption.\n\nWhen he was finished, Roland turned to Eddie, his eyes bright with a mixture of\nemotions Eddie initially took for wonder. Then he realized he was looking at\npowerful excitement . . . and deep fear. His mouth went dry. Because if Roland\nwas afraid—\n\n“Do you still doubt that our worlds overlap each other, Eddie?”\nHe shook his head. “Of course not. I walked down the same street, and I did it\nin his clothes! But . . . Jake, can I see that book? Charlie the Choo-Choo?”\nJake reached for his pack, but Roland stayed his hand. “Not yet,” he said. “Go\nback to the vacant lot, Jake. Tell that part once more. Try to remember\neverything.”\n\n“Maybe you should hypnotize me,” Jake said hesitantly. “Like you did before, at\nthe way station.”\n\nRoland shook his head. “There’s no need. What happened to you in that lot was\nthe most important thing ever to happen in your life, Jake. In all our lives.\nYou can remember everything.”\n\nSo Jake went through it again. It was clear to all of them that his experience\nin the vacant lot where Tom and Gerry’s once had stood was the secret heart of\nthe ka-tet they shared. In Eddie’s dream, the Artistic Deli had still been\nstanding; in Jake’s reality it had been torn down, but in both cases it was a\nplace of enormous, talismanic power. Nor did Roland doubt that the vacant lot\nwith its broken bricks and shattered glass was another version of what Susannah\nknew as the Drawers and the place he had seen at the end of his vision in the\nplace of bones.\n\nAs he told this part of his story for the second time, speaking very slowly now,\nJake found that what the gunslinger had said was true: he could remember\neverything. His recall improved until he almost seemed to be reliving the\nexperience. He told them of the sign which said that a building called Turtle\nBay Condominiums was slated to stand on the spot where Tom and Gerry’s had once\nstood. He even remembered the little poem which had been spray-painted on the\nfence, and recited it for them:\n\n“See the TURTLE of enormous girth!\n\nOn his shell he holds the earth.\n\nIf you want to run and play,\n\nCome along the BEAM today.”\n\nSusannah murmured, “His thought is slow but always kind; He holds us all within\nhis mind . . . isn’t that how it went, Roland?”\n“What?” Jake asked. “How what went?”\n\n“A poem I learned as a child,” Roland said. “It’s another connection, one that\nreally tells us something, although I’m not sure it’s anything we need to know .\n. . still, one never knows when a little understanding may come in handy.”\n“Twelve portals connected by six Beams,” Eddie said. “We started at the Bear.\nWe’re only going as far as the middle—to the Tower—but if we went all the way to\nthe other end, we’d come to the Portal of the Turtle, wouldn’t we?”\nRoland nodded. “I’m sure we would.”\n\n“Portal of the Turtle,” Jake said thoughtfully, rolling the words in his mouth,\n\nseeming to taste them. Then he finished by telling them again about the gorgeous\n\nvoice of the choir, his realization that there were faces and stories and\nhistories everywhere, and his growing belief that he had stumbled on something\nvery like the core of all existence. Last of all, he told them again about\nfinding the key and seeing the rose. In the totality of his recall, Jake began\nto weep, although he seemed unaware of it.\n“When it opened,” he said, “I saw the middle was the brightest yellow you ever\nsaw in your life. At first I thought it was pollen and it only looked bright\nbecause everything in that lot looked bright. Even looking at the old\ncandy-wrappers and beer-bottles was like looking at the greatest paintings you\never saw. Only then I realized it was a sun. I know it sounds crazy, but that’s\nwhat it was. Only it was more than one. It was—”\n“It was all suns,” Roland murmured. “It was everything real.” “Yes! And it was\nright—but it was wrong, too. I can’t explain how it was wrong, but it was. It\nwas like two heartbeats, one inside of the other, and the one inside had a\ndisease. Or an infection. And then I fainted.”\n\n“You SAW THE SAME thing at the end of your dream, Roland, didn’t you?” Susannah\nasked. Her voice was soft with awe. “The blade of grass you saw near the end of\nit … you thought that blade was purple because it was splattered with paint.”\n“You don’t understand,” Jake said. “It really was purple. When I was seeing it\nthe way it really was, it was purple. Like no grass I ever saw before. The paint\nwas just camouflage. The way the doorkeeper camouflaged itself to look like an\nold deserted house.”\n\nThe sun had reached the horizon. Roland asked Jake if he would now show them\nCharlie the Choo-Choo and then read it to them. Jake handed the book around.\nBoth Eddie and Susannah looked at the cover for a long time.\n“I had this book when I was a little lad,” Eddie said at last. He spoke in the\nflat tones of utter surety. “Then we moved from Queens to Brooklyn—I wasn’t even\nfour years old—and I lost it. But I remember the picture on the cover. And I\nfelt the same way you do, Jake. I didn’t like it. I didn’t trust it.”\nSusannah raised her eyes to look at Eddie. “I had it, too—how could I ever\nforget the little girl with my name . . . although of course it was my middle\nname back in those days. And I felt the same way about the train. I didn’t like\nit and I didn’t trust it.” She tapped the front of the book with her finger\nbefore passing it on to Roland. “I thought that smile was a great big fake.”\nRoland gave it only a cursory glance before returning his eyes to Susannah. “Did\nyou lose yours, too?”\n\n“Yes.”\n\n“And I’ll bet I know when,” Eddie said.\n\nSusannah nodded. “I’ll bet you do. It was after that man dropped the brick on my\nhead. I had it when we went north to my Aunt Blue’s wedding. I had it on the\ntrain. I remember, because I kept asking my dad if Charlie the Choo-Choo was\npulling us. I didn’t want it to be Charlie, because we were supposed to go to\nElizabeth, New Jersey, and I thought Charlie might take us anywhere. Didn’t he\nend up pulling folks around a toy village or something like that, Jake?”\n“An amusement park.”\n\n“Yes, of course it was. There’s a picture of him hauling kids around that place\nat the end, isn’t there? They’re all smiling and laughing, except I always\nthought they looked like they were screaming to be let off.”\n“Yes!” Jake cried. “Yes, that’s right! That’s just right!”\n“I thought Charlie might take us to his place—wherever he lived— instead of to\nmy Aunt’s wedding, and never let us go home again.”\n“You can’t go home again,” Eddie muttered, and ran his hands ner-vously through\nhis hair.\n\n“All the time we were on that train I wouldn’t let go of the book. I even\nremember thinking, ‘If he tries to steal us, I’ll rip out his pages until he\n\nquits.’ But of course we arrived right where we were supposed to, and on time,\n\ntoo. Daddy even took me up front, so I could see the engine. It was a diesel,\nnot a steam engine, and I remember that made me happy. Then, after the wedding,\nthat man Mort dropped the brick on me and I was in a coma for a long time. I\nnever saw Charlie the Choo-Choo after that. Not until now.” She hesitated, then\nadded: “This could be my copy, for all I know—or Eddie’s.”\n“Yeah, and probably is,” Eddie said. His face was pale and solemn . . . and then\nhe grinned like a lad. ” ‘See the TURTLE, ain’t he keen? All things serve the\nfuckin Beam.’ “\n\nRoland glanced west. “The sun’s going down. Read the story before we lose the\nlight, Jake.”\n\nJake turned to the first page, showed them the picture of Engineer Bob in\nCharlie’s cab, and began: ” ‘Bob Brooks was an engineer for The Mid-World\nRailway Company, on the St. Louis to Topeka run…..’ ”\n\n” ‘. . . AND EVERY NOW AND then the children hear him singing his old song in\nhis soft, gruff voice,’ ” Jake finished. He showed them the last picture—the\nhappy children who might actually have been screaming— and then closed the book.\nThe sun had gone down; the sky was purple.\n“Well, it’s not a perfect fit,” Eddie said, “more like a dream where the water\nsometimes runs uphill—but it fits well enough to scare me silly. This is\nMid-World—Charlie’s territory. Only his name over here isn’t Charlie at all.\nOver here it’s Blaine the Mono.”\n\nRoland was looking at Jake. “What do you think?” he asked. “Should we go around\nthe city? Stay away from this train?”\n\nJake thought it over, head down, hands working distractedly through Oy’s thick,\nsilky fur. “I’d like to,” he said at last, “but if I’ve got this stuff about ka\nright, I don’t think we’re supposed to.”\nRoland nodded. “If it’s ka, questions of what we’re supposed to or not supposed\nto do aren’t even in it; if we tried to go around, we’d find circumstances\nforcing us back. In such cases it’s better to give in to the inevitable promptly\ninstead of putting it off. What do you think, Eddie?”\nEddie thought as long and as carefully as Jake had done. He didn’t want anything\nto do with a talking train that ran by itself, and whether you called it Charlie\nthe Choo-Choo or Blaine the Mono, everything Jake had told them and read them\nsuggested that it might be a very nasty piece of work. But they had a tremendous\ndistance to cross, and some-where, at the end of it, was the thing they had come\nto find. And with that thought, Eddie was amazed to discover he knew exactly\nwhat he thought, and what he wanted. He raised his head and for almost the first\ntime since he had come to this world, he fixed Roland’s faded blue eyes firmly\nwith his hazel ones.\n\n“I want to stand in that field of roses, and I want to see the Tower that stands\nthere. I don’t know what comes next. Mourners please omit flowers, probably, and\nfor all of us. But I don’t care. I want to stand there. I guess I don’t care if\nBlaine’s the devil and the train runs through hell itself on the way to the\nTower. I vote we go.”\n\nRoland nodded and turned to Susannah.\n\n“Well, I didn’t have any dreams about the Dark Tower,” she said, “so I can deal\nwith the question on that level—the level of desire, I suppose you’d say. But\nI’ve come to believe in ka, and I’m not so numb that I can’t feel it when\nsomeone starts rapping on my head with his knuckles and saying, ‘That way,\nidiot.’ What about you, Roland? What do you think?”\n“I think there’s been enough talk for one day, and it’s time to let it go until\ntomorrow.”\n\n“What about Riddle-De-Dum!—” Jake asked, “do you want to look at that?”\n“There’ll be time enough for that another day,” Roland said. “Let’s get some\nsleep.”\n\nBUT THE GUNSLINGER LAY long awake, and when the rhythmic drum-ming began again,\nhe got up and walked back to the road. He stood looking toward the bridge and\nthe city. He was every inch the diplomat Susannah had suspected, and he had\nknown the train was the next step on the road they must travel almost from the\nmoment he had heard of it … but he’d felt it would be unwise to say so. Eddie\nin particular hated to feel pushed; when he sensed that was being done, he\nsimply lowered his head, planted his feet, made his silly jokes, and balked like\na mule. This time he wanted what Roland wanted, but he was still apt to say day\nif Roland said night, and night if Roland said day. It was safer to walk softly,\nand surer to ask instead of telling.\n\nHe turned to go back . . . and his hand dropped to his gun as he saw a dark\nshape standing on the edge of the road, looking at him. He didn’t draw, but it\nwas a near thing.\n\n“I wondered if you’d be able to sleep after that little performance,” Eddie\nsaid. “Guess the answer’s no.”\n\n“I didn’t hear you at all, Eddie. You’re learning . .. only this time you almost\ngot a bullet in the gut for your pains.”\n“You didn’t hear me because you have a lot on your mind.” Eddie joined him, and\neven by starlight, Roland saw he hadn’t fooled Eddie a bit. His respect for\nEddie continued to grow. It was Cuthbert Eddie reminded him of, but in many ways\nhe had already surpassed Cuthbert.\n\nIf I underestimate him, Roland thought, I’m apt to come away with a bloody paw.\nAnd if I let him down, or do something that looks to him like a double-cross,\nhe’ll probably try to kill me.\n\n“What’s on your mind, Eddie?”\n\n“You. Us. I want you to know something. I guess until tonight I just assumed\nthat you knew already. Now I’m not so sure.”\n“Tell me, then.” He thought again: How like Cuthbert he is!\n“We’re with you because we have to be—that’s your goddamned ka. But we’re also\nwith you because we want to be. I know that’s true of me and Susannah, and I’m\npretty sure it’s true of Jake, too. You’ve got a good brain, me old khef-mate,\nbut I think you must keep it in a bomb-shelter, because it’s bitchin hard to get\nthrough sometimes. I want to see it, Roland. Can you dig what I’m telling you? I\nwant to see the Tower.” He looked closely into Roland’s face, apparently did not\nsee what he’d hoped to find there, and raised his hands in exasperation. “What I\nmean is I want you to let go of my ears.”\n“Let go of your ears?”\n\n“Yeah. Because you don’t have to drag me anymore. I’m coming of my own accord.\nWe’re coming of our own accord. If you died in your sleep tonight, we’d bury you\nand then go on. We probably wouldn’t last long, but we’d die in the path of the\nBeam. Now do you understand?”\n\n“Yes. Now I do.”\n\n“You say you understand me, and I think you do … but do you believe me, as\nwell?”\n\nOf course, he thought. Where else do you have to go, Eddie, in this world that’s\nso strange to you? And what else could you do? You’d make a piss-poor farmer.\nBut that was mean and unfair, and he knew it. Denigrating free will by confusing\nit with ka was worse than blasphemy; it was tiresome and stupid. “Yes,” he said.\n“I believe you. Upon my soul, I do.”\n\n“Then stop behaving like we’re a bunch of sheep and you’re the shepherd walking\nalong behind us, waving a crook to make sure we don’t trot our stupid selves off\nthe road and into a quicksand bog. Open your mind to us. If we’re going to die\nin the city or on that train, I want to die knowing I was more than a marker on\nyour game-board.”\n\nRoland felt anger heat his cheeks, but he had never been much good at\nself-deception. He wasn’t angry because Eddie was wrong but because Eddie had\n\nseen through him. Roland had watched him come steadily forward, leaving his\n\nprison further and further behind—and Susannah, too, for she had also been\nimprisoned—and yet his heart had never quite accepted the evidence of his\nsenses. His heart apparently wanted to go on seeing them as different, lesser\ncreatures.\n\nRoland drew in deep air. “Gunslinger, I cry your pardon.”\nEddie nodded. “We’re running into a whole hurricane of trouble here … I feel\nit, and I’m scared to death. But it’s not your trouble, it’s our trouble. Okay?”\n“Yes.”\n\n“How bad do you think it can get in the city?”\n“I don’t know. I only know that we have to try and protect Jake, because the old\nauntie said both sides would want him. Some of it depends on how long it takes\nus to find this train. A lot more depends on what happens when we find it. If we\nhad two more in our party, I’d put Jake in a moving box with guns on every side\nof him. Since we don’t, we’ll move in column—me first, Jake pushing Susannah\nbehind, and you on drogue.”\n\n“How much trouble, Roland? Make a guess.”\n“I can’t.”\n\n“I think you can. You don’t know the city, but you know how the people in your\nworld have been behaving since things started to fall apart. How much trouble?”\nRoland turned toward the steady sound of the drumbeats and thought it over.\n“Maybe not too much. I’d guess the fighting men who are still there are old and\ndemoralized. It may he that yon have the straight of it, and some will even\noffer to help us on our way, as the River Crossing ka-tet did. Mayhap we won’t\nsee them at all—they’ll see MS, see we’re packing iron, and just put their heads\ndown and let us go our way. If that fails, I’m hoping that they’ll scatter like\nrats if we gun a few.”\n\n“And if they decide to make a fight of it?”\nRoland smiled grimly. “Then, Eddie, we’ll all remember the faces of our\nfathers.”\n\nEddie’s eyes gleamed in the darkness, and Roland was once more reminded forcibly\nof Cuthbert—Cuthbert who had once said he would believe in ghosts when he could\ncatch one in his teeth, Cuthbert with whom he had once scattered breadcrumbs\nbeneath the hangman’s gibbet.\n\n“Have I answered all your questions?”\n\n“Nope—but I think you played straight with me this time.”\n“Then goodnight, Eddie.”\n\n“Goodnight.”\n\nEddie turned and walked away. Roland watched him go. Now that he was listening,\nhe could hear him . . . but just barely. He started back himself, then turned\ntoward the darkness where the city of Lud was.\nHe’s what the old woman called a Pube. She said both sides would want him.\nYou won’t let me drop this time?\n\nNo. Not this time, not ever again.\n\nBut he knew something none of the others did. Perhaps, after the talk he’d just\nhad with Eddie, he should tell them . . . yet he thought he would keep the\nknowledge to himself a little while longer.\nIn the old tongue which had once been his world’s lingua franca, most words,\nlike khef and ka, had many meanings. The word char, how-ever—char as in Charlie\nthe Choo-Choo—had only one.\n\nChar meant death.\n\nV\n\nBRIDGE AND\n\nCITY\n\nV\n\nBRIDGE AND CITY\n\nTHEY CAME UPON THE downed airplane three days later.\nJake pointed it out first at midmorning—a flash of light about ten miles away,\nas if a mirror lay in the grass. As they drew closer, they saw a large dark\nobject at the side of the Great Road.\n\n“It looks like a dead bird,” Roland said. “A big one.”\n“That’s no bird,” Eddie said. “That’s an airplane. I’m pretty sure the glare is\nsunlight bouncing off the canopy.”\n\nAn hour later they stood silently at the edge of the road, looking at the\nancient wreck. Three plump crows stood on the tattered skin of the fuselage,\nstaring insolently at the newcomers. Jake pried a cobble from the edge of the\nroad and shied it at them. The crows lumbered into the air, cawing indignantly.\nOne wing had broken off in the crash and lay thirty yards away, a shadow like a\ndiving board in the tall grass. The rest of the plane was pretty much intact.\nThe canopy had cracked in a starburst pattern where the pilot’s head had struck\nit. There was a large, rust-colored stain there.\nOy trotted over to where three rusty propeller blades rose from the grass,\nsniffed at them, then returned hastily to Jake.\nThe man in the cockpit was u dust-dry mummy wearing a padded leather vest and a\nhelmet with a spike on top. His lips were gone, his teeth exposed in a final\ndesperate grimace. Fingers which had once been as large as sausages but were now\nonly skin-covered bones clutched the wheel. His skull was caved in where it had\nhit the canopy, and Roland guessed that the greenish-gray scales which coated\nthe left side of his face were all that remained of his brains. The dead man’s\nhead was tilted back, as if he had been sure, even at the moment of his death,\nthat he could regain the sky again. The plane’s remaining wing still jutted from\nthe encroaching grass. On it was a fading insignia which depicted a fist holding\na thunderbolt.\n\n“Looks like Aunt Talitha was wrong and the old albino man had the right of it,\nafter all,” Susannah said in an awed voice. “That must be David Quick, the\noutlaw prince. Look at the size of him, Roland—they must have had to grease him\nto get him into the cockpit!”\n\nRoland nodded. The heat and the years had wasted the man in the mechanical bird\nto no more than a skeleton wrapped in dry hide, but he could still see how broad\nthe shoulders had been, and the misshapen head was massive. “So fell Lord\nPerth,” he said, “and the countryside did shake with that thunder.”\nJake looked at him questioningly.\n\n“It’s from an old poem. Lord Perth was a giant who went forth to war with a\nthousand men, but he was still in his own country when a little boy threw a\nstone at him and hit him in the knee. He stumbled, the weight of his armor bore\nhim down, and he broke his neck in the fall.”\nJake said, “Like our story of David and Goliath.”\n“There was no fire,” Eddie said. “I bet he just ran out of gas and tried a\ndead-stick landing on the road. He might have been an outlaw and a barbarian,\nbut he had a yard of guts.”\n\nRoland nodded, and looked at Jake. “You all right with this?”\n“Yes. If the guy was still, you know, runny, I might not be.” Jake looked from\nthe dead man in the airplane to the city. I did a school paper on air combat when I was in the fifth grade, and I\nthink I recognize it. Roland, can I take a closer look?”\nRoland nodded. “I’ll go with you.”\n\nTogether they walked over to the plane with the high grass swishing at their\npants. “Look,” Jake said. “See the machine-gun under the wing? Maybe it’s a great big doorway between our worlds—one that’s almost\nalways open.” Eddie hunched his shoulders and essayed a bad Rod Serling\nimitation. “Fasten your seatbelts and prepare for turbu-lence: you’re flying\ninto . . . the Roland Zone!”\n\nJake and Roland, who were now standing beneath the plane’s remaining wing,\nignored him.\n\n“Boost me up, Roland.”\n\nRoland shook his head. “That wing looks solid, but it’s not—this thing has been\nhere a long time, Jake. You’d fall.”\n\n“Make a step, then.”\n\nEddie said, “I’ll do it, Roland.”\n\nRoland studied his diminished right hand for a moment, shrugged, then laced his\nhands together. “This’ll do. He’s light.”\nJake shook off his moccasin and then stepped lightly into the stirrup Roland had\nmade. Oy began to bark shrilly, though whether in excitement or alarm, Roland\ncouldn’t tell.\n\nJake’s chest was now pressing against one of the airplane’s rusty flaps, and he\nwas looking right at the fist-and-thunderbolt design. There was another symbol beneath the\nfist-and-thunderbolt, and now it was almost totally revealed. It was a swastika.\n“I just wanted to see it. You can put me down now.”\nThey started out again, but they could see the tail of the plane every time they\nlooked back that afternoon, looming out of the high grass like Lord Perth’s\nburial monument.

They did, and stood looking at the\nhive without speaking.
