It had shown a crowd of survivors leaving the area\nwhere a gas-main had exploded, flattening almost a whole city block in some\nCalifornia town. These bees reminded him of those dazed, shellshocked survivors.\n“You had a nuclear war, didn’t you?” he asked—almost accused. “These Great Old\nOnes you like to talk about . . . they blew their great old asses straight to\nhell. Didn’t they?”\n\n“I don’t know what happened. No one knows. The records of those times are lost,\nand the few stories are confused and conflicting.”\n“Let’s get out of here,” Jake said in a trembling voice. “Looking at those\nthings makes me sick.”\n\n“I’m with you, sugar,” Susannah said.\n\nSo they left the bees to their aimless, shattered life in the grove of ancient\ntrees, and there was no honey that night.\n\n“WHEN ARE YOU GOING to tell us what you do know?” Eddie asked the next morning.\nThe day was bright and blue, but there was a bite in the air; their first autumn\nin this world was almost upon them.\n\nRoland glanced at him. “What do you mean?”\n“I’d like to hear your whole story, from beginning to end, starting with Gilead.\nHow you grew up there and what happened to end it all. I want to know how you\nfound out about the Dark Tower and why you started chasing after it in the first\nplace. I want to know about your first bunch of friends, too. And what happened\nto them.”\n\nRoland removed his hat, armed sweat from his brow, then replaced it. “You have\nthe right to know all those things, I suppose, and I’ll tell them to you . . .\nbut not now. It’s a very long story. I never expected to tell it to anyone, and\nI’ll only tell it once.”\n\n“When?” Eddie persisted.\n\n“When the time is right,” Roland said, and with that they had to be content.\n\nROLAND CAME AWAKE THE moment before Jake began to shake him. He sat up and\nlooked around, but Eddie and Susannah were still fast asleep and in the first\nfaint light of morning, he could see nothing amiss.\n“What is it?” he asked Jake in a low voice.\n“I don’t know. Fighting, maybe. Come and listen.”\nRoland threw his blanket aside and followed Jake out to the road. He reckoned\nthey were now only three days’ walk from the place where the Send passed in\nfront of the city, and the bridge—built squarely along the path of the\nBeam—dominated the horizon. Its pronounced tilt was more clearly visible than\never, and he could see at least a dozen gaps where over-stressed cables had\nsnapped like the strings of a lyre.\n\nTonight the wind blew directly into their faces as they looked toward the city,\nand the sounds it carried to them were faint but clear.\n“Is it fighting?” Jake asked.\n\nRoland nodded and held a finger to his lips.\nHe heard faint shouts, a crash that sounded like some huge object falling,\nand—of course—the drums. Now there was another crash, this one more musical: the\nsound of breaking glass.\n\n“Jeepers,” Jake whispered, and moved closer to the gunslinger.\nThen came the sounds which Roland had hoped not to hear: a fast, sandy rattle of\nsmall-arms fire followed by a loud hollow bang—clearly an explosion of some\nland. It rolled across the flatlands toward them like an invisible bowling ball.\nAfter that, the shouts, thuds, and sounds of breakage quickly sank below the\nlevel of the drums, and when the drums quit a few minutes later with their usual\nunsettling suddenness, the city was silent again. But now that silence had an\nunpleasant waiting quality.\n\nRoland put an arm around Jake’s shoulders. “Still not too late to detour\naround,” he said.\n\nJake glanced up at him. “We can’t.”\n\n“Because of the train?”\n\nJake nodded and singsonged: “Blaine is a pain, but we have to take the train.\nAnd the city’s the only place where we can get on.”\nRoland looked thoughtfully at Jake. “Why do you say we have to? Is it ka?\nBecause, Jake, you have to understand that you don’t know much about ka yet—it’s\nthe sort of subject men study all their lives.”\n“I don’t know if it’s ka or not, but I do know that we can’t go into the waste\nlands unless we’re protected, and that means Blaine. Without him we’ll die, like\nthose bees we saw are going to die when winter comes. We have to be protected.\nBecause the waste lands are poison.”\n\n“How do you know these things?”\n\n“I don’t know!” Jake said, almost angrily. “I just do.”\n\n“All right,” Roland said mildly. He looked toward Lud again. “But we’ll have to\nbe damned careful. It’s unlucky that they still have gunpow-der. If they have\nthat, they may have things that are even more powerful. I doubt if they know how\nto use them, but that only increases the danger. They could get excited and blow\nus all to hell.”\n\n“Ell,” a grave voice said from behind them. They glanced around and saw Oy\nsitting by the side of the road, watching them.\n\nLATER THAT DAY THEY came to a new road which swept toward them out of the west\nand joined their own way. Beyond this point, the Great Road—now much wider and\nsplit down the middle by a median divider of some polished dark stone—began to\nsink, and the crumbling concrete embankments which rose on either side of them\ngave the pilgrims a claustrophobic trapped feeling. They stopped at a point\nwhere one of these concrete dikes had born broken open, affording a comforting\nline of sight to the open land beyond, and ate a light, unsatisfying meal.\n“Why do you think they dropped the road down like this, Eddie?” Jake asked. “I\nmean, someone did do it this way on purpose, didn’t they?”\nEddie looked through the break in the concrete, where the flatlands stretched on\nas smoothly as ever, and nodded.\n\n“Then why?”\n\n“Dunno, champ,” Eddie said, but he thought he did. He glanced at Roland and\nguessed that he knew, too. The sunken road leading to the bridge had been a\ndefensive measure. Troops placed atop the concrete slopes were in control of two\ncarefully engineered redoubts. If the defenders didn’t like the look of the\nfolks approaching Lud along the Great Road, they could rain destruction down on\nthem.\n\n“You sure you don’t know?” Jake asked.\n\nEddie smiled at Jake and tried to stop imagining that there was some nut up\nthere right now, getting ready to roll a large, rusty bomb down one of those\ndecayed concrete ramps. “No idea,” he said.\nSusannah whistled disgustedly between her teeth. “This road’s goin to hell,\nRoland. I was hoping we were done with that damn harness, but you better get it\nout again.” He nodded and rummaged in his purse for it without a word.\nThe condition of the Great Road deteriorated as other, smaller roads joined it\nlike tributaries joining a great river. As they neared the bridge, the cobbles\nwere replaced with a surface Roland thought of as metal and the rest of them\nthought of as asphalt or hot-top. It had not held up as well as the cobbles.\nTime had done some damage; the passage of count-less horses and wagons since the\nlast repairs were made had done more. The surface had been chewed into\ntreacherous rubble. Foot travel would be difficult, and the idea of pushing\nSusannah’s wheelchair over that crumbled surface was ridiculous.\nThe banks oh either side had grown steadily steeper, and now, at their tops,\nthey could see slim, pointed shapes looming against the sky. Roland thought of\narrowheads—huge ones, weapons made by a tribe of giants. To his companions, they\nlooked like rockets or guided missiles. Susannah thought of Redstones fired from\nCape Canaveral; Eddie thought about SAMs, some built to be fired from the backs\nof flatbed trucks, stored all over Europe; Jake thought of ICBMs hiding in\nrein-forced concrete silos under the plains of Kansas and the unpopulated\nmountains of Nevada, programmed to hit back at China or the USSR in the event of\nnuclear armageddon. All of them felt as if they had passed into a dark and\nwoeful zone of shadow, or into a countryside laboring under some old but still\npowerful curse.\n\nSome hours after they entered this area— Jake called it The Gauntlet—the\nconcrete embankments ended at a place where half a dozen access roads drew\ntogether, like the strands of a spiderweb, and here the land opened out again\n… a fact which relieved all of them, although none of them said so out loud.\n\nAnother traffic-light swung over the junc-tion. This one was more familiar to\n\nEddie, Susannah, and Jake; it had once had lenses on its four faces, although\nthe glass had been broken out long ago.\n\n“I’ll bet this road was the eighth wonder of the world, once upon a time,”\nSusannah said, “and look at it now. It’s a minefield.”\n“Old ways are sometimes the best ways,” Roland agreed.\nEddie was pointing west. “Look.”\n\nNow that the high concrete barriers were gone, they could see exactly what old\nSi had described to them over cups of bitter coffee in River Crossing. “One\ntrack only,” he had said, “set up high on a colyum of man-made stone, such as\nthe Old Ones used to make their streets and walls.” The track raced toward them\nout of the west in a slim, straight line, then flowed across the Send and into\nthe city on a narrow golden trestle. It was a simple, elegant construction—and\nthe only one they had seen so far which was totally without rust—but it was\nbadly marred, all the same. Halfway across, a large piece of the trestle had\nfallen into the rushing river below. What remained were two long, jutting piers\nthat pointed at each other like accusing fingers. Jutting out of the water below\nthe hole was a streamlined tube of metal. Once it had been bright blue, but now\nthe color had been dimmed by spreading scales of rust. It looked very small from\nthis distance.\n\n“So much for Blaine,” Eddie said. “No wonder they stopped hearing it. The\nsupports finally gave way while it was crossing the river and it fell in the\ndrink. It must have been decelerating when it happened, or it would have carried\nstraight across and all we’d see would be a big hole like a bomb-crater in the\nfar bank. Well, it was a great idea while it lasted.”\n“Mercy said there was another one,” Susannah reminded him.\n“Yeah. She also said she hadn’t heard it in seven or eight years, and Aunt\nTalitha said it was more like ten. What do you think, Jake . . . Jake? Earth to\nJake, Earth to Jake, come in, little buddy.”\nJake, who had been staring intently at the remains of the train in the river,\nonly shrugged.\n\n“You’re a big help, Jake,” Eddie said. “Valuable input—that’s why I love you.\nWhy we all love you.”\n\nJake paid no attention. He knew what he was seeing, and it wasn’t Blaine. The\nremains of the mono sticking out of the river were blue. In his dream, Blaine\nhad been the dusty, sugary pink of the bubblegum you got with baseball trading\ncards.\n\nRoland, meanwhile, had cinched the straps of Susannah’s carry-har-ness across\nhis chest. “Eddie, boost your lady into this contraption. It’s time we moved on\nand saw for ourselves.”\n\nJake now shifted his gaze, looking nervously toward the bridge loom-ing ahead.\nHe could hear a high, ghostly humming noise in the distance— the sound of the\nwind playing in the decayed steel hangers which con-nected the overhead cables\nto the concrete deck below.\n\n“Do you think it’ll be safe to cross?” Jake asked.\n“We’ll find out tomorrow,” Roland replied.\n\nTHE NEXT MORNING, ROLAND’S band of travellers stood at the end of the long,\nrusty bridge, gazing across at Lud. Eddie’s dreams of wise old elves who had\npreserved a working technology on which the pilgrims could draw were\ndisappearing. Now that they were this close, he could see holes in the\ncity-scape where whole blocks of buildings appeared to have been either burned\nor blasted. The skyline reminded him of a diseased jaw from which many teeth\nhave already fallen.\n\nIt was true that most of the buildings were still standing, but they had a\ndreary, disused look that filled Eddie with an uncharacteristic gloom, and the\nbridge between the travellers and that shuttered maze of steel and concrete\n\nlooked anything but solid and eternal. The vertical hangers on the left sagged\n\nslackly; the ones remaining on the right almost screamed with tension. The deck\nhad been constructed of hollow con-crete boxes shaped like trapezoids. Some of\nthese had buckled upward, displaying empty black interiors; others had slipped\naskew. Many of these latter had merely cracked, but others were badly broken,\nleaving gaps big enough to drop trucks—big trucks—into. In places where the\nbottoms of the box-sections as well as the tops had shattered, they could see\nthe muddy riverbank and the gray-green water of the Send beyond it. Eddie put\nthe distance between the deck and the water as three hundred feet at the center\nof the bridge. And that was probably a conservative estimate.\nEddie peered at the huge concrete caissons to which the main cables were\nanchored and thought the one on the right side of the bridge looked as if it had\nbeen pulled partway out of the earth. He decided he might do well not to mention\nthis fact to the others; it was bad enough that the bridge was swaying slowly\nbut perceptibly back and forth. Just looking at it made him feel seasick.\n“Well?” he asked Roland. “What do you think?”\nRoland pointed to the right side of the bridge. Here was a canted walkway about\nfive feet wide. It had been constructed atop a series of smaller concrete boxes\nand was, in effect, a separate deck. This seg-mented deck appeared to be\nsupported by an undercable—or perhaps it was a thick steel rod—anchored to the\nmain support cables by huge bow-clamps. Eddie inspected the closest one with the\navid interest of a man who may soon be entrusting his life to the object he is\nstudying. The bow-clamp appeared rusty but still sound. The words LaMERK FOUNDRY\nhad been stamped into its metal. Eddie was fascinated to realize he no longer\nknew if the words were in the High Speech or in English.\n“I think we can use that,” Roland said. “There’s only one bad place. Do you see\nit?”\n\n“Yeah—it’s land of hard to miss.”\n\nThe bridge, which had to be at least three quarters of a mile long, might not\nhave had any proper maintenance for over a thousand years, but Roland guessed\nthat the real destruction might have been going on for only the last fifty or\nso. As the hangers on the right snapped, the bridge had listed farther and\nfarther to the left. The greatest twist had occurred in the center of the\nbridge, between the two four-hundred-foot cable-towers. At the place where the\npressure of the twist was the great-est, a gaping, eye-shaped hole ran across\nthe deck. The break in the walkway was narrower, but even so, at least two\nadjoining concrete box-sections had fallen into the Send, leaving a gap at least\ntwenty or thirty feet wide. Where these boxes had been, they could clearly see\nthe rusty steel rod or cable which supported the walkway. They would have to use\nit to get across the gap.\n\n“I think we can cross,” Roland said, calmly pointing. “The gap is inconvenient,\nbut the side-rail is still there, so we’ll have something to hold onto.”\nEddie nodded, but he could feel his heart pounding hard. The exposed walkway\nsupport looked like a big pipe made of jointed steel, and was probably four feet\nacross at the top. In his mind’s eye he could see how they would have to edge\nacross, feet on the broad, slightly curved back of the support, hands clutching\nthe rail, while the bridge swayed slowly like a ship in a mild swell.\n“Jesus,” he said. He tried to spit, but nothing came out. His mouth was too dry.\n“You sure, Roland?”\n\n“So far as I can see, it’s the only way.” Roland pointed downriver and Eddie saw\na second bridge. This one had fallen into the Send long ago. The remains stuck\nout of the water in a rusted tangle of ancient steel.\n“What about you, Jake?” Susannah asked.\n\n“Hey, no problem,” Jake said at once. He was actually smiling.\n“I hate you, kid,” Eddie said.\n\nRoland was looking at Eddie with some concern. “If you feel you can’t do it, say\nso now. Don’t get halfway across and then freeze up.”\nEddie looked along the twisted surface of the bridge for a long time, then\nnodded. “I guess I can handle it. Heights have never been my favorite thing, but\n\nI’ll manage.”\n\n“Good.” Roland surveyed them. “Soonest begun, soonest done. I’ll go first, with\nSusannah. Then Jake, and Eddie’s drogue. Can you handle the wheelchair?”\n“Hey, no problem,” Eddie said giddily.\n\n“Let’s go, then.”\n\nAs SOON AS HE stepped onto the walkway, fear filled up Eddie’s hollow places\nlike cold water and he began to wonder if he hadn’t made a very dangerous\nmistake. From solid ground, the bridge seemed to be swaying only a little, but\nonce he was actually on it, he felt as if he were standing on the pendulum of\nthe world’s biggest grandfather clock. The movement was very slow, but it was\nregular, and the length of the swings was much longer than he had anticipated.\nThe walkway’s surface was badly cracked and canted at least ten degrees to the\nleft. His feet gritted in loose piles of powdery concrete, and the low squealing\nsound of the box-segments grinding together was constant. Beyond the bridge, the\ncity skyline tilted slowly back and forth like the artificial horizon of the\nworld’s slowest-moving video game.\n\nOverhead, the wind hummed constantly in the taut hangers. Below, the ground fell\naway sharply to the muddy northwest bank of the river. He was thirty feet up …\nthen sixty . . . then a hundred and ten. Soon he would be over the water. The\nwheelchair banged against his left leg with every step.\nSomething furry brushed between his feet and he clutched madly for the rusty\nhandrail with his right hand, barely holding in a scream. Oy went trotting past\nhim with a brief upward glance, as if to say Excuse me—-just passing.\n“Fucking dumb animal,” Eddie said through gritted teeth.\nHe discovered that, although he didn’t like looking down, he had an even greater\naversion to looking at the hangers which were still managing to hold the deck\nand the overhead cables together. They were sleeved with rust and Eddie could\nsee snarls of metal thread poking out of most—these snarls looked like metallic\npuffs of cotton. He knew from his Uncle Reg, who had worked on both the George\nWashington and Triborough bridges as a painter, that the hangers and overhead\ncables were “spun” from thousands of steel threads. On this bridge, the spin was\nfinally letting go. The hangers were quite literally becoming unravelled, and as\nthey did, the threads were snapping, one interwoven strand at a time.\nIt’s held this long, it’ll hold a little longer. You think this thing’s going to\nfall into the river just because you’re crossing it? Don’t flatter yourself.\nHe wasn’t comforted, however. For all Eddie knew, they might be the first people\nto attempt the crossing in decades. And the bridge, after all, would have to\ncollapse sometime, and from the look of things, it was going to be soon. Their\ncombined weight might be the straw that broke the camel’s back.\nHis moccasin struck a chunk of concrete and Eddie watched, sick-ened but\nhelpless to look away, as the chunk fell down and down and down, turning over as\nit went. There was a small—very small—splash when it hit the river. The\nfreshening wind gusted and stuck his shirt against his sweaty skin. The bridge\ngroaned and swayed. Eddie tried to remove his hands from the side-rail, but they\nseemed frozen to the pitted metal in a deathgrip.\nHe closed his eyes for a moment. You’re not going to freeze. You’re not. I … I\nforbid it. If you need something to look at, make it long tall and ugly. Eddie\nopened his eyes again, fixed them on the gunslinger, forced his hands to open,\nand began to move forward again.\n\nROLAND REACHED THE GAP and looked back. Jake was five feet behind him. Oy was at\nhis heels. The bumbler was crouched down with his neck stretched forward. The\nwind was much stronger over the river-cut, and Roland could see it rippling Oy’s\nsilky fur. Eddie was about twenty-five feet behind Jake. His face was tightly\n\ndrawn, but he was still shuffling grimly along with Susannah’s collapsed\n\nwheelchair in his left hand. His right was clutching the rail like grim death.\n“Susannah?”\n\n“Yes,” she responded at once. “Fine.”\n\n“Jake?”\n\nJake looked up. He was still grinning, and the gunslinger saw there was going to\nbe no problem there. The boy was having the time of his life. His hair blew back\nfrom his finely made brow in waves, and his eyes sparkled. He jerked one thumb\nup. Roland smiled and returned the gesture.\n“Eddie?”\n\n“Don’t worry about me.”\n\nEddie appeared to be looking at Roland, but the gunslinger decided he was really\nlooking past him, at the windowless brick buildings which crowded the riverbank\nat the far end of the bridge. That was all right; given his obvious fear of\nheights, it was probably the best thing he could do to keep his head.\n“All right, I won’t,” Roland murmured. “We’re going to cross the hole now,\nSusannah. Sit easy. No quick movements. Understand?”\n“Yes.”\n\n“If you want to adjust your position, do it now.”\n“I’m fine, Roland,” she said calmly. “I just hope Eddie will be all right.”\n“Eddie’s a gunslinger now. He’ll behave like one.”\nRoland turned to the right, so he was facing directly downriver, and grasped the\nhandrail. Then he began to edge out across the hole, shuffling his boots along\nthe rusty cable.\n\nJAKE WAITED UNTIL ROLAND and Susannah were part of the way across the gap and\nthen started himself. The wind gusted and the bridge swayed back and forth, but\nhe felt no alarm at all. He was, in fact, totally buzzed. Unlike Eddie, he’d\nnever had any fear of heights; he liked being up here where he could see the\nriver spread out like a steel ribbon under a sky which was beginning to cloud\nover.\n\nHalfway across the hole in the bridge (Roland and Susannah had reached the place\nwhere the uneven walkway resumed and were watching the others), Jake looked back\nand his heart sank. They had forgotten one member of the party when they were\ndiscussing how to cross. Oy was crouched, frozen and clearly terrified, on the\nfar side of the hole in the walkway. He was sniffing at the place where the\nconcrete ended and the rusty, curved support took over.\n“Come on, Oy!” Jake called.\n\n“Oy!” the bumbler called back, and the tremble in his hoarse voice was almost\nhuman. He stretched his long neck forward toward Jake but didn’t move. His\ngold-ringed eyes were huge and dismayed.\nAnother gust of wind struck the bridge, making it sway and squall. Something\ntwanged beside Jake’s head—the sound of a guitar string which has been tightened\nuntil it snaps. A steel thread had popped out of the nearest vertical hanger,\nalmost scratching his cheek. Ten feet away, Oy crouched miserably with his eyes\nfixed on Jake.\n\n“Come on!” Roland shouted. “Wind’s freshening! Come on, Jake!”\n“Not without Oy!”\n\nJake began to shuffle back the way he had come. Before he had gone more than two\nsteps, Oy stepped gingerly onto the support rod. The claws at the ends of his\nstiffly braced legs scratched at the rounded metal surface. Eddie stood behind\nthe bumbler now, feeling helpless and scared to death.\n“That’s it, Oy!” Jake encouraged. “Come to me!”\n“Oy-Oy! Ake-Ake!” the bumbler cried, and trotted rapidly along the rod. He had\nalmost reached Jake when the traitorous wind gusted again. The bridge swung.\nOy’s claws scratched madly at the support rod for purchase, but there was none.\nHis hindquarters slued off the edge and into space. He tried to cling with his\n\nforepaws, but there was nothing to cling to. His rear legs ran wildly in midair.\n\nJake let go of the rail and dived for him, aware of nothing but Oy’s gold-ringed\neyes.\n\n“No, Jake!” Roland and Eddie bellowed together, each from his own side of the\ngap, each too far away to do anything but watch.\nJake hit the cable on his chest and belly. His pack bounced against his\nshoulderblades and he heard his teeth click together in his head with the sound\nof a cueball breaking a tight rack. The wind gusted again. He went with it,\nlooping his right hand around the support rod and reaching for Oy with his left\nas he swayed out into space. The bumbler began to fall, and clamped his jaws on\nJake’s reaching hand as he did. The pain was immediate and excruciating. Jake\nscreamed but held on, head down, right arm clasping the rod, knees pressing hard\nagainst its wretchedly smooth surface. Oy dangled from his left hand like a\ncircus acrobat, staring up with his gold-ringed eyes, and Jake could now see his\nown blood flowing along the sides of the bumbler’s head in thin streams.\nThen the wind gusted again and Jake began to slip outward.\n\nEDDIE’S FEAR LEFT HIM in its place came that strange yet welcome coldness. He\ndropped Susannah’s wheelchair to the cracked cement with a clatter and raced\nnimbly out along the support cable, not even both-ering with the handrail. Jake\nhung head-down over the gap with Oy swinging at the end of his left hand like a\nfurry pendulum. And the boy’s right hand was slipping.\nEddie opened his legs and seat-dropped to a sitting position. His undefended\nballs smashed painfully up into his crotch, but for the moment even this\nexquisite pain was news from a distant country. He seized Jake by the hair with\none hand and one strap of his pack with the other. He felt himself beginning to\ntilt outward, and for a nightmarish moment he thought all three of them were\ngoing to go over in a daisy-chain.\n\nHe let go of Jake’s hair and tightened his grip on the packstrap, praying the\nlad hadn’t bought the pack at one of the cheap discount outlets. He flailed\nabove his head for the handrail with his free hand. After an interminable moment\nin which their combined outward slide continued, he found it and seized it.\n“ROLAND!” he bawled. “I COULD USE A LITTLE HELP HERE!”\nBut Roland was already there, with Susannah still perched on his back. When he\nbent, she locked her arms around his neck so she wouldn’t drop headfirst from\nthe sling. The gunslinger wrapped an arm around Jake’s chest and pulled him up.\nWhen his feet were on the support rod again, Jake put his right arm around Oy’s\ntrembling body. His left hand was an agony of fire and ice.\n“Let go, Oy,” he gasped. “You can let go now we’re—safe.”\nFor a terrible moment he didn’t think the billy-bumbler would. Then, slowly,\nOy’s jaws relaxed and Jake was able to pull his hand free. It was covered with\nblood and dotted with a ring of dark holes.\n“Oy,” the bumbler said feebly, and Eddie saw with wonder that the animal’s\nstrange eyes were full of tears. He stretched his neck and licked Jake’s face\nwith his bloody tongue.\n\n“That’s okay,” Jake said, pressing his face into the warm fur. He was crying\nhimself, his face a mask of shock and pain. “Don’t worry, that’s okay. You\ncouldn’t help it and I don’t mind.”\n\nEddie was getting slowly to his feet. His face was dirty gray, and he felt as if\nsomeone had driven a bowling ball into his guts. His left hand stole slowly to\nhis crotch and investigated the damage there.\n“Cheap fucking vasectomy,” he said hoarsely.\n“Are you going to faint, Eddie?” Roland asked. A fresh gust of wind flipped his\nhat from his head and into Susannah’s face. She grabbed it and jammed it down\nall the way to his ears, giving Roland the look of a half-crazed hillbilly.\n“No,” Eddie said. “I almost wish I could, but—”\n“Take a look at Jake,” Susannah said. “He’s really bleeding.”\n\n“I’m fine,” Jake said, and tried to hide his hand. Roland took it gently in his\n\nown hands before he could. Jake had sustained at least a dozen puncture-wounds\nin the back of his hand, his palm, and his fingers.\nMost of them were deep. It would be impossible to tell if bones had been broken\nor tendons severed until Jake tried to flex the hand, and this wasn’t the time\nor place for such experiments.\n\nRoland looked at Oy. The billy-bumbler looked back, his expressive eyes sad and\nfrightened. He had made no effort to lick Jake’s blood from his chops, although\nit would have been the most natural thing in the world for him to have done so.\n“Leave him alone,” Jake said, and wrapped the encircling arm more tightly about\nOy’s body. “It wasn’t his fault. It was my fault for forgetting him. The wind\nblew him off.”\n\n“I’m not going to hurt him,” Roland said. He was positive the billy-bumbler\nwasn’t rabid, but he still did not intend for Oy to taste any more of Jake’s\nblood than he already had. As for any other diseases Oy might be carrying in his\nblood . . . well, ka would decide, as, in the end, it always did. Roland pulled\nhis neckerchief free and wiped Oy’s lips and muzzle. “There,” he said. “Good\nfellow. Good boy.”\n\n“Oy,” the billy-bumbler said feebly, and Susannah, who was watch-ing over\nRoland’s shoulder, could have sworn she heard gratitude in that voice.\nAnother gust of wind struck them. The weather was turning dirty, and fast.\n“Eddie, we have to get off the bridge. Can you walk?”\n“No, massa; I’sa gwinter shuffle.” The pain in his groin and the pit of his\nstomach was still bad, but not quite so bad as it had been a minute ago.\n“All right. Let’s move. Fast as we can.”\nRoland turned, began to take a step, and stopped. He might have been thirty, forty, or sixty. He held one hand high\nover his head. In it was something Roland could not make out, except that its\nshape was too regular to be a stone.
15Part 14Darktower 3 - The Waste Lands\n\nBehind this apparition, the city loomed with a kind of weird clarity in the\ndarkening day. As Eddie looked past the huddles of brick buildings on the other\nshore—warehouses long since scooped empty by looters, he had no doubt—and into\nthose shadowy canyons and stone mazes, he understood for the first time how\nterribly mistaken, how terribly foolish, his dreams of hope and help had been.\nNow he saw the shattered facades and broken roofs; now he saw the shaggy birds’\nnests on cornices and in glassless, gaping windows; now hr allowed himself to\nactually smell the city, and that odor was not of fabulous spices and savory\nfoods of the sort his mother had sometimes brought home from Zabar’s but rather\nthe stink of a mattress that has caught fire, smoldered awhile, and then been\nput out with sewer-water. He suddenly understood Lud, understood it completely.\nThe grinning pirate who had appeared while their attention was elsewhere was\nprobably as close to a wise old elf as this broken, dying place could provide.\nRoland pulled his revolver.\n\n“Put it away, my cully,” the man in the yellow scarf said in an accent so thick\nthat the sense of his words was almost lost. “Put it away, my dear heart. Ye’re\na fierce trim, ay, that’s clear, but this time you’re outmatched.”\n\nTHE NEWCOMER’S PANTS WERE patched green velvet, and as he stood on the edge of\nthe hole in the bridge, he looked like a buccaneer at the end of his days of\nplunder: sick, ragged, and still dangerous.\n“Suppose I choose not to?” Roland asked. “Suppose I choose to simply put a\n\nbullet through your scrofulous head?”\n\n“Then I’ll get to hell just enough ahead of ye to hold the door,” the man in the\nyellow scarf said, and chuckled chummily. He wiggled the hand he held in the\nair. “It’s all the same jolly fakement to me, one way or t’other.”\nRoland guessed that was the truth. The man looked as if he might have a year to\nlive at most . . . and the last few months of that year would probably be very\nunpleasant. The oozing sores on his face had nothing to do with radiation;\nunless Roland was badly deceived, this man was in the late stages of what the\ndoctors called mandrus and everyone else called whore’s blossoms. Facing a\ndangerous man was always a bad busi-ness, but at least one could calculate the\nodds in such an encounter. When you were facing the dead, however, everything\nchanged.\n\n“Do yer know what I’ve got here, my dear ones?” the pirate asked. “Do yer ken\nwhatcher old friend Gasher just happens to have laid his hands on? All humor left it, as if a switch had been turned some-where in his\ndegenerating brains.\n\n“My finger is all that’s holdin the pin now, dearie. The young buck stand-ing behind you and\npointing that toy pistol in my face might live, but only until he hits the water\n. . . and hit it he would, because this bridge has been hangin by a thread these\nlast forty year, and all it’d take to finish it is one little push.
