For the Gasherman loves\nhis treaties, so he does!”\n\n“There’s nothing in there but-“\n\nGasher’s hand flashed out and rocked Jake’s head back with a hard slap that sent\na fresh spray of bloody froth flying from the boy’s nose.\n“What did you do that for?” Jake cried, hurt and outraged.\n“For tellin me what my own beshitted eyes can see!” Gasher yelled, and cast\nJake’s pack aside. He bared his remaining teeth at the boy in a dangerous,\nterrible grin. “And fer almost bringin the whole beshitted works down on us!” He\npaused, then added in a quieter voice: “And because I felt like it—I must admit\nthat. Your stupid sheep’s face puts me wery much in a slappin temper, so it\ndoes.” The grin widened, reveal-ing his oozing whitish gums, a sight Jake could\nhave done without. “If your hardcase friend follows us this far, he’ll have a\nsurprise when he runs into those wires, won’t he?” Gasher looked up, still\ngrinning. “There’s a city bus balanced up there someplace, as I remember.”\nJake began to weep—tired, hopeless tears that cut through the dirt on his cheeks\nin narrow channels.\n\nGasher raised an open, threatening hand. “Get moving, cully, before I start\ncryin myself … for a wery sentermental fellow is yer old pal, so he is, and\nwhen he starts to grieve and mourn, a little slappin is the only thing to put a\nsmile on his face again. Run!”\n\nThey ran. Gasher chose pathways leading deeper into the smelly, creaking maze\nseemingly at random, indicating his choices with hard whacks to the shoulders.\nAt some point the sound of the drums began. It seemed to come from everywhere\nand nowhere, and for Jake it was the final straw. He gave up hope and thought\nalike, and allowed himself to descend wholly into the nightmare.\n\nROLAND HALTED IN FRONT of the barricade which choked the street from side to\nside and top to bottom. Unlike Jake, he had no hopes of emerging into the open\non the other side. The buildings lying east of this point would be\nsentry-occupied islands emerging from an inland sea of trash, tools, artifacts .\n. . and booby-traps, he had no doubt. Some of these leavings undoubtedly still\nremained where they had fallen five hun-dred or seven hundred or a thousand\nyears ago, but Roland thought most of it had been dragged here by the Grays a\npiece at a time. The eastern portion of Lud had become, in effect, the castle of\nthe Grays, and Roland was now standing outside its wall.\nHe walked forward slowly and saw the mouth of a passageway half-hidden behind a\nragged cement boulder. There were footprints in the powdery dust—two sets, one\nbig, one small. Roland started to get up, looked again, and squatted on his\nhunkers once more. Not two sets but three, the third marking the paws of a small\nanimal.\n\n“Oy?” Roland called softly. For a moment there was no response, and then a\nsingle soft bark came from the shadows. Roland stepped into the passageway and\nsaw gold-ringed eyes peering at him from around the first crooked corner. Roland\ntrotted down to the humbler. Oy, who still didn’t like to come really close to\n\nanyone but Jake, backed up a step and then held his ground, looking anxiously up\n\nat the gunslinger.\n\n“Do you want to help me?” Roland asked. He could feel the dry red curtain that\nwas battle fever at the edge of his consciousness, but this was not the time for\nit. The time would come, but for now he must not allow himself that\ninexpressible relief. “Help me find Jake?”\n“Ake!” Oy barked, still watching Roland with his anxious eyes.\n“Go on, then. Find him.”\n\nOy turned away at once and ran rapidly down the alley, nose skim-ming the\nground. Roland followed, his eyes only occasionally flicking up to glance at Oy.\nMostly he kept his gaze fixed on the ancient pavement, looking for sign.\n\n“JESUS,” EDDIE SAID. “WHAT land of people are these guys?”\nThey had followed the avenue at the base of the ramp for a couple of blocks, had\nseen the barricade (missing Roland’s entry into the partially hidden passageway\nby less than a minute) which lay ahead, and had turned north onto a broad\nthoroughfare which reminded Eddie of Fifth Avenue. He hadn’t dared to tell\nSusannah that; he was still too bitterly disappointed with this stinking,\nlittered ruin of a city to articulate anything hopeful.\n“Fifth Avenue” led them into an area of large white stone buildings that\nreminded Eddie of the way Rome looked in the gladiator movies he’d watched on TV\nas a kid. They were austere and, for the most part, still in good shape. He was\npretty sure they had been public buildings of some sort—galleries, libraries,\nmaybe museums. One, with a big domed roof that had cracked like a granite egg,\nmight have been an observatory, although Eddie had read someplace that\nastronomers liked to be away from big cities, because all the electric lights\nfucked up their star-gazing.\n\nThere were open areas between these imposing edifices, and although the grass\nand flowers which had once grown there had been choked off by weeds and tangles\nof underbrush, the area still had a stately feel, and Eddie wondered if it had\nonce been the center of Lud’s cultural life. Those days were long gone, of\ncourse; Eddie doubted if Gasher and his pals were very interested in ballet or\nchamber music.\n\nHe and Susannah had come to a major intersection from which four more broad\navenues radiated outward like spokes on a wheel. At the hub of the wheel was a\nlarge paved square. Ringing it were loudspeakers on forty-foot steel posts. In\nthe center of the square was a pedestal with the remains of a statue upon it—a\nmighty copper war-horse, green with verdigris, pawing its forelegs at the air.\nThe warrior who had once ridden this charger lay off to the side on one corroded\nshoulder, waving what looked like a machine-gun in one hand and a sword in the\nother. His legs were still bowed around the shape of the horse he had once\nridden, hut his boots remained welded to the sides of his metal mount. GRAYS\nDIE! was written across the pedestal in fading orange letters.\nGlancing down the radiating streets, Eddie saw more of the speaker-poles. A few\nhad fallen over, but most still stood, and each of these had been festooned with\na grisly garland of corpses. As a result, the square into which “Fifth Avenue”\nemptied and the streets which led away were guarded by a small army of the dead.\n“What kind of people are they?” Eddie asked again.\nHe didn’t expect an answer and Susannah didn’t give one . . . but she could\nhave. She’d had insights into the past of Roland’s world before, but never one\nas clear and sure as this. All of her earlier insights, like those which had\ncome to her in River Crossing, had had a haunting visionary quality, like\ndreams, but what came now arrived in a single flash, and it was like seeing the\ntwisted face of a dangerous maniac illuminated by a stroke of lightning.\nThe speakers . . . the hanging bodies . . . the drums. She suddenly understood\nhow they went together as clearly as she had understood that the heavy-laden\nwagons passing through River Crossing on their way to Jimtown had been pulled by\n\noxen rather than mules or horses.\n\n“Never mind this trash,” she said, and her voice only quivered a little. “It’s\nthe train we want—which way is it, d’you think?”\nEddie glanced up at the darkening sky and easily picked out the path of the Beam\nin the rushing clouds. He looked back down and wasn’t much surprised to see that\nthe entrance to the street corresponding most closely to the path of the Beam\nwas guarded by a large stone turtle. Its reptilian head peered out from beneath\nthe granite lip of its shell; its deepset eyes seemed to stare curiously at\nthem. Eddie nodded toward it and managed a small dry smile. “See the turtle of\nenormous girth?”\n\nSusannah took a brief look of her own and nodded. He pushed her across the city\nsquare and into The Street of the Turtle. The corpses which lined it gave off a\ndry, cinnamony smell that made Eddie’s stomach clench . . . not because it was\nbad but because it was actually rather pleasant—the sugar-spicy aroma of\nsomething a kid would enjoy shaking onto his morning toast.\nThe Street of the Turtle was mercifully broad, and most of the corpses hanging\nfrom the speaker-poles were little more than mummies, but Susannah saw a few\nwhich were relatively fresh, with flies still crawl-ing busily across the\nblackening skin of their swollen faces and maggots still squirming out of their\ndecaying eyes.\n\nAnd below each speaker was a little drift of bones.\n“There must be thousands,” Eddie said. “Men, women, and kids.”\n“Yes.” Susannah’s calm voice sounded distant and strange to her own ears.\n“They’ve had a lot of time to kill. And they’ve used it to kill each other.”\n“Bring on those wise fuckin elves!” Eddie said, and the laugh that followed\nsounded suspiciously like a sob. He thought he was at last begin-ning to fully\nunderstand what that innocuous phrase—the world has moved on—really meant. What\na breadth of ignorance and evil it covered.\nAnd what a depth.\n\nThe speakers were a wartime measure, Susannah thought. Of course they were. God\nonly knows which war, or how long ago, but it must have been a doozy. The rulers\nof Lud used the speakers to make city-wide announcements from some central,\nbomb-proof location—a bunker like the one Hitler and his high command retreated\nto at the end of World War II.\n\nAnd in her ears she could hear the voice of authority which had come rolling out\nof those speakers—could hear it as clearly as she had heard the creak of the\nwagons passing through River Crossing, as clearly as she had heard the crack of\nthe whip above the backs of the straining oxen.\nRation centers A and D will be closed today; please proceed to cen-ters B, C, E,\nand F with proper coupons.\n\nMilitia squads Nine, Ten, and Twelve report to Sendside.\nAerial bombardment is likely between the hours of eight and ten of the clock.\nAll noncombatant residents should report to their designated shelters. Bring\nyour gas masks. Repeat, bring your gas masks.\nAnnouncements, yes . . . and some garbled version of the news—a propagandized,\nmilitant version George Orwell would have called double-speak. And in between\nthe news bulletins and the announcements, squall-ing military music and\nexhortations to respect the fallen by sending more men and women into the red\nthroat of the abattoir.\n\nThen the war had ended and silence had fallen … for a while. But at some\npoint, the speakers had begun broadcasting again. How long ago? A hundred years?\nFifty? Did it matter? Susannah thought not. What mattered was that when the\nspeakers were reactivated, the only thing they broadcast was a single tape-loop\n. . . the loop with the drum-track on it. And the descendents of the city’s\noriginal residents had taken it for … what? The Voice of the Turtle? The Will\nof the Beam?\n\nSusannah found herself remembering the time she had asked her father, a quiet\nhut deeply cynical man, if he believed there was a God in heaven who guided the\ncourse of human events. Well, he had said, I think it’s sort of half ‘n half,\n\nOdetta. I’m sure there’s a God, but I don’t think He has much if anything to do\n\nwith us these days; I believe that after we killed His son, He finally got it\nthrough His head that there wasn’t nothing to be done with the sons of Adam or\nthe daughters of Eve, and He washed His hands of us. Wise fella.\nShe had responded to this (which she had fully expected; she was eleven at the\ntime, and knew the turn of her father’s mind quite well) by showing him a squib\non the Community Churches page of the local newspaper. It said that Rev. Murdock\nof the Grace Methodist Church would that Sunday elucidate on the topic “God\nSpeaks to Each of Us Every Day”—with a text from First Corinthians. Her father\nhad laughed over that so hard that tears had squirted from the corners of his\neyes. Well, I guess each of us hears someone talking, he had said at last, and\nyou can bet your bottom dollar on one thing, sweetie: each of us—includ-ing this\nhere Reverend Murdock—hears that voice say just exactly what he wants to hear.\nIt’s so convenient that way.\n\nWhat these people had apparently wanted to hear in the recorded drum-track was\nan invitation to commit ritual murder. And now, when the drums began to throb\nthrough these hundreds or thousands of speak-ers—a hammering back-beat which was\nonly the percussion to a Z.Z. Top song called “Velcro Fly,” if Eddie was\nright—it became their signal to unlimber the hangropes and run a few folks up\nthe nearest speaker-posts.\n\nHow many? she wondered as Eddie rolled her along in her wheel-chair, its nicked\nand dented hard rubber tires crackling over broken glass and whispering through\ndrifts of discarded paper. How many have been killed over the years because some\nelectronic circuit under the city got the hiccups? Did it start because they\nrecognized the essential alienness of the music, which came somehow—like us, and\nthe airplane, and some of the cars along these streets—-from another world?\nShe didn’t know, but she knew she had come around to her father’s cynical point\nof view on the subject of God and the chats He might or might not have with the\nsons of Adam and the daughters of Eve. These people had been looking for a\nreason to slaughter each other, that was all, and the drums had been as good a\nreason as any.\n\nShe found herself thinking of the hive they had found—the mis-shapen hive of\nwhite bees whose honey would have poisoned them if they had been foolish enough\nto eat of it. Here, on this side of the Send, was another dying hive; more\nmutated white bees whose sting would be no less deadly for their confusion,\nloss, and perplexity.\n\nAnd how many more will have to die before the tape finally breaks?\nAs if her thoughts had caused it to happen, the speakers suddenly began to\ntransmit the relentless, syncopated heartbeat of the drums. Eddie yelled in\nsurprise. Susannah screamed and clapped both hands to her ears—but before she\ndid, she could faintly hear the rest of the music: the track or tracks which had\nbeen muted decades ago when someone (probably quite by accident) had bumped the\nbalance control, knocking it all the way to one side and burying both the\nguitars and the vocal.\n\nEddie continued to push her along The Street of the Turtle and the Path of the\nBeam, trying to look in all directions at once and trying not to smell the odor\nof putrefaction. Thank God for the wind, he thought.\nHe began to push the wheelchair faster, scanning the weedy gaps between the big\nwhite buildings for the graceful sweep of an overhead monorail track. He wanted\nto get out of this endless aisle of the dead. As he took yet another deep breath\nof that speciously sweet cinnamon smell, it seemed to him that he had never\nwanted anything so badly in his whole life.\n\nJAKE’S DAZE WAS BROKEN abruptly when Gasher grabbed him by the collar and yanked\nwith all the force of a cruel rider braking a galloping horse. He stuck one leg\n\nout at the same time and Jake went crashing backward over it. His head connected\n\nwith the pavement and for a moment all the lights went out. Gasher, no\nhumanitarian, brought him around quickly by seizing Jake’s lower lip and yanking\nit upward and outward.\n\nJake screamed and bolted to a sitting position, striking out blindly with his\nfists. Gasher dodged the blows easily, hooked his other hand into Jake’s armpit,\nand yanked him to his feet. Jake stood there, rocking drunkenly back and forth.\nHe was beyond protest now; almost beyond understanding. All he knew for sure was\nthat every muscle in his body felt sprung and his wounded hand was howling like\nan animal caught in a trap.\n\nGasher apparently needed a breather, and this time he was slower getting his\nwind back. He stood bent over with his hands planted on the knees of his green\ntrousers, panting in fast little whistling breaths. His yellow headscarf had\nslipped askew. His good eye glittered like a trum-pery diamond. The white silk\neyepatch was now wrinkled, and curds of evil-looking yellow muck oozed onto his\ncheek from beneath it.\n\n“Take a look over your head, cully, and you’ll see why I brung you up short. Get\nan eyeful!”\n\nJake tilted his head upward, and in the depths of his shock he was not at all\nsurprised to see a marble fountain as big as a house-trailer dangling eighty\nfeet above them. He and Gasher were almost below it. The fountain was held\nsuspended by two rusty cables which were mostly hidden within huge, unsteady\nstacks of church pews. Even in his less-than-acute state, Jake saw that these\ncables were more seriously frayed than the remaining hangers on the bridge had\nbeen.\n\n“See it?” Gasher asked, grinning. He raised his left hand to his covered eye,\nscooped a mass of the pussy material from beneath it, and flicked it\nindifferently aside. “Beauty, ain’t it? Oh, the Tick-Tock Man’s a trig cove, all\nright, and no mistake. (Where’s those goat-fucking drums? They should have\nstarted by now—if Copperhead’s forgot em, I’ll ram a stick so far up his arse\nhe’ll taste bark.) Now look ahead of you, my delicious little squint.”\nJake did, and Gasher immediately clouted him so hard that he stag-gered backward\nand almost fell.\n\n“Not across, idiot child! Down! See them two dark cobblestones?”\nAfter a moment, Jake did. He nodded apathetically.\n“Yer don’t wanter step on em, for that’d bring the whole works down on your\nhead, cully, and anybody who wanted yer after that’d have to pick yer up with a\nblotter. Understand?”\n\nJake nodded again.\n\n“Good.” Gasher took a final deep breath and slapped Jake’s shoulder. “Go on,\nthen, whatcher waitin for? Hup!”\n\nJake stepped over the first of the discolored stones and saw it wasn’t really a\ncobblestone at all but a metal plate which had been rounded to look like one.\nThe second was just ahead of it, cunningly placed so that if an unaware intruder\nhappened to miss the first one, he or she would almost certainly step on the\nsecond.\n\nGo ahead and do it, then, he thought. Why not? The gunslinger’s never going to\nfind you in this maze, so go ahead and bring it down. It’s got to be cleaner\nthan what Gasher and his friends have got planned for you. Quicker, too.\nHis dusty moccasin wavered in the air above the booby-trap.\nGasher hit him with a fist in the middle of the back, but not hard. “Thinkin\nabout takin a ride on the handsome, are you, my little cull?” he asked. The\nlaughing cruelty in his voice had been replaced by simple curiosity. If it was\ntinged with any other emotion, it wasn’t fear but amusement. “Well, go ahead, if\nit’s what yer mean to do, for I have my ticket already. Only be quick about it,\ngods blast your eyes.”\n\nJake’s foot came down beyond the trigger of the booby-trap. His decision to live\na little longer was not based on any hope that Roland would find him; it was\njust that this was what Roland would do—go on until someone made him stop, and\n\nthen a few yards farther still if he could.\n\nIf he did it now, he could take Gasher with him, but Gasher alone wasn’t\nsufficient—one look was enough to make it clear that he was telling the truth\nwhen he said he was dying already. If he went on, he might have a chance to take\nsome of the Gasherman’s friends, too— maybe even the one he called the Tick-Tock\nMan.\n\nIf I’m going to ride what he calls the handsome, Jake thought, I’d just as soon\ngo with plenty of company.\n\nRoland would have understood.\n\nJAKE WAS WRONG IN his assessment of the gunslinger’s ability to follow their\npath through the maze; Jake’s pack was only the most obvious bit of sign they\nleft behind them, but Roland quickly realized he did not have to pause to look\nfor sign. He only had to follow Oy.\n\nHe paused at several intersecting passages nevertheless, wanting to make sure,\nand each time he did, Oy looked back and uttered his low, impatient bark that\nseemed to say, Hurry up! Do you want to lose them? After the signs he saw—a\ntrack, a thread from Jake’s shirt, a scrap of bright yellow cloth from Gasher’s\nscarf—had three times confirmed the bumbler’s choices, Roland simply followed\nOy. He did not give up look-ing for sign, but he quit making stops to hunt for\nit. Then the drums started up, and it was the drums—plus Gasher’s nosiness about\nwhat Jake might be carrying—that saved Roland’s life that afternoon.\nHe skidded to a halt in his dusty boots, and his gun was in his hand before he\nrealized what the sound was. When he did realize, he dropped the revolver back\ninto its holster with an impatient grunt. He was about to go on again when his\neye happened first on Jake’s pack . . . and then on a pair of faint, gleaming\nstreaks in midair just to the left of it. Roland narrowed his eyes and made out\ntwo thin wires which crisscrossed at knee level not three feet in front of him.\nOy, who was built low to the ground, had scurried neatly through the inverted V\nformed by the wires, but if not for the drums and spotting Jake’s castoff pack,\nRoland would have run right into them. As his eyes moved upward, tracing the\nnot-quite-random piles of junk poised on either side of the passageway at this\npoint, Roland’s mouth tightened. It had been a close call, and only ka had saved\nhim.\n\nOy barked impatiently.\n\nRoland dropped to his belly and crawled beneath the wires, moving slowly and\ncarefully—he was bigger than either Jake or Gasher, and he realized a really big\nman wouldn’t be able to get under here at all without triggering the carefully\nprepared avalanche. The drums pumped and thumped in his ears. I wonder if\nthey’ve all gone mad, he thought. If I had to listen to that every day, I think\nI would have.\n\nHe got to the far side of the wires, picked up the pack, and looked inside.\nJake’s books and a few items of clothing were still in there, so were the\ntreasures he had picked up along the way—a rock which gleamed with yellow flecks\nthat looked like gold but weren’t; an arrow-head, probably the leaving of the\nold forest folk, which Jake had found in a grove of trees the day after his\ndrawing; some coins from his own world; his father’s sunglasses; a few other\nthings which only a boy not yet in his teens could really love and understand.\nThings he would want back again … if, that was, Roland got to him before\nGasher and his friends could change him, hurt him in ways that would cause him\nto lose interest in the innocent pursuits and curiosities of pre-adolescent\nboyhood.\n\nGasher’s grinning face swam into Roland’s mind like the face of a demon or a\ndjinni from a bottle: the snaggle teeth, the vacant eyes, the mandrus crawling\nover the cheeks and spreading beneath the stubbly lines of the jaws. If you hurt\nhim … he thought, and then forced his mind away, because that line of thought\nwas a blind alley. If Gasher hurt the boy (Jake! his mind insisted fiercely—Not\n\njust the boy but Jake! Jake!), Roland would kill him, yes. But the act would\n\nmean nothing, for Gasher was a dead man already.\nThe gunslinger lengthened the straps of the pack, marvelling at the clever\nbuckles which made this possible, slipped it onto his own back, and stood up\nagain. Oy turned to be off, but Roland called his name and the bumbler looked\nback.\n\n“To me, Oy.” Roland didn’t know if the bumbler could understand (or if he would\nobey even if he did), but it would be better—safer—if he stayed close. Where\nthere was one booby-trap, there were apt to be more. Next time Oy might not be\nso lucky.\n\n“Ake!” Oy barked, not moving. The bark was assertive, but Roland thought he saw\nmore of the truth about how Oy felt in his eyes: they were dark with fear.\n“Yes, but it’s dangerous,” Roland said. “To me, Oy.”\nBack the way they had come, there was a thud as something heavy fell, probably\ndislodged by the punishing vibration of the drums. Roland could now see\nspeaker-poles here and there, poking out of the wreckage like strange\nlong-necked animals.\n\nOy trotted back to him and looked up, panting.\n“Stay close.”\n\n“Ake! Ake-Ake!”\n\n“Yes. Jake.” He began to run again, and Oy ran beside him, heeling as neatly as\nany dog Roland had ever seen.\n\nFOR EDDIE, IT WAS, as some wise man had once said, deja vu all over again: he\nwas running with the wheelchair, racing time. The beach had been replaced by The\nStreet of the Turtle, but somehow everything else was the same. Oh, there was\none other relevant difference: now it was a railway station (or a cradle) he was\nlooking for, not a free-standing door.\n\nSusannah was sitting bolt upright with her hair blowing out behind her and\nRoland’s revolver in her right hand, its barrel pointed up at the cloudy,\ntroubled sky. The drums thudded and pounded, bludgeoning them with sound. A\ngigantic, dish-shaped object lay in the street just ahead, and Eddie’s\noverstrained mind, perhaps cued by the classical buildings on either side of\nthem, produced an image of Jove and Thor playing Frisbee. Jove throws one wide\nand Thor lets it fall through a cloud—what the hell, it’s Miller Time on\nOlympus, anyway.
16Part 15Darktower 3 - The Waste Lands\n\nFrisbees of the gods, he thought, swerving Susannah between two crumbling, rusty\ncars, what a concept.\n\nHe bumped the chair up on the sidewalk to get around the artifact, which looked\nlike some sort of telecommunications dish now that he was really close to it. He\nwas easing the wheelchair over the curb and back into the street again—the\nsidewalk was too littered with crap to make any real time—when the drums\nsuddenly cut out. The echoes rolled away into a new silence, except it wasn’t\nreally silent at all, Eddie realized. Up ahead, the arched entrance to a marble\nbuilding stood at the intersection of The Street of the Turtle and another\navenue. This building had been overgrown by vines and some straggly green stuff\nthat looked like cypress beards, but it was still magnificent and somehow\ndignified. Beyond it, around the corner, a crowd was babbling excitedly.\n“Don’t stop!” Susannah snapped. “We haven’t got time to—”\nA hysterical shriek drilled through the babble. It was accompanied by yells of\napproval, and, incredibly, the sort of applause Eddie had heard in Atlantic City\nhotel-casinos after some lounge act had finished doing its thing. The shriek was\nchoked into a long, dying gargle that sounded like the buzz of a cicada. Eddie\nfelt the hair on the nape of his neck coming to attention. He glanced at the\ncorpses hanging from the nearest speaker-pole and understood that the fun-loving\nPubes of Lud were hold-ing another public execution.\nMarvellous, he thought. Now if they only had Tony Orlando and Dawn to sing\n\n“Knock Three Times,” they could all die happy.\n\nEddie looked curiously at the stone pile on the corner. This close, the vines\nwhich overgrew it had a powerful herbal smell. That smell was eye-wateringly\nbitter, but he still liked it better than the cinnamon-sweet odor of the\nmummified corpses. The beards of greenery growing from the vines drooped in\nratty sheaves, creating waterfalls of vegetation where once there had been a\nseries of arched entrances. A figure suddenly barrelled out through one of these\nwaterfalls and hurried toward them. It was a kid, Eddie realized, and not that\nmany years out of diapers, judging by the size. He was wearing a weird little\nLord Fauntleroy outfit, complete with ruffled white shirt and velveteen short\npants. There were ribbons in his hair. Eddie felt a sudden mad urge to wave his\nhands above his head and scream But-wheat say, “Lud is o-tay!”\n“Come on!” the kid cried in a high, piping voice. Several sprays of the green\nstuff had gotten caught in his hair; he brushed absently at these with his left\nhand as he ran. “They’re gonna do Spankers! It’s the Spankerman’s turn to go to\nthe land of the drums! Come on or you’ll miss the whole fakement, gods cuss it!”\nSusannah was equally stunned by the child’s appearance, but as he got closer, it\nstruck her that there was something extremely odd and awkward about the way he\nwas brushing at the crumbles and strands of greenery which had gotten caught in\nhis beribboned hair: he kept using just that one hand. His other had been behind\nhis back when he ran out through the weedy waterfall, and there it remained.\nHow awkward that must be! she thought, and then a tape-player turned on in her\nmind and she heard Roland speaking at the end of the bridge. I knew something\nlike this could happen . . . if we’d seen the fellow earlier, while we were\nstill beyond the range of his exploding egg . . . Damn the luck!\nShe levelled Roland’s gun at the child, who had leaped from the curb and was\nrunning straight for them. “Hold it!” she screamed. “Stand still, you!”\n“Suze, what are you doing?” Eddie yelled.\nSusannah ignored him. In a very real sense, Susannah Dean was no longer even\nhere; it was Detta Walker in the chair now, and her eyes were glittering with\nfeverish suspicion. “Stop or I’ll shoot!”\nLittle Lord Fauntleroy might have been deaf for all the effect her warning had.\n“Hoss it!” he shouted jubilantly. “Yer gointer miss the whole show! Spanker’s\ngointer—“\n\nHis right hand finally began to come out from behind his back. As it did, Eddie\nrealized they weren’t looking at a kid but at a misshapen dwarf whose childhood\nwas many years past. The expression Eddie had at first taken for childish glee\nwas actually a chilly mixture of hate and rage. The dwarf’s cheeks and brow were\ncovered with the oozing, discol-ored patches Roland called whore’s blossoms.\nSusannah never saw his face. Her attention was fixed on the emerg-ing right\nhand, and the dull green sphere it held. That was all she needed to see.\nRoland’s gun crashed. The dwarf was hammered backward. A shrill cry of pain and\nrage rose from his tiny mouth as he landed on the sidewalk. The grenade bounced\nout of his hand and rolled back into the same arch through which he had emerged.\nDetta was gone like a dream, and Susannah looked from the smoking gun to the\ntiny, sprawled figure on the sidewalk with surprise, horror, and dismay. “Oh, my\nJesus! I shot him! Eddie, I shot him!”\n\n“Grays . . . die!”\n\nLittle Lord Fauntleroy tried to scream these words defiantly, but they came out\nin a bubbling choke of blood that drenched the few remaining white patches on\nhis frilly shirt. There was a muffled explosion from inside the overgrown plaza\nof the corner building, and the shaggy carpets of green stuff hanging in front\nof the arches billowed outward like flags in a brisk gale. With them came clouds\nof choking, acrid smoke. Eddie flung himself on top of Susannah to shield her,\nand felt a gritty shower of concrete fragments—all small ones, luckily—patter\ndown on his back, his neck, and the crown of his head. There was a series of\nunpleasantly wet smacking sounds to his left. He opened his eyes a crack, looked\nin that direction, and saw Little Lord Fauntleroy’s head just com-ing to a stop\nin the gutter. The dwarf’s eyes were still open, his mouth still fixed in its\n\nfinal snarl.\n\nNow there were other voices, some shrieking, some yelling, all furi-ous. Eddie\nrolled off Susannah’s chair—it tottered on one wheel before deciding to stay\nup—and stared in the direction from which the dwarf had come. A ragged mob of\nabout twenty men and women had appeared, some coming from around the corner,\nothers pushing through the mats of foliage which obscured the corner building’s\narches, materializing from the smoke of the dwarf’s grenade like evil spirits.\nMost were wearing blue headscarves and all were carrying weapons—a varied (and\nsomehow pitiful) assortment of them which included rusty swords, dull knives,\nand splintery clubs. Eddie saw one man defiantly waving a hammer. Pubes, Eddie\nthought. We interrupted their necktie party, and they’re pissed as hell about\nit.\n\nA tangle of shouts—Kill the Grays! Kill them both! They’ve done for Luster, God\nkill their eyes!—arose from this charming group as they caught sight of Susannah\nin her wheelchair and Eddie, who was now crouched on one knee before it. The man\nin the forefront was wearing a kilt-like wrap and waving a cutlass. He\nbrandished this wildly (he would have decapitated the heavyset woman standing\nclose behind him, had she not ducked) and then charged. The others followed,\nyelling happily.\n\nRoland’s gun pounded its bright thunder into the windy, overcast day, and the\ntop of the kilt-wearing Pube’s head lifted off. The sallow skin of the woman who\nhad almost been decapitated by his cutlass was suddenly stippled with red rain\nand she voiced a sound of barking dismay. The others came on past the woman and\nthe dead man, raving and wild-eyed.\n\n“Eddie!” Susannah screamed, and fired again. A man wearing a silk-lined cape and\nknee-boots collapsed into the street.\n\nEddie groped for the’ Ruger and had one panicky moment when he thought he had\nlost it. The butt of the gun had somehow slipped down inside the waistband of\nhis pants. He wrapped his hand around it and yanked hard. The fucking thing\nwouldn’t come. The sight at the end of the barrel had somehow gotten stuck in\nhis underwear.\n\nSusannah fired three closely spaced shots. Each found a target, but the oncoming\nPubes didn’t slow.\n\n“Eddie, help me!”\n\nEddie tore his pants open, feeling like some cut-rate version of Superman, and\nfinally managed to free the Ruger. He hit the safety with the heel of his left\npalm, placed his elbow on his leg just above the knee, and began to fire. There\nwas no need to think—no need to even aim. Roland had told them that in battle a\ngunslinger’s hands worked on their own, and Eddie now discovered it was true. It\nwould have been hard for a blind man to miss at this range, anyway. Susannah had\ncut the numbers of the charging Pubes to no more than fifteen; Eddie went\nthrough the remainder like a storm wind in a wheatfield, dropping four in less\nthan two seconds.\n\nNow the single face of the mob, that look of glazed and mindless eagerness,\nbegan to break apart. The man with the hammer abruptly tossed his weapon aside\nand ran for it, limping extravagantly on a pair of arthritis-twisted legs. He\nwas followed by two others. The rest of them milled uncertainly in the street.\n“Come on, you deucies!” a relatively young man snarled. He wore his blue scarf\naround his throat like a rally-racer’s ascot. He was bald except for two fluffs\nof frizzy red hair, one on each side of his head. To Susannah, this fellow\nlooked like Clarabell the Clown; to Eddie he looked like Ronald McDonald; to\nboth of them he looked like trouble. He threw a home-made spear that might have\nstarted life as a steel tableleg. It clattered harmlessly into the street to\nEddie and Susannah’s right. “Come on, I say! We can get em if we all stick\ntogeth—“\n\n“Sorry, guy,” Eddie murmured, and shot him in the chest.\nClarabell/Ronald staggered backward, one hand going to his shirt.\nHe stared at Eddie with huge eyes that told his tale with heartbreaking clarity:\nthis wasn’t supposed to happen. The hand dropped heavily to the young man’s\n\nside. A single runlet of blood, incredibly bright in the gray day, slipped from\n\nthe comer of his mouth. The few remaining Pubes stared at him mutely as he\nslipped to his knees, and one of them turned to run.\n“Not at all,” Eddie said. “Stay put, my retarded friend, or you’re going to get\na good look at the clearing where your path ends.” He raised his voice. “Drop\nem, boys and girls! All of em! Now!”\n\n“You …” the dying man whispered. “You . . . gunslinger?”\n“That’s right,” Eddie said. His eyes surveyed the remaining Pubes grimly.\n“Cry your . . . pardon,” the man with the frizzy red hair gasped, and then he\nfell forward onto his face.\n\n“Gunslingers?” one of the others asked. His tone was one of dawning horror and\nrealization.\n\n“Well, you’re stupid, but you ain’t deaf,” Susannah said, “and that’s somethin,\nanyway.” She waggled the barrel of the gun, which Eddie was quite sure was\nempty. For that matter, how many rounds could be left in the Ruger? He realized\nhe didn’t have any idea how many rounds the clip held, and cursed himself for a\nfool . . . but had he really believed it could come to something like this? He\ndidn’t think so. “You heard him, folks. Drop em. Recess is over.”\nOne by one, they complied. The woman who was wearing a pint or so of Mr.\nSword-and-Kilt’s blood on her face said, “You shouldn’t've killed Winston,\nmissus—’twas his birthday, so it was.”\n\n“Well, I guess he should have stayed home and eaten some more birthday cake,”\nEddie said. Given the overall quality of this experience, he didn’t find either\nthe woman’s comment or his own response at all surreal.\nThere was one other woman among the remaining Pubes, a scrawny thing whose long\nblonde hair was coming out in big patches, as if she had the mange. Eddie\nobserved her sidling toward the dead dwarf—and the potential safety of the\novergrown arches beyond him—and put a bullet into the cracked cement close by\nher foot. He had no idea what he wanted with her, but what he didn’t want was\none of them giving the rest of them ideas. For one thing, he was afraid of what\nhis hands might do if the sickly, sullen people before him tried to run.\nWhatever his head thought about this gunslinging business, his hands had\ndiscovered they liked it just fine.\n\n“Stand where you are, beautiful. Officer Friendly says play it safe.” He glanced\nat Susannah and was disturbed by the grayish quality of her complexion. “Suze,\nyou all right?” he asked in a lower voice.\n“Yes.”\n\n“You’re not going to faint or anything, are you? Because—”\n“No.” She looked at him with eyes so dark they were like caves. “It’s just that\nI never shot anyone before . . . okay?”\n\nWell, you better get used to it rose to his lips. He bit it back and returned\nhis gaze to the five people who remained before them. They were looking at him\nand Susannah with a species of sullen fear which nevertheless stopped well short\nof terror.\n\nShit, most of them have forgotten what terror is, he thought. Joy, sadness, love\n. . . same thing. I don’t think they feel much of anything, anymore. They’ve\nbeen living in this purgatory too long.\n\nThen he remembered the laughter, the excited cries, the lounge-act applause, and\nrevised his thinking. There was at least one thing that still got their motors\nrunning, one thing that still pushed their buttons. Spanker could have testified\nto that.\n\n“Who’s in charge here?” Eddie asked. He was watching the intersec-tion behind\nthe little group very carefully in case the others should get their courage\nback. So far he saw and heard nothing alarming from that direction. He thought\nthat the others had probably left this ragged crew to its fate.\nThey looked at each other uncertainly, and finally the woman with the\nblood-spattered face spoke up. “Spanker was, but when the god-drums started up\nthis time, it was Spanker’s stone what come out of the hat and we set him to\ndance. I guess Winston would have come next, but you did for him with your\n\ngod-rotted guns, so you did.” She wiped blood deliberately from her cheek,\n\nlooked at it, and then returned her sullen glance to Eddie.\n“Well, what do you think Winston was trying to do to me with his god-rotted\nspear?” Eddie asked. He was disgusted to find the woman had actually made him\nfeel guilty about what he had done. “Trim my sideburns?”\n“Killed Frank ‘n Luster, too,” she went on doggedly, “and what are you? Either\nGrays, which is bad, or a couple of god-rotted outlanders, which is worse. Who’s\nleft for the Pubes in City North? Topsy, I sup-pose—Topsy the Sailor—but he\nain’t here, is he? Took his boat and went off downriver, ay, so he did, and god\nrot him, too, says I!”\n\nSusannah had ceased listening; her mind had fixed with horrified fascination on\nsomething the woman had said earlier. It was Spanker’s stone what come out of\nthe hat and we set him to dance. She remembered reading Shirley Jackson’s story\n“The Lottery” in college and understood that these people, the degenerate\ndescendents of the original Pubes, were living Jackson’s nightmare. No wonder\nthey weren’t capable of any strong emotion when they knew they would have to\nparticipate in such a grisly drawing not once a year, as in the story, but two\nor three times each day.\n\n“Why?” she asked the bloody woman in a harsh, horrified voice. “Why do you do\nit?”\n\nThe woman looked at Susannah as if she was the world’s biggest fool. “Why? So\nthe ghosts what live in the machines won’t take over the bodies of those who\nhave died here—Pubes and Grays alike—and send them up through the holes in the\nstreets to eat us. Any fool knows that.”\n“There are no such things as ghosts,” Susannah said, and her voice sounded like\nso much meaningless quacking to her own ears. Of course there were. In this\nworld, there were ghosts everywhere. Nevertheless, she pushed ahead. “What you\ncall the god-drums is only a tape stuck in a machine. That’s really all it is.”\nSudden inspiration struck her and she added: “Or maybe the Grays are doing it on\npurpose—did you ever think of that? They live in the other part of the city,\ndon’t they? And under it, as well? They’ve always wanted you out. Maybe they’ve\njust hit on a really efficient way of getting you guys to do their work for\nthem.”\n\nThe bloody woman was standing next to an elderly gent wearing what looked like\nthe world’s oldest bowler hat and a pair of frayed khaki shorts. Now he stepped\nforward and spoke to her with a patina of good manners that turned his\nunderlying contempt into a dagger with razor-sharp edges. “You are quite wrong,\nMadam Gunslinger. There are a great many machines under Lud, and there are\nghosts in all of them— demonous spirits which bear only ill will to mortal men\nand women. These demon-ghosts are very capable of raising the dead . . . and in\nLud, there are a great many dead to raise.”\n“Listen,” Eddie said. “Have you ever seen one of these zombies with your own\neyes, Jeeves? Have any of you?”\n\nJeeves curled his lip and said nothing—but that lip-curl really said it all.\nWhat else could one expect, it asked, from outlanders who used guns as a\nsubstitute for understanding?\n\nEddie decided it would be best to close off the whole line of discus-sion. He\nhad never been cut out for missionary work, anyway. He wag-gled the Ruger at the\nbloodstained woman. “You and your friend there— the one who looks like an\nEnglish butler on his day off—are going to take us to the railroad station.\nAfter that, we can all say goodbye, and I’ll tell you the truth: that’s going to\nmake my fuckin day.”\n\n“Railroad station?” the guy who looked like Jeeves the Butler asked. “What is a\nrailroad station?”\n\n“Take us to the cradle,” Susannah said. “Take us to Blaine.”\nThis finally rattled Jeeves; an expression of shocked horror replaced the\nworld-weary contempt with which he had thus far treated them. “You can’t go\nthere!” he cried. “The cradle is forbidden ground, and Blaine is the most\ndangerous of all Lud’s ghosts!”\n\nForbidden ground? Eddie thought. Great. If it’s the truth, at least we’ll be\n\nable to stop worrying about you assholes. It was also nice to hear that there\nstill was a Blaine … or that these people thought there was, anyway.\nThe others were staring at Eddie and Susannah with expressions of\nuncomprehending amazement; it was as if the interlopers had suggested to a bunch\nof born-again Christians that they hunt up the Ark of the Covenant and turn it\ninto a pay toilet.\n\nEddie raised the Ruger until the center of Jeeves’s forehead lay in the sight.\n“We’re going,” he said, “and if you don’t want to join your ancestors right here\nand now, I suggest you stop pissing and moaning and take us there.”\nJeeves and the bloodstained woman exchanged an uncertain glance, but when the\nman in the bowler hat looked back at Eddie and Susannah, his face was firm and\nset. “Shoot us if you like,” he said. “We’d sooner die here than there.”\n“You folks are a bunch of sick motherfuckers with dying on the brain!” Susannah\ncried at them. “Nobody has to die! Just take us where we want to go, for the\nlove of God!”\n\nThe woman said somberly, “But it is death to enter Blaine’s cradle, mum, so it\nis. For Blaine sleeps, and he who disturbs his rest must pay a high price.”\n“Come on, beautiful,” Eddie snapped. “You can’t smell the coffee with your head\nup your ass.”\n\n“I don’t know what that means,” she said with an odd and perplexing dignity.\n“It means you can take us to the cradle and risk the Wrath of Blaine, or you can\nstand your ground here and experience the Wrath of Eddie. It doesn’t have to be\na nice clean head-shot, you know. I can take you a piece at a time, and I’m\nfeeling just mean enough to do it. I’m having a very bad day in your city—the\nmusic sucks, everybody has a bad case of b.o., and the first guy we saw threw a\ngrenade at us and kidnapped our friend. So what do you say?”\n“Why would you go to Blaine in any case?” one of the others asked. “He stirs no\nmore from his berth in the cradle—not for years now. He has even stopped\nspeaking in his many voices and laughing.”\nSpeaking in his many voices and laughing? Eddie thought. He looked at Susannah.\nShe looked back and shrugged.\n\n“Ardis was the last to go nigh Blaine,” the bloodstained woman said.\nJeeves nodded somberly. “Ardis always was a fool when he were in drink. Blaine\nasked him some question. I heard it, hut it made no sense to me—something about\nthe mother of ravens, I think—and when Ardis couldn’t answer what was asked,\nBlaine slew him with blue fire.”\n\n“Electricity?” Eddie asked.\n\nJeeves and the bloodstained woman both nodded. “Ay,” the woman said.\n“Electricity, so it were called in the old days, so it were.”\n“You don’t have to go in with us,” Susannah proposed suddenly. “Just get us\nwithin sight of the place. We’ll go the rest of the way on our own.”\nThe woman looked at her mistrustfully, and then Jeeves pulled her head close to\nhis lips and mumbled in her ear for a while. The other Pubes stood behind them\nin a ragged line, looking at Eddie and Susannah with the dazed eyes of people\nwho have survived a bad air-raid.\n\nAt last the woman looked around. “Ay,” she said. “We’ll take you nigh the\ncradle, and then it’s good riddance to bad swill.”\n“My idea exactly,” Eddie said. “You and Jeeves. The rest of you, scatter.” He\nswept them with his eyes. “But remember this—one spear thrown from ambush, one\narrow, one brick, and these two die.” This threat came out sounding so weak and\npointless that Eddie wished he hadn’t made it. How could they possibly care for\nthese two, or for any of the individual members of their clan, when they dusted\ntwo or more of them each and every day? Well, he thought, watching the others\ntrot off without so much as a backward glance, it was too late to worry about\nthat now.\n\n“Come on,” the woman said. “I want to be done with you.”\n“The feeling’s mutual,” Eddie replied.\n\nBut before she and Jeeves led them away, the woman did something which made\n\nEddie repent a little of his hard thoughts: knelt, brushed back the hair of the\n\nman in the kilt, and placed a kiss on his dirty cheek. “Goodbye, Winston,” she\nsaid. “Wait for me where the trees clear and the water’s sweet. I’ll come to ye,\nay, as sure as dawn makes shadows run west.”\n“I didn’t want to kill him,” Susannah said. “I want you to know that. But I\nwanted to die even less.”\n\n“Ay.” The face that turned toward Susannah was stem and tearless. “But if ye\nmean to enter Blaine’s cradle, ye’ll die anyway. And the chances are that ye’ll\ndie envying poor old Winston. He’s cruel, is Blaine. The crudest of all demons\nin this cruel, cruel place.”\n\n“Come on, Maud,” Jeeves said, and helped her up.\n“Ay. Let’s finish with them.” She surveyed Susannah and Eddie again, her eyes\nstem but somehow confused, as well. “Gods curse my eyes that they should ever\nhave happened on you two in the first place.\nAnd gods curse the guns ye carry, as well, for they were always the springhead\nof our troubles.”\n\nAnd with that attitude, Susannah thought, your troubles are going to last at\nleast a thousand years, sugar.\n\nMaud set a rapid pace along The Street of the Turtle. Jeeves trotted beside her.\nEddie, who was pushing Susannah in the wheelchair, was soon panting and\nstruggling to keep up. The palatial buildings which lined their way spread out\nuntil they resembled ivy-covered country houses on huge, run-to-riot lawns, and\nEddie realized they had entered what had once been a very ritzy neighborhood\nindeed. Ahead of them, one building loomed above all others. It was a\ndeceptively simple square construction of white stone blocks, its overhanging\nroof supported by many pillars. Eddie thought again of the gladiator movies he’d\nso enjoyed as a kid. Susannah, educated in more formal schools, was reminded of\nthe Par-thenon. Both saw and marvelled at the gorgeously sculpted bestiary— Bear\nand Turtle, Fish and Rat, Horse and Dog—which ringed the top of the building in\ntwo-by-two parade, and understood it was the place they had come to find.\nThat uneasy sensation that they were being watched by many eyes— eyes filled\nequally with hate and wonder—never left them. Thunder boomed as they came in\nsight of the monorail track; like the storm, the track came sweeping in from the\nsouth, joined The Street of the Turtle, and ran straight on toward the Cradle of\nLud. And as they neared it, ancient bodies began to twist and dance in the\nstrengthening wind on either side of them.\n\nAFTER THEY HAD RUN for God knew how long (all Jake knew for sure was that the\ndrums had stopped again), Gasher once more yanked him to a stop. This time Jake\nmanaged to keep his feet. He had gotten his second wind. Gasher, who would never\nsee eleven again, had not.\n\n“Hoo! My old pump’s doing nip-ups, sweetie.”\n“Too bad,” Jake said unfeelingly, then stumbled backward as Gasher’s gnarled\nhand connected with the side of his face.\n“Yar, you’d cry a bitter tear if I dropped dead right here, woontcher? Too\nlikely! But no such luck, my fine young squint—old Gasher’s seen em come and\nseen em go, and I wasn’t born to drop dead at the feet of any little sweetcheeks\nberry like you.”\n\nJake listened to these incoherencies impassively. He meant to see Gasher dead\nbefore the day was over. Gasher might take Jake with him, but Jake no longer\ncared about that. He dabbed blood from his freshly split lip and looked at it\nthoughtfully, wondering at how quickly the desire to do murder could invade and\nconquer the human heart.\n\nGasher observed Jake looking at his bloody fingers and grinned. “Sap’s runnin,\nennet? Nor will it be the last your old pal Gasher beats out of your young tree,\nunless you look sharp; unless you look wery sharp indeed.” He pointed down at\nthe cobbled surface of the narrow alley they were currently negotiating. There\n\nwas a rusty manhole cover there, and Jake realized he had seen the words stamped\n\ninto the steel not long ago: LaMERK FOUNDRY, they said.\n“There’s a grip on the side,” Gasher said. “Yer see? Get your hands into that\nand pull away. Step lively, now, and maybe ye’ll still have all your teeth when\nye meet up with Tick-Tock.”\n\nJake grasped the steel cover and pulled. He pulled hard, but not quite as hard\nas he could have done. The maze of streets and alleys through which Gasher had\nrun him was bad, but at least he could see. He couldn’t imagine what it might be\nlike in the underworld below the city, where the blackness would preclude even\ndreams of escape, and he didn’t intend to find out unless he absolutely had to.\nGasher quickly made it clear to him that he did.\n“It’s too heavy for—” Jake began, and then the pirate seized him by the throat\nand yanked him upward until they were face to face. The long run through the\nalleys had brought a thin, sweaty flush to his cheeks and turned the sores\neating into his flesh an ugly yellow-purple color. Those which were open exuded\nthick infected matter and threads of blood in steady pulses. Jake caught just a\nwhiff of Gasher’s thick stench before his wind was cut off by the hand which had\nencircled his throat.\n\n“Listen, you stupid cull, and listen well, for this is your last warning. You\nyank that fucking streethead off right now or I’ll reach into your mouth and rip\nthe living tongue right out of it. And feel free to bite all you want while I do\nit, for what I have runs in the blood and you’ll see the first blossoms on yer\nown face before the week’s out—if yer lives that long. Now, do you see?”\nJake nodded frantically. Gasher’s face was disappearing into deepen-ing folds of\ngray, and his voice seemed to be coming from a great distance.\n“All right.” Gasher shoved him backward. Jake fell in a heap beside the manhole\ncover, gagging and retching. He finally managed to draw in a deep, whooping\nbreath that burned like liquid fire. He spat out a blood-flecked wad of stuff\nand almost threw up at the sight of it.\n\n“Now yank back that cover, my heart’s delight, and let’s have no more natter\nabout it.\n\nJake crawled over to it, slid his hands into the grip, and this time pulled with\nall his might. For one terrible moment he thought he was still not going to be\nable to budge it. Then he imagined Gasher’s fingers reaching into his mouth and\nseizing his tongue, and found a little extra. There was a dull, spreading agony\nin his lower back as something gave there, but the circular lid slipped slowly\naside, grinding on the cobbles and exposing a grinning crescent of darkness.\n“Good, cully, good!” Gasher cried cheerfully. “What a little mule y’are! Keep\npulling—don’t give up now!”\n\nWhen the crescent had become a half-moon and the pain in Jake’s lower back was a\nwhite-hot fire, Gasher booted him in the ass, knocking him asprawl.\n“Wery good!” Gasher said, peering in. “Now, cully, go smartly down the ladder on\nthe side. Mind you don’t lose your grip and tumble all the way to the bottom,\nfor those rungs are fearsome slick and greezy. There’s twenty or so, as I\nremember. And when you get to the bottom, stand stock-still and wait for me. You\nmight feel like runnin from yer old pal, but do you think that would be a good\nidea?”\n\n“No,” Jake said. “I suppose not.”\n\n“Wery intelligent, old son!” Gasher’s lips spread in his hideous smile, once\nmore revealing his few surviving teeth. “It’s dark down there, and there are a\nthousand tunnels going every which-a-way. Yer old pal Gasher knows em like the\nback of his hand, so he does, but you’d be lost in no time. Then there’s the\nrats—wery big and wery hungry they are. So you just wait.”\n“I will.”\n\nGasher regarded him narrowly. “You speak just like a little triggie, you do, but\nyou’re no Pube—I’ll set my watch and warrant to that. Where are you from,\nsquint?”\n\nJake said nothing.\n\n“Bumbler got your tongue, do he? Well, that’s all right; Tick-Tock’ll get it all\n\nout of you, so he will. He’s got a way about him, Ticky does; just naturally\n\nwants to make people conwerse. Once he gets em goin, they sometimes talks so\nfast and screams so loud someone has to hit em over the head to slow em down.\nBumblers ain’t allowed to hold no one’s tongue around the Tick-Tock Man, not\neven fine young triggers like you. Now get the fuck down that ladder. Hup!”\nHe lashed out with his foot. This time Jake managed to tuck in and dodge the\nblow. He looked into the half-open manhole, saw the ladder, and started down. He\nwas still chest-high to the alley when a tremendous stonelike crash hammered the\nair. It came from a mile or more away, but Jake knew what it was without having\nto be told. A cry of pure misery burst from his lips.\nA grim smile tugged at the corners of Gasher’s mouth. “Your hard-case friend\ntrailed ye a little better than ye thought he would, didn’t he?\nNot better than I thought, though, cully, for I got a look at his eyes— wery\npert and cunning they were. I thought he’d come arter his juicy little\nnight-nudge a right smart, if he was to come at all, and so he did. He spied the\ntripwires, but the fountain’s got him, so that’s all right. Get on,\nsweetcheeks.”\n\nHe aimed a kick at Jake’s protruding head. Jake ducked it, but one foot slipped\non the ladder bolted to the side of the sewer shaft and he only saved himself\nfrom falling by clutching Gasher’s scab-raddled ankle. He looked up, pleading,\nand saw no softening on that dying, infected face.\n“Please,” he said, and heard the word trying to break into a sob. He kept seeing\nRoland lying crushed beneath the huge fountain. What had Gasher said? If anyone\nwanted him, they would have to pick him up with a blotter.\n“Beg if you want, dear heart. Just don’t expect no good to come of it, for mercy\nstops on this side of the bridge, so it does. Now go down, or I’ll kick your\nbleedin brains right outcher bleedin ears.”\nSo Jake went down, and by the time he reached the standing water at the bottom,\nthe urge to cry had passed. He waited, shoulders slumped and head down, for\nGasher to descend and lead him to his fate.\n\nROLAND HAD COME CLOSE to tripping the crossed wires which held back the\navalanche of junk, but the dangling fountain was absurd—a trap which might have\nbeen set by a stupid child. Cort had taught them to constantly check all visual\nquadrants as they moved in enemy territory, and that included above as well as\nbehind and below.\n\n“Stop,” he told Oy, raising his voice to be heard over the drums.\n“Op!” Oy agreed, then looked ahead and immediately added, “Ake!”\n“Yes.” The gunslinger took another look up at the suspended marble fountain,\nthen examined the street, looking for the trigger. There were two, he saw.\nPerhaps their camouflage as cobblestones had once been effective, but that time\nwas long past. Roland bent down, hands on his knees, and spoke into Oy’s\nupturned face. “Going to pick you up for a minute now. Don’t fuss, Oy.”\n“Oy!”\n\nRoland put his arms around the bumbler. At first Oy stiffened and attempted to\npull away, and then Roland felt the small animal give in. He wasn’t happy about\nbeing this close to someone who wasn’t Jake, but he clearly intended to put up\nwith it. His aim was as true as it had\nbeen when he had thrown at the ancient traffic signal in River Crossing; the\nmissile struck one of the discolored triggers dead center, and one of the rusty\ncables snapped with a harsh twang. The passageway, narrow to begin with, was now\ncom-pletely blocked.\n\nHe wondered if Jake had heard the fall of the fountain, and what he had made of\nit if he had. He didn’t waste such speculation on Gasher; Gasher would think he\nhad been crushed to paste, which was exactly what Roland wanted him to think.\nWould Jake think the same thing? The boy should know better than to believe a\ngunslinger could be killed by such a simple device, but if Gasher had terrorized\nhim enough, Jake might not be thinking that clearly. Well, it was too late to\nworry about it now, and if he had it to do over again, he would do exactly the\nsame thing. Dying or not, Gasher had displayed both courage and animal cun-ning.\nIf he was off his guard now, the trick was worth it.\nRoland got to his feet. “Oy—find Jake.”\n\n“Ake!” Oy stretched his head forward on his long neck, sniffed around in a\nsemicircle, picked up Jake’s scent, and was off again with Roland running after.\nTen minutes later he came to a stop at a manhole cover in the street, sniffed\nall the way around it, then looked up at Roland and barked shrilly.\nThe gunslinger dropped to one knee and observed both the confu-sion of tracks\nand a wide path of scratches on the cobbles. He thought this particular manhole\ncover had been moved quite often. His eyes narrowed as he saw the wad of bloody\nphlegm in a crease between two nearby cobbles.\n“The bastard keeps hitting him,” he murmured.\nHe pulled the manhole cover back, looked down, then untied the rawhide lacings\nwhich held his shirt closed. He pulled the rawhide lace from the eyelets in his shirt and\nfound another, longer, lace in his purse.\n“I’m going to leash you. I don’t like it and you’re going to like it even less,\nbut it’s going to be very dark down there.”\nHe tied the two lengths of rawhide together and formed one end into a wide loop\nwhich he slipped over Oy’s head. He felt for the top\nrung of the ladder and found it. Some-where nearby, water was dripping\nhollowly and monotonously. Farther off, Roland could hear the rumble of\nmachinery. The Cradle was so clean it almost hurt her eyes. No vines overgrew\nits sides; no graffiti daubed its blinding white walls and steps and columns.\nThe yellow plains dust which had coated everything else was absent here. As they\ndrew closer, Susannah saw why: streams of water coursed endlessly down the sides\nof the Cradle, issuing from nozzles hidden in the shadows of the copper-sheathed\neaves. Interval sprays created by other hidden nozzles washed the steps, turning\nthem into off-and-on waterfalls.\n\n“Wow,” Eddie said. “It makes Grand Central look like a Greyhound station in\nButtfuck, Nebraska.”\n\n“What a poet you are, dear,” Susannah said dryly.\nThe steps surrounded the entire building and rose to a great open lobby. The Totems of the Beam marched all the way around the\nbuilding, two by two, but the corners were reserved for creatures Susannah\nfervently hoped never to meet outside of the occasional nightmare—hideous stone\ndragons with scaly bodies, clutching, claw-tipped hands, and nasty peering eyes.\nEddie touched her shoulder and pointed higher. Standing astride the peak of the roof,\nfar above The Totems of the Beam and the dragonish gargoyles, as if given\ndominion over them, was a golden warrior at least sixty feet high. A battered\ncowboy hat was shoved back to reveal his lined and careworn brow; a bandanna\nhung askew on his upper chest, as if it had just been pulled down after serving\nlong, hard duty as a dust-muffle. That man was a gunslinger, and the resemblance between him,\nwho’s probably been dead a thousand years or more, and Roland is all the truth\nof ka-tet you’ll ever need to know.\n\nThunder slammed out of the south. Lightning harried racing clouds across the\nsky. She wished she had more time to study both the golden statue which stood\natop the Cradle and the animals which surrounded it; each of these latter\nappeared to have words carved upon them, and she had an idea that what was\nwritten there might be knowledge worth having. I don’t think there’s any future in\nthis town, anyway.”\n\nHe pushed her forward, and they both stiffened as the wheels of the chair passed\nover what Maud had called the dead-line, fearful that they would trip some\nancient protective device and die together. But nothing happened. Eddie pushed\nher into the plaza, and as they approached the steps leading up to the Cradle, a\ncold, wind-driven rain began to fall.\n\nAlthough neither of them knew it, the first of the great autumn storms of\nMid-World had arrived.\n\nONCE THEY WERE IN the smelly darkness of the sewers, Gasher slowed the killing\npace he’d maintained aboveground. Jake didn’t think it was because of the\ndarkness; Gasher seemed to know every twist and turn of the route he was\nfollowing, just as advertised. Jake believed it was because his captor was\nsatisfied that Roland had been squashed to jelly by the deadfall trap.\nJake himself had begun to wonder.\n\nIf Roland had spotted the tripwires—a far more subtle trap than the one which\nfollowed—was it really likely that he had missed seeing the fountain? Jake\nsupposed it was possible, but it didn’t make much sense. Jake thought it more\nlikely that Roland had tripped the fountain on purpose, to lull Gasher and\nperhaps slow him down. He didn’t believe Roland could follow them through this\nmaze under the streets—the total darkness would defeat even the gunslinger’s\ntracking abilities—but it cheered his heart to think that Roland might not have\ndied in an attempt to keep his promise.\n\nThey turned right, left, then left again. As Jake’s other senses sharp-ened in\nan attempt to compensate for his lack of sight, he had a vague perception of\nother tunnels around him. The muffled sounds of ancient, laboring machinery\nwould grow loud for a moment, then fade as the stone foundations of the city\ndrew close around them again. Drafts blew intermittently against his skin,\nsometimes warm, sometimes chilly. Their splashing footfalls echoed briefly as\nthey passed the intersecting tunnels from which these stenchy breaths blew, and\nonce Jake nearly brained himself on some metal object jutting down from the\nceiling. He slapped at it with one hand and felt something that might have been\na large valve-wheel. After that he waved his hands as he trotted along in an\n\nattempt to read the air ahead of him.\n\nGasher guided him with taps to the shoulders, as a waggoner might have guided\nhis oxen. They moved at a good clip, trotting but not run-ning. Gasher got\nenough of his breath back to first hum and then begin singing in a low,\nsurprisingly tuneful tenor voice.\n\n“Bibble-ti-tibble-ti-ting-ting-ting,\n\nI’ll get a job and buy yer a ring,\n\nWhen I get my -mitts\n\nOn yerjiggly tits,\n\nRibble-ti-tibble-ti-ting-ting-ting!\n\nO ribble-ti-tibble,\n\nI just wanter fiddle,\n\nFiddle around with your ting-ting-ting!”\nThere were five or six more verses along this line before Gasher quit. “Now you\nsing somethin, squint.”\n\n“I don’t know anything,” Jake puffed. He hoped he sounded more out of breath\nthan he actually was. He didn’t know if it would do him any good or not, but\ndown here in the dark any edge seemed worth trying for.\nGasher brought his elbow down in the center of Jake’s back, almost hard enough\nto send him sprawling into the ankle-high water running sluggishly through the\ntunnel they were traversing. “Yon better know sominat, ‘less you want me to rip\nyour ever-lovin spine right outcher back.” He paused, then added: “There’s\nhaunts down here, boy. They live inside the fuckin machines, so they do. Singin\nkeeps em off . . . don’t you know that? Now sing!”\nJake thought hard, not wanting to earn another love-tap from Gasher, and came up\nwith a song he’d learned in summer day camp at the age of seven or eight. He\nopened his mouth and began to bawl it into the darkness, listening to the echoes\nbounce back amid the sounds of running water, falling water, and ancient\nthudding machinery.
17Part 16Darktower 3 - The Waste Lands\n\n“My girl’s a corker, she’s a New Yorker,\nI buy her everything to keep her in style,\nShe got a pair of hips\n\nJust like two battleships,\n\nOh boy, that’s how my money goes.\n\nMy girl’s a dilly, she comes from Philly,\nI buy her everything to keep her in style,\nShe’s got a pair of eyes\n\nJust like two pizza pies,\n\nOh boy, that’s how—“\n\nGasher reached out, seized Jake’s ears as if they were jug-handles, and yanked\nhim to a stop. “There’s a hole right ahead of yer,” he said. “With a voice like\nyours, squint, it’d be doin the world a mercy to letcher fall in, so it would,\nbut Tick-Tock wouldn’t approve at all, so I reckon ye’re safe for a little\nlonger.” Gasher’s hands left Jake’s ears, which burned like fire, and fastened\non the back of his shirt. “Now lean forward until you feel the ladder on the\nt’other side. And mind you don’t slip and drag us both down!”\nJake leaned cautiously forward, hands outstretched, terrified of fall-ing into a\npit he couldn’t see. His fingers touched a steel rung and closed\nover it. What are you waitin for, gods damn it!” Gasher let go of his\nshirt, and Jake could imagine him drawing his foot back, meaning to hurry him\nalong with a kick in the ass. The machines\nstill didn’t sound right, but his ears told him these were in better shape than\nthe ones above. And when he finally reached the bottom, he found the floor was\ndry. Cool in the summer, warm in the win-ter, and so much food\nthat five hunnert men couldn’t eat it in five hunnert years. And do yer know the\nbest part, squint?
