The\nTick-Tock Man was a trig enough cove, all right—he had known that it would be\ndifficult to persuade Gasher, who was dying anyway, to speak the password even\nif Roland had taken him prisoner. What Tick-Tock hadn’t taken into account was\nGasher’s defective memory.\n\nDon’t laugh. If you do, he really will beat your brains out.\nIn spite of his brave words, Gasher was watching Jake with real anxiety, and\nJake realized a potentially powerful fact: Gasher might not be afraid of dying .\n. . but he was afraid of being humiliated.\n“All right, Gasher,” he said calmly. “The word on this piece of paper is\nbountiful.”\n\n“Gimme that.” Gasher snatched the paper back, returned it to his scarf, and\nquickly wrapped the yellow cloth around his head again. He thumbed the intercom\nbutton. “Tick-Tock? Yer still there?”\n\n“Where else would I be? The West End of the World?” The drawl-ing voice now\nsounded mildly amused.\n\nGasher stuck his whitish tongue out at the speaker, but his voice was\ningratiating, almost servile. “The password’s bountyful, and a fine word it is,\ntoo! Now let me in, gods cuss it!”\n\n“Of course,” the Tick-Tock Man said. A machine started up some-where nearby,\nmaking Jake jump. The valve-wheel in the center of the door spun. When it\nstopped, Gasher seized it, yanked it outward, grabbed Jake’s arm, and propelled\nhim over the raised lip of the door and into the strangest room he had ever seen\nin his life.\n\nROLAND DESCENDED INTO DUSKY pink light. Oy’s bright eyes peered out from the\nopen V of his shirt; his neck stretched to the limit of its considerable length\nas he sniffed at the warm air that blew through the ventilator grilles. Roland\nhad had to depend completely on the bumbler’s nose in the dark passages above,\nand he had been terribly afraid the animal would lose Jake’s scent in the\nrunning water . . . but when he had heard the sound of singing—first Gasher,\nthen Jake—echoing back through the pipes, he had relaxed a little. Oy had not\nled them wrong.\n\nOy had heard it, too. Up until then he had been moving slowly and cautiously,\neven backtracking every now and again to be sure of himself, but when he heard\nJake’s voice he began to run, straining the rawhide leash. Roland was afraid he\nmight call after Jake in his harsh voice—Ake! Ake!—but he hadn’t done so. And,\njust as they reached the shaft which led to the lower levels of this Dycian\nMaze, Roland had heard the sound of some new machine—a pump of some sort,\nperhaps—followed by the metallic, echoing crash of a door being slammed shut.\nHe reached the foot of the square tunnel and glanced briefly at the double line\nof lighted tubes which led off in either direction. They were lit with\nswamp-fire, he saw, like the sign outside the place which had belonged to\nBalazar in the city of New York. He looked more closely at the narrow chrome\nventilation strips running along the top of each wall, and the arrows below\nthem, then slipped the rawhide loop off Oy’s neck. Oy shook his head\nimpatiently, clearly glad to be rid of it.\n“We’re close,” he murmured into the bumbler’s cocked ear, “and so we have to be\nquiet. Do you understand, Oy? Very quiet.”\n“I-yet,” Oy replied in a hoarse whisper that would have been funny under other\ncircumstances.\n\nRoland put him down and Oy was immediately off down the tunnel, neck out, muzzle\nto the steel floor. Roland could hear him muttering Ake-Ake! Ake-Ake! under his\nbreath. Roland unholstered his gun and followed him.\n\nEDDIE AND SUSANNAH LOOKED up at the vastness of Blaine’s Cradle as the skies\nopened and the rain began to fall in torrents.\n“It’s a hell of a building, but they forgot the handicap ramps!” Eddie yelled,\nraising his voice to be heard over the rain and thunder.\n“Never mind that,” Susannah said impatiently, slipping out of the wheelchair.\n“Let’s get up there and out of the rain.”\nEddie looked dubiously up the incline of steps. The risers were shallow . . .\nbut there were a lot of them. “You sure, Suze?”\n“Race you, white boy,” she said, and began to wriggle upward with uncanny ease,\nusing hands, muscular forearms, and the stumps of her legs.\nAnd she almost did beat him; Eddie had the ironmongery to contend with, and it\nslowed him down. Both of them were panting when they reached the top, and\ntendrils of steam were rising from their wet clothes. Eddie grabbed her under\nthe arms, swung her up, and then just held her with his hands locked together in\nthe small of her back instead of dropping her back into the chair, as he had\nmeant to do. He felt randy and half-crazy without the slightest idea why.\nOh, give me a break, he thought. You’ve gotten this far alive; that’s what’s got\nyour glands pumped up and ready to party.\nSusannah licked her full lower lip and wound her strong fingers into his hair.\nShe pulled. It hurt . . . and at the same time it felt wonderful. “Told you I’d\nbeat you, white boy,” she said in a low, husky voice.\n“Get outta here—I had you … by half a step.” He tried to sound less out of\nbreath than he was and found it was impossible.\n“Maybe . . . but it blew you out, didn’t it?” One hand left his hair, slid\ndownward, and squeezed gently. A smile gleamed in her eyes. “Somethin ain’t\nblown out, though.”\n\nThunder rumbled across the sky. They flinched, then laughed together.\n\n“Come on,” he said. “This is nuts. The time’s all wrong.”\nShe didn’t contradict him, but she squeezed him again before returning her hand\nto his shoulder. Eddie felt a pang of regret as he swung her back into her chair\nand ran her across vast flagstones and under cover of the roof. He thought he\nsaw the same regret in Susannah’s eyes.\n\nWhen they were out of the downpour, Eddie paused and they looked back. The Plaza\nof the Cradle, The Street of the Turtle, and all the city beyond was rapidly\ndisappearing into a shifting gray curtain. Eddie wasn’t a bit sorry. Lud hadn’t\nearned itself a place in his mental scrapbook of fond memories.\n“Look,” Susannah murmured. She was pointing at a nearby down-spout. It ended in\na large, scaly fish-head that looked like a close relation to the\ndragon-gargoyles which decorated the corners of the Cradle. Water ran from its\nmouth in a silver torrent.\n\n“This isn’t just a passing shower, is it?” Eddie asked.\n“Nope. It’s gonna rain until it gets tired of it, and then it’s gonna rain some\nmore, just for spite. Maybe a week; maybe a month. Not that it’s gonna matter to\nus, if Blaine decides he doesn’t like our looks and fries us. Fire a shot to let\nRoland know we got here, sugar, and then we’ll have us a look around. See what\nwe can see.”\n\nEddie pointed the Ruger into the gray sky, pulled the trigger, and fired the\nshot, which Roland heard a mile or more away, as he followed Jake and Gasher\nthrough the booby-trapped maze. Eddie stood where he was a moment longer, trying\nto persuade himself that things might still turn out all right, that his heart\nwas wrong in its stubborn insistence that they had seen the last of the\ngunslinger and the boy Jake. Then he made the automatic safe again, returned it\nto the waistband of his pants, and went back to Susannah. He turned her chair\naway from the steps and rolled her along an aisle of columns which led deeper\ninto the build-ing. She popped the cylinder of Roland’s gun and reloaded it as\nthey went.\n\nUnder the roof the rain had a secret, ghostly sound and even the harsh\nthundercracks were muted. The columns which supported the structure were at\nleast ten feet in diameter, and their tops were lost in the gloom. From up there\nin the shadows, Eddie heard the cooing con-versation of pigeons.\nNow a sign hanging on thick chrome-silver chains swam out of the shadows:\nNORTH CENTRAL POSITRONICS WELCOMES YOU\n\nTO THE CRADLE OF LUD\n\nNORTHWEST TRAVEL (PATRICIA) —>\n\n“Now we know the name of the one that fell in the river,” Eddie said. “Patricia.\nThey got their colors wrong, though. It’s supposed to be pink for girls and blue\nfor boys, not the other way around.”\n\n“Maybe they’re both blue.”\n\n“They’re not. Blaine’s pink.”\n\n“How would you know that?”\n\nEddie looked confused. “I don’t know how . . . but I do.”\nThey followed the arrow pointing toward Blaine’s berth, entering what had to be\na grand concourse. Eddie didn’t have Susannah’s ability to see the past in\nclear, visionary flashes, but his imagination nonetheless filled this vast,\npillared space with a thousand hurrying people; he heard clicking heels and\nmurmuring voices, saw embraces of homecoming and farewell. And over everything,\nthe speakers chanting news of a dozen different destinations.\nPatricia is now boarding for Northwest Baronies . . .\nWill Passenger Killington, passenger Killington, please report to the\ninformation booth on the lower level?\n\nBlaine is now arriving at Berth #2, and will be debarking shortly . . .\nNow there was only the pigeons.\n\nEddie shivered.\n\n“Look at the faces,” Susannah murmured. “I don’t know if they give you the\nwillies, but they sure do me.” She was pointing to the right.\nHigh up on the wall, a series of sculpted heads seemed to push out of tin-\nmarble, peering down at them from the shadows—stern men with the harsh faces of\nexecutioners who are happy in their work. Some of the faces had fallen from\ntheir places and lay in granite shards and splinters seventy or eighty feet\nbelow their peers. Those remaining were spider-webbed with cracks and splattered\nwith pigeon dung.\n\n“They must have been the Supreme Court, or something,” Eddie said, uneasily\nscanning all those thin lips and cracked, empty eyes. “Only judges can look so\nsmart and so completely pissed off at the same time— you’re talking to a guy who\nknows. There isn’t one of them who looks like he’d give a crippled crab a\ncrutch.”\n\n” ‘A heap of broken images, where the sun beats and the dead tree gives no\nshelter,’ ” Susannah murmured, and at these words Eddie felt gooseflesh waltz\nacross the skin of his arms and chest and legs.\n“What’s that, Suze?”\n\n“A poem by a man who must have seen Lud in his dreams,” she said. “Come on,\nEddie. Forget them.”\n\n“Easier said than done.” But he began to push her again.\nAhead, a vast grilled barrier like a castle barbican swam out of the gloom . . .\nand beyond it, they caught their first glimpse of Blaine the Mono. It was pink,\njust as Eddie had said it would be, a delicate shade which matched the veins\nrunning through the marble pillars. Blaine flowed above the wide loading\nplatform in a smooth, streamlined bullet shape which looked more like flesh than\nmetal. Its surface was broken only once—by a triangular window equipped with a\nhuge wiper. Eddie knew there would be another triangular window with another big\nwiper on the other side of the mono’s nose, so that if you looked at Blaine\nhead-on, it would seem to have a face, just like Charlie the Choo-Choo. The\nwipers would look like slyly drooping eyelids.\nWhite light from the southeastern slot in the Cradle fell across Blaine in a\nlong, distorted rectangle. To Eddie, the body of the train looked like the\nbreaching back of some fabulous pink whale—one that was utterly silent.\n“Wow.” His voice had fallen to a whisper. “We found it.”\n“Yes. Blaine the Mono.”\n\n“Is it dead, do you think? It looks dead.”\n“It’s not. Sleeping, maybe, but a long way from dead.”\n“You sure?”\n\n“Were you sure it would be pink?” It wasn’t a question he had to answer, and he\ndidn’t. The face she turned up to him was strained and badly frightened. “It’s\nsleeping, and you know what? I’m scared to wake it up.”\n“Well, we’ll wait for the others, then.”\nShe shook her head. “I think we better try to lx- ready for when they get here .\n. . because I’ve got an idea that they’re going to come on the run. Push me over\nto that box mounted on the bars. It looks like an intercom. See it?”\nHe did, and pushed her slowly toward it. It was mounted on one side of a closed\ngate in the center of the barrier which ran the length of the Cradle. The\nvertical bars of the barrier were made of what looked like stainless steel;\nthose of the gate appeared to be ornamental iron, and their lower ends\ndisappeared into steel-ringed holes in the floor. There was no way either of\nthem was going to wriggle through those bars, either, Eddie saw. The gap between\neach set was no more than four inches. It would have been a tight squeeze even\nfor Oy.\n\nPigeons ruffled and cooed overhead. The left wheel of Susannah’s chair squawked\nmonotonously. My kingdom for an oilcan, Eddie thought, and realized he was a lot\n\nmore than just scared. The last time he had felt this level of terror had been\n\non the day when he and Henry had stood on the sidewalk of Rhinehold Street in\nDutch Hill, looking at the slumped ruin of The Mansion. They hadn’t gone in on\nthat day in 1977; they had turned their backs on the haunted house and walked\naway, and he remembered vowing to himself that he would never, never, ever go\nback to that place. It was a promise he’d kept, but here he was, in another\nhaunted house, and there was the haunter, right over there— Blaine the Mono, a\nlong low pink shape with one window peering at him like the eye of a dangerous\nanimal who is shamming sleep.\n\nHe stirs no more from his berth in the Cradle. . . . He has even stopped\nspeaking in his many voices and laughing. . . . Ardis was the last to go nigh\nBlaine . . . and when Ardis couldn’t answer what was asked, Blaine slew him with\nblue fire.\n\nIf it speaks to me, I’ll probably go crazy, Eddie thought.\nThe wind gusted outside, and a fine spray of rain flew in through the tall\negress slot cut in the side of the building. He saw it strike Blaine’s window\nand bead up there.\n\nEddie shuddered suddenly and looked sharply around. “We’re being watched—I can\nfeel it.”\n\n“I wouldn’t be at all surprised. Push me closer to the gate, Eddie. I want to\nget a better look at that box.”\n\n“Okay, but don’t touch it. If it’s electrified—”\n“If Blaine wants to cook us, he will,” Susannah said, looking through the bars\nat Blaine’s back. “You know it, and I do, too.”\nAnd because Eddie knew that was only the truth, he said nothing.\nThe box looked like a combination intercom and burglar alarm. There was a\nspeaker set into the top half, with what looked like a TALK/LISTEN button next\nto it. Below this were numbers arranged in a shape which made a diamond:\n\n2 5 8 9 12 13 14 17 18 19 20 23 24 25 26 27 30 31 32 33 34 35 38 39 40 41 42 43 44 47 48 49 50 51 52 53 54 57 58 59 60 61 62 63 66 67 68 69 70 71 74 75 76 77 78 81 82 83 84 87 88 89 92 93 96\n\nUnder the diamond were two other buttons with words of the High Speech printed\non them: COMMAND and ENTER.\n\nSusannah looked bewildered and doubtful. “What is this thing, do you think? It\nlooks like a gadget in a science fiction movie.”\nOf course it did, Eddie realized. Susannah had probably seen a home security\nsystem or two in her time—she had, after all, lived among the Manhattan rich,\neven if she had not been very enthusiastically accepted by them—but there was a\nworld of difference between the electronics gear available in her when, 1963,\nand his own, which was 1987. We’ve never talked much about the differences,\neither, he thought. I wonder what she’d think if I told her Ronald Reagan was\nPresident of the United States when Roland snatched me? Probably that I was\ncrazy.\n\n“It’s a security system,” he said. Then, although his nerves and instincts\n\nscreamed out against it, he forced himself to reach out with his right hand and\nthumb the TALK/LISTEN switch.\n\nThere was no crackle of electricity; no deadly blue fire went racing up his arm.\nNo sign that the thing was even still connected.\nMaybe Blaine is dead. Maybe he’s dead, after all.\nBut he didn’t really believe that.\n\n“Hello?” he said, and in his mind’s eye saw tin- unfortunate Ardis, screaming as\nhe- was microwaved by the blue fire dancing all over his face and body, melting\nhis eyes and setting his hair ablaze. “Hello . . . Blaine? Anybody ?”\nHe let go of the button and waited, stiff with tension. Susannah’s hand crept\ninto his, cold and small. There was still no answer, and Eddie—now more\nreluctant than ever—pushed the button again.\n“Blaine?”\n\nHe let go of the button. Waited. And when there was still no answer, a dangerous\ngiddiness overcame him, as it often did in moments of stress and fear. When that\ngiddiness took him, counting the cost no longer seemed to matter. Nothing\nmattered. It had been like that when he had outfaced Balazar’s sallow-faced\ncontact man in Nassau, and it was like that now. And if Roland had seen him in\nthe moment this lunatic impa-tience overtook him, he would have seen more than\njust a resemblance between Eddie and Cuthbert; he would have sworn Eddie was\nCuthbert.\n\nHe jammed the button in with his thumb and began to bellow into the speaker,\nadopting a plummy (and completely bogus) British accent. “Hullo, Blaine!\nCheerio, old fellow! This is Robin Leach, host of Life-styles of the Rich and\nBrainless, here to tell you that you have won six billion dollars and a new Ford\nEscort in the Publishers Clearing House Sweepstakes!”\nPigeons took flight above them in soft, startled explosions of wings. Susannah\ngasped. Her face wore the dismayed expression of a devout woman who has just\nheard her husband blaspheme in a cathedral. “Eddie, stop it! Stop it!”\nEddie couldn’t stop it. His mouth was smiling, but his eyes glittered with a\nmixture of fear, hysteria, and frustrated anger. “You and your monorail\ngirlfriend, Patricia, will spend a lux-yoo-rious month in scenic Jimtown, where\nyou’ll drink only the finest wine and eat only the finest virgins! You—”\n“. . . shhhh . . .”\n\nEddie broke off, looking at Susannah. He was at once sure that it had been she\nwho had shushed him—not only because she had already tried but because she was\nthe only other person here—and yet at the same time he knew it hadn’t been\nSusannah. That had been another voice: the voice of a very young and very\nfrightened child.\n\n“Suze? Did you—“\n\nSusannah was shaking her head and raising her hand at the same time. She pointed\nat the intercom box, and Eddie saw the button marked COMMAND was glowing a very\nfaint shell-pink. It was the same color as the mono sleeping in its berth on the\nother side of the barrier.\n\n“Shhh… don’t wake him up,” the child’s voice mourned. It drifted from the\nspeaker, soft as an evening breeze.\n\n“What . . .” Eddie began. Then he shook his head, reached toward the TALK/LISTEN\nswitch and pressed it gently. When he spoke again, it was not in the blaring\nRobin Leach bellow but in the almost-whisper of a conspirator. “What are you?\nWho are you?”\n\nHe released the button. He and Susannah regarded each other with the big eyes of\nchildren who now know they are sharing the house with a dangerous—perhaps\npsychotic—adult. How have they come by the knowledge? Why, because another child\nhas told them, a child who has lived with the psychotic adult for a long time,\nhiding in corners and stealing out only when it knows the adult is asleep; a\nfrightened child who happens to be almost invisible.\nThere was no answer. Eddie let the seconds spin out. Each one seemed long enough\nto read a whole novel in. He was reaching for the button again when the faint\n\npink glow reappeared.\n\n“I’m Little Blaine,” the child’s voice whispered. “The one he doesn’t see. The\none he forgot. The one he thinks he left behind in the rooms of ruin and the\nhalls of the dead.”\n\nEddie pushed the button again with a hand that had picked up an uncontrollable\nshake. He could hear that shake in his voice, as well. “Who? Who is the one who\ndoesn’t see? Is it the Bear?”\n\nNo—not the bear; not he. Shardik lay dead in the forest, many miles behind them;\nthe world had moved on even since then. Eddie suddenly remembered what it had\nbeen like to lay his ear against that strange unfound door in the clearing where\ndie bear had lived its violent half-life, that door with its somehow terrible\nstripes of yellow and black. It was all of a piece, he realized now; all part of\nsome awful, decaying whole, a tattered web with the Dark Tower at its center\nlike an incompre-hensible stone spider. All of Mid-World had become one vast\nhaunted mansion in these strange latter days; all of Mid-World had become The\nDrawers; all of Mid-World had become a waste land, haunting and haunted.\nHe saw Susannah’s lips form the words of the real answer before the voice from\nthe intercom could speak them, and those words were as obvious as the solution\nto a riddle once the answer is spoken.\n\n“Big Blaine,” the unseen voice whispered. “Big Blaine is the ghost in the\nmachine—the ghost in all the machines.”\n\nSusannah’s hand had gone to her throat and was clutching it, as if she intended\nto strangle herself. Her eyes were full of terror, but they were not glassy, not\nstunned; they were sharp with understanding. Per-haps she knew a voice like this\none from her own when—the when where the integrated whole that was Susannah had\nbeen shunted aside by the warring personalities of Detta and Odetta. The\nchildish voice had sur-prised her as well as him, but her agonized eyes said she\nwas no stranger to the concept being expressed.\nSusannah knew all about the madness of duality.\n“Eddie we have to go,” she said. Her terror turned the words into an\nunpunctuated auditory smear. He could hear air whistling in her wind-pipe like a\ncold wind around a chimney. “Eddie we have to get away Eddie we have to get away\nEddie—“\n\n“Too late,” the tiny, mourning voice said. “He’s awake. Big Blaine is awake. He\nknows you are here. And he’s coming.”\n\nSuddenly lights—bright orange arc-sodiums—began to flash on in pairs above them,\nbathing the pillared vastness of the Cradle in a harsh glare that banished all\nshadows. Hundreds of pigeons darted and swooped in frightened, aimless flight,\nstartled from their complex of interlocked nests high above.\n“Wait!” Eddie shouted. “Please, wait!”\n\nIn his agitation he forgot to push the button, but it made no differ-ence;\nLittle Blaine responded anyway. “No! I can’t let him catch me! I can’t let him\nkill me, too!”\n\nThe light on the intercom box went dark again, but only for a moment. This time\nboth COMMAND and ENTER lit up, and their color was not pink but the lurid dark\nred of a blacksmith’s forge.\n\n“WHO ARE YOU?” a voice roared, and it came not just from the box but from every\nspeaker in the city which still operated. The rotting bodies hanging from the\npoles shivered with the vibrations of that mighty voice; it seemed that even the\ndead would run from Blaine, if they could. .\nSusannah shrank back in her chair, the heels of her hands pressed to her ears,\nher face long with dismay, her mouth distorted in a silent scream. Eddie felt\nhimself shrinking toward all the fantastic, hallucinatory terrors of eleven. Had\nit been this voice he had feared when he and Henry stood outside The Mansion?\nThat he had perhaps even antici-pated? He didn’t know . . . but he did know how\nJack in that old story must have felt when he realized that he had tried the\nbeanstalk once too often, and awakened the giant.\n“HOW DARE YOU DISTURB MY SLEEP? TELL ME NOW, OR DIE WHERE YOU STAND.”\nHe might have frozen right there, leaving Blaine—Big Blaine—to do to them\n\nwhatever it was he had done to Ardis (or something even worse); perhaps should\n\nhave frozen, locked in that down-the-rabbit-hole, fairy-tale terror. It was the\nmemory of the small voice which had spoken first that enabled him to move. It\nhad been the voice of a terrified child, but it had tried to help them,\nterrified or not.\n\nSo now you have to help yourself, he thought. You woke it up; deal with it, for\nChrist’s sake!\n\nEddie reached out and pushed the button again. “My name is Eddie Dean. The woman\nwith me is my wife, Susannah. We’re . . .”\nHe looked at Susannah, who nodded and made frantic motions for him to go on.\n“We’re on a quest. We seek the Dark Tower which lies in the Path of the Beam.\nWe’re in the company of two others, Roland of Gilead and . . . and Jake of New\nYork. We’re from New York too. If you’re—” He paused for a moment, biting back\nthe words Big Blaine. If he used them, he might make the intelligence behind the\nvoice aware that they had heard another voice; a ghost inside the ghost, so to\nspeak.\n\nSusannah gestured again for him to go on, using both hands.\n“If you’re Blaine the Mono . . . well … we want you to take us.”\nHe released the button. There was no response for what seemed like a very long\ntime, only the agitated flutter of the disturbed pigeons from overhead. When\nBlaine spoke again, his voice came only from the speaker-box mounted on the gate\nand sounded almost human.\n\n“DO NOT TRY MY PATIENCE. ALL THE DOORS TO THAT WHERE ARE CLOSED. GILEAD IS NO\nMORE, AND THOSE KNOWN AS GUNSLINGERS ARE ALL DEAD. NOW ANSWER MY QUESTION: WHO\nARE YOU? THIS IS YOUR LAST CHANCE.”\n\nThere was a sizzling sound. A ray of brilliant blue-white light lanced down from\nthe ceiling and seared a hole the size of a golf-ball in the marble floor less\nthan five feet to the left of Susannah’s wheelchair. Smoke that smelled like the\naftermath of a lightning-bolt rose lazily from it. Susannah and Eddie stared at\neach other in mute terror for a moment, and then Eddie lunged for the\ncommunicator-box and thumbed the button.\n“You’re wrong! We did come from New York! We came through the doors, on the\nbeach, only a few weeks ago!”\n\n“It’s true!” Susannah called. “I swear it is!”\nSilence. Beyond the long barrier, Blaine’s pink back humped smoothly. The window\nat the front seemed to regard them like a vapid glass eye. The wiper could have\nbeen a lid half-closed in a sly wink.\n\n“PROVE IT,” Blaine said at last.\n\n“Christ, how do I do that?” Eddie asked Susannah.\n“I don’t know.”\n\nEddie pushed the button again. “The Statue of Liberty! Does that ring a bell?”\n“GO ON,” Blaine said. Now the voice sounded almost thoughtful.\n“The Empire State Building! The Stock Exchange! The World Trade Center! Coney\nIsland Red-Hots! Radio City Music Hall! The East Vil—”\nBlaine cut him off . . . and now, incredibly, the voice which came from the\nspeaker was the drawling voice of John Wayne.\n“OKAY, PILGRIM. I BELIEVE YOU.”\n\nEddie and Susannah shared another glance, this one of confusion and relief. But\nwhen Blaine spoke again, the voice was again cold and emotionless.\n“ASK ME A QUESTION, EDDIE DEAN OF NEW YORK. AND IT BETTER BE A GOOD ONE.” There\nwas a pause, and then Blaine added: “BECAUSE IF IT’S NOT, YOU AND YOUR WOMAN ARE\nGOING TO DIE, NO MATTER WHERE YOU CAME FROM.”\nSusannah looked from the box on the gate to Eddie. “What’s it talking about?”\nshe hissed.\n\nEddie shook his head. “I don’t have the slightest idea.”\n\nTo JAKE, THE ROOM Gasher dragged him into looked like a Minuteman missile silo\n\nwhich had been decorated by the inmates of a lunatic asylum: part museum, part\n\nliving room, part hippie crash pad. Above him, empty space vaulted up to a\nrounded ceiling and below him it dropped seventy-five or a hundred feet to a\nsimilarly rounded base. Running all around the single curved wall in vertical\nlines were tubes of neon in alternating strokes of color: red, blue, green,\nyellow, orange, peach, pink. These long tubes came together in roaring rainbow\nknots at the bottom and top of the silo … if that was what it had been.\nThe room was about three-quarters of the way up the vast capsule-shaped space\nand floored with rusty iron grillework. Rugs that looked Turkish (he later\nlearned that such rugs were actually from a barony called Kashmin) lay on the\ngrilled floor here and there. Their corners were held down with brass-bound\ntrunks or standing lamps or the squat legs of over-stuffed chairs. If not, they\nwould have flapped like strips of paper tied to an electric fan, because a\nsteady warm draft rushed up from below. Another draft, this one issuing from a\ncircular band of venti-lators like the ones in the tunnel they had followed\nhere, swirled about four or five feet above Jake’s head. On the far side of the\nroom was a door identical to the one through which he and Gasher had entered,\nand Jake assumed it was a continuation of the subterranean corridor following\nthe Path of the Beam.\n\nThere were half a do/en people in the room, four men and two women. Jake guessed\nthat he was looking at the Gray high command— if, that was, there were enough\nGrays left to warrant a high command. None of them were young, but all were\nstill in the prime of their lives. They looked at Jake as curiously as he looked\nat them.\n\nSitting in the center of the room, with one massive leg thrown casu-ally over\nthe arm of a chair big enough to be a throne, was a man who looked like a cross\nbetween a Viking warrior and a giant from a child’s fairy-tale. His heavily\nmuscled upper body was naked except for a silver band around one bicep, a\nknife-scabbard looped over one shoulder, and a strange charm about his neck. His\nlower body was clad in soft, tight-fitting leather breeches which were tucked\ninto high boots. He wore a yellow scarf tied around one of these. His hair, a\ndirty gray-blonde, cas-caded almost to the middle of his broad back; his eyes\nwere as green and curious as the eyes of a tomcat who is old enough to be wise\nbut not old enough to have lost that refined sense of cruelty which passes for\nfun in feline circles. Hung by its strap from the back of the chair was what\nlooked like a very old machine-gun.\n\nJake looked more closely at the ornament on the Viking’s chest and saw that it\nwas a coffin-shaped glass box hung on a silver chain. Inside it, a tiny gold\nclock-face marked the time at five minutes past three. Below the face, a tiny\ngold pendulum went back and forth, and despite the soft whoosh of circulating\nair from above and below, he could hear the tick-tock sound it made. The hands\nof the clock were moving faster than they should have done, and Jake was not\nvery surprised to see that they were moving backward.\nHe thought of the crocodile in Peter Pan, the one that was always chasing after\nCaptain Hook, and a little smile touched his lips. Gasher saw it, and raised his\nhand. Jake cringed away, putting his own hands to his face.\nThe Tick-Tock Man shook his finger at Gasher in an amusing school-marmish\ngesture. “Now, now … no need of that, Gasher,” he said.\nGasher lowered his hand at once. His face had changed completely. Before, it had\nalternated between stupid rage and a species of cunning, almost existential\nhumor. Now he only looked servile and adoring. Like the others in the room (and\nJake himself), the Gasherman could not look away from Tick-Tock for long; his\neyes were drawn inexorably back. And Jake could understand why. The Tick-Tock\nMan was the only person here who seemed wholly vital, wholly healthy, and wholly\nalive.\n\n“If you say there’s no need, there ain’t,” Gasher said, but he favored Jake with\na dark look before shifting his eyes back to the blonde giant on the throne.\n“Still, he’s wery pert, Ticky. Wery pert, Ticky. Wery pert indeed, so he is, and\nif you want my opinion, he’ll take a deal of training!”\n\n“When I want your opinion, I’ll ask for it, the Tick-Tock Man said. “Now close\n\nthe door, Cash—was yon lx>re in a barn?”\nA dark-haired woman laughed shrilly, a sound like the caw of a crow. Tick-Tock\nflicked his eyes toward her; she quieted at once and cast her eyes down to the\ngrilled floor.\n\nThe door through which Gasher had dragged him was actually two doors. The\narrangement reminded Jake of the way spaceship airlocks looked in the more\nintelligent science fiction movies. Gasher shut them both and turned to\nTick-Tock, giving him a thumbs-up gesture. The Tick-Tock Man nodded and reached\nlanguidly up to press a button set into a piece of furniture that looked like a\nspeaker’s podium. A pump began to cycle wheezily within the wall, and the neon\ntubes dimmed perceptibly. There was a faint hiss of air and the valve-wheel of\nthe inside door spun shut. Jake supposed the one in the outer door was doing the\nsame. This was some sort of bomb-shelter, all right; no doubt of that. When the\npump died, the long neon tubes resumed their former muted brilliance.\n“There,” Tick-Tock said pleasantly. His eyes began to look Jake up and down.\nJake had a clear and very uncomfortable sense of being expertly catalogued and\nfiled. “All safe and sound, we are. Snug as bugs in a rug. Right, Hoots?”\n“Yar!” a tall, skinny man in a black suit replied promptly. His face was covered\nwith some sort of rash which he scratched obsessively.\n“I brung him,” Gasher said. “I told yer you could trust me to do it, and didn’t\nI?”\n\n“You did,” Tick-Tock said. “Bang on. I had some doubts about your ability to\nremember the password at the end, there, but—”\nThe dark-haired woman uttered another shrill caw. The Tick-Tock Man half-turned\nin her direction, that lazy smile dimpling the corners of his mouth, and before\nJake was able to grasp what was happening—what had already happened—she was\nstaggering backward, her eyes bulging in surprise and pain, her hands groping at\nsome strange tumor in the middle of her chest which hadn’t been there a second\nbefore.\n\nJake realized die Tick-Tock Man had made some sort of move as he was turning, a\nmove so quick it had been no more than a flicker. The slim white hilt which had\nprotruded from the scabbard looped over the Tick-Tock Man’s shoulder was gone.\nThe knife was now on the other side of the room, sticking out of the dark-haired\nwoman’s chest. Tick-Tock had drawn and thrown with an uncanny speed Jake wasn’t\nsure even Roland could match. It had been like some malign magic trick.\nThe others watched silently as the woman staggered toward Tick-Tock, gagging\nharshly, her hands wrapped loosely around the hilt of the knife. Her hip bumped\none of the standing lamps and the one called Hoots darted forward to catch it\nbefore it could fall. Tick-Tock himself never moved; he only went on sitting\nwith his leg tossed over the arm of his throne, watching the woman with his lazy\nsmile.\n\nHer foot caught beneath one of the rugs and she tumbled forward. Once more the\nTick-Tock Man moved with that spooky speed, pulling back the foot which had been\ndangling over the arm of the chair and then driving it forward again like a\npiston. It buried itself in the pit of the dark-haired woman’s stomach and she\nwent flying backward. Blood spewed from her mouth and splattered the furniture.\nShe struck the wall, slid down it, and ended up sitting with her chin on her\nbreastbone. To Jake she looked like a movie Mexican taking a siesta against an\nadobe wall. It was hard for him to believe she had gone from living to dead with\nsuch terrible speed. Neon tubes turned her hair into a haze that was half red\nand half blue. Her glazing eyes stared at the Tick-Tock Man with terminal\namazement.\n\n“I told her about that laugh,” Tick-Tock said. His eyes shifted to the other\nwoman, a heavyset redhead who looked like a long-haul trucker. “Didn’t I,\nTilly?”\n\n“Ay,” Tilly said at once. Her eyes were lustrous with fear and excite-ment, and\nshe licked her lips obsessively. “So you did, many and many a time. I’ll set my\nwatch and warrant on it.”\n\n“So you might, if you could reach up your fat ass far enough to find them,”\n\nTick-Tock said. “Bring me my knife, Brandon, and mind you wipe that slut’s stink\noff it before you put it in my hand.”\n\nA short, bandy-legged man hopped to do as he had been bidden. The knife wouldn’t\ncome free at first; it seemed caught on the unfortu-nate dark-haired woman’s\nbreastbone. Brandon threw a terrified glance over his shoulder at the Tick-Tock\nMan and then tugged harder.\n\nTick-Tock, however, appeared to have forgotten all about both Bran-don and the\nwoman who had literally laughed herself to death. His bril-liant green eyes had\nfixed on something which interested him much more than the dead woman.\n“Come here, cully,” he said. “I want a better look at you.”\nGasher gave him a shove. Jake stumbled forward. He would have fallen if\nTick-Tock’s strong hands hadn’t caught him by the shoulders. It was Jake’s Seiko which had drawn his interest.\n“If this here’s what I think it is, it’s an omen for sure and true,” Tick-Tock\nsaid. “Talk to me, boy—what’s this sigul you wear?”\nJake, who hadn’t the slightest idea what a sigul was, could only hope for the\nbest. “It’s a watch. But it doesn’t work, Mr. Tick-Tock.”\nHoots chuckled at that, then clapped both hands over his mouth when the\nTick-Tock Man turned to look at him. After a moment, Tick-Toc looked back at\nJake, and a sunny smile replaced the frown. Looking at that smile almost made\nyou forget that it was a dead woman and not a movie Mexican taking a siesta over\nthere against the wall. Looking at it almost made you forget that these people\nwere crazy, and the Tick-Tock Man was likely the craziest inmate in the whole\nasylum.\n\n“Watch,” Tick-Tock said, nodding. “Ay, a likely enough name for such; after all,\nwhat does a person want with a timepiece but to watch it once in a while? Oh! No. No, it’s not boobyrigged.” Jake touched his own finger to the face\nof the watch.\n\n“That means nothing, if it’s set to the frequency of your own body,” the\nTick-Tock Man said. He spoke in the sharp, scornful tone Jake’s father used when\nhe didn’t want people to figure out that he didn’t have the slightest idea what\nhe was talking about. He thought it was partly pleasure at his own courage but\nmostly simple wonder and interest.\n\n“May I have it?” he asked Jake silkily. “As a gesture of your goodwill, shall we\nsay? Why, my father—”\n“Your father died so blowed-out-rotten with the mandrus that not even the dogs\nwould eat him,” the Tick-Tock Man interrupted. “Now shut up, you idiot.”\nAt first Gasher looked furious . . . and then only abashed. At least I’m pretty sure it does. I’ve\nnever had to replace it, and I lost the instruction folder a long time ago.”\nThe Tick-Tock Man looked at him for a long time without speaking, and Jake\nrealized with dismay that the blonde man was trying to decide if Jake had been\nmaking fun of him. If he decided Jake had been making fun, Jake had an idea that\nthe abuse he had suffered on the way here would seem like tickling compared to\nwhat the Tick-Tock Man might do. He suddenly wanted to divert Tick-Tock’s train\nof thought—wanted that more than anything in the world. If Tick-Tock chose to tighten his grip and pull sharply forward,\nhe would snap Jake’s collarbones like pencils. David Quick.”\n\nThe Tick-Tock Man’s eyes widened in surprise and amazement. Then he threw back\nhis head and roared out a gust of laughter that echoed off the domed ceiling\nhigh above. But we’ll take care of that, won’t we, Gasher?”\nIt’s not Gasher he’s talking to, Jake thought. It’s me. He thinks he’s\nhypnotizing me . . . and maybe he is.\n\n“Ay,” Gasher breathed.\n\nJake felt he was drowning in those wide green eyes. Although the Tick-Tock Man’s
18Part 17Darktower 3 - The Waste Lands\n\ngrip was still not really tight, he couldn’t get enough breath into his lungs.\nHe summoned all of his own force in an effort to break the blonde man’s hold\nover him, and again spoke the first words which came to mind:\n“So fell Lord Perth, and the countryside did shake with that thunder.”\nIt acted upon Tick-Tock like a hard open-handed blow to the face. Just what might that be, cully?”\n“Jake Chambers.” With his nose pinched shut, his voice sounded nasal and foggy.\n“And are you a Not-See, Jake Chambers?”\n\nFor a moment Jake wondered if this was a peculiar way of asking him if he was\nblind . . . but of course they could all see he wasn’t. “I don’t understand\nwhat—“\n\nTick-Tock shook him back and forth by the nose. “Not-See! Not-See! It wouldn’t budge. That didn’t much surprise him, but it presented serious\nproblems.\n\nOy stood by his left boot, looking up anxiously, waiting for Roland to open the\ndoor so they could continue the journey to Jake. The gunslinger only wished it\nwas that easy. It wouldn’t do to simply stand out here and wait for someone to\nleave; it might be hours or even days before one of the Grays decided to use\nthis particular exit again. Gasher and his friends might take it into their\nheads to flay Jake alive while the gunslinger was waiting for it to happen.\nHe leaned his head against the steel but heard nothing. That didn’t surprise\nhim, either. He had seen doors like this a long time ago—you couldn’t shoot out\nthe locks, and you certainly couldn’t hear through them. There might be one;\nthere might be two, facing each other, with some dead airspace in between.\nSomewhere, though, there would be a button which would spin the wheel in the\nmiddle of the door and release the locks. If Jake could reach that button, all\nmight still be well.\n\nRoland understood that he was not a full member of this ka-tet; he guessed that\neven Oy was more fully aware than he of the secret life which existed at its\nheart (he very much doubted that the bumbler had tracked Jake with his nose\nalone through those tunnels where water ran in polluted streamlets).\nNevertheless, he had been able to help Jake when die boy had been trying to\ncross from his world to this one. He had been able to see . . . and when Jake\nhad been trying to regain the key he had dropped, he had been able to send a\nmessage.\n\nHe had to be very careful about sending messages this time. At best, the Grays\nwould realize something was up. At worst, Jake might misinterpret what Roland\ntried to tell him and do something foolish.\nBut if he could see . . .\n\nRoland closed his eyes and bent all his concentration toward Jake. He thought of\n\nthe boy’s eyes and sent his ka out to find them.\nAt first there was nothing, but at last an image began to form. It was a face\nframed by long, gray-blonde hair. Green eyes gleamed in deep sockets like\nfiredims in a cave. Roland quickly understood that this was the Tick-Tock Man,\nand that he was a descendent of the man who had died in the\nair-carriage—interesting, but of no practical value in this situation. He tried\nto look beyond the Tick-Tock Man, to see the rest of the room in which Jake was\nbeing held, and the people in it.\n\n“Ake,” Oy whispered, as if reminding Roland that this was neither the time nor\nthe place to take a nap.\n\n“Shhh,” the gunslinger said, not opening his eyes.\nBut it was no good. He caught only blurs, probably because Jake’s concentration\nwas focused so tightly on the Tick-Tock Man; everyone and everything else was\nlittle more than a series of gray-shrouded shapes on the edges of Jake’s\nperception.\n\nRoland opened his eyes again and pounded his left fist lightly into the open\npalm of his right hand. He had an idea that he could push harder and see more .\n. . but that might make the boy aware of his presence. That would be dangerous.\nCasher might smell a rat, and if he didn’t the Tick-Tock Man would.\nHe looked up at the narrow ventilator grilles, then down at Oy. He had wondered\nseveral times just how smart he was; now it looked as though he was going to\nfind out.\n\nRoland reached up with his good left hand, slipped his fingers between the\nhorizontal slats of the ventilator grille closest to the hatchway through which\nJake had been taken, and pulled. The grille popped out in a shower of rust and\ndried moss. The hole behind it was far too small for a man . . . but not for a\nbilly-bumbler. Just\ngo and see and come back.”\n\nOy gazed up into his face, saying nothing, not even Jake’s name. Jake, to whom he had made a promise he intended\n\nto keep.\n\nThe gunslinger thought deeply in his strange fashion—that combina-tion of dry\npragmatism and wild intuition which had probably come from his strange\ngrandmother, Deidre the Mad, and had kept him alive all these years after his\nold companions had passed. Get it done. My heart\ngoes with you.”\n\n“Oy! Art! Ake!” the bumbler whispered, and then scurried off into the darkness\nagain.\n\nRoland waited for all hell to break loose.\n\nASK ME A QUESTION, Eddie Dean of New York. And it better be a good one . . . if\nit’s not, you and your woman are going to die, no matter where you came from.\nAnd, dear God, how did you respond to something like that?\nThe dark red light had gone out; now the pink one reappeared. “Hurry,” the faint\nvoice of Little Blaine urged them. “He’s worse than ever before . . . hurry or\nhe’ll kill you!”\n\nEddie was vaguely aware that flocks of disturbed pigeons were still swooping\naimlessly through the Cradle, and that some of them had smashed headfirst into\nthe pillars and dropped dead on the floor.\n“What does it want?” Susannah hissed at the speaker and the voice of Little\nBlaine somewhere behind it. “For God’s sake, what does it want?”\nNo reply. And Eddie could feel any period of grace they might have started with\nslipping away. He thumbed the TALK/LISTEN and spoke with frantic vivacity as the\nsweat trickled down his cheeks and neck.\nAsk me a question.\n\n“So—Blaine! What have you been up to these last few years? I guess you haven’t\nbeen doing the old southeast run, huh? Any reason why not? Haven’t been feeling\nup to snuff?”\n\nNo sound but the rustle and flap of the pigeons. In his mind he saw Ardis trying\nto scream as his cheeks melted and his tongue caught fire.\nHe felt the hair on the nape of his neck stirring and clumping together. Fear?\nOr gathering electricity?\n\nHurry . . . he’s worse than ever before.\n“Who built you, anyway?” Eddie asked frantically, thinking: If I only knew what\nthe fucking thing wanted! “Want to talk about that? Was it the Grays? Nah . . .\nprobably the Great Old Ones, right? Or . . .”\nHe trailed off. Now he could feel Blaine’s silence as a physical weight on his\nskin, like fleshy, groping hands.\n\n“What do you want?” he shouted. “Just what in hell do you want to hear?”\nNo answer—but the buttons on the box were glowing an angry dark red again, and\nEddie knew their time was almost up. He could hear a low buzzing sound nearby—a\nsound like an electrical generator—and he didn’t believe that sound was just his\nimagination, no matter how much he wanted to think so.\n“Blaine!” Susannah shouted suddenly. “Blaine, do you hear me?”\nNo answer . . . and Eddie felt the air was filling up with electricity as a bowl\nunder a tap fills up with water. He could feel it crackling bitterly in his nose\nwith every breath he took; could feel his fillings buzzing like angry insects.\n“Blaine, I’ve got a question, and it is a pretty good one! Listen!” She closed\nher eyes for a moment, fingers rubbing frantically at her temples, and then\nopened her eyes again. ” ‘There is a thing that . . . uh . . . that nothing is,\nand yet it has a name; ’tis sometimes tall and . . . and sometimes short . . .’\n” She broke off and stared at Eddie with wide, agonized eyes. “Help me! I can’t\nremember how the rest of it goes!”\n\nEddie only stared at her as if she had gone mad. What in the name of God was she\n\ntalking about? Then it came to him, and it made a weirdly perfect sense, and the\nrest of the riddle clicked into his mind as neatly as the last two pieces of a\njigsaw puzzle. He swung toward the speaker again.\n” ‘It joins our talks, it joins our sport, and plays at every game.’ What is it?\nThat’s our question, Blaine—what is it?”\nThe red light illuminating the COMMAND and ENTER buttons below the diamond of\nnumbers blinked out. There was an endless moment of silence before Blaine spoke\nagain . . . but Eddie was aware that the feeling of electricity crawling all\nover his skin was diminishing.\n\n“A SHADOW, OF COURSE,” the voice of Blaine responded. “AN EASY ONE . . . BUT NOT\nBAD. NOT BAD AT ALL.”\n\nThe voice coming out of the speaker was animated by a thoughtful quality . . .\nand something else, as well. Pleasure? Longing? Eddie couldn’t quite decide, but\nhe did know there was something in that voice that reminded him of Little\nBlaine. He knew something else, as well: Susannah had saved their bacon, at\nleast for the time being. He bent down and kissed her cold, sweaty brow.\n“DO YOU KNOW ANY MORE RIDDLES?” Blaine asked.\n“Yes, lots,” Susannah said at once. “Our companion, Jake, has a whole book of\nthem.”\n\n“FROM THE NEW YORK PLACE OF WHERE?” Blaine asked, and now the tone of his voice\nwas perfectly clear, at least to Eddie. Blaine might be a machine, but Eddie had\nbeen a heroin junkie for six years, and he knew stone greed when he heard it.\n“From New York, right,” he said. “But Jake has been taken prisoner. A man named\nGasher took him.”\n\nNo answer . . . and then the buttons glowed that faint, rosy pink again. “Good\nso far,” the voice of Little Blaine whispered. “But you must be careful . . .\nhe’s tricky. …”\n\nThe red lights reappeared at once.\n\n“DID ONE OF YOU SPEAK?” Blaine’s voice was cold and—Eddie could have sworn it\nwas so—suspicious.\n\nHe looked at Susannah. Susannah looked back with the wide, fright-ened eyes of a\nlittle girl who has heard something unnameable moving slyly beneath the bed.\n“I cleared my throat, Blaine,” Eddie said. He swallowed and armed sweat from his\nforehead. “I’m . . . shit, tell the truth and shame the devil. I’m scared to\ndeath.”\n\n“THAT IS VERY WISE OF YOU. THESE RIDDLES OF WHICH YOU SPEAK—ARE THEY STUPID? I\nWON’T HAVE MY PATIENCE TRIED WITH STUPID RIDDLES.”\n“Most are smart,” Susannah said, but she looked anxiously at Eddie as she said\nit.\n\n“YOU LIE. YOU DON’T KNOW THE QUALITY OF THESE RIDDLES AT ALL.”\n“How can you say—“\n\n“VOICE ANALYSIS. FRICTIVE PATTERNS AND DIPH-THONG STRESS-EMPHASIS PROVIDE A\nRELIABLE QUOTIENT OF TRUTH/UNTRUTH. PREDICTIVE RELIABILITY IS 97 PER CENT, PLUS\nOR MINUS .5 PER CENT.” The voice fell silent for a moment, and when it spoke\nagain, it did so in a menacing drawl that Eddie found very familiar. It was the\nvoice of Humphrey Bogart. “I SHUGGEST YOU SHTICK TO WHAT YOU KNOW, SHWEET-HEART.\nTHE LAST GUY THAT TRIED SHADING THE TRUTH WITH ME WOUND UP AT THE BOTTOM OF THE\nSEND IN A PAIR OF SHEMENT COWBOY BOOTS.”\n“Christ,” Eddie said. “We walked four hundred miles or so to meet the computer\nversion of Rich Little. How can you imitate guys like John Wayne and Humphrey\nBogart, Blaine? Guys from our world?”\n\nNothing.\n\n“Okay, you don’t want to answer that one. How about this one—if a riddle was\nwhat you wanted, why didn’t you just say so?”\nAgain there was no answer, but Eddie discovered that he didn’t really need one.\nBlaine liked riddles, so he had asked them one. Susannah had solved it. Eddie\nguessed that if she had failed to do so, the two of them would now look like a\ncouple of giant-economy-size charcoal bri-quets lying on the floor of the Cradle\n\nof Lud.\n\n“Blaine?” Susannah asked uneasily. There was no answer. “Blaine, are you still\nthere?”\n\n“YES. TELL ME ANOTHER ONE.”\n\n“When is a door not a door?” Eddie asked.\n“WHEN IT’S AJAR. YOU’LL HAVE TO DO BETTER THAN THAT IF YOU REALLY EXPECT ME TO\nTAKE YOU SOME-WHERE. CAN YOU DO BETTER THAN THAT?”\n“If Roland gets here, I’m sure we can,” Susannah said. “Regardless of how good\nthe riddles in Jake’s book may be, Roland knows hundreds— he actually studied\nthem as a child.” Having said this, she realized she could not conceive of\nRoland as a child. “Will you take us, Blaine?”\n“I MIGHT,” Blaine said, and Eddie was quite sure he heard a dim thread of\ncruelty running through that voice. “BUT YOU’LL HAVE TO PRIME THE PUMP TO GET ME\nGOING, AND MY PUMP PRIMES BACKWARD.”\n\n“Meaning what?” Eddie asked, looking through the bars at the smooth pink line of\nBlaine’s back. But Blaine did not reply to this or any of the other questions\nthey asked. The bright orange lights stayed on, but both Big Blaine and Little\nBlaine seemed to have gone into hiberna-tion. Eddie, however, knew better.\nBlaine was awake. Blaine was watch-ing them. Blaine was listening to their\nfrictive patterns and diphthong stress-emphasis.\nHe looked at Susannah.\n\n” ‘You’ll have to prime the pump, but my pump primes backward,’ ” he said\nbleakly. “It’s a riddle, isn’t it?”\n\n“Yes, of course.” She looked at the triangular window, so like a half-lidded,\nmocking eye, and then pulled him close so she could whisper in his ear. “It’s\ntotally insane, Eddie—schizophrenic, paranoid, probably delusional as well.”\n“Tell me about it,” he breathed back. “What we’ve got here is a lunatic genius\nghost-in-the-computer monorail that likes riddles and goes faster than the speed\nof sound. Welcome to the fantasy version of One Flew Over the Cuckoo’s Nest.”\n“Do you have any idea what the answer is?”\nEddie shook his head. “You?”\n\n“A little tickle, way back in my mind. False light, probably. I keep thinking\nabout what Roland said: a good riddle is always sensible and always solvable.\nIt’s like a magician’s trick.”\n\n“Misdirection.”\n\nShe nodded. “Go fire another shot, Eddie—let em know we’re still here.”\n“Yeah. Now if we could only be sure that they’re still there.”\n“Do you think they are, Eddie?”\n\nEddie had started away, and he spoke without stopping or looking back. “I don’t\nknow—that’s a riddle not even Blaine could answer.”\n\n“COULD I HAVE SOMETHING to drink?” Jake asked. His voice came out sounding furry\nand nasal. Both his mouth and the tissues in his abused nose were swelling up.\nHe looked like someone who has gotten the worst of it in a nasty street-fight.\n“Oh, yes,” Tick-Tock replied judiciously. “You could. I’d say you certainly\ncould. We have lots to drink, don’t we, Copperhead?”\n“Ay,” said a tall, bespectacled man in a white silk shirt and a pair of black\nsilk trousers. He looked like a college professor in a turn-of-the-century Punch\ncartoon. “No shortage of po-ter-bulls here.”\nThe Tick-Tock Man, once more seated at ease in his throne-like chair, looked\nhumorously at Jake. “We have wine, beer, ale, and, of course, good old water.\nSometimes that’s all a body wants, isn’t it? Cool, clear, sparkling water. How\ndoes that sound, cully?”\n\nJake’s throat, which was also swollen and as dry as sandpaper, prick-led\npainfully. “Sounds good,” he whispered.\n\n“It’s woke my thirsty up, I know that,” Tick-Tock said. His lips spread in a\nsmile. His green eyes sparkled. “Bring me a dipper of water, Tilly—I’ll be\n\ndamned if I know what’s happened to my manners.”\n\nTilly stepped through the hatchway on the far side of the room—it was opposite\nthe one through which Jake and Gasher had entered. Jake watched her go and\nlicked his swollen lips.\n\n“Now,” Tick-Tock said, returning his gaze to Jake, “you say the American city\nyou came from—this New York—is much like Lud.”\n“Well . . . not exactly …”\n\n“But you do recognize some of the machinery, Tick-Tock pressed. “Valves and\npumps and such. Not to mention the firedim tubes.”\n“Yes. We call it neon, but it’s the same.”\nTick-Tock reached out toward him. Jake cringed, but Tick-Tock only patted him on\nthe shoulder. “Yes, yes; close enough.” His eyes gleamed. “And you’ve heard of\ncomputers?”\n\n“Sure, but—“\n\nTilly returned with the dipper and timidly approached the Tick-Tock Man’s\nthrone. He took it and held it out to Jake. When Jake reached for it, Tick-Tock\npulled it back and drank himself. As Jake watched the water trickle from\nTick-Tock’s mouth and roll down his naked chest, he began to shake. He couldn’t\nhelp it.\n\nThe Tick-Tock Man looked over the dipper at him, as if just remem-bering that\nJake was still there. Behind him, Gasher, Copperhead, Bran-don, and Hoots were\ngrinning like schoolyard kids who have just heard an amusing dirty joke.\n“Why, I got thinking about how thirsty I was and forgot all about you!”\nTick-Tock cried. “That’s mean as hell, gods damn my eyes! But, of course, it\nlooked so good . . . and it is good . . . cold . . . clear …”\nHe held the dipper out to Jake. When Jake reached for it, Tick-Tock pulled it\nback.\n\n“First, cully, tell me what you know about dipolar computers and transitive\ncircuits,” he said coldly.\n\n“What …” Jake looked toward the ventilator grille, but the golden eyes were\nstill gone. He was beginning to think he had imagined them after all. He shifted\nhis gaze back to the Tick-Tock Man, understanding one thing clearly: he wasn’t\ngoing to get any water. He had been stupid to even dream he might. “What are\ndipolar computers?”\n\nThe Tick-Tock Man’s face contorted with rage; he threw the remain-der of the\nwater into Jake’s bruised, puffy face. “Don’t you play it light with me!” he\nshrieked. He stripped off the Seiko watch and shook it in front of Jake. “When I\nasked you if this ran on a dipolar circuit, you said it didn’t! So don’t tell me\nyou don’t know what I’m talking about when you already made it clear that you\ndo!”\n\n“But . . . but …” Jake couldn’t go on. His head was whirling with fear and\nconfusion. He was aware, in some far-off fashion, that he was licking as much\nwater as he could off his lips.\n\n“There’s a thousand of those ever-fucking dipolar computers right under the\never-fucking city, maybe a HUNDRED thousand, and the only one that still works\ndon’t do a thing except play Watch Me and run those drums! I want those\ncomputers! I want them working for ME!”\n\nThe Tick-Tock Man bolted forward on his throne, seized Jake, shook him back and\nforth, and then threw him to the floor. Jake struck one of the lamps, knocking\nit over, and the bulb blew with a hollow coughing sound. Tilly gave a little\nshriek and stepped backward, her eyes wide and frightened. Copperhead and\nBrandon looked at each other uneasily.\n\nTick-Tock leaned forward, elbows on his thighs, and screamed into Jake’s face:\n“I want them AND I MEAN TO HAVE THEM!”\n\nSilence fell in the room, broken only by the soft whoosh of warm air pouring\nfrom the ventilators. Then the twisted rage on the Tick-Tock Man’s face\ndisappeared so suddenly it might never have existed at all. It was replaced by\nanother charming smile. He leaned further forward and helped Jake to his feet.\n“Sorry. I get thinking about the potential of this place and sometimes I get\n\ncarried away. Please accept my apology, cully.” He picked up the overturned\n\ndipper and threw it at Tilly. “Fill this up, you useless bitch! What’s the\nmatter with you?”\n\nHe turned his attention back to Jake, still smiling his TV game-show host smile.\n“All right; you’ve had your little joke and I’ve had mine. Now tell me\neverything you know about dipolar computers and transitive circuits. Then you\ncan have a drink.”\n\nJake opened his mouth to say something—he had no idea what— and then,\nincredibly, Roland’s voice was in his mind, filling it.\nDistract them, Jake—and if there’s a button that opens the door, get close to\nit.\n\nThe Tick-Tock Man was watching him closely. “Something just came into your mind,\ndidn’t it, cully? I always know. So don’t keep it a secret; tell your old friend\nTicky.”\n\nJake caught movement in the corner of his eye. Although he did not dare glance\nup at the ventilator panel—not with all the Tick-Tock Man’s notice bent upon\nhim—he knew that Oy was back, peering down through the louvers.\nDistract them . . . and suddenly Jake knew just how to do that.\n“I did think of something,” he said, “but it wasn’t about computers. It was\nabout my old pal Gasher. And his old pal, Hoots.”\n“Here! Here!” Gasher cried. “What are you talking about, boy?”\n“Why don’t you tell Tick-Tock who really gave you the password, Gasher? Then I\ncan tell Tick-Tock where you keep it.”\n\nThe Tick-Tock Man’s puzzled gaze shifted from Jake to Gasher. “What’s he talking\nabout?”\n\n“Nothin!” Gasher said, but he could not forbear a quick glance at Hoots. “He’s\njust runnin his gob, tryin to get off the hot-seat by puttin me on it, Ticky. I\ntold you he was pert! Didn’t I say—“\n\nTake a look in his scarf, why don’t you?” Jake asked. “He’s got a scrap of paper\nwith the word written on it. I had to read it to him because he couldn’t even do\nthat.”\n\nThere was no sudden rage on Tick-Tock’s part this time; his face darkened\ngradually instead, like a summer sky before a terrible thunderstorm.\n“Let me see your scarf, Gasher,” he said in a soft, thick voice. “Let your old\npal sneak a peek.”\n\n“He’s lyin, I tell you!” Gasher cried, putting his hands on his scarf and taking\ntwo steps backward toward the wall. Directly above him, Oy’s gold-ringed eyes\ngleamed. “All you got to do is look in his face to see lyin’s what a pert little\ncull like him does best!”\n\nThe Tick-Tock Man shifted his gaze to Hoots, who looked sick with fear. “What\nabout it?” Tick-Tock asked in his soft, terrible voice. “What about it,\nHooterman? I know you and Gasher was butt-buddies of old, and I know you’ve the\nbrains of a hung goose, but surely not even you could be stupid enough to write\ndown a password to the inner chamber . . . could you? Could you?”\n“I … I oney thought . . .” Hoots began.\n“Shut up!” Gasher shouted. He shot Jake a look of pure, sick hate. “I’ll kill\nyou for this, dearie—see if I don’t.”\n\n“Take off your scarf, Gasher,” the Tick-Tock Man said. “I want a look inside\nit.”\n\nJake sidled a step closer to the podium with the burtons on it.\n“No!” Gasher’s hands returned to the scarf and pressed against it as if it might\nfly away of its own accord. “Be damned if I will!”\n“Brandon, grab him,” Tick-Tock said.\n\nBrandon lunged for Gasher. Gasher’s move wasn’t as quick as Tick-Tock’s had\nbeen, but it was quick enough; he bent, yanked a knife from the top of his boot,\nand buried it in Brandon’s arm.\n\n“Oh, you barstard!” Brandon shouted in surprise and pain as blood began to pour\nout of his arm.\n\n“Lookit what you did!” Tilly screamed.\n\n“Do I have to do everything around here myself?” Tick-Tock shouted, more\n\nexasperated than angry, it seemed, and rose to his feet. Gasher retreated from\nhim, weaving the bloody knife back and forth in front of his face in mystic\npatterns. He kept his other hand planted firmly on top of his head.\n“Draw back,” he panted. “I loves you like a brother, Ticky, but if you don’t\ndraw back, I’ll hide this blade in your guts—so I will.”\n“You? Not likely,” the Tick-Tock Man said with a laugh. He removed his own knife\nfrom its scabbard and held it delicately by the bone hilt. All eyes were on the\ntwo of them. Jake took two quick steps to the podium with its little cluster of\nbuttons and reached for the one he thought the Tick-Took man had pushed.\nGasher was backing along the curved wall, the tubes of light painting his\nmandrus-riddled face in a succession of sick colors: bile-green, fever-red,\njaundice-yellow. Now it was the Tick-Tock Man standing below the ventilator\ngrille where Oy was watching.\n\n“Put it down, Gasher,” Tick-Tock said in a reasonable tone of voice. “You\nbrought the boy as I asked; if anyone else gets pricked over this, it’ll be\nHoots, not you. Just show me—“\n\nJake saw Oy crouching to spring and understood two things: what the humbler\nmeant to do and who had put him up to it.\n“Oy, no!” he screamed.\n\nAll of them turned to look at him. At that moment Oy leaped, hitting the flimsy\nventilator grille and knocking it free. The Tick-Tock Man wheeled toward die\nsound, and Oy fell onto his upturned face, biting and slashing.\n\nROLAND HEARD IT FAINTLY even through the twin doors—Oy, no!—and his heart sank.\nHe waited for the valve-wheel to turn, but it did not. He closed his eyes and\nsent with all his might: The door, Jake! Open the door!\nHe sensed no response, and the pictures were gone. His communica-tion line with\nJake, flimsy to begin with, had now been severed.\n\nTHE TICK-TOCK MAN blundered backward, cursing and screaming and grabbing at the\nwrithing, biting, digging thing on his face. He felt Oy’s claws punch into his\nleft eye, popping it, and a horrible red pain sank into his head like a flaming\ntorch thrown down a deep well. At that point, rage overwhelmed pain. He seized\nOy, tore him off his face, and held him over his head, meaning to twist him like\na rag.\n\n“No!” Jake wailed. He forgot about the button which unlocked the doors and\nseized the gun hanging from the back of the chair.\nTilly shrieked. The others scattered. Jake levelled the old German machine-gun\nat the Tick-Tock Man. Oy, upside down in those huge, strong hands and bent\nalmost to the snapping point, writhed madly and slashed his teeth into the air.\nHe shrieked in agony—a horribly human sound.\n“Leave him alone, you bastard!” Jake screamed, and pressed the trigger.\nHe had enough presence of mind left to aim low. The roar of the Schmeisser .\nwas ear-splitting in the enclosed space, although it fired only five or six\nrounds. One of the lighted tubes popped in a burst of cold orange fire. A hole\nappeared an inch above the left knee of the Tick-Tock Man’s tight-fitting\ntrousers, and a dark red stain began to spread at once. Tick-Tock’s mouth opened\nin a shocked O of surprise, an expression which said more clearly than words\ncould have done that, for all his intelligence, Tick-Tock had expected to live a\nlong, happy life where he shot people but was never shot himself. Shot at,\nperhaps, but actually hit? That surprised expression said that just wasn’t\nsupposed to be in the cards.\n\nWelcome to the real world, you fuck, Jake thought.\nTick-Tock dropped Oy to the iron grillework floor to grab at his wounded leg.\n\nCopperhead lunged at Jake, got an arm around his throat, and then Oy was on him,\n\nbarking shrilly and chewing at Copperhead’s ankle through the black silk pants.\nCopperhead screamed and danced away, shaking Oy back and forth at the end of his\nleg. Oy clung like a limpet. Jake turned to see the Tick-Tock Man crawling\ntoward him. He had retrieved his knife and the blade was now clamped between his\nteeth.\n\n“Goodbye, Ticky,” Jake said, and pressed the Schmeisser’s trigger again. Nothing\nhappened. Jake didn’t know if it was empty or jammed, and this was hardly the\ntime to speculate. He took two steps backward before finding further retreat\nblocked by the big chair which had served the Tick-Tock Man as a throne. Before\nhe could slip around, putting the chair between them, Tick-Tock had grabbed his\nankle. His other hand went to the hilt of his knife. The ruins of his left eye\nlay on his cheek like a glob of mint jelly; the right eye glared up at Jake with\ninsane hatred.\n\nJake tried to pull away from the clutching hand and went sprawling on the\nTick-Tock man’s throne. His eye fell on a pocket which had been sewn into the\nright-hand arm-rest. Jutting from the elasticized top was the cracked pearl\nhandle of a revolver.\n\n“Oh, cully, how you’ll suffer!” the Tick-Tock Man whispered ecstati-cally. The O\nof surprise had been replaced by a wide, trembling grin. “Oh how you’ll suffer!\nAnd how happy I’ll be to … What—?”\n\nThe grin slackened and the surprised O began to reappear as Jake pointed the\ncheesy nickel-plated revolver at him and thumbed back the hammer. The grip on\nJake’s ankle tightened until it seemed to him that the bones there must snap.\n“You dasn’t!” Tick-Tock said in a screamy whisper.\n“Yes I do,” Jake said grimly, and pulled the trigger of the Tick-Tock Man’s\nrunout gun. There was a Hat crack, much less dramatic than the Schmeisser’s\nTeutonic roar. A small black hole appeared high up on the right side of\nTick-Tock’s forehead. The Tick-Tock Man went on staring up at Jake, disbelief in\nhis remaining eye.\n\nJake tried to make himself shoot him again and couldn’t do it.\nSuddenly a flap of the Tick-Tock Man’s scalp peeled away like old wallpaper and\ndropped on his right cheek. Roland would have known what this meant; Jake,\nhowever, was now almost beyond coherent thought. A dark, panicky horror was\nspinning across his mind like a tornado funnel. He cringed back in the big chair\nas the hand on his ankle fell away and the Tick-Tock Man collapsed forward on\nhis face.\n\nThe door. He had to open the door and let the gunslinger in.\nFocusing on that and nothing but, Jake let the pearl-handled revolver clatter to\nthe iron grating and pushed himself out of the chair. He was reaching again for\nthe button he thought he had seen Tick-Tock push when a pair of hands settled\naround his throat and dragged him back-ward, away from the podium.\n“I said I’d kill you for it, my narsty little pal,” a voice whispered in his\near, “and the Gasherman always keeps his promises.”\nJake flailed behind him with both hands and found nothing but thin air. Gasher’s\nfingers sank into his throat, choking relentlessly. The world started to turn\ngray in front of his eyes. Gray quickly deepened to purple, and purple to black.\n\nA PUMP STARTED UP, and the valve-wheel in the center of the hatch spun rapidly.\nGods be thanked! Roland thought. He seized the wheel with his right hand almost\nbefore it had stopped moving and yanked it open. The other door was ajar; from\nbeyond it came the sounds of men fighting and Oy’s bark, now shrill with pain\nand fury.\n\nRoland kicked the door open with his boot and saw Gasher throttling Jake. Oy had\nleft Copperhead and was now trying to make Gasher let go of Jake, but Gasher’s\nboot was doing double duty: protecting its owner from the bumbler’s teeth, and\nprotecting Oy from the virulent infection which ran in Gasher’s blood. Brandon\n\nstabbed Oy in the flank again in an effort to make him stop worrying Gasher’s\n\nankle, but Oy paid no heed. Jake hung from his captor’s dirty hands like a\npuppet whose strings have been cut. His face was bluish-white, his swollen lips\na delicate shade of lavender.\n\nGasher looked up. “You,” he snarled.\n\n“Me,” Roland agreed. He fired once and tin; left side of Gasher’s head\ndisintegrated. The man went flying backward, bloodstained yellow scarf\nunravelling, and landed on top of the Tick-Tock Man. His feet drummed\nspastically on the iron grillework for a moment and then fell still.\nThe gunslinger shot Brandon twice, fanning the hammer of his revolver with the\nflat of his right hand. Brandon, who had been bent over Oy for another stroke,\nspun around, struck the wall, and slid slowly down it, clutching at one of the\ntubes. Green swamplight spilled out from between his loosening fingers.\nOy limped to where Jake lay and began licking his pale, still face.\nCopperhead and Hoots had seen enough. They ran side by side for the small door\nthrough which Tilly had gone to get the dipper of water. It was the wrong time\nfor chivalry; Roland shot them both in the back. He would have to move fast now,\nvery fast indeed, and he would not risk being waylaid by these two if they\nshould chance to rediscover their guts.\n\nA cluster of bright orange lights came on at the top of the capsule-shaped\nenclosure, and an alarm began to go off: in broad, hoarse blats that bartered\nthe walls.
