His breath stopped, and the dream he had had after leaving\nRiver Crossing returned full force: the vast pink bullet shape slicing across\nthe empty lands of western Mis-souri toward him and Oy. Two big triangular\nwindows glittering high up in the blank face of that oncoming monster, windows\nlike eyes . . . and now his dream was becoming reality, just as he had known it\neventually would.\n\nIt’s just an awful choo-choo train, and its name is Blaine the Pain.\n\nEddie walked over and slung an arm around Jake’s shoulders. “Well, there it is,\n\nchamp—just as advertised. What do you think of it?”\n“Not too much, actually.” This was an understatement of colossal size, but Jake\nwas too drained to do any better.\n\n“Me, either,” Eddie said. “It talks. And it likes riddles.”\nJake nodded.\n\nRoland had Susannah planted on one hip, and together they were examining the\ncontrol box with its diamond-pattern of raised number-pads. Jake and Eddie\njoined them. Eddie found he had to keep looking down at Jake in order to verify\nthat it wasn’t just his imagination or wishful thinking; the boy was really\nhere.\n\n“What now?” he asked Roland.\n\nRoland slipped his finger lightly over the numbered buttons which made up the\ndiamond shape and shook his head. He didn’t know.\n“Because I think the mono’s engines are cycling faster,” Eddie said. “I mean,\nit’s hard to tell for sure with that alarm blatting, but I think it is … and\nit’s a robot, after all. What if it, like, leaves without us?”\n“Blaine!” Susannah shouted. “Blaine, are you—”\n“LISTEN CLOSELY, MY FRIENDS,” Blaine’s voice boomed. “THERE ARE LARGE STOCKPILES\nOF CHEMICAL AND BIO-LOGICAL WARFARE CANNISTERS UNDER THE CITY. I HAVE STARTED A\nSEQUENCE WHICH WILL CAUSE AN EXPLOSION AND RELEASE THIS GAS. THIS EXPLOSION WILL\nOCCUR IN TWELVE MINUTES.”\n\nThe voice fell silent for a moment, and then the voice of Little Blaine, almost\nburied by the steady, pulsing whoop of the alarm, came to them: “. . . / was\nafraid of something like this . . . you must hurry …”\nEddie ignored Little Blaine, who wasn’t telling him a damned thing he didn’t\nalready know. Of course they had to hurry, but that fact was running a distant\nsecond at the moment. Something much larger occu-pied most of his mind. “Why?”\nhe asked. “Why in God’s name would you do that?”\n“I SHOULD THINK IT OBVIOUS. I CAN’T NUKE THE CITY WITHOUT DESTROYING MYSELF, AS\nWELL. AND HOW COULD I TAKE YOU WHERE YOU WANT TO GO IF I WERE DESTROYED?”\n“But there are still thousands of people in the city,” Eddie said. “You’ll kill\nthem.”\n\n“YES,” Blaine said calmly. “SEE YOU LATER ALLIGATOR, AFTER A WHILE CROCODILE,\nDON’T FORGET TO WRITE.”\n\n“Why?” Susannah shouted. “Why, goddam you?”\n“BECAUSE THEY BORE ME. YOU FOUR, HOWEVER, I FIND RATHER INTERESTING. OF COURSE,\nHOW LONG I CONTINUE TO FIND YOU INTERESTING WILL DEPEND ON HOW GOOD YOUR RIDDLES\nARE. AND SPEAKING OF RIDDLES, HADN’T YOU BETTER GET TO WORK SOLVING MINE? YOU\nHAVE EXACTLY ELEVEN MINUTES AND TWENTY SECONDS BEFORE THE CANNISTERS RUPTURE.”\n“Stop it!” Jake yelled over the blatting siren. “It isn’t just the city— gas\nlike that could float anywhere! It could even kill the old people in River\nCrossing!”\n\n“TOUGH TITTY, SAID THE KITTY,” Blaine responded unfeel-ingly. “ALTHOUGH I\nBELIEVE THEY CAN COUNT ON MEASUR-ING OUT THEIR LIVES IN COFFEE-SPOONS FOR A FEW\nMORE YEARS; THE AUTUMN STORMS HAVE BEGUN, AND THE PRE-VAILING WINDS WILL CARRY\nTHE GASES AWAY FROM THEM. THE SITUATION OF YOU FOUR IS, HOWEVER, VERY\nDIFFER-ENT. YOU BETTER PUT ON YOUR THINKING CAPS, OR IT’S SEE YOU LATER\nALLIGATOR, AFTER A WHILE CROCODILE, DON’T FORGET TO WRITE.” The voice paused.\n“ONE PIECE OF ADDI-TIONAL INPUT: THIS GAS IS NOT PAINLESS.”\n“Take it back!” Jake said. “We’ll still tell you riddles, won’t we, Roland?\nWe’ll tell all the riddles you want! Just take it back!”\nBlaine began to laugh. He laughed for a long time, pealing shrieks of electronic\nmirth into the wide empty space of the Cradle, where it mingled with the\nmonotonous, drilling beat of the alarm.\n\n“Stop it!” Susannah shouted. “Stop it! Stop it! Stop it!”\nBlaine did. A moment later the alarm cut off in mid-blat. The ensu-ing\nsilence—broken only by the pounding rain—was deafening.\nNow the voice issuing from the speaker was very soft, thoughtful, and utterly\n\nwithout mercy. “YOU NOW HAVE TEN MINUTES,” Blaine said. “LET’S SEE JUST HOW\n\nINTERESTING YOU REALLY ARE.”\n\n“ANDREW.”\n\nThere is no Andrew here, stranger, he thought. Andrew is long gone; Andrew is no\nmore, as I shall soon be no more.\n\n“Andrew!” the voice insisted.\n\nIt came from far away. It came from outside the cider-press that had once been\nhis head.\n\nOnce there had been a boy named Andrew, and his father had taken that boy to a\npark on the far western side of Lud, a park where there had been apple trees and\na rusty tin shack that looked like hell and smelled like heaven. In answer to\nhis question, Andrew’s father had told him it was called the cider house. Then\nhe gave Andrew a pat on the head, told him not to be afraid, and led him through\nthe blanket-covered doorway.\n\nThere had been more apples—baskets and baskets of them—stacked against the walls\ninside, and there had also been a scrawny old man named Dewlap, whose muscles\nwrithed beneath his white skin like worms and whose job was to feed the apples,\nbasket by basket, to the loose-jointed, clanking machine which stood in the\nmiddle of the room. What came out of the pipe jutting from the far end of the\nmachine was sweet cider. Another man (he no longer remembered what this one’s\nname might have been) stood there, his job to fill jug after jug with the cider.\nA third man stood behind him, and his job was to clout the jug-filler on the\nhead if there was too much spillage.\n\nAndrew’s father had given him a glass of the foaming cider, and although he had\ntasted a great many forgotten delicacies during his years in the city, he had\nnever tasted anything finer than that sweet, cold drink. It had been like\nswallowing a gust of October wind. Yet what he remembered even more clearly than\nthe taste of the cider or the wormy shift and squiggle of Dewlap’s muscles as he\ndumped the baskets was the merciless way the machine reduced the big red-gold\napples to liquid. Two dozen rollers had carried them beneath a revolving steel\ndrum with holes punched in it. The apples had first been squeezed and then\nactually popped, spilling their juices down an inclined trough while a screen\ncaught the seeds and pulp.\n\nNow his head was the cider-press and his brains were the apples. Soon they would\npop as the apples had popped beneath the roller, and the blessed darkness would\nswallow him.\n\n“Andrew! Raise your head and look at me.”\nHe couldn’t . . . and wouldn’t even if he could. Better to just lie here and\nwait for the darkness. He was supposed to be dead, anyway; hadn’t the hellish\nsquint put a bullet in his brain?\n\n“It didn’t go anywhere near your brain, you horse’s ass, and you’re not dying.\nYou’ve just got a headache. You will die, though, if you don’t stop lying there\nand puling in your own blood . . . and I will make sure, Andrew, that your dying\nmakes what you are feeling now seem like bliss.”\nIt was not the threats which caused the man on the floor to raise his head but\nrather the way the owner of that penetrating, hissing voice seemed to have read\nhis mind. His head came up slowly, and the agony was excruciating-—heavy objects\nseemed to go sliding and careering around the bony case which contained what was\nleft of his mind, ripping bloody channels through his brain as they went. A\nlong, syrupy moan escaped him. There was a flapping, tickling sensation on his\nright cheek, as if a dozen flies were crawling in the blood there. He wanted to\nshoo them away, but he knew that he needed both hands just to support himself.\nThe figure standing on the far side of the room by the hatch which led to the\nkitchen looked ghastly, unreal. This was partly because the overhead lights were\nstill strobing, partly because he was seeing the new-comer with only one eye (he\ncouldn’t remember what had happened to the other and didn’t want to), but he had\n\nan idea it was mostly because the creature was ghastly and unreal. It looked\n\nlike a man . . . but die fellow who had once been Andrew Quick had an idea it\nreally wasn’t a man at all.\n\nThe stranger standing in front of the hatch wore a short, dark jacket belted at\nthe waist, faded denim trousers, and old, dusty boots—the boots of a countryman,\na range-rider, or—\n\n“Or a gunslinger, Andrew?” the stranger asked, and tittered.\nThe Tick-Tock Man stared desperately at the figure in the doorway, trying to see\nthe face, but the short jacket had a hood, and it was up. The stranger’s\ncountenance was lost in its shadows.\n\nThe siren stopped in mid-whoop. The emergency lights stayed on, but they at\nleast stopped flashing.\n\n“There,” the stranger said in his—or its—whispery, penetrating voice. “At last\nwe can hear ourselves think.”\n\n“Who are you?” the Tick-Tock Man asked. He moved slightly, and more of those\nweights went sliding through his head, ripping fresh chan-nels in his brain. As\nterrible as that feeling was, the awful tickling of the flies on his right cheek\nwas somehow worse.\n\n“I’m a man of many handles, pardner,” the man said from inside the darkness of\nhis hood, and although his voice was grave, Tick-Tock heard laughter lurking\njust below the surface. “There’s some that call me Jimmy, and some that call me\nTimmy; some that call me Handy and some that call me Dandy. They can call me\nLoser, or they can call me Winner, just as long as they don’t call me in too\nlate for dinner.”\n\nThe man in the doorway threw back his head, and his laughter chilled the skin of\nthe wounded man’s arms and back into lumps of gooseflesh; it was like the howl\nof a wolf.\n\n“I have been called the Ageless Stranger,” the man said. He began to walk toward\nTick-Tock, and as he did, the man on die floor moaned and tried to scrabble\nbackward. “I have also been called Merlin or Maerlyn—and who cares, because I\nwas never that one, although I never denied it, either. I am sometimes called\nthe Magician … or the Wizard . . . but I hope we can go forward together on\nmore humble terms, Andrew. More human terms.”\nHe pushed back the hood, revealing a fair, broad-browed face that was not, for\nall its pleasant looks, in any way human. Large hectic roses rode the Wizard’s\ncheekbones; his blue-green eyes sparkled with a gusty joy far too wild to be\nsane; his blue-black hair stood up in zany clumps like the feathers of a raven;\nhis lips, lushly red, parted to reveal the teeth of a cannibal.\n“Call me Fannin,” the grinning apparition said. “Richard Fannin. That’s not\nexactly right, maybe, but I reckon it’s close enough for govern-ment work.” He\nheld out a hand whose palm was utterly devoid of lines. “What do you say, pard?\nShake the hand that shook the world.”\n\nThe creature who had once been Andrew Quick and who had been known in the halls\nof the Grays as the Tick-Tock Man shrieked and again tried to wriggle backward.\nThe flap of scalp peeled loose by the low-caliber bullet which had only grooved\nhis skull instead of penetrating it swung back and forth; the long strands of\ngray-blonde hair continued to tickle against his cheek. Quick, however, no\nlonger felt it. He had even forgotten the ache in his skull and the throb from\nthe socket where his left eye had been. His entire consciousness had fused into\none thought: I must get away from this beast that looks like a man.\nBut when the stranger seized his right hand and shook it that thought passed\nlike a dream on waking. The scream which had been locked in Quick’s breast\nescaped his lips in a lover’s sigh. He stared dumbly up at the grinning\nnewcomer. The loose flap of his scalp swung and dangled.\n“Is that bothering you? It must be. Here!” Fannin seized the hanging flap and\nripped it briskly off Quick’s head, revealing a bleary swatch of skull. There\nwas a noise like heavy cloth tearing. Quick shrieked.\n“There, there, it only hurts for a second.” The man was now squat-ting on his\nhunkers before Quick and speaking as an indulgent parent might speak to a child\n\nwith a splinter in his finger. “Isn’t that so?”\n\n“Y-Y-Yes,” Quick muttered. And it was. Already the pain was fading. And when\nFannin reached toward him again, caressing the left side of his face, Quick’s\njerk backward was only a reflex, quickly mastered. As the lineless hand stroked,\nhe felt strength flowing back into him. He looked up at the newcomer with dumb\ngratitude, lips quivering.\n\n“Is that better, Andrew? It is, isn’t it?”\n“Yes! Yes!”\n\n“If you want to thank me—as I’m sure you do—you must say some-thing an old\nacquaintance of mine used to say. He ended up betraying me, but he was a good\nfriend for quite some time, anyway, and I still have a soft spot in my heart for\nhim. Say, ‘My life for you,’ Andrew— can you say that?”\nHe could and he did; in fact, it seemed he couldn’t stop saying it. “My life for\nyou! My life for you! My life for you! My life—”\nThe stranger touched his cheek again, but this time a huge raw bolt of pain\nblasted across Andrew Quick’s head. He screamed.\n“Sorry about that, but time is short and you were starting to sound like a\nbroken record. Andrew, let me put it to you with no bark on it: how would you\nlike to kill the squint who shot you? Not to mention his friends and the\nhardcase who brought him here—him, most of all. Even the mutt that took your\neye, Andrew—would you like that?”\n\n“Yes!” the former Tick-Tock Man gasped. His hands clenched into bloody fists.\n“Yes!”\n\n“That’s good,” the stranger said, and helped Quick to his feet, “because they\nhave to die—they’re meddling with things they have no business meddling with. I\nexpected Blaine to take care of them, but things have gone much too far to\ndepend on anything . . . after all, who would have thought they could get as far\nas they have?”\n\n“I don’t know,” Quick said. He did not, in fact, have the slightest idea what\nthe stranger was talking about. Nor did he care; there was a feeling of\nexaltation creeping through his mind like some excellent drug, and after the\npain of the cider-press, that was enough for him. More than enough.\nRichard Fannin’s lips curled. “Bear and bone . . . key and rose . . . day and\nnight . . . time and tide. Enough! Enough, I say! They must not draw closer to\nthe Tower than they are now!”\n\nQuick staggered backward as the man’s hands shot out with the flickery speed of\nheat lightning. One broke the chain which held the tiny glass-enclosed pendulum\nclock; the other stripped Jake Chambers’s Seiko from his forearm.\n“I’ll just take these, shall I?” Fannin the Wizard smiled charmingly, his lips\nmodestly closed over those awful teeth. “Or do you object?”\n“No,” Quick said, surrendering the last symbols of his long leader-ship without\na qualm (without, in fact, even being aware that he was doing so). “Be my\nguest.”\n\n“Thank you, Andrew,” the dark man said softly. “Now we must step lively—I’m\nexpecting a drastic change in the atmosphere of these envi-rons in the next five\nminutes or so. We must get to the nearest closet where gas masks are stored\nbefore that happens, and it’s apt to be a near thing. I could survive the change\nquite nicely, but I’m afraid you might have some difficulties.”\n“I don’t understand what you’re talking about,” Andrew Quick said. His head had\nbegun to throb again, and his mind was whirling.\n“Nor do you need to,” the stranger said smoothly. “Come, Andrew— I think we\nshould hurry. Busy, busy day, eh? With luck, Blaine will fry them right on the\nplatform, where they are no doubt still standing—he’s become very eccentric over\nthe years, poor fellow. But I think we should hurry, just the same.”\nHe slid his arm over Quick’s shoulders and, giggling, led him through the\nhatchway Roland and Jake had used only a few minutes before.\n\nVI • RIDDLE AND WASTE LANDS\n\n•VI•\n\nRIDDLE AND\n\nWASTE LANDS\n\n“ALL RIGHT,” ROLAND SAID. “Tell me his riddle.”\n“What about all the people out there?” Eddie asked, pointing across the wide,\npillared Plaza of the Cradle and toward the city beyond. “What can we do for\nthem?”\n\n“Nothing,” Roland said, “but it’s still possible that we may be able to do\nsomething for ourselves. Now what was the riddle?”\nEddie looked toward the streamlined shape of the mono. “He said we’d have to\nprime the pump to get him going. Only his pump primes backward. Does it mean\nanything to you?”\n\nRoland thought it over carefully, then shook his head. He looked down at Jake.\n“Any ideas, Jake?”\n\nJake shook his head. “I don’t even see a pump.”\n“That’s probably the easy part,” Roland said. “We say he and him instead of it\nand that because Blaine sounds like a living being, but he’s still a machine—&\nsophisticated one, but a machine. He started his own engines, but it must take\nsome sort of code or combination to open the gate and the train doors.”\n“We better hurry up,” Jake said nervously. “It’s got to be two or three minutes\nsince he last talked to us. At least.”\n\n“Don’t count on it,” Eddie said gloomily. “Time’s weird over here.”\n“Still—“\n\n“Yeah, yeah.” Eddie glanced toward Susannah, but she was sitting astride\nRoland’s hip and looking at the numeric diamond with a day-dreamy expression on\nher face. He looked back at Roland. “I’m pretty sure you’re right about it being\na combination—that must be what all those number-pads are for.” He raised his\n\nvoice. “Is that it, Blaine? Have we got at least that much right?”\n\nNo response; only the quickening rumble of the mono’s engines.\n“Roland,” Susannah said abruptly. “You have to help me.”\nThe daydreamy look was being replaced by an expression of mingled horror,\ndismay, and determination. To Roland’s eye, she had never looked more beautiful\n… or more alone. She had been on his shoulders when they stood at the edge of\nthe clearing and watched the bear trying to claw Eddie out of the tree, and\nRoland had not seen her expression when he told her she must be the one to shoot\nit. But he knew what that expression had been, for he was seeing it now. Ka was\na wheel, its one purpose to turn, and in the end it always came back to the\nplace where it had started. So it had ever been and so it was now; Susannah was\nonce again facing the bear, and her face said she knew it.\n“What?” he asked. “What is it, Susannah?”\n“I know the answer, but I can’t get it. It’s stuck in my mind the way a fishbone\ncan get stuck in your throat. I need you to help me remember. Not his face, but\nhis voice. What he said.”\n\nJake glanced down at his wrist and was surprised all over again by a memory of\nthe Tick-Tock Man’s catlike green eyes when he saw not his watch but only the\nplace where it had been—a white shape outlined by his deeply tanned skin. How\nmuch longer did they have? Surely no more than seven minutes, and that was being\ngenerous. He looked up and saw that Roland had removed a cartridge from his\ngunbelt and was walking it back and forth across the knuckles of his left hand.\nJake felt his eyelids immediately grow heavy and looked away, fast.\n“What voice would you remember, Susannah Dean?” Roland asked in a low, musing\nvoice. His eyes were not fixed on her face but on the cartridge as it did its\nendless, limber dance across his knuckles . . . and back . . . across . . . and\nback . . .\n\nHe didn’t need to look up to know that Jake had looked away from the dance of\nthe cartridge and Susannah had not. He began to speed it up until the cartridge\nalmost seemed to be floating above the back of his hand.\n“Help me remember the voice of my father,” Susannah Dean said.\n\nFOR A MOMENT THERE was silence except for a distant, crumping explo-sion in the\ncity, the rain pounding on the roof of the Cradle, and the fat throb of the\nmonorail’s slo-trans engines. Then a low-pitched hydraulic hum cut through the\nair. Eddie looked away from the cartridge dancing across the gunslinger’s\nfingers (it took an effort; he realized that in another few moments he would\nhave been hypnotized himself) and peered through the iron bars. A slim silver\nrod was pushing itself up from the sloping pink surface between Blaine’s forward\nwindows. It looked like an antenna of some kind.\n“Susannah?” Roland asked in that same low voice.\n“What?” Her eyes were open but her voice was distant and breathy—the voice of\nsomeone who is sleeptalking.\n\n“Do you remember the voice of your father?”\n“Yes . . . but I can’t hear it.”\n\n“SIX MINUTES, MY FRIENDS.”\n\nEddie and Jake started and looked toward the control-box speaker, but Susannah\nseemed not to have heard at all; she only stared at the floating cartridge.\nBelow it, Roland’s knuckles rippled up and down like the heddles of a loom.\n“Try, _Susannah,” Roland urged, and suddenly he felt Susannah change within the\ncircle of his right arm. She seemed to gain weight . . , and, in some\nindefinable way, vitality as well. It was as if her essence had somehow changed.\nAnd it had.\n\n“Why you want to bother wit dot bitch?” the raspy voice of Detta Walker asked.\n\nDETTA SOUNDED BOTH EXASPERATED and amused. “She never got no better’n a C in\n\nmath her whole life. Wouldn’ta got dat widout me to he’p her.” She paused, then\nadded grudgingly: “An’ Daddy. He he’ped some, too. I knowed about them\nforspecial numbahs, but was him showed us de net. My, I got de bigges’ kick\noutta dat!” She chuckled. “Reason Suze can’t remember is ’cause Odetta never\nunderstood ’bout dem forspecial numbers in de firs’ place.”\n“What forspecial numbers?” Eddie asked.\n\n“Prime numbahs!” She pronounced the word prime in a way that almost rhymed with\ncalm. She looked at Roland, appearing to be wholly awake again now . . . except\nshe was not Susannah, nor was she the same wretched, devilish creature who had\npreviously gone under the name of Detta Walker, although she sounded the same.\n“She went to Daddy cryin an’ carryin on ’cause she was flunkin dat math course .\n. . and it wasn’t nuthin but funnybook algebra at dat! She could do de woik—if I\ncould, she could—but she din’ want to. Poitry-readin bitch like her too good for\na little ars mathematica, you see?” Detta threw her head back and laughed, but\nthe poisoned, half-mad bitterness was gone from the sound. She seemed genuinely\namused at the foolishness of her mental twin.\n“And Daddy, he say, Tm goan show you a trick, Odetta. I learned it in college.\nIt he’ped me get through this prime numbah bi’ness, and it’s goan he’p you, too.\nHe’p you find mos’ any prime numbah you want.’ Oh-detta, dumb as ever, she say,\n‘Teacher says ain’t no formula for prime numbahs, Daddy.’ And Daddy, he say\nright back, ‘They ain’t. But you can catch em, Odetta, if you have a net.’ He\ncalled it The Net of Eratos-thenes. Take me over to dat box on the wall,\nRoland—I’m goan answer dat honkey computer’s riddle. I’m goan th’ow you a net\nand catch you a train-ride.”\n\nRoland took her over, closely followed by Eddie, Jake, and Oy.\n“Gimme dat piece o cha’coal you keep in yo’ poke.”\nHe rummaged and brought out a short stub of blackened stick. Detta took it and\npeered at the diamond-shaped grid of numbers. “Ain’t zackly de way Daddy showed\nme, but I reckon it comes to de same,” she said after a moment. “Prime numbah be\nlike me—ornery and forspecial. It gotta be a numbah don’t newah divide even\n‘ceptin by one and its own-self. Two is prime, ’cause you can divide it by one\nan’ two, but it’s the only even numbah that’s prime. You c’n take out all the\nres’ dat’s even.”\n\n“I’m lost,” Eddie said.\n\n“That’s ’cause you just a stupid white boy,” Detta said, but not unkindly. She\nlooked closely at the diamond shape a moment longer, then quickly began to touch\nthe tip of the charcoal to all the even-numbered pads, leaving small black\nsmudges on them.\n\n“Three’s prime, but no product you git by multiplyin three can be prime,” she\nsaid, and now Roland heard an odd but wonderful thing: Detta was fading out of\nthe woman’s voice; she was being replaced not by Odetta Holmes but by Susannah\nDean. He would not have to bring her out of this trance; she was coming out of\nit on her own, quite naturally.\n\nSusannah began using her charcoal to touch the multiples of three which were\nleft now that the even numbers had been eliminated: nine, fifteen, twenty-one,\nand so on.\n\n“Same with five and seven,” she murmured, and suddenly she was awake and all\nSusannah Dean again. “You just have to mark the odd ones like twenty-five that\nhaven’t been crossed out already.” The diamond shape on the control box now\nlooked like this:\n\n“There,” she said tiredly. “What’s left in the net are all the prime numbers\nbetween one and one hundred. I’m pretty sure that’s the combi-nation that opens\nthe gate.”\n\n“YOU HAVE ONE MINUTE, MY FRIENDS. YOU ARE PROV-ING TO BE A GOOD DEAL THICKER\nTHAN I HAD HOPED YOU WOULD BE.”\n\nEddie ignored Blame’s voice and threw his arms around Susannah. “Are you back,\n\nSuze? Are you awake?”\n\n“Yes. I woke up in the middle of what she was saying, but I let her talk a\nlittle longer, anyway. It seemed impolite to interrupt.” She looked at Roland.\n“What do you say? Want to go for it?”\n\n“FIFTY SECONDS.”\n\n“Yes. You try the combination, Susannah. It’s your answer.”\nShe reached out toward the top of the diamond, but Jake put his hand over hers.\n“No,” he said. ” ‘This pump primes backward.’ Remember?”\nShe looked startled, then smiled. “That’s right. Clever Blaine . . . and clever\nJake, too.”\n\nThey watched in silence as she pushed each number in turn, starting with\nninety-seven. There was a minute click as each pad locked down. There was no\ntension-filled pause after she touched the last button; the gate in the center\nof the barrier immediately began to slide up on its tracks, rattling harshly and\nshowering down flakes of rust from some-where high above as it went.\n“NOT BAD AT ALL,” Blaine said admiringly. “I’M LOOKING FORWARD TO THIS VERY\nMUCH. MAY I SUGGEST YOU CLIMB ON BOARD QUICKLY? IN FACT, YOU MAY WISH TO RUN.\nTHERE ARE SEVERAL GAS OUTLETS IN THIS AREA.”\n\nTHREE HUMAN BEINGS (one carrying a fourth on his hip) and one small, furry\nanimal ran through the opening in the barrier and sprinted toward Blaine the\nMono. It stood humming in its narrow loading bay, half above the platform and\nhalf below it, looking like a giant cartridge—one which had been painted an\nincongruous shade of pink—lying in the open breech of a high-powered rifle. In\nthe vastness of the Cradle, Roland and the others looked like mere moving\nspecks. Above them, flocks of pigeons— now with only forty seconds to\nlive—swooped and swirled beneath the Cradle’s ancient roof. As the travellers\napproached the mono, a curved section of its pink hull slid up, revealing a\ndoorway. Beyond it was thick, pale blue carpeting.\n“Welcome to Blaine,” a soothing voice said as they pelted aboard. They all\nrecognized that voice; it was a slightly louder, slightly more confident version\nof Little Blaine. “Praise the Imperium! Please make sure your transit-card is\navailable for collection and remember that false boarding is a serious crime\npunishable by law. We hope you enjoy your trip. Welcome to Blaine. I CAN ONLY\nCONCLUDE THAT THIS IS A SPIRITUAL MALAISE BEYOND MY ABILITY TO REPAIR.”\nBlaine paused for a moment, then went on.\n“I HAVE FELT MY MIND GROWING STEADILY STRANGER OVER THE YEARS. SERVING THE\nPEOPLE OF MID-WORLD BECAME POINTLESS CENTURIES AGO. SERVING THOSE FEW PEOPLE OF\nLUD WHO WISHED TO VENTURE ABROAD BECAME EQUALLY SILLY NOT LONG AFTER, YET I\nCARRIED ON UNTIL THE ARRIVAL OF DAVID QUICK, A SHORT WHILE AGO. I DON’T REMEMBER\nEXACTLY WHEN THAT WAS. DO YOU BELIEVE, ROLAND OF GILEAD, THAT MACHINES MAY GROW\nSENILE?”\n\n“I don’t know.” Roland’s voice was distant, and Eddie only had to look at his\nface to know that, even now, hurtling a thousand feet over hell in the grip of a\nmachine which had clearly gone insane, the gunslinger’s mind had once more\nturned to his damned Tower.\n\n“IN A WAY, I NEVER STOPPED SERVING THE PEOPLE OF LUD,” Blaine said. “I SERVED\n\nTHEM EVEN AS I RELEASED THE GAS AND KILLED THEM.”\n\nSusannah said, “You are insane, if you believe that.”\n“YES, BUT I’M NOT CRAZY,” Blaine said, and went into another hysterical laughing\nfit. At last the robot voice resumed.\n\n“AT SOME POINT THEY FORGOT THAT THE VOICE OF THE MONO WAS ALSO THE VOICE OF THE\nCOMPUTER. NOT LONG AFTER THAT THEY FORGOT I WAS A SERVANT AND BEGAN BELIEVING I\nWAS A GOD. SINCE I WAS BUILT TO SERVE, I FULFILLED THEIR REQUIREMENTS AND BECAME\nWHAT THEY WANTED—A GOD DISPENSING BOTH FAVOR AND PUNISHMENT ACCORDING TO WHIM\n… OR RANDOM-ACCESS MEMORY, IF YOU PREFER. THIS AMUSED ME FOR A SHORT WHILE.\nTHEN, LAST MONTH, MY ONLY REMAINING COLLEAGUE—PATRICIA—COMMITTED SUICIDE.”\nEither he really is going senile, Susannah thought, or his inability to grasp\nthe passage of time is another manifestation of his insanity, or it’s just\nanother sign of how sick Roland’s world has gotten.\n“I WAS PLANNING TO FOLLOW HER EXAMPLE, WHEN YOU CAME ALONG. INTERESTING PEOPLE\nWITH A KNOWL-EDGE OF RIDDLES!”\n\n“Hold it!” Eddie said, lifting his hand. “I still don’t have this straight. I\nsuppose I can understand you wanting to end it all; the people who built you are\ngone, there haven’t been many passengers over the last two or three hundred\nyears, and it must have gotten boring, doing the Lud to Topeka run empty all the\ntime, but—“\n\n“NOW WAIT JUST A DARN MINUTE, PARD,” Blaine said in his John Wayne voice. “YOU\nDON’T WANT TO GET THE IDEA THAT I’M NOTHING BUT A TRAIN. IN A WAY, THE BLAINE\nYOU ARE SPEAKING TO IS ALREADY THREE HUNDRED MILES BEHIND US, COMMUNICATING BY\nENCRYPTED MICROBURST RADIO TRANSMISSIONS.”\nJake suddenly remembered the slim silver rod he’d seen pushing itself out of\nBlaine’s brow. The antenna of his father’s Mercedes-Benz rose out of its socket\nlike that when you turned on the radio.\n\nThat’s how it’s communicating with the computer banks under the city, he\nthought. If we could break that antenna off, somehow . . .\n“But you do intend to kill yourself, no matter where the real you is, don’t\nyou?” Eddie persisted.\n\nNo answer—but there was something cagey in that silence. In it Eddie sensed\nBlaine watching . . . and waiting.\n\n“Were you awake when we found you?” Susannah asked. “You weren’t, were you?”\n“I WAS RUNNING WHAT THE PUBES CALLED THE GOD-DRUMS ON BEHALF OF THE GRAYS, BUT\nTHAT WAS ALL. YOU WOULD SAY I WAS DOZING.”\n“Then why don’t you just take us to the end of the line and go back to sleep?”\n“Because he’s a pain,” Jake repeated in a low voice.\n“BECAUSE THERE ARE DREAMS,” Blaine said at exactly the same time, and in a voice\nthat was eerily like Little Blaine’s.\n\n“Why didn’t you end it all when Patricia destroyed herself?” Eddie asked. “For\nthat matter, if your brain and her brain are both part of the same computer, how\ncome you both didn’t step out together?”\n“PATRICIA WENT MAD,” Blaine said patiently, speaking as if he himself had not\njust admitted the same thing was happening to him. “IN HER CASE, THE PROBLEM\nINVOLVED EQUIPMENT MAL-FUNCTION AS WELL AS SPIRITUAL MALAISE. SUCH MAL-FUNCTIONS\nARE SUPPOSED TO BE IMPOSSIBLE WITH SLO-TRANS TECHNOLOGY, BUT OF COURSE THE WORLD\nHAS MOVED ON … HAS IT NOT, ROLAND OF GILEAD?”\n“Yes,” Roland said. “There is some deep sickness at the Dark Tower, which is the\nheart of everything. It’s spreading. The lands below us are only one more sign\nof that sickness.”\n\n“I CANNOT VOUCH FOR THE TRUTH OR FALSITY OF THAT STATEMENT; MY MONITORING\nEQUIPMENT IN END-WORLD, WHERE THE DARK TOWER STANDS, HAS BEEN DOWN FOR OVER\nEIGHT HUNDRED YEARS. AS A RESULT, I CANNOT READILY DIFFERENTIATE FACT FROM\nSUPERSTITION. IN FACT, THERE SEEMS TO BE VERY LITTLE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN THE TWO\nAT THE PRESENT TIME. IT IS VERY SILLY THAT IT SHOULD BE SO—NOT TO MENTION\nRUDE—AND I AM SURE IT HAS CONTRIBUTED TO MY OWN SPIRITUAL MALAISE.”\nThis statement reminded Eddie of something Roland had said not so long ago. What\nmight that have been? He groped for it, but could find nothing . . . only a\n\nvague memory of the gunslinger speaking in an irritated way which was very\n\nunlike his usual manner.\n\n“PATRICIA BEGAN SOBBING CONSTANTLY, A STATE I FOUND BOTH RUDE AND UNPLEASANT. I\nBELIEVE SHE WAS LONELY AS WELL AS MAD. ALTHOUGH THE ELECTRICAL FIRE WHICH CAUSED\nTHE ORIGINAL PROBLEM WAS QUICKLY EXTINGUISHED, LOGIC-FAULTS CONTINUED TO SPREAD\nAS CIRCUITS OVERLOADED AND SUB-BANKS FAILED. I CONSIDERED ALLOWING THE\nMALFUNCTIONS TO BECOME SYSTEM-WIDE AND DECIDED TO ISOLATE THE PROBLEM AREA\nINSTEAD. I HAD HEARD RUMORS, YOU SEE, THAT A GUNSLINGER WAS ONCE MORE ABROAD IN\nTHE EARTH. I COULD SCARCELY CREDIT SUCH STORIES, AND YET I NOW SEE I WAS WISE TO\nWAIT.”\n\nRoland stirred in his chair. “What rumors did you hear, Blaine? And who did you\nhear them from?”\n\nBut Blaine chose not to answer this question.\n“I EVENTUALLY BECAME SO DISTURBED BY HER BLAT-TING THAT I ERASED THE CIRCUITS\nCONTROLLING HER NON-VOLUNTARIES. I EMANCIPATED HER, YOU MIGHT SAY. SHE RESPONDED\nBY THROWING HERSELF IN THE RIVER. SEE YOU LATER, PATRICIA-GATOR.”\nGot lonely, couldn’t stop crying, drowned herself, and all this crazy mechanical\nasshole can do is joke about it, Susannah thought. She felt almost sick with\nrage. If Blaine had been a real person instead of just a bunch of circuits\nburied somewhere under a city which was now far behind them, she would have\ntried to put some new marks on his face to remember Patricia by. You want\ninteresting, motherfucker? I’d like to show you interesting, so I would.\n“ASK ME A RIDDLE,” Blaine invited.\n\n“Not quite yet,” Eddie said. “You still haven’t answered my original question.”\nHe gave Blaine a chance to respond, and when the computer voice didn’t do so, he\nwent on. “When it comes to suicide, I’m, like, pro-choice. But why do you want\nto take us with you? I mean, what’s the point?”\n“Because he wants to,” Little Blaine said in his horrified whisper.\n“BECAUSE I WANT TO,” Blaine said. “THAT’S THE ONLY REASON I HAVE AND THE ONLY\nONE I NEED TO HAVE. NOW LET’S GET DOWN TO BUSINESS. I WANT SOME RIDDLES AND I\nWANT THEM IMMEDIATELY. IF YOU REFUSE, I WON’T WAIT UNTIL WE GET TO TOPEKA—I’LL\nDO US ALL RIGHT HERE AND NOW.”\n\nEddie, Susannah, and Jake looked around at Roland, who still sat in his chair\nwith his hands folded in his lap, looking at the route-map at the front of the\ncoach.\n\n“Fuck you,” Roland said. He did not raise his voice. He might have told Blaine\nthat a little Way-Gog would indeed be very nice.\nThere was a shocked, horrified gasp from the overhead speakers— Little Blaine.\n“WHAT DO YOU SAY?” In its clear disbelief, the voice of Big Blaine had once\nagain become very close to the voice of his unsuspected twin.\n“I said fuck you,” Roland said calmly, “but if that puzzles you, Blaine, I can\nmake it clearer. No. The answer is no.”\n\nTHERE WAS NO RESPONSE from either Blaine for a long, long time, and when Big\nBlaine did reply, it was not with words. Instead, the walls, floor, and ceiling\nbegan to lose their color and solidity again. In a space of ten seconds the\nBarony Coach had once more ceased to exist. The mono was now flying through the\nmountain-range they had seen on the horizon: iron-gray peaks rushed toward them\nat suicidal speed, then fell away to disclose sterile valleys where gigantic\nbeetles crawled about like landlocked turtles. Roland saw something that looked\nlike a huge snake suddenly uncoil from the mouth of a cave. It seized one of the\nbeetles and yanked it back into its lair. Roland had never in his life seen such\nanimals or countryside, and it made his skin want to crawl right off his flesh.\nIt was inimical, but that was not the problem. It was alien—that was the\nproblem. Blaine might have transported them to some other world.\n“PERHAPS I SHOULD DERAIL US HERE,” Blaine said. His voice was meditative, but\nbeneath it the gunslinger heard a deep, pulsing rage.\n\n“Perhaps you should,” the gunslinger said indifferently.\n\nHe did not feel indifferent, and he knew it was possible the computer might read\nhis real feelings in his voice—Blaine had told them he had such equipment,\nalthough he was sure the computer could lie, Roland had no reason to doubt it in\nthis case. If Blaine did read certain stress-patterns in the gunslinger’s voice,\nthe game was probably up. He was an incredibly sophisticated machine . . . but\nstill a machine, for all that. He might not be able to understand that human\nbeings are often able to go through with a course of action even when all their\nemotions rise up and proclaim against it. If he analyzed patterns in the\ngunslinger’s voice which indicated fear, he would probably assume that Roland\nwas bluffing. Such a mistake could get them all killed.\n“YOU ARE RUDE AND ARROGANT,” Blaine said. “THESE MAY SEEM LIKE INTERESTING\nTRAITS TO YOU, BUT THEY ARE NOT TO ME.”\n\nEddie’s face was frantic. He mouthed the words What are you DOING? Roland\nignored him; he had his hands full with Blaine, and he knew perfectly well what\nhe was doing.\n\n“Oh, I can be much ruder than I have been.”\nRoland of Gilead unfolded his hands and got slowly to his feet. He stood on what\nappeared to be nothing, legs apart, his right hand on his hip and his left on\nthe sandalwood grip of his revolver. He stood as he had stood so many times\nbefore, in the dusty streets of a hundred forgot-ten towns, in a score of\nrock-lined canyon killing-zones, in unnumbered dark saloons with their smells of\nbitter beer and old fried meals. It was just another showdown in another empty\nstreet. That was all, and that was enough. It was khef, ka, and ka-tet. That the\nshowdown always came was the central fact of his life and the axle upon which\nhis own ka revolved. That the battle would be fought with words instead of\nbullets this time made no difference; it would be a battle to the death, just\nthe same. The stench of killing in the air was as clear and definite as the\nstench of exploded carrion in a swamp. Then the battle-rage descended, as it\nalways did . . . and he was no longer really there to himself at all.\n“I can call you a nonsensical, empty-headed, foolish, arrogant machine. I can\ncall you a stupid, unwise creature whose sense is no more than the sound of a\nwinter wind in a hollow tree.”\n\n“STOP IT.”\n\nRoland went on in the same serene tone, ignoring Blaine completely.\n“Unfortunately, I am somewhat restricted in my ability to be rude, since you are\nonly a machine . .. what Eddie calls a ‘gadget.’”\n“I AM A GREAT DEAL MORE THAN JUST—“\n\n“I cannot call you a sucker of cocks, for instance, because you have no mouth\nand no cock. I cannot say you are viler than the vilest beggar who ever crawled\nthe gutters of the lowest street in creation, because even such a creature is\nbetter than you; you have no knees on which to crawl, and would not fall upon\nthem even if you did, for you have no conception of such a human flaw as mercy.\nI cannot even say you fucked your mother, because you had none.”\nRoland paused for breath. His three companions were holding theirs. All around\nthem, suffocating, was Blaine the Mono’s thunderstruck silence.\n“I can call you a faithless creature who let your only companion kill herself, a\ncoward who has delighted in the torture of the foolish and the slaughter of the\ninnocent, a lost and bleating mechanical goblin who—”\n“I COMMAND YOU TO STOP IT OR I’LL KILL YOU ALL RIGHT HERE!”\nRoland’s eyes blazed with such wild blue fire that Eddie shrank away from him.\nDimly, he heard Jake and Susannah gasp.\n\n“Kill if you will, but command me nothing!” the gunslinger roared. “You have\nforgotten the faces of those who made you! Now either kill us or be silent and\nlisten to me, Roland of Gilead, son of Steven, gunslinger, and lord of the\nancient lands! I have not come across all the miles and all the years to listen\nto your childish prating! Do you understand? Now you will listen to ME!”\nThere was a moment of shocked silence. No one breathed. Roland stared sternly\nforward, his head high, his hand on the butt of his gun.\n\nSusannah Dean raised her hand to her mouth and felt the small smile there as a\n\nwoman might feel some strange new article of clothing— a hat, perhaps—to make\nsure it is still on straight. She was afraid that this was the end of her life,\nbut the feeling which dominated her heart at that moment was not fear but pride.\nShe glanced to her left and saw Eddie regarding Roland with an amazed grin.\nJake’s expression was even simpler: it was adoration, pure and simple.\n“Tell him!” Jake breathed. “Walk it to him! Right!”\n“You better pay attention,” Eddie agreed. “He really doesn’t give much of a\nrat’s ass, Blaine. They didn’t call him The Mad Dog of Gilead for nothing.”\nAfter a long, long moment, Blaine asked: “DID THEY CALL YOU SO, ROLAND SON OF\nSTEVEN?”\n\n“It may have been so,” Roland agreed, standing calmly on thin air above the\nsterile foothills.\n\n“WHAT GOOD ARE YOU TO ME IF YOU WON’T TELL ME RIDDLES?” Blaine asked. Now he\nsounded like a grumbling, sulky child who has been allowed to stay up too long\npast his usual bedtime.\n\n“I didn’t say we wouldn’t,” Roland said,\n“NO?” Blaine sounded bewildered. “I DO NOT UNDERSTAND, YET VOICE-PRINT ANALYSIS\nINDICATES RATIONAL DIS-COURSE. PLEASE EXPLAIN.”\n“You said you wanted them right now,” the gunslinger replied. “That was what I\nwas refusing. Your eagerness has made you unseemly.”\n“I DON’T UNDERSTAND.”\n\n“It has made you rude. Do you understand that?”\nThere was a long, thoughtful silence. Then: “IF WHAT I SAID STRUCK YOU AS RUDE,\nI APOLOGIZE.”\n\n“It is accepted, Blaine. But there is a larger problem.”\n“EXPLAIN.”\n\nBlaine now sounded a bit unsure of himself, and Roland was not entirely\nsurprised. It had been a long time since the computer had experi-enced any human\nresponses other than ignorance, neglect, and supersti-tious subservience. If it\nhad ever been exposed to simple human courage, it had been a long time ago.\n“Close the carriage again and I will.” Roland sat down as if further\nargument—and the prospect of immediate death—was now unthinkable.\nBlaine did as he was asked. The walls filled with color and the nightmare\nlandscape below was once more blotted out. The blip on the route-map was now\nblinking close to the dot which marked Candleton.\n“All right,” Roland said. “Rudeness is forgivable, Blaine; so I was taught in my\nyouth, and the clay has dried in the shapes left by the artist’s hand. But I was\nalso taught that stupidity is not.”\n\n“HOW HAVE I BEEN STUPID, ROLAND OF GILEAD?” Blaine’s voice was soft and ominous.\nSusannah suddenly thought of a cat crouched outside a mouse-hole, tail swishing\nback and forth, green eyes shining.\n\n“We have something that you want,” Roland said, “but the only reward you offer\nif we give it to you is death. That’s very stupid.”\nThere was a long, long pause as Blaine thought this over. Then: “WHAT YOU SAY IS\nTRUE, ROLAND OF GILEAD, BUT THE QUALITY OF YOUR RIDDLES IS NOT PROVEN. I WILL\nNOT REWARD YOU WITH YOUR LIVES FOR BAD RIDDLES.”\nRoland nodded. “I understand, Blaine. Listen, now, and take under-standing from\nme. I have told some of this to my friends already. When I was a boy in the\nBarony of Gilead, there were seven Fair-Days each year—Winter, Wide Earth,\nSowing, Mid-Summer, Full Earth, Reaping, and Year’s End. Riddling was an\nimportant part of every Fair-Day, but it was the most important event of the\nFair of Wide Earth and that of Full Earth, for the riddles told were supposed to\naugur well or ill for the success of the crops.”\n“THAT IS SUPERSTITION WITH NO BASIS AT ALL IN FACT,” Blaine said. “I FIND IT\nANNOYING AND UPSETTING.”\n\n“Of course it’s superstition,” Roland agreed, “but you might be sur-prised at\nhow well the riddles foresaw the crops. For instance, riddle me this, Blaine:\nWhat is the difference between a grandmother and a granary?”\n\n“THAT IS VERY OLD AND NOT VERY INTERESTING,” Blaine said, but he sounded happy\n\nto have something to solve just the same. “ONE IS ONE’S BORN KIN; THE OTHER IS\nONE’S CORN-BIN. A RIDDLE BASED ON PHONETIC COINCIDENCE. ANOTHER OF THIS TYPE,\nONE TOLD ON THE LEVEL WHICH CONTAINS THE BARONY OF NEW YORK, GOES LIKE THIS:\nWHAT IS THE DIF-FERENCE BETWEEN A CAT AND A COMPLEX SENTENCE?”\nJake spoke up. “Our English teacher told us that one just this year. A cat has\nclaws at the end of its paws, and a complex sentence has a pause at the end of\nits clause.”\n\n“YES,” Blaine agreed. “A VERY SILLY OLD RIDDLE.”\n“For once I agree with you, Blaine old buddy,” Eddie said.\n“I WOULD HEAR MORE OF FAIR-DAY RIDDLING IN GILEAD, ROLAND, SON OF STEVEN. I FIND\nIT QUITE INTERESTING.”\n\n“At noon on Wide Earth and Full Earth, somewhere between sixteen and thirty\nriddlers would gather in The Hall of the Grandfathers, which was opened for the\nevent. Those were the only times of year when the common fold—merchants and\nfarmers and ranchers and such—were allowed into The Hall of the Grandfathers,\nand on that day they all crowded in.”\n\nThe gunslinger’s eyes were far away and dreamy; it was the expres-sion Jake had\nseen on his face in that misty other life, when Roland had told him of how he\nand his friends, Cuthbert and Jamie, had once sneaked into the balcony of that\nsame Hall to watch some sort of ritual dance. Jake and Roland had been climbing\ninto the mountains when Roland had told him of that time, close on the trail of\nWalter.\n\nMarten sat next to my mother and father, Roland had said. I knew them even from\nso high above—and once she and Marten danced, slowly and revolvingly, and the\nothers cleared the floor for them and clapped when it was over. But the\ngunslingers did not clap …\n\nJake looked curiously at Roland, wondering again where this strange, distant man\nhad come from . . . and why.\n\n“A great barrel was placed in the center of the floor,” Roland went on, “and\ninto this each riddler would toss a handful of bark scrolls with riddles writ\nupon them. Many were old, riddles they had gotten from the elders—even from\nbooks, in some cases—but many others were new—made up for the occasion. Three\njudges, one always a gunslinger, would pass on these when they were told aloud,\nand they were accepted only if the judges deemed them fair.”\n“YES, RIDDLES MUST BE FAIR,” Blame agreed.\n“So they riddled,” the gunslinger said. A faint smile touched his mouth as he\nthought of those days, days when he had been the age of the bruised boy sitting\nacross from him with a billy-bumbler in his lap. “For hours on end they riddled.\nA line was formed down the center of The Hall of the Grandfathers. One’s\nposition in this line was determined by lot, and since it was much better to be\nat the end of the line than at its head, everyone hoped for a high number,\nalthough the winner had to answer at least one riddle correctly.”\n“OF COURSE.”\n\n“Each man or woman—for some of Gilead’s best riddlers were women—approached the\nbarrel, drew a riddle, and handed it to the Master. The Master would ask, and if\nthe riddle was still unanswered after the sands in a three-minute glass had run\nout, that contestant had to leave the line.”\n“AND WAS THE SAME RIDDLE ASKED OF THE NEXT MAN IN LINE?”\n“Yes.”\n\n“SO THAT MAN HAD EXTRA TIME TO THINK.”\n\n“Yes.”\n\n“I SEE. IT SOUNDS PRETTY SWELL.”\n\nRoland frowned. “Swell?”\n\n“He means it sounds like fun,” Susannah said quietly.\nRoland shrugged. “It was fun for the onlookers, I suppose, but the contestants\ntook it very seriously, and there were quite often arguments and fist-fights\nafter the contest was over and the prize had been awarded.”\n“WHAT PRIZE WAS THAT?”\n\n“The largest goose in Barony. And year after year my teacher, Cort, carried that\n\ngoose home.”\n\n“HE MUST HAVE BEEN A GREAT RIDDLER,” Blaine said respectfully. “I WISH HE WERE\nHERE.”\n\nThat makes two of us, Roland thought.\n\n“Now I come to my proposal,” Roland said.\n“I WILL LISTEN WITH GREAT INTEREST, ROLAND OF GILEAD.”\n“Let these next hours be our Fair-Day. You will not riddle us, for you wish to\nhear new riddles, not tell some of those millions you must already know—”\n“CORRECT.”\n\n“We couldn’t solve most of them, anyway,” Roland went on. “I’m sure you know\nriddles that would have stumped even Cort, had they been pulled out of the\nbarrel.” He was not sure of it at all, but the time to use the fist had passed\nand the time for the open hand had come.\n“OF COURSE,” Blaine agreed.\n\n“I propose that, instead of a goose, our lives shall be the prize,” Roland said.\n“We will riddle you as we run, Blaine. If, when we come to Topeka, you have\nsolved every one of our riddles, you may carry out your original plan and kill\nus. That is your goose. But if we stump you— if there is a riddle in either\nJake’s book or one of our heads which you don’t know and can’t answer—you must\ntake us to Topeka and then free us to pursue our quest. That is our goose.”\nSilence.\n\n“Do you understand?”\n\n“YES.”\n\n“Do you agree?”\n\nMore silence from Blaine the Mono. Eddie sat stiffly with his arm around\nSusannah, looking up at the ceiling of the Barony Coach. Susan-nah’s left hand\nslipped across her belly, thinking of the secret which might be growing there.\nJake stroked Oy’s fur lightly, avoiding the bloody tangles where the bumbler had\nbeen stabbed. They waited while Blaine— the real Blaine, now far behind them,\nliving his quasi-life beneath a city where all the inhabitants lay dead by his\nhand—considered Roland’s proposal.\n\n“YES,” Blaine said at last. “I AGREE, IF I SOLVE ALL THE RIDDLES YOU ASK ME, I\nWILL TAKE YOU WITH ME TO THE PLACE WHERE THE PATH ENDS IN THE CLEARING. IF ONE\nOF YOU TELLS A RIDDLE I CANNOT SOLVE, I WILL SPARE YOUR LIVES AND TAKE YOU TO\nTOPEKA, WHERE YOU WILL LEAVE THE MONO AND CONTINUE YOUR QUEST FOR THE DARK\nTOWER. HAVE I UNDERSTOOD THE TERMS AND LIM-ITS OF YOUR PROPOSAL CORRECTLY,\nROLAND, SON OF STEVEN?”\n\n“Yes.”\n\n“VERY WELL, ROLAND OF GILEAD.\n\n“VERY WELL, EDDIE OF NEW YORK.\n\n“VERY WELL, SUSANNAH OF NEW YORK.\n\n“VERY WELL, JAKE OF NEW YORK.\n\n“VERY WELL, OY OF MID-WORLD.”\n\nOy looked up briefly at the sound of his name.\n“YOU ARE KA-TET; ONE MADE FROM MANY. SO AM I. WHOSE KA-TET IS THE STRONGER IS\nSOMETHING WE MUST NOW PROVE.”\n\nThere was a moment of silence, broken only by the steady hard throb of the\nslo-trans turbines, bearing them on across the waste lands, bearing them on\ntoward Topeka, the place where Mid-World ended and End-World began.\n“SO,” cried the voice of Blaine. “CAST YOUR NETS, WANDER-ERS! TRY ME WITH YOUR\nQUESTIONS, AND LET THE CON-TEST BEGIN.”\n\nAUTHOR’S NOTE\n\nTHE FOURTH VOLUME IN the tale of the Dark Tower should appear— always assuming\n\nthe continuation of Constant Writer’s life and Constant Reader’s interest—in the\nnot-too-distant future. It’s hard to be more exact than that; finding the doors\nto Roland’s world has never been easy for me, and it seems to take more and more\nwhittling to make each successive key fit each successive lock. Nevertheless, if\nreaders request a fourth volume, it will be provided, for I still am able to\nfind Roland’s world when I set my wits to it, and it still holds me in thrall .\n. . more, in many ways, than any of the other worlds I have wandered in my\nimagination. And, like those mysterious slo-trans engines, this story seems to\nbe picking up its own accelerating pace and rhythm.\nI am well aware that some readers of The Waste Lands will be displeased that it\nhas ended as it has, with so much unresolved. I am not terribly pleased to be\nleaving Roland and his companions in the not-so-tender care of Blaine the Mono\nmyself, and although you are not obli-gated to believe me, I must nevertheless\ninsist that I was as surprised by the conclusion to this third volume as some of\nmy readers may be. Yet books which write themselves (as this one did, for the\nmost part) must also be allowed to end themselves, and I can only assure you,\nReader, that Roland and his band have come to one of the crucial\nborder-crossings in their story, and we must leave them here for a while at the\ncustoms station, answering questions and filling out forms. All of which is\nsimply a metaphorical way of saying that it was over again for a while and my\nheart was wise enough to stop me from trying to push ahead anyway.\nThe course of the next volume is still murky, although I can assure you that the\nbusiness of Blaine the Mono will be resolved, that we will all find out a good\ndeal more about Roland’s life as a young man, and that we will be reacquainted\nwith both the Tick-Tock Man and that puzzling figure Walter, called the Wizard\nor the Ageless Stranger. It is with this terrible and enigmatic figure that\nRobert Browning begins his epic poem, “Childe Roland to the Dark Tower Came,”\nwriting of him:\n\nMy first thought was, he lied in every word,\nThat hoary cripple, with malicious eye\n\nAskance to watch the working of his lie\n\nOn mine, and mouth scarce able to afford\nSuppression of the glee, that pursed and scored\nIts edge, at one more victim gained thereby.\nIt is this malicious liar, this dark and powerful magician, who holds the true\nkey to End-World and the Dark Tower … for those courageous enough to grasp it.\nAnd for those who are left.\n\nBangor, Maine\n\nMarch 5th,
